REGULATION OF COMMERCE

PREFACE. 

For the objects and activities of the Legal Research Service and the purpose of these annual reviews, the reader is referred to the preface of the 1928-1929 volume (U. S. 

Supreme Court, 1928-1929).

Classification of Material. 

The critics of the 1928-1929 volume have commented on our classification of the cases considered by the Supreme Court. We have adopted a classification along economic, political and other social problems, rather than a classification stressing generally accepted legal subjects. Some of the critics have expressed themselves approvingly on this method; others thought that a book on law should adopt a strictly legal classification.

If the object of these reviews were merely to provide a tool for the practitioner in keeping up with the work of the Court along technical lines, then we should agree to the need of a purely legal classification. This object, however, is accomplished by our U. S. Supreme Court Service, which gives an analysis of the legal questions in each case brought before the Court (see p. 461).

These annual reviews on the Supreme Court of the United States are intended to give the reader a bird's eye view of the work of the Court as an institution of government in a highly complex society. The book is intended not only for the lawyer, but also for the layman. It is our object to treat of the problems of the law as a vehicle for the settlement of important social issues. [ix]

Sometimes an important point of law is made applicable to a relatively unimportant social problem. Sometimes the reverse is true: A relatively unimportant rule of law may play a part in settling or in leaving unsettled an unimportant economic problem. As an illustration how a relatively unimportant rule of law may prevent the solution of an important economic problem, let us take the following:

If A, whose rights against B depend on C's rights against B, and both A and C bring action against B, resulting in favor of B, and then A appeals but C does not, A has no standing on appeal. This rule of law can be made applicable to any legal dispute, such as an action on contract or an action in tort. But this was the rule applied in the Export Cotton 

Rate Case (see p. 62). Offhand, one might say, from the standpoint of the question of law involved, that this was an unimportant case. But the importance of the economic questions involved in that case can not be overestimated. 

Here was involved an important question of competition between various groups of business interests participating in the export of cotton. Here was also involved the still greater question, how far the Interstate Commerce Commission may regulate rates upon considerations other than or in addition to transportation factors. One might go further and say that it also involved the more fundamental question, whether it is possible to regulate rates without consideration of economic factors when the economic factors themselves give rise to the dispute concerning rates. 

A relatively unimportant rule of law is often invoked in the solution of important economic or other social problems. 

Where A contracts with B, an agent of C, to perform a service at a given price, if B has no authority to enter into such contract, A is not obliged to perform. This is a rule which can be made applicable to any contract made with an agent, such as painting a house or building a railroad. But it is also a rule which may involve the important question, how [x]

far are public utilities bound to render service at rates fixed in their contracts with the municipalities, rather than at rates which would yield a fair return on their investments. 

And this was the question which was involved in the Los Angeles Street Railway Case (see p. 93). These illustrations can be multiplied indefinitely, using the very problems discussed in this book. 

Since the main issues before the Supreme Court are economic, political or social, and since the rules of law are merely a means for settling these disputes, we have, in this book, stressed the economic aspects of those problems even more than in the preceding volume. And we recommend this treatment to the members of the legal profession as well as to the laymen, so that they might follow the evolution of the Constitution and laws in terms of the problems which they regulate.

History versus Value. 

The main object of these annual reviews is to present an impartial description or a current history of the work of the Supreme Court. In the 1928-1929 volume we refrained from expressing our own views on the decisions of the Court, with two exceptions: The first was the chapter on the Federal Employers' Liability Act (pp. 152-166), and the second was a comment concerning the admissibility of evidence obtained through unlawful searches and seizures (pp. 198-200). 

This plan evoked favorable comment on the part of some critics, while others expressed the hope that in the future we would give a critical discussion throughout the volume. This year we have expressed our opinions whenever we believed that such elaboration would help to clarify the problems under discussion. In doing so, we have not adhered to any set of doctrines or to any evaluative classification of ideas. At times we found ourselves in disagree- [xi]

ment with "liberals"; at others we found ourselves in disagreement with "conservatives". Nor have we adhered to or favored the views of one group of justices as against another, and on several occasions we found ourselves in disagreement with each other.

We took special care, however, to indicate specifically where we express our own opinions, leaving the remainder of the text as descriptive of the work of the Court. 

Washington, D. C., September 15, 1930.

GREGORY HANKIN, CHARLOTTE A. HANKIN. 

[ xii]

CITATIONS. 

Since at the time of writing this book, the final volumes of the U. S. Reports, of the 

Supreme Court Reporter, or of the Lawyers' 

Edition for October Term, 1929, had not yet been issued, the cases treated herein are cited by docket number, title and date of decision. 

All the cases disposed of by the Supreme Court during the 1929 Term will be found in Volumes 280 and 281 of the U. S. Reports, in 

Volume 50 of the Supreme Court Reporter, and in Volume 74 of the Lawyers' Edition. 

The great majority of the cases considered by the Court were disposed of without opinions. For a ready reference to the questions involved in all the cases docketed during the term, as well as the orders or opinions of the Court in the disposition of the cases, the reader is referred to the U. S. Supreme Court 

Service, October Term, 1929, published by the Legal Research Service. [ xiii]

CHAPTER I. 

Recent Changes in the Supreme Court 

It was a different Supreme Court which adjourned on 

June 2nd, 1930, from the one which opened the term on 

October 3rd, 1929. Chief Justice Taft was succeeded by Chief Justice Hughes, and after a lengthy battle which resulted in the defeat of Judge Parker, Mr. Owen J. Roberts succeeded the late Justice Sanford.

Accomplishments of Chief Justice Taft. 

Chief Justice Taft came to the Supreme Court at a time when this highest tribunal of the country began to feel the increasing amount of litigation which had already clotted the dockets of the state and federal courts. The Supreme 

Court was prepared for work. Yearly it disposed of some 

700 cases, but these were coming into the Court in increasing numbers, and the Court found itself more and more in arrears. At the end of Chief Justice Taft's first year, the 

Court was about 400 cases behind. The next year, the number of cases carried over to the succeeding term was 462. 

In the following year, it was 558. Something had to be done to enable the Court to keep up with its work, and, what was more important, to set an example for the other courts in efficiency in the administration of justice. 

The method adopted by the late Chief Justice was to persuade the members of Congress to pass a Jurisdictional Act,' which enabled the Supreme Court through the use of its own discretion, to hear and consider only those cases 

1 Act of February 13, 1925, 43 Stat. 936. [1]

which were of sufficient public importance. The effect of this Jurisdictional Act has been marvelous. Throughout the years since the effective date of that Act, in 1925, the Supreme Court has been disposing on the average of some 845 cases per year, made constant inroads into the number of cases carried over, so that when the Court adjourned on June 3rd, 1929, it carried over only 143 cases to the term which has just closed. Most of these consisted of the cases which had come so near the end of the term, that the Court under its rules could not have passed upon them. The late 

Chief Justice therefore saw the complete accomplishment of this great task. 

The theory of the Jurisdictional Act is that after trial and one appeal in the federal courts, the parties have had their day in court. Likewise, with cases which have gone through all the courts in their respective states. Under the 

Jurisdictional Act, the main object is not that justice be done to the particular litigants, but that the public interest be served through the settling of troublesome questions for the future, or to unify the law by solving conflicts among the lower courts on federal questions. Until recently, each district court was bound by the decisions of its own circuit, and on many questions of federal law, each circuit had its own viewpoint.

In the rules adopted by the Supreme Court pursuant to the Jurisdictional Act, the Court, in general terms, defined the classes of cases, in which it will grant writs to review the decisions of the lower courts.2 One of these is where a state court has decided a federal question in a manner which is probably untenable or which is in conflict with the decisions of the Supreme Court. Another rule is where the decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals is in conflict with 

2 Rule 38. Revised Rules of the Supreme Court of the United States, June 5, 1928. [2]

the decisions of the Supreme Court, and a third, where the various circuit courts of appeal have rendered decisions in conflict with each other. These rules lay a foundation for the unification of federal law throughout the country. 

Chief Justice Taft went further in unifying the federal law through a unification of the federal judiciary itself. 

Through his efforts was created the Judicial Council, in which he and the senior judges of the various circuits passed upon the policies of the federal courts; on the manner and methods of conducting cases which come before them and on expediting the work so that they could meet the new pace which the country had set for itself after the war. 

The Judicial Council, known as the Judicial Conference of Senior Circuit Judges, came into being through an Act of 

Congress of September 14, 1922,3 shortly after Chief Justice

Taft took office. Its main task was to consider ways and means of improving federal procedure and to solve the problem of increasingly congested dockets. 

In 1924, the Conference recommended additional judges for those federal courts where congestion was greatest; recommended that the Prohibition Unit be transferred from the Treasury Department to the Department of Justice; that amendments be passed to the Bankruptcy laws so that all judgments, decrees, etc., be reviewed by speedy appeal.

A great improvement was effected by recommendations to supply the judges with proper library facilities. 

In 1925, the Conference recommended the urgent need of additional judges especially in the Southern District of 

New York. Most of the recommendations during this session related to criminal procedure, for the criminal cases 3 42 Stat. 837. For the origin of the Judicial Council, see Frank furter and Landis: The Business of the Supreme Court, Chapter VI. 

For the anual recommendations of the Judicial Council, see Annual Reports of the Attorney General of the United States, 1922-1929. [3]

were increasing with great rapidity, particularly the offenses arising under the National Prohibition Act.

In 1926, after the passage of the Jurisdictional Act, the Conference reported that there was an appreciable decrease in the business of the trial courts of the United States and a reduction in the delays and congestion, which the additional judges had helped to bring about. The figures in the annual reports of the Attorney General, however, do not seem to substantiate that there was an appreciable decrease. 

Beginning with 1927, the Conference began a systematic effort to clear the dockets in the federal courts. The first recommendation in this respect, was a suggestion to dismiss all inactive cases, which had remained on the dockets for more than one year. In the Supreme Court, Chief Justice 

Taft had established a rule that all criminal cases be advanced on the docket, and this procedure was recommended to the lower courts. In addition, Chief Justice Taft adopted the method of assigning federal judges to places outside of their respective districts or circuits to assist in the disposition of cases wherever crowded dockets required additional assistance.

The Conference also adopted improved methods for collecting statistics on the federal courts. There is now a regular reporting system, whereby the senior district judge reports to the senior circuit judge, who in turn reports to the Conference, and in this manner a unified view of the entire federal judiciary is obtained. 

In 1929, the Conference again met in Washington during the first week in October. This was the last Conference over which Chief Justice Taft presided. He reported that statistics for the various districts showed an increase in new business in 1929; that there were 8,034 more cases commenced in federal courts in 1929 than in 1928, and that the largest single class of cases in the federal courts came under [4]

the National Prohibition Act. For in 1928 there were commenced 9,928 civil and 55,729 criminal cases under that Act, whereas in 1929 there were 11,237 civil and 56,787 criminal cases commenced.

Thus the federal Judiciary began functioning as a unit of government rather than as isolated courts. This was the second great accomplishment of the late Chief Justice.

The third accomplishment can be realized only by those who are closely connected with the work of the Court, and this was the need for a Supreme Court building. This may not appear important, until one realizes how difficult it is for the Supreme Court to function when it is cramped for space in a few rooms in the Capitol, with the records scattered throughout the Capitol, the Senate Office Building, the Library of Congress and other places; until one realizes that seven of the nine Justices use their own homes as their offices, and that the present Chief Justice has desk space in the Conference Room of the Supreme Court. Unfortunately, Chief Justice Taft did not live to see the day when the Supreme Court will open its sessions in its own home. 

Although Chief Justice Taft was thus burdened with great administrative tasks, and he was neither young nor strong, he did more than his share in the disposition of cases which came before the Court.

There are many details from which the associate justices are relieved, but which devolve upon the Chief Justice. He passes on the cases which are accompanied by petitions to proceed in forma pauperis. He outlines the matters to be taken up at the weekly conference of the Court. Upon him devolves the task of assigning the cases to the various justices. And after every conference, he prepares the orders of the Court to be delivered on the following decision day.

In addition, there are always many questions which counsel are obliged to take up with the judges in chambers, and the [5]

first logical person to consult is the Chief Justice. With all that, Chief Justice Taft rendered more opinions every year than most of the other justices.*

Nor must one overlook the unusual genial personality of the late Chief Justice, which to a large extent contributed to the harmonious working of the Court. Always of a cheerful disposition, always beloved by those who came in contact with him, despite any differences on questions of law or policy, he has been able to achieve unison among the members of the Court. This is best demonstrated by the letter sent to him by the members of the Court upon his resignation. They wrote: "Dear Chief Justice: We call you Chief Justice still, for we can not quickly give up the title by which we have known you for all these later years and which you have made so dear to us. 

We can not let you leave us without trying to tell you how dear you have made it. You came to us from achievements in other fields, and with the prestige of the illustrious place that you lately had held, and you showed in a new form your voluminous capacity for work and for getting work done, your humor that smoothed the rough places, your golden heart that has brought you love from every side, and, most of all, from your brethren whose tasks you have made happy and light. 

We grieve at your illness but your spirit has given life and impulse that will abide whether you are with us or are away. 

Affectionately yours, 

(Signed) OLIVER WENDELL HOLMES, WILLIS VAN DEVANTER, J. C. MCREYNOLDS, LOUIS D. BRANDEIS, GEO. SUTHERLAND, PIERCE BUTLER, EDWARD T. SANFORD, HARLAN F. STONE." 4 Frankfurter and Landis: The Supreme Court at October Term, 1928. Harvard Law Review, November, 1929, Vol. XLIII, Number 1, p. 49. In this article the authors set out in tabular form the numbers of opinions rendered by the members of the Court for October Term, 1923, through October Term, 1928. It appears that the greatest number of opinions was rendered by Justice Brandeis, who exceeded the number of opinions rendered by Chief Justice Taft by three. Justice Holmes ran close to Chief Justice Taft with only two opinions less. [6]

Appointment of Chief Justice Hughes. 

Chief Justice Taft resigned on February 4, 1930, and three hours later the President nominated Charles Evans 

Hughes as Chief Justice of the United States. Then the battle began. Some Senators resented the President's speed, for it was felt that the appointment had been prearranged. 

Although the provision in the Constitution that the members of the Court shall be appointed "with the advice and consent of the Senate" had long been reduced to appointments with consent, but without advice, still it was felt that the President should at least have had the advice of the Senators, if not of the Senate itself. 

The resentment to the President's action was now transferred to the nominee. From the debates which followed one could glean out suggestions as to the qualifications which a nominee to this highest judicial office should possess, in addition to the qualifications expected of any person in high public office. The suggestions may be summed up in the following three propositions. 

First, the nominee must be a great lawyer,—one who has shown outstanding abilities in practice before the courts, or one who has shown great legal acumen as a judge, or one who has by his contributions to the law so advanced this field of learning as to be entitled to recognition as a great authority in the law. It is only fair that this qualification be demanded of all nominees to the Supreme Court of the United States. Since the members of this Court wield supreme judicial power over the decisions of all the other courts of the land, it should be expected that this greater authority be based on wider knowledge and greater abilities. This requirement was fully met by Mr. Hughes. By nominating him, the President chose one of the greatest, if not the greatest lawyer of the country, one who has long demonstrated his abilities both as a lawyer and as a judge. [7]

The second qualification relates to the nominee's scope of vision in matters outside of the law. It is high time to abandon the fiction that the Supreme Court concerns itself with purely legal questions. The cases which come before the Court are mainly economic, political or other social controversies. It should therefore be expected that those who adjudicate on the most difficult problems pertaining to our social structure should also have a wide scope and knowledge concerning extra legal facts. This qualification was likewise fully met by Mr. Hughes, who had demonstrated his abilities as Governor of the State of New York, as Secretary of State, as Judge of the World Court and in numerous other activities in which he participated in a public or private capacity.

The third qualification relates to a correlation of the other two. It is the ability to apply the principles, rules, standards and conceptions of the law to the economic, social and political problems, so that the law may keep pace with the needs of a progressive society. Since the cases which come before the Court are mainly social problems, it is highly important that the members of the Court choose proper premises for the application of the legal rules. 

Which are the proper premises is, of course, a matter of dispute, and that dispute formed the sole basis of the debates on Mr. Hughes' nomination.

It was supposed that since Mr. Hughes had in recent years represented big corporations, he would assume a favorable attitude toward them as a member of the Court. His opponents had not forgotten his recent defense of the Interborough Rapid Transit Company, in which he argued that the company was entitled to charge a seven-cent fare, despite its agreements with the City of New York, in which it stipulated to operate the New York subways on a five-cent fare basis. The belief among his opponents prevailed that [8]

Mr. Hughes was a champion of increased burdens for the great mass of the people, who of necessity must depend upon such means of transportation, and that he would therefore favor increased fares whenever the problem arose in the 

Supreme Court.

It will be remembered that when Justice Stone was appointed by President Coolidge, similar objections were raised against him by the "liberals" in the Senate. But it has so turned out that on questions where there is a cleavage between what may be loosely termed as conservatives and liberals, Justice Stone has more often adhered to the views expressed by Justices Brandeis and Holmes. And thus far it may be observed that every opinion rendered by Chief Justice Hughes has been in favor of what might be termed the "liberal" viewpoint.

In the field of public utilities, he upheld the constitutionality of the Oklahoma Cotton Gin Law, which favors cooperative enterprises as against privately owned cotton gins.5 In a valuation case, involving the validity of an order of the Wisconsin Railroad Commission, fixing the rate base of a water company, Chief Justice Hughes reversed the decision of the District Court which held the order confiscatory; he remanded that case with directions. that the lower court make specific findings of fact and conclusions of law, adding that the legislative and executive orders of state commissions should not be lightly set aside." 

In the field of railroad problems, Chief Justice Hughes held that the federal district courts have no jurisdiction to enjoin intra-state rates ordered by state commissions, on the theory that such rates might result in discrimination against 

5 No. 454-Corporation Comm'n vs. Lowe. Decided May 19, 1930. 

See p. 115 infra.

No. 301-Railroad Comm'n of Wisconsin vs. Maxcy. Decided 

March 12, 1930. See p. 111 infra. [9]

interstate commerce, but that such matters must first be passed upon by the Interstate Commerce Commission." In the field of labor problems, the Chief Justice sustained the validity of the Railway Labor Act of 1926 and held that railroads may be enjoined from interfering with, influencing or coercing their employees in the matter of their representation before the United States Board of Mediation. He also upheld the constitutionality of a pro vision in the New York Workmen's Compensation Act which requires that in case of an employee's death, where compensation cannot be had under the Act, the negligent party causing the death, must pay, in addition to damages, a sum of $1,000 into funds maintained by the State for vocational education of disabled employees and for additional compensation to employees who are totally disabled after partial disability." 

Before Mr. Hughes became Chief Justice, he was one of the outstanding proponents of the powers of the Supreme Court over the constitutionality of legislation.10 Yet, in a case involving the constitutionality of a provision in the Ohio constitution, limiting the judicial veto power of the highest court of that state, Chief Justice Hughes held that this provision was not contrary to the federal constitution."1 

On the other hand, it must also be mentioned that in the two cases where there was a sharp dissent by Justices 

Holmes, Brandeis and Stone, Chief Justice Hughes sided 

7 No. 364-Board of Railroad Comm'rs of N. D. vs. Gt. Northern 

R. R. Co. Decided May 19, 1930. See p. 43 infra. 

8 No. 469-Texas & Orleans R. R. Co. vs. Brotherhood of Railway & 

Steamship Clerks. Decided May 26, 1930. See p. 268 infra. 

9 No. 307-Staten Island Rapid Transit Ry. Co. vs. Phoenix Indem nity Co. Decided March 17, 1930. See p. 283 infra. 

10 Hughes: Supreme Court of the United States. Page 

11 Nos. 237 and 238-State of Ohio ex rel. Bryant vs. Akron Metropolitan Park District, etc., and State of Ohio ex rel. Wadsworth vs. 

Zangerle, etc. Decided March 12, 1930. [ 10 ]

with the majority. In one of these cases, the question was raised whether a state may impose an inheritance tax on a debt owed by a resident to a non-resident decedent.12 The other involved the question whether in imposing a tax on the net worth of a corporation, government bonds owned by the corporation may be included in the basis of the tax.13 

After a debate which lasted several days, the nomination of Mr. Hughes was confirmed by a vote of 52 to 26. On 

February 24th, 1930, Mr. Hughes took his oath of office as 

Chief Justice of the United States.

To this day, it still seems strange to see the members of the Court file in without Chief Justice Taft in the lead. 

One misses his slow gait, his broad smile and his chuckle, which were always with him as he stepped to the bench. 

With measured step, full of vigor, Chief Justice Hughes strides into the courtroom. His penetrating look bespeaks the seriousness with which he regards his tasks, as it also suggests a determination to keep up with the rapid movement of the present day. 1 This contrast may be observed outside the court as well as in it. A conference with the late Chief Justice usually meant a pleasant chat. If the appointment was for nine o'clock in the morning, the Chief Justice would entertain the visitor in the living room of his spacious house. If in the evening, the meeting would usually take place in his private study. In addition to conversation relating to the matter of business, Mr. Taft would give his visitor many an interesting sidelight on his life as Chief Justice and also on his experiences as President of the United States. The 

12 No. 417 Baldwin et al. vs. Missouri. Decided May 26, 1930. * 

See p. 224 infra. 

13 No. 222-Missouri ex rel. Mo. Ins. Co. vs. Gehner, etc. Decided 

April 14, 1930.

(The Chief Justice concurred on the ground that the case was governed by National Life Insurance Co. vs. United tates, 277 

U. S. 508). See p. 135 infra. [11]

conversation once turned on the unnecessary correspondence which taxes a man's time these days. Mr. Taft took off his house slippers, made himself more comfortable by putting his feet on a chair near by, and chuckling, narrated how among the numerous letters he received were complaints that some justice of the peace in a smail town of a far western state failed to do justice, how an erring husband absconded leaving his poor wife without support, all these asking the Chief Justice of the United States to use his authority. Thus time rolled on, until the visitor began to feel that he was imposing on the Chief Justice's time, and yet it would not be proper to leave until he had signified that the conference should be terminated. 

As one steps into the presence of Chief Justice Hughes in chambers, one feels that time is precious, that the Chief Justice means business, that one must state the object of his visit clearly and succinctly, listen to what Mr. Hughes has to say, and then take his leave. One gets the impression that 

Mr. Hughes is a man who is jealously guarding his time. 

The contrast between the two Chief Justices was again demonstrated in an incident which occurred on the day of 

Mr. Taft's funeral. It was on Tuesday, March 11th, 1930, when Chief Justice Hughes alone opened Court. There were few people in the courtroom, the majority consisting of Mr. Taft's family. At twelve o'clock sharp, one heard a click, which meant that the Court was entering. The marshal struck his gavel, and everyone rose. In his usual stentorian voice, the marshal announced: "The Honorable, the Supreme Court of the United States. Oyez, Oyez, Oyez, etc." Chief Justice Hughes with firm, almost military step, marched to the bench. No sooner was the marshal through with his announcement, than the Chief Justice sat down and said: "The Court will now adjourn." Again the sound of the gavel. Everybody rose, and the marshal said: [12]

"The Honorable Court will now adjourn till tomorrow at twelve o'clock." But before he finished his sentence, the

Chief Justice had already marched the length of the courtroom and left. All this took place in about one minute. 

And what a striking contrast this was to the funeral procession, which at that moment began from the steps of the 

Capitol. 

Nomination of Judge Parker. 

The day Chief Justice Taft died, the country witnessed a strange admixture of joy and sorrow. The day began with the announcement of the eighty-ninth birthday of Justice

Holmes, and plans were on the way for a celebration of the ninetieth birthday of the great jurist. The Associated Harvard Clubs had appointed a committee, of which Justice Sanford was a member, for this purpose. At noon, as the 

Court met for its regular Saturday conference, the sudden death of Justice Sanford was announced. Five hours later 

Chief Justice Taft passed away.

Within a few days the eyes of the country turned on 

President Hoover. Senators still remembered with resentment the speed with which he had appointed Mr. Hughes, and it was expected that there would be no repetition of that incident. Ten days after Justice Sanford's death, President

Hoover nominated Judge Parker of the Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Fourth Circuit, to succeed Justice Sanford.

Later, when in the course of the debates on the nomination. it became known that Judge Parker's nomination had been withheld merely as a matter of form, the resentment of the Senators redoubled. Senator Ashurst bitterly attacked the nomination in the following terms: "When before did a high executive officer, such as an Assistant Secretary of the Interior, before the eyes were closed upon one judge who [13]

had gone to his long reward, before the funeral obsequies. had been had, say: 'Now the master political stroke!' " ¹4 

Since Justice Sanford occasionally sided with Justices

Holmes, Brandeis and Stone in their dissent from the majority and since the "liberals" saw an arch reactionary in the new Chief Justice, they felt they had the right to demand that a liberal be appointed to take the place of the deceased associate justice. It was a foregone conclusion, therefore, that they would oppose the nomination of any man with views like that of Judge Parker.

Applying the first qualification discussed in connection. with the Senate debates on Mr. Hughes, the opponents of 

Judge Parker contended that he did not come up to the standard as an authority in the law. On the contrary, some doubts were cast upon his abilities as a lawyer, when he handled several war fraud cases, as Special Assistant to the Attorney General. But none of the Senators set himself to work on the some 135 opinions theretofore rendered by Judge Parker in order to evaluate his contributions to the law. So the discussion on this qualification was rather superficial. On the other hand, it was indeed pitiful to find that Judge Parker's proponents stressed such facts as that he had worked his way through college, received a prize in Greek, was elected to the Phi Beta Kappa, and had been on the debating team at his college. All this, as if Judge 

Parker were applying for the job of an assistant district attorney in some "main street" locality. Towards the close of the debates, Attorney General Mitchell issued a statement that he had made a painstaking inquiry into the opinions rendered by Judge Parker and that he had come to the conclusion that Judge Parker was one of the outstanding circuit judges of the country.' The statement, 14 Cong. Rec., 71st Cong., 2nd Sess. 

15 Cong. Rec., 71st Cong., 2nd Sess. 15 

May 6, 1930, p. 8712. 

May 5, 1930, p. 8621. [ 14 ]

however, fell flat. The impression produced on the opposition was that the Attorney General was forced to make the statement to save the face of the administration. 

Nothing was said concerning Judge Parker's wide scope of knowledge and abilities in matters outside the law, except as has already been mentioned, that he distinguished himself at the University of North Carolina. 

The third qualification, namely, what kind of economic or social premises the nominee would be apt to choose for the application of the rules of law, was given ample consideration in the discussion of Judge Parker's attitude on the negro question and his decision in the Red Jacket 

Case.¹ 16

The discussion on the negro question centered about a statement which Judge Parker was alleged to have made during his gubernatorial campaign as Republican candidate in North Carolina, in 1920,¹7 "The negro, as a class, does not desire to enter politics. The Republican Party of North Carolina does not desire him to do so. We recognize the fact that he has not yet reached the stage in his development when he can share the burdens and responsibilities of government. This being true, and every intelligent man in North Carolina knows that it is true *** the participation of the negro in politics is a source of evil and danger to both races."

Upon his nomination for the Supreme Bench, this statement was recalled by those who take the Thirteenth, Fourteenth and Fifteenth Amendments more seriously than they are apt to be regarded by a Southern candidate for office, 16 United Mine Workers of America vs. Red Jacket Consolidated Coal and Coke Company, 18 F. (2) 839. 

17 Cong. Rec., 71st Cong., 2nd Sess. April 14, 1930, p. 7295. [ 15 ]

and they brought pressure on their own Senators to reject 

Judge Parker.

Judge Parker explained the reasons for having made that statement. It appears that the Democrats in North 

Carolina were going to inject the race issue into the campaign, and Mr. Parker undertook to convince the white voters of the state that his candidacy did not imply a relinquishment of white supremacy. In fairness to Judge Parker, it must be mentioned that in the only case involving a race question, which came before his court, he, together with the other judges upheld the negroes, by affirming the decision. of the lower court, without an opinion. This case will be discussed more fully in a subsequent chapter.18 

In the Red Jacket Case, Judge Parker sustained a decree rendered by the District Court in West Virginia enjoining the Mine Workers of America from counseling, advising or persuading the employees of the coal companies from breaking their contracts of employments. The contracts in question were what are more popularly known as "yellow dog" contracts, that is to say, contracts in which employees agree that they will not join a union while they keep their jobs. This decree therefore meant that the United Mine 

Workers could not peacefully persuade the non-union workers to join their union, for that would mean to persuade them to break their contracts. 

The "yellow dog" contract was attacked by the opposing

Senators both as to its validity from the standpoint of the law of contracts and from the standpoint of public policy. 

None of the Senators, it appeared, undertook to defend the "yellow dog" contract itself or the merits of Judge Parker's decision. The proponents of Judge Parker, however, excused his position in the case, as it were, through a plea of confession and avoidance, namely, that Judge Parker 

18 No. 729-City of Richmond vs. Deans. Decided May 19, 1930. [16]

could not help himself and that he was absolutely bound by the decision of the Supreme Court in the Hitchman Case.19 

Senator Borah replied that had Judge Parker not favored the "yellow dog" contract, he could have distinguished the

Red Jacket Case from the Hitchman Case, in accordance with the distinctions laid down by the Supreme Court in what is known as the Tri-City Case.20

The opponents were at a greater advantage here than in the debates on the Hughes nomination. When they attacked 

Mr. Hughes because of his probable views on public utilities, the obvious answer was that Mr. Hughes had acted as an advocate, and that it had been his duty to represent the views of his clients, but that he would act differently as a judge. This was a correct forecast, especially in view of the decisions he had rendered as associate justice of the 

Supreme Court, notably in the Minnesota Rate Cases.21 The proponents of Judge Parker pushed the argument somewhat further by insisting that as a judge of a lower federal court, he was bound by the previous decisions of the Supreme Court of the United States. His opponents could have answered this by discussing two other decisions of Judge Parker on questions which came before him as of first instance and in which he was not bound by any previous decisions of the Supreme Court. These were the decisions in the Lake Cargo Case22 and in the case of United States vs.

Munson Steamship Line.23 But the Senators failed to discuss these cases in their prolonged debates. However, the insurgents accomplished their object, and Judge Parker was defeated by the narrow vote of 39 to 41. 

19 Hitchman Coal & Coke Co. vs. Mitchell, 245 U. S. 229. 

20 American Steel Foundries vs. Tri-City Central Trades Council, 257 

U. S. 184. 

21 230 U. S. 352. 

22 Anchor Coal Co. et al. vs. United States et al., 25 F. (2) 462. 

23 United States vs. Munson Steamship Line, 37 F. (2) 681. [ 17 ]

During these debates on the Supreme Court nominations, a question of policy came up time and again, as to how far the Senate should participate in the discussion of the qualifications of candidates for high judicial office. For at present, it is evident there is no settled policy on this question, and there is no consistency in the manner in which the Senate acts upon such nominations. Two definite expressions of policy appeared. Some suggested that every nominee for the Supreme Bench be called before the Senate Judiciary Committee and be examined as to his economic and social views. Others regarded the debates on the nominee's views as improper and as undermining the independence of the judiciary. Their contention was based on the assumption that the members of the Court should not be swayed by economic views of one kind or another, but should apply the rules and principles of law without regard to persons and should dispense justice equally for the rich and the poor.

The reason for this difference of opinion rests upon another difference. The liberals say that the members of the Supreme Court give effect to their economic views in their decisions, and that they should not do so. The conservatives, on the other hand, state that the justices do not give effect to their economic views when deciding on questions of law. They also say that the Court should not, but they really think that it should. We believe with the liberals that the members of the Court do give effect to their economic views, and we agree with the unexpressed thought of the conservatives that the justices should give effect to their economic views when passing on important problems. 

That the members of the Court do apply their economic views and principles in their decisions may be deduced from expressions of the minority concerning the majority of the Court. The latest example of this came from Justice [18]

Holmes in his dissenting opinions in the cases of Farmers 

Loan and Trust Company vs. Minnesota2+ and Baldwin vs.

Missouri.25 One can also find many instances in which the majority of the Court might have returned the compliment, that the minority was applying its economic views in their dissenting opinions.

But we are not concerned with the attacks of the members of the Court upon each other. One illustration will suffice to show that there is a class of important cases in which the judges will apply the same legal rules and principles, but will arrive at different conclusions, depending on their choice of economic premises, and there is nothing in the law dictating to the judges that they choose one premise or another for reaching their conclusions. We have in mind the recent decision of the Supreme Court in the first Oklahoma Cotton Gin Case.26 There the main problem was the constitutionality of a provision in the law of Oklahoma requiring every cotton gin to operate under a license, which could be obtained only upon a showing of public convenience and necessity, except that cooperative cotton gins could obtain licenses without such showing. 

This law undoubtedly placed the privately owned cotton gins at a disadvantage in their competition with the cooperatives.

A privately owned cotton gin claimed that the law constituted a denial of equal protection, contrary to the 

Fourteenth Amendment of the Constitution. The rules of law applicable to the case were very simple, and both the majority and minority subscribed to them: (1) the Constitution forbids a denial of the equal protection of the 

24 No. 26-Farmers Loan and Trust Co. vs. Minnesota. 

January 6, 1930. See p. 222 infra. 

25 No. 417 

224 infra. Decided 

Baldwin vs. Missouri. Decided May 26, 1930. See p. 

26 Frost vs. Corporation Comm'n of Oklahoma, 278 U. S. 515. 

[ 19 ]

laws; and (2) a classification of persons for the purpose of imposing obligations or granting privileges is not in itself a denial of equal protection, unless the classification bears no reasonable relation to the distinction made by the law. 

In order to decide on the constitutionality of the Oklahoma Cotton Gin Law, it was necessary to determine whether the differences between privately owned and cooperative cotton gins had any reasonable relation to the economic advantages given by the law to the latter as against the former. The differences between the two types of cotton gins are not a matter of law, but a matter of economics. Justice Sutherland, representing the majority, saw no reasonable connection between these two economic enterprises, so far as competition was concerned, and held the discrimination unlawful. Justice Brandeis, with whom Justices Holmes and Stone agreed, reviewed the history of the economic struggle between the farmers and the capitalistic enterprises in the ginning of cotton in Oklahoma, and came to the conclusion that there was ample reason for the state to differentiate between them and that therefore the law was valid.

The difference in the result lay in the difference of economic premises chosen by the members of the Court and not in any difference of legal principles or rules employed by them. This illustration indicates that not only do the justices permit their economic views to influence their decisions, but that in many instances, they must. 

The next question is whether it is desirable that the members of the Court apply their economic views in the solution of the problems before them. This question does not imply that the members of the Court should generally apply such views for the purpose of arriving at results deemed by them desirable. After all, the Court is bound by the laws of Congress, by the principles of the common law, [ 20 ]

and by their own judicial precedents, which in a large measure must be followed in order to lend certainty to the law and stability to society. 

There are numerous questions coming to the Court which are not expressly covered by existing law, and where the decision must be based upon the choice of premises adopted by those charged with the duty of applying the law. 

We do not think that in such cases, the Supreme Court should act with the proverbial blindness of the Goddess of Justice. In the administration of justice in a growing society judges must take cognizance of the economic and other social facts involved in the controversies before them. 

And if giving effect to their economic ideals means that the decisions are "unjust," at least it may also be said that the decisions are intelligent.

This brings us to the much debated subject of the independence of the judiciary. In the course of the debates on 

Judge Parker, Senator Hastings suggested that the Senate should not adopt a policy of putting on the Supreme Court only persons who have certain fixed ideas as to the interpretation of the law; that these men should be left to their own judgment." Senator Fess carried the argument further. 

After stating that of the three branches of our government, the judicial branch is the weakest because it has no purse and no power of the sword, he said: "Of the three, the judiciary is naturally the weakest, because the legislative has the power.

It (the judiciary) is independent in arriving at its own decisions, but that is the only way it is independent. In order that that judgment might be unbiased it must remain independent of the other two." 28 And later the Senator said: "If they (the judiciary) were other than independent, they would not be fit to be on the bench. The 

27 Cong. Rec., 71st Congr., 2nd Sess. 

28 Cong. Rec., 71st Congr., 2nd Sess. 

April 30, 1930, p. 8335. 

April 29, 1930, p. 8237. [21]

mere fact that any man would yield to any sort of pressure from any sort of organized propaganda would be the strongest reason for me to vote against him because we do not want that kind of men on the bench." 29

We think that Senator Fess has much underestimated the power of the judiciary. Whether one likes it or not, the fact remains that the judiciary is by far the strongest and most powerful branch of our government. With reference to the independence of the judiciary, one may well ask, independent of what and independent of whom? The President and the Senate are elected by the people supposedly because of their economic or political views. Upon them, under the Constitution, devolves the duty of appointing the judges who wield still greater economic power. Is it reasonable to expect that the President and Senate should fail to inquire into the three qualifications we have discussed, and especially into the third qualification, which is the application of the rules and principles of law to economic problems?

The justification of such inquiry was well illustrated in the Parker debates. Senator Borah was discussing the Red 

Jacket Decision, when one of the Senators challenged the correctness of Senator Borah's conclusions and asked whether it was not up to Congress to have amended the law after the decision of the Supreme Court in the Hitchman 

Case, so as to have avoided Judge Parker's decision; and if

Congress failed to do this, the responsibility rested with it rather than with Judge Parker.30 To this Senator Borah replied: "No. We have tried by an Act of Congress to repudiate that principle, but the Supreme Court of the United States said that our action was null and void. Mr. 

29 Cong. Rec., 71st Congr., 2nd Sess. 

30 Cong. Rec., 71st Congr., 2nd Sess. 

May 6, 1930, p. 8722. 

April 29, 1930, p. 8237. 

April 29, 1930, p. 8220; also [ 22 ]

President, that is what makes this matter so very important. 

They pass upon what we do. Therefore, it is exceedingly important that we pass upon them before they decide upon all of these matters. *We declare a national policy.

They reject it. I feel I am well justified in inquiring of men on their way to the Supreme Bench something of their views on these questions."

It is a poor rule which does not work both ways, and it does not follow that with the application of the views expressed by Senator Borah, the Supreme Court would consist of “liberals." It so happened that in these debates, not a single Senator came out in favor of the Red Jacket decision itself, or it may be that it was not politic to favor such views as were expressed therein. The debate might have turned on another economic, political or social problem in which Senators Borah, Norris and other opponents of Judge Parker might have constituted a small minority, which is usually the case. If one should poll the Senate on the various questions which the Supreme Court is called upon to adjudicate, it is doubtful that the Senators would have arrived at a different conclusion from that reached by the Court. There are many decisions of the Supreme Court and of the lower federal courts on fundamental economic issues, which might have been cured by legislation. But indeed few have been the instances in which Congress and the President have shown an inclination to take such steps.

It is doubtful whether the adoption of Senator Borah's suggestion that the Senate should inquire into the views of the nominees, would result in a different Supreme Court, unless there should also be elected a different type of executive and legislature. There is no doubt, however, that Senator 

Borah's suggestion, if adopted, would result in a more intelligent participation on the part of the Senate in the appointments of members to the Supreme Bench. [ 23 ]

Appointment of Justice Roberts.

Whatever may be said for or against the merits of the opposition to Chief Justice Hughes and to Judge Parker, one cannot escape the conclusion that their nominations were intelligently considered, that is in both instances, the Senate debated and acted upon an investigation of the candidates' qualifications. Unfortunately, this cannot be said concerning the confirmation of Justice Roberts. We say that entirely irrespective of the merits of the appointment, and despite our own high regard and respect for the new 

Associate Justice of the Supreme Court.

After the defeat of Judge Parker, there was a breathless pause awaiting the action of the President on the vacancy in the Court. It was evident that the insurgents in the Senate had spent their forces, and that unless the President had retaliated by an impossible nomination, they would have stood no chance of defeating the nominee. And indeed any opposition without success, would have only reacted against them. Then came the nomination of Mr. Roberts.

Nothing was known about him except that he was an able lawyer and that he had made a wonderful record and rendered a great public service in connection with the civil and criminal prosecutions in the oil scandals. Did the

Senators make a thorough search into his views? It was a clever political move on the part of the insurgent Senators to regard his nomination as a culmination of their victory in the Parker case. And Mr. Roberts was confirmed without as much as the taking of a vote on his nomination.31 

31 Cong. Rec., 71st Congr., 2nd Sess. May 20, 1930, p. 9583. [24]

CHAPTER II. 

Problems In Administration 

Since the passage of the Jurisdictional Act of 1925, public interest has been aroused concerning the progress of the Court in clearing its docket. It has already been mentioned that the Act worked wonderfully in this respect.¹ But it was accomplished not without work. The summer vacations were turned into working periods, during which the Justices studied the hundreds of cases which came into the 

Court every year between terms. On November 31, 1927, the Court surprised the country by rendering decisions in 130 cases, but that was only a sample of what the Court could do. On October 21, 1929, Chief Justice Taft announced the orders of the Court in 184 cases, and a broad smile of satisfaction lit up his face.

The speed with which the Court acted did not meet with universal approval. Some time ago, Chief Justice Taft addressing the American Law Institute, said that some of the members of the profession might think that the pace set by the Court was contrary to the Constitution. characteristic pleasantry of the Chief Justice. of fact, however, it was a truth spoken in jest. The writers have often heard such expressions on the part of the members of the bar. 

It was a 

As a matter

One can imagine that lawyers, who were accustomed to having their cases stored in the Supreme Court for years, might feel disappointed, if their cases were disposed of 

1 See p. 2 supra; also Hankin: U. S. Supreme Court, 1928-1929, Pp. 2-3. 

2 Proceedings of the American Law Institute, 1928. 

[ 25 ]

within a week's time after they were submitted. It is not surprising, therefore, that some lawyers should invoke the omnipresent and everlasting due process clause of the Constitution in support of their opinion that the diligence and efficiency of the Court is unconstitutional. 

When Mr. Hughes took office, the first question which arose among the members of the bar, was whether under the new Chief Justice the Court would maintain this speed record. At the time of Chief Justice Taft's resignation, the

Court still maintained this speed, but it was felt that it was a speed maintained through the law of inertia, with the impetus removed. This change was hardly noticeable, and perhaps it was a mere psychological illusion on our part. 

We gained this impression from the fact that a number of jurisdictional statements submitted to the Court had not been acted upon for several weeks, whereas ordinarily they would have been acted upon within a week after submission to the Court.

Soon after Mr. Hughes took office, there was again a feeling of acceleration. This, too, may be a psychological illusion, but it is also based on the action of the Court in specific cases. In one case,³ an appeal together with a motion to advance, was filed on February 21st; on Monday, February 24th the Chief Justice took office; next Monday the Court advanced the case to be argued on April 14th; on March 17th, the jurisdictional statement in the case was first submitted to the Court. On that day, the Court took a recess until April 14th, and when it reconvened, it announced that probable jurisdiction had been shown. The argument took place on the same day, but lasted only a few minutes, the Court having declined to hear any further. 

Usually this process is reversed. First, the jurisdictional 

3 No. 680-Pittsburgh & West Virginia Railway Co. vs. Wheeling & 

Lake Erie Railroad Co. See p. 79 infra. [ 26 ]

statement is filed; then it is formally submitted to the Court; then the Court renders an order that probable jurisdiction has been shown; then the case may be advanced for argument. This case was argued within two months after it was filed. Compare this record with the fact that only a few years ago, several years elapsed between the filing of an appeal and the hearing of arguments on the merits, and in 1928, when Mr. Hughes wrote his book on the Supreme 

Court of the United States, he commented on the great strides the Court had made when it heard arguments only twelve to fifteen months after the filing of the cases.*

Here is another illustration indicating the accellerated speed after Chief Justice Hughes took office. One petition for a writ of certiorari was suddenly acted upon before it was formally submitted to the Court, and before the case was entered on the docket.5 That came as a distinct surprise to all those who were accustomed to the systematized procedure under the administration of Chief Justice Taft. 

These are but isolated incidents and it is difficult to see how the Court can adopt a general programme which would dispose of cases more quickly than under the system introduced by Chief Justice Taft, unless, if one may express himself so lightly concerning a serious matter, the Court should dispose of cases while on their way to the Court. 

This is not at all impossible. For example, the Court might adopt a rule whereby every case should first be presented to the Justice in charge of the circuit from which the case. originates, and if he advises against the filing of the appeal or the petition, and the appellant or petitioner does file the case, then this might be regarded in the nature of an appeal to the Court en banc. Such procedure might be worth try- 

4 Hughes: Supreme Court of the United States, p. 56. 

5 No. 707-Rishel vs. County of McPherson, Kans. Certiorari denied 

March 3, 1930. [ 27 ]

ing, in order to see whether the number of cases coming to the Court would thereby materially diminish. On the other hand, those who now complain that the efficiency of the Court is contrary to the law of the land, might then complain that it is contrary to the laws governing time and space.

For the present, it is too early to estimate with certainty the trend of the Court in matters of its own administration, but during the past term in which both Chief Justice Taft and Chief Justice Hughes administered the Court, the docket showed as follows: The number of cases carried over from the preceding term was 143; 841 additional cases were docketed during the year; the Court disposed of 794 cases, leaving 190 to be carried over to the next term of Court. Thus at the end of this term, the Court has carried over 190 cases, as compared with 143 carried over from the last term. The difference, however, does not mean that the 

Court has slowed down in its work. 

First, at the end of the 1928-1929 term, there were 41 petitions which came so late that they could not have been acted upon. This term, there were 53 such petitions, and, in addition, a larger number of appeals on which the Court had no time to act. Second, in the 1928-1929 term, there were 778 cases docketed, while during this term there were 841 cases docketed, a difference of 63 cases. Third, during the larger part of this term, there were only eight justices who carried the burden of the nine justices sitting throughout the previous term. During the early part of the term, Chief Justice Taft became seriously ill, and about a month before his resignation, he left Washington with the hope of recuperating from his illness. Immediately upon his resignation, the President appointed Mr. Hughes, but he 

6 Compare with the work of the Court since October Term, 1922.

Hankin: U. S. Supreme Court, 1928-1929, p. 2. 

[ 28 ]

did not take office until February 24th. Shortly afterwards, Justice Sanford died. This again left only eight judges, until the very last day of the term, when Mr. Roberts took his oath of office.

With all that, the Court disposed of all the cases which had been argued. This occurred for the first time in about thirty five years. Within the last few years, there were always a few such cases carried over to the succeeding term. 

In this connection, we must state, however, that in part this remarkable accomplishment was also due to the fact that during the last few weeks of the term the Court ordered a number of cases to be reargued in the next term. Some of these cases had been argued before Chief Justice Hughes took office, which left only seven of the nine justices to participate in the consideration and decision of these cases. In still others, it was felt that the questions involved should be considered by a full court. Altogether, the Court has shown a remarkable record when it completed the work of the term.

We have suggested a change in the method of submitting cases to the Supreme Court, since manifestly, one of the chief objects of the Jurisdictional Act has thus far failed of accomplishment. The Act was intended to be more than merely an accelerator for the work of the Court. It was intended in a way as an antidote to the litigious germ with which the country seems to be infected. Whether the blame rests with the contending parties or their attorneys, there seems to be a desire to carry matters to a finish, by appealing decisions through the entire hierarchy of the federal courts. or of the state courts, and finally to the Supreme Court of the United States. If the blame rests with the attorneys, it is also difficult to tell how far this is due to a desire to earn fees, or to vanity in appearing before the Supreme Court of the United States, and how far it is due to an earnest desire [ 29 ]

on their part to do all they can for their clients, or to the insufficient knowledge as to the types of cases which should be taken to the Supreme Court.

The Supreme Court should undertake to pass on the decisions of the lower courts, where the question is of genuine public importance. However, there seems to be a difference of opinion between the Supreme Court and the members of the bar as to what constitutes a question of sufficient public importance. During the 1928-1929 term, there were about 700 petitions filed to review the decisions of the lower courts and of these only 95 were granted. In other words, about one out of seven. During the term which just closed, the proportion of petitions granted was somewhat higher, still there were about 776 petitions considered by the Court, and of this number only 134 were granted." Certiorari.

Apparently the Court must spend a great portion of its time in sifting out the cases which it will consider. This is the only method whereby the Court can limit its work to an amount which it can humanly handle. And so during this term, although 794 cases were disposed of, the Court heard arguments in only 186 cases and rendered only 134 opinions. 

At the same time, the number of cases brought to the Supreme Court every year does not seem to be diminishing. 

On the contrary, there is every indication that it is increasing. 

In 1922-1923, the number of cases docketed during the term was 720; the next year the number rose to 731; the next two years were abnormal, for in 1924-1925, the Jurisdictional Act was passed and many attorneys thought that they would gain an advantage by filing their appeals or writs of error in the Supreme Court before the effective date of that Act. 

7 For the questions involved in all the petitions for certiorari filed. during this term, see U. S. Supreme Court Service, Oct. Term, 1929. [ 30 ]

In this they were mistaken. But during that year there were 

854 cases filed, and in the following year the number of cases was 754. In part this abnormal increase was due to a large number of war claims which came to the Supreme Court from the Court of Claims. In 1926-1927, the number of cases returned to "normalcy" with 732 filed. The next year the number rose to 754, then to 778, and during the very last term to 841.

In part, the difficulty lies in the fact that when an attorney works on a case, he becomes so absorbed in the importance of the issues he is fighting for, that he overlooks the relative importance of his problems in the range of questions before the Supreme Court. Often he becomes unable to state his own problem impartially and judiciously. 

The writer has had occasion to advise attorneys whether they should file petitions for certiorari. Invariably the inquiry would be accompanied by such partial or biased statement of facts, that the writer found it necessary to require further information. When the additional information arrived in the form of the bill of complaint, the answers and the opinions of the lower courts, an entirely different situation presented itself. While on the question originally presented by the attorney, the writer would have advised by all means to file a petition for certiorari, there were usually several good reasons for advising the contrary, after an impartial consideration of both sides of the controversy. 

The Supreme Court itself, however, has also contributed to this difficulty. The members of the bar are not apprised of the reasons for the Court's refusal to review the decisions of the lower courts, and there is always a chance, one calculates, that the denial of the writ was for some reason other than the merits of the controversy involved, or something other than the importance of the question presented. [31]

During the administration of Chief Justice Taft, the 

Court declined to give reasons for denials of certiorari, except in very few instances. In one case, Chief Justice 

Taft rendered an opinion denying a writ of certiorari.

This case involved the question whether a defendant on a murder charge was denied due process and a public trial, when the trial court excluded newspaper correspondents and the general public from the Court. In his opinion, however, the Chief Justice carefully stated that this did not indicate a change of policy to render opinions or to give reasons for denial of petitions for certiorari.

In a conversation with the late Chief Justice, the writer suggested that assigning reasons for denial of petitions for certiorari might in time have the effect of lessening the number of cases filed in the Supreme Court. The Chief 

Justice was for the moment impressed with this argument, but immediately changed his mind and thought this would not do, because the Court would be swamped with motions for reconsideration of petitions for certiorari which had been denied. Toward the close of this term there were several cases in which the reasons for dismissal of writs of certiorari were given by the Court. In one of these, the petition was filed too late under the provisions of the Jurisdictional Act, and was dismissed for that reason. Three petitions¹0 were filed asking the Court to review decisions of the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia in radio cases, appealed to that court from the decisions of the Federal Radio Com- 

8 Gaines vs. State of Washington, 277 U. S. 81. 

9 No. 832-Gotham Can Co. vs. U. S. Certiorari dismissed June 2, 1930. 

10 No. 944-Great Lakes Broadcasting Co. vs. Federal Radio Comm'n; 

No. 945-Voliva vs. Federal Radio Comm'n, and No. 946-Agricultural 

Broadcasting Co. vs, Federal Radio Comm'n. Certiorari dismissed June 2, 1930. [32]

mission. These petitions were dismissed on the authority of the Supreme Court's decision in the WGY case,¹¹ rendered earlier in the term, in which the Court had held that it had no jurisdiction to review such decisions, because the Court of Appeals acted in an administrative and not in a judicial capacity. It would undoubtedly be very helpful, if the members of the bar were apprised in all cases of the reasons why the Court declines to review the decisions of the lower courts.

There is still another reason for taking this step. In many instances denials of petitions for writs of certiorari show signs of inconsistency, either with reference to the questions of law involved or with reference to the importance of the questions. For example, during the 1927 term, a petition was filed presenting the question whether a railroad was bound by an obvious typographical error in the tariff filed by it with the Interstate Commerce Commission.12 

The Supreme Court of Minnesota had ruled that the railroad was not bound, and that the shipper was liable for the correct amount of the freight. The shipper petitioned the 

Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied. In the following term, several railroads petitioned the Supreme Court to review a decision in which the Circuit Court of Appeals had held to the contrary. Their petitions were likewise denied.13 There are many such instances, with varying degrees of similarity among the cases. 

Of course, the obvious answer is that the Court declined to review the decisions, because it did not deem the question of sufficient importance. How should it be explained that 

11 No. 122-Federal Radio Comm'n vs. General Electric Co. De cided May 19, 1930. See p. 117 infra. 

12 Van Dusen Harrington Co. vs. Illinois Central R. R. Co., 275 U. 

S. 554. 

13 Beaumont, Sour Lake & Western Railway Co. vs. Magnolia Pro vision Co., 278 U. S. 620. [33]

during this term, the Court granted a review in the Barker Painting Company case,14 whereas it failed to grant reviews to the same company on the same question on two previous occasions ?15 It is true, the last case presented the additional question whether the controversy had become moot, but when the Court granted the writ, it did not limit consideration of the case to this question. 

The Court has time and again stated that when it denies a petition for certiorari, the denial throws no light whatever on the Court's attitude toward the merits of the controversy. Yet, the Supreme Court is too important a body for anyone to disregard its orders, even if the Court itself says that they do not mean anything. Time and again, members of the bar, in their arguments, have pointed to the fact that in certain cases the Supreme Court had denied writs of review and have often added: "which probably implies that the Supreme Court acquiesced in the decision of the lower court," whereupon they were called down by one member of the Court or another.

The Court is often put in a wrong light by its failure to assign reasons for denials of petitions for writs of review. A serious illustration of this point came up in the debates in the Senate on the nomination of Judge Parker. Senator

Jones of Washington insisted that the blame in the Red 

Jacket Decision¹6 should not be placed at the door of Judge

Parker, but at the door of the Supreme Court itself. For in that case, the United Mine Workers had applied for a writ of review, which was denied by the Supreme Court," and, 14 No. 477-Barker Painting Co. vs. Brotherhood of Painters, Paper hangers and Decorators. See p. 278 infra. 

15 Barker Painting Co. vs. Brotherhood of Painters, Decorators and 

Paperhangers, etc., et al., 273 U. S. 747; 276 U. S. 631. 

16 United Mine Workers vs. Red Jacket Coal & Coke Co. See p. 

15 supra. 

17 United Mine Workers vs. Red Jacket Consolidated Coal & Coke 

Co. et al., 275 U. S. 536. [34]

as Senator Jones stated, not even Justice Brandeis dissented. from the order denying the writ. We quote what Senator 

Jones said because of the manner in which the question was 

"MR. JONES: This is an attempt to show that the 

Hitchman decision did not bind him (Judge Parker) to render the decision he felt he must render. It is also sought to show that there were other decisions of the Supreme Court justifying a different decision by him. Be that as it may, I most humbly beg to call attention to certain facts which to my mind wholly exonerate Judge Parker from any blame for the Red Jacket Decision and put the blame, if any blame there be, upon the Supreme Court of the United States itself. If there was error in Judge Parker's decision, an appeal in the nature of a writ of certiorari could be made to the Supreme Court of the United States, the court of final resort. Such a writ was applied for at the hands of the Supreme Court and filed on June 28, 1927. The

Supreme Court had the power to grant such a writ. If there was error in Judge Parker's decision, it should have done so. The Supreme Court, on October 17, 1927, denied the writ, continued the injunction, and in effect affirmed Judge Parker's decision and made it its own. According to the record, that decision was unanimous. 

MR. BORAH: The Senator does not mean to say that by reason of the fact that the Supreme Court denied the writ, therefore they affirmed the holding of Judge Parker? 

18 Cong. Rec., 71st Congr., 2nd Sess. May 6, 1930, p. 8722. [35]

MR. JONES: It seems to me that if they thought there was error in Judge Parker's holding, they should have granted the writ and brought the case up to them for review. 

MR. BORAH: The Supreme Court has said many times that in the refusal of the writ they do not pass upon the merits of the controversy at all. 

MR. JONES: I do not think that excuses the Supreme

Court in its responsibility in denying the writ asked for in a case where the decision of the Court below was based on a decision rendered by the Supreme Court. 

I understand that Justice Brandeis dissented in the Hitchman case. He was on the Court when the writ of certiorari was denied in the Red Jacket Case, and no dissent was made. As a matter of fact, if any justice on the bench at that time had questioned the basis of that decision, the writ of certiorari, in my judgment, would have been granted and the case brought up to the Supreme Court for review.

Judge Parker followed what he thought was the law as laid down by the highest court of the land. He could not overthrow the decision of the Supreme Court. That court, however, could have overthrown his decision. 

It did not do it. Hence I say in all humility that blame, if blame there is, for the decision in the Red Jacket Case, rests upon the Supreme Court of the United States. Why is it and I ask it in all sincerity-that when the questions involved in the Red Jacket Case came up, the judges who had dissented in the Hitchman case did not dissent in that case upon the refusal of the writ? 

It seems to me that if those judges felt that the Red 

Jacket Case was decided erroneously they should have disagreed with the denial of this writ." [36]

We quoted what Senator Jones said because of the manner in which this question was treated. In fairness to Senator Jones, it must be stated that upon examination of the record in the Red Jacket Case, there appears to be no reason why a writ of certiorari could have been denied, except that the decision of the lower court was right or that the case did not involve a question of importance which should have been settled by the Supreme Court. Assuming that the case did involve a question of great public importance, then the only conclusion remains that the Supreme Court acquiesced in the decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals. 

This, however, is merely conjecture, and it by no means appears that all the members of the Court took that view. 

But whatever might have been the views of the various justices in the Red Jacket Case, the fact remains that in very few instances have reasons ever been given for denials of petitions for certiorari, and there never have been any dissenting opinions written in such cases. That does not mean that the Justices acquiesced in the decisions of the lower courts, or that they were unanimous in the denial of the writ. The Court, however, would have avoided this and other incorrect implications by assigning reasons in the disposition of such cases. Appeals.

The Jurisdictional Act, as finally adopted,19 permitted some cases to come to the Supreme Court as a matter of right, either through a writ of error or by appeal. Later, Congress abolished the writ of error,20 and now such cases may come to the Supreme Court on appeal. The number of cases coming on appeal, is of course, much smaller than 

19 43 Stat. 936. See p. 1 supra. 

20 Act of January 31, 1928; Act of April 26, 1928. 45 Stat. 54 and 466.

[37]

those coming on petition for certiorari, and an appeal supposedly carries with it greater rights than are attached to the petitions for review.

The Court, it is evident, also guards itself against further extensions of the right of appeal. This attitude was apparent in an appeal which came from a decision of the Court of Claims. Congress had passed a special act authorizing the claimant to bring suit in the Court of Claims and also provided that either party shall have the right to appeal to the Supreme Court of the United States. The Supreme 

Court, in an opinion by Chief Justice Taft held that the words "that either party hereto may appeal to the Supreme Court of the United States upon or from any conclusion of law or judgment, from which appeals now lie in other cases," meant "that the review provided for was a petition for certiorari."21 22 

We said that appeals supposedly carry greater rights, but actually there is not much difference. Under the rules, every appellant must, within thirty days after filing an ap-¸ peal, show that the court has jurisdiction in the case. This subjects the appeals to preliminary review which many of them do not survive. During this term there were 124 appeals filed.23 Of these, 10 were not submitted to the Court, having been filed too late in the term. 30 appeals were carried over to the next term. This left 84 appeals which were disposed of. In 27 out of the 84, opinions were rendered; in 5, the decisions of the lower courts were affirmed without opinions. The remaining 52 were dismissed for lack of jurisdiction, upon the preliminary showing. Apparently, the members of the bar are no more successful in getting

An interesting question appeared in an appeal taken from the Circuit Court of Appeals suggesting a weakness in the provisions of the Jurisdictional Act. If an aggrieved party files an appeal from the decision of a state court, whereas he should apply for a writ of review, there is no harm done, for the law provides that if the Court finds that the appeal is not the proper remedy, it shall treat the appeal as a petition for a writ of certiorari.30 Where an appeal is filed from a decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals, and the proper remedy is by petition for writ of certiorari, then

the Supreme Court may not treat the appeal as a petition for review.3

The case in question was the State of North Dakota vs. 

Olson, Collector of Internal Revenue,32 in which the Circuit Court of Appeals held that the Bank of North Dakota, all of whose stock was owned by the State, was obliged to pay a capital stock tax under the provisions of the Revenue Act. The State maintained that the tax, if imposed, would be unconstitutional, because the bank is an instrumentality of the state. The Circuit Court of Appeals held, however, that in so far as the State engaged in the private business of banking, it did so as a private person and to that extent divested itself of its sovereignty; therefore, the Court held the tax was properly imposed. The State of North Dakota appealed to the Supreme Court. It should have petitioned for a writ of certiorari, because the only class of cases in which appeals may be taken from the Circuit Court of Appeals is where that court holds a state statute repugnant to the federal constitution, laws or treaties. The appeal was dismissed for lack of jurisdiction.

The Attorney General of the State should have known and followed the provisions of the Jurisdictional Act. On the other hand, it may be said that in this respect, the Jurisdictional Act carries the art of pleading to an absurdity. If a party aggrieved by a decision of the state court says "I appeal," then the Court in effect says: "You say you appeal. 

You should say that you petition for a review, and we shall grant (or deny) your petition." If, however, a party aggrieved by a decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals says, "I appeal," then the Court in effect says: "You say
