C. A. 

32 No. 435-North Dakota vs. Olson, Collector. Dismissed December 9, 1929. [ 40 ]

you appeal, you should have said that you petition for a review, therefore we dismiss the case for lack of jurisdiction." 

The appeals from the statutory three-judge courts constitute the most important class of appeals. These cases involve suits against state officers or commissions to enjoin them from enforcing laws, regulations or orders on the ground that they are repugnant to the constitution, laws or treaties of the United States;33 to enjoin orders of the Secretary of Agriculture issued under the provisions of the Packers and Stockyards Act;3 and to enjoin or set aside the orders of the Interstate Commerce Commission other than orders for the payment of money. 35 The provisions in the law relating to such suits are analogous, except that an application to enjoin the enforcement of a state law or order may be brought before a Justice of the Supreme Court of the United States, who may also be a member of the three-judge court. In all such cases, it should be noticed Congress provided that jurisdiction shall be in a court of three judges, and also that such cases must be expedited.36

In two interesting cases this term, the Court had occasion. to define the duties and powers of the district judges to whom applications were made for a three-judge court. In one case, the Supreme Court issued a writ of mandamus compelling a district judge to call a three-judge court and to vacate whatever orders he had issued, sitting as a single

36 The sections provide that "whenever such application as the aforesaid is presented to a justice of the Supreme Court, or to a judge, he shall immediately call to his assistance to hear and determine the application two other judges." And again, "the hearing upon such application for an interlocutory injunction shall be given precedence and shall be in every way expedited and be assigned for a hearing at the earliest possible day. ... judge.In the other case, a petition for writ of mandamus was denied, on the ground that the particular order of the district judge did not call for a three-judge court.38

The first case involved a rate order of the Railroad Commission of Montana. The Commission had ordered a reduction in intra-state rates chargeable for transportation of petroleum or petroleum products. The Northern Pacific 

Railroad Company and other railroads filed an application before the Interstate Commerce Commission complaining that the intra-state rates would result in discrimination against interstate commerce. At the same time, they filed a complaint in the U. S. District Court in Montana to enjoin the State Commission from enforcing its order, until the Interstate Commerce Commission will have determined on the validity of those rates.

The complaint contained an application for a threejudge court, asked for a temporary restraining order, for a preliminary injunction, and for a final injunction. Judge

Pray of the District Court in Montana granted a temporary restraining order, and the Railroad Commissioners then filed a motion to dismiss the bill of complaint and to dissolve the temporary restraining order. The hearing on this motion came before Judge Bourquin, another federal district judge in Montana, who granted the motion of the Railroad Commission and dissolved the temporary restraining order on the ground that the railroads had failed to sustain their burden of showing that the intra-state rates would unduly discriminate against interstate commerce. As it happened, in dismissing the bill of complaint, Judge Bourquin reached the correct conclusion, though not on correct reasoning, as was held later in a similar case which came to the 

37 No. 21, Original-Ex Parte: Northern Pacific Railway Co. Writ granted December 2, 1929. 

38 No. 20, Original-Ex Parte: Hobbs, Commissioner of Insurance. 

Mandamus denied December 9, 1929. [42]

Supreme Court from the statutory district court in North Dakota.39 

However, when Judge Bourquin dismissed the bill of complaint, the railroads petitioned the Supreme Court for a writ of mandamus to compel Judge Bourquin to vacate his order of dismissal and to convene a three-judge court. 

This writ was issued. The Supreme Court held that Judge 

Bourquin “sitting alone was without jurisdiction to hear either the motion to dissolve the temporary restraining order or the motion to dismiss the bill on the merits. In the presence of the application for an interlocutory injunction, which was at no time abandoned but constantly pressed, a single judge was as much without authority to dismiss the bill on the merits as he would be to grant either an interlocutory or a permanent injunction."

The Court laid down this rule for District Judges to follow in such cases: "When Judge Pray granted a temporary restraining order*** it became his duty immediately to call two other judges, one of whom should be either a circuit justice or a circuit judge." 

The second case involved a rate making order of the Superintendent of Insurance of the State of Kansas, revising the rates chargeable by the stock fire insurance companies doing business in the state. This order was based on the earnings of the insurance companies for a test period of ten years, and the earnings were computed on the basis of premiums received less losses and expenditures paid out during that period. In addition, the Superintendent included that portion of the income of the insurance companies from their investments, which their premiums in Kansas bore to the premiums throughout the country. 

One hundred and fifty insurance companies in the state 

39 No. 364-Board of Railroad Commissioners vs. Great Northern 

Railroad Co. See p. 10 supra. [ 43 ]

filed suit in the state courts, questioning the validity of this order. They maintained that the Superintendent exceeded his statutory authority and that the rates ordered were confiscatory. The Supreme Court of Kansas sustained the order, and the Supreme Court of the United States declined to review that decision.41 The denial of the writ was probably based on the Court's decision in a similar case which came from Missouri.42

Since the Supreme Court of the United States disposed of the case without passing on the merits of the controversy, these insurance companies instituted 150 separate suits in the United States District Court in Kansas to enjoin the enforcement of the rate making order. In the test case, which gave rise to the present suit, the bill of complaint alleged that the Federal District Court had jurisdiction on the ground of diversity of citizenship, since the plaintiff was a corporation of the state of New York; that the rate order was confiscatory; that in accordance with the ruling of the state court, all premiums in excess of the rates ordered, were deposited in a separate fund; and that unless restrained from enforcing this order, the Commissioner of Insurance13 would proceed against this fund, enforce the penalties and revoke the licenses of the company and its agents; all of which, it maintained, was contrary to the due process clause of the Constitution. The complaint also asked that a temporary restraining order be issued and that a three-judge court be convened for the purpose of issuing an interlocutory and a final injunction. 

Judge Pollock, before whom the case came, did not issue 

40 Aetna Insurance Co. et al. vs. Travis, Superintendent, etc., 259 P. 1068. 

41 Aetna Insurance Co. et al. vs. Baker, Superintendent, 276 U. S. 628. 

42 Aetna Insurance Co. vs. Hyde, 275 U. S. 440. 

43 Between the issuance of the order and the filing of this complaint the office of Superintendent of Insurance was changed to that of Commissioner of Insurance. [44]

a temporary restraining order, as was requested by the Insurance Company, but issued a rule for the Commissioner of Insurance and the Attorney General of the State to appear and show cause why a temporary restraining order should not be issued.

These defendants appeared and joined in the plaintiff's request that the judge convene a three-judge court to hear and determine the plaintiff's application for an interlocutory injunction. Judge Pollock then suggested that if the 

Commissioner would agree not to take any action which might be the subject of a restraining order, without first giving notice to the insurance company, a restraining order would not be issued. The Commissioner agreed to this condition, and Judge Pollock refrained from entering a restraining order, permitting the company, however, to review its application.

This happened in April, 1928. In the following month, the Commissioner of Insurance filed a motion to dismiss the suit on the ground that the matter had already been adjudicated in the previous suit brought by all the insurance companies doing business in the state. He argued that since the insurance company had chosen the state courts as its forum, it was bound by the final decision of the Supreme Court of the State, and it could not afterwards attack that judgment collaterally by bringing a new suit in the federal courts. This motion was overruled by Judge Pollock on 

January 8, 1929, and he allowed the defendants to file an answer to the bill of complaint. 44 44 A similar question of procedure presented itself in the case of Grubb vs. Ohio Public Utilities Commission, in which an operator of a bus line first appealed from the order of the Commission to the Supreme Court of the state and then brought suit in a federal three-judge court to enjoin the order of the Commission. The federal court dismissed the bill of complaint on the ground that the judgment of the state court had disposed of the controversy. This was affirmed by the Supreme Court of the United 

States in No. 491-Grubb vs. Ohio Public Utilities Commission, decided 

May 19, 1930. [45]

On May 4, 1929, the Insurance Company appeared and asked that a master be appointed to make findings of fact and conclusions of law concerning the issues in the case. An order of reference was made on May 10th. In the meantime, on May 6th, the Commissioner and the Attorney General notified the Company that they would proceed to enforce the rate order on and after May 20th, 1929. On the next day, May 7th, they again filed a motion for a hearing before three judges on the plaintiff's application for an interlocutory injunction. This came up on May 10th, together with the plaintiff's motion to refer the case to a master. On this day, the plaintiff orally declared in open court, that it had withdrawn its motion for a three-judge court. The judge held that the defendants' motion did not relate to any matter before the court and intimated that he was ready to grant a restraining order should the plaintiff apply for it.

The Insurance Company then filed a motion for an interlocutory injunction reciting that the Commissioner and Attorney General, if not restrained, would proceed "to enforce the rate order,* ** meaning and intending thereby, as this plaintiff believes and therefore alleges, to cancel the license of this plaintiff to do business in the State of Kansas, and otherwise proceed and act against the plaintiff to its irreparable injury and damage." Judge Pollock enjoined the defendants from cancelling the licenses of the plaintiff or its agents.

The Commissioner of Insurance and the Attorney Gen eral then petitioned the Supreme Court for a writ of mandamus to compel Judge Pollock to vacate his order and to assemble a three-judge court. The Supreme Court issued a rule to show cause why a writ of mandamus should not be issued, and Judge Pollock filed his return, in which he justified his course of action. His defense was that the com- [46]

plainant had abandoned its application for a three-judge court, and that the defendants had acquiesced in the abandonment; that the motion for the preliminary injunction finally presented by the plaintiff was not one which required a three-judge court, because it was not based on constitutional grounds; and the injunction he granted restraining the defendants from revoking the plaintiff's license, was likewise not based on constitutional grounds, but on the ground that under the state statute the state officers lacked power to revoke licenses for failure to comply with the rate order; finally that having issued this preliminary injunction merely upon the construction of the state statute, the state officers had a perfectly good remedy by appealing to the Circuit Court of Appeals, but could not insist on a threejudge court.

It is most unfortunate that in a controversy of this type between a federal judge and important state officers, there should be so much bitterness and so many controverted facts. The defendants alleged that from the very beginning, Judge Pollock was unwilling to call a three-judge court; that when the defendants first joined in the request for a three-judge court, Judge Pollock said in substance: 

You will have a difficult time in getting a three judge court out of me. And that later, when the defendants renewed their request, he stated that since the plaintiff no longer moved for a three-judge court, he was not going to call any; that he did not like to call a court of three judges; and that he thought the matter could be disposed of before it would be necessary to call a three-judge court. 

The defendants took the position that the injunction issued by Judge Pollock was clearly erroneous, and that it was merely a scheme for deciding the case without permitting it to go before a three-judge court; that while it was true that the motion for the interlocutory injunction was not based [ 47 ]

on any constitutional grounds, it was also true that the motion did not state any grounds at all, but that nevertheless it was only a link in the entire proceeding to enjoin the enforcement of the rate order on the ground that the statute authorizing such order was unconstitutional. 

This charge of bad faith was countercharged by Judge Pollock who characterized the actions of the state officers as "a mere device to reconstitute the court so that the merits of the controversy may be submitted to a different tribunal." 

On the facts of the controversy, Judge Pollock stated that the plaintiff and the defendants had abandoned the application for a three-judge court, when the defendants agreed not to take any action which might be subject to a restraining order, without first giving notice to the Company. This was contradicted by the state officers. Their view was that the enforcement of the rate order was to be postponed pending the outcome of a similar suit in the federad district court in Missouri, where a similar question was raised with reference to the validity of an order of the Superintendent of Insurance of that State.45 (This, by the way, was also contradicted by Judge Pollock.) The state officers maintained that since the postponement of action in their case did not mean an abandonment of the application for a three-judge court, it was the duty of Judge Pollock to call two other judges to his assistance, but that he had no jurisdiction to pass on the motion to dismiss the case nor to appoint a master to find the facts and make conclusions of law. 46 

45 National Fire Insurance Co. of Hartford vs. Jos. B. Thompson, 

Superintendent of Insurance, 34 F. (2) 185. See p. 131 infra. 

46 In the Bourquin Case, supra, after the Supreme Court held that a single judge had no jurisdiction to pass on the motions in the case, Judge Bourquin appointed a special master to take testimony and fix the time for the final hearing of the case before a statutory three-judge court. The 

Supreme Court then ordered that a formal writ of mandamus be issued and [48]

Since it appeared that the decision in the case might depend on controverted facts, the State officers moved the Supreme Court that a special master be appointed to take testimony and to report on his findings as to facts. This motion, however, was overruled.

In this manner, the petition for writ of mandamus was presented to the Supreme Court. The Court sustained 

Judge Pollock on what it found to be "incontrovertible portions of the return" to the rule to show cause why the writ of mandamus should not be issued. The substance of the decision was this: In the complaint filed by the Insurance 

Company, jurisdiction was invoked by reason of diversity of citizenship as well as on constitutional grounds. The district court therefore had jurisdiction in the case as an ordinary court of equity, and the plaintiff had a right to present a much narrower claim in its motion for the interlocutory✓ injunction than in the original complaint; and Judge Pollock was right in denying to the state officers "the power to force upon the plaintiffs a constitutional issue which at that moment they did not care to raise."

We cannot and will not assume either the facts as stated by the state officers or their allegations of bad faith on the part of Judge Pollock. It seems to us, however, that the 

Supreme Court might have granted the motion for the appointment of a special master to take testimony, so that if the proof did not sustain the allegations presented by the state officers, the federal judge might have been exonerated from such imputations.

Treating the allegations of the state officers as a hypothetical case, and applying the decision of the Supreme Court to it, it seems to us that while the court, in the Bourquin case laid down a rule that a single district judge canthat there be included in the writ a direction requiring Judge Bourquin to vacate the order appointing the special master and fixing the time for the final hearing. [49]

not do anything in such case except to assemble a threejudge court, in this case, the Supreme Court left a loophole whereby a single district judge can dispose of a case or at least thwart the efforts of the state officers in the enforcement of the state law.

We come now to the substance of the cases treated by the Supreme Court during this term. Twenty one cases came to the Court as a court of original jurisdiction. Several of these involved controversies among the states as to boundaries; several suits were brought to enforce contracts among the states; in a number of cases, several states sought to enjoin others from injuring the sovereign or proprietory rights of the complaining states, through diversion or pollution of waters; and in two cases the power of the Supreme Court was invoked to settle jurisdictional difficulties in the lower courts through a writ of mandamus.

There were 963 cases on the appellate docket. Any exhaustive classification of these might fill a volume. Furthermore, the classification must in all instances depend on the object to be attained. Since the purpose of this volume is to present the work of the Court on important questions which are of general public interest, the classification of the cases will be mainly along economic, political or other social problems, irrespective of their places in the technical study of the law.¹7 

47 A classification of the questions of law involved in all the cases filed during this term will be found in the Subject Index of the U. S. Supreme 

Court Service, October Term, 1929. [ 50 ]

CHAPTER III. 

Railroad Problems* 

We have already mentioned how the nomination of 

Judge Parker brought forth an unprecedented amount of criticism concerning his views on economic and social problems. This criticism was transferred immediately from 

Judge Parker to the Supreme Court itself. Some of the newspapers at the time adopted a slogan that Judge Parker was merely an incident and that the Supreme Court was the issue. But Senator Norris, in his debate on the nomination, went a step further and declared that Judge Parker was merely an incident, that the Supreme Court was only an incident, the real issue was human liberty.¹

Of the 135 opinions rendered by Judge Parker during his first five years on the Circuit Court of Appeals, there were only two which were of tremendous public importance. The Red Jacket Decision was undoubtedly one of these.2 It is interesting to note, however, that both the opponents and proponents of Judge Parker carefully refrained from mentioning the only other significant decision in which he had rendered an opinion, and that was in the 

Lake Cargo Cases.3 

Space limitations forbid covering all the important railroad cases which were considered by the Supreme Court during this term. For analysis of all the railroad cases, see U. S. Supreme Court Service, October 

Term, 1929, Subject Index-RAILROADS. 

1 Congressional Record, 71st Congr., 2nd Sess., May 2, 1930, p. 8476. 

2 See p. 15 supra. 

3 Anchor Coal Company et al. vs. United States and Interstate Commerce Commission et al., 25 F. (2) 462. [51]

For this reason, it may fairly be said that, although the debates on Judge Parker's nomination were of serious character, nevertheless they were tainted with superficiality. 

For had the debates been thorough, the Senators would undoubtedly have found a great deal to say concerning the Lake Cargo decision. The problem there, however, was difficult to handle. For how could the opponents of Judge 

Parker, presumably the champions of human rights, denounce him for having set aside an order of the Interstate Commerce Commission, directing the railroads to charge higher rates? And how could the proponents of the nominee point to him as a friend of the people because he upheld lower rates in that case, without also disclosing that thereby he invalidated to a great extent, an Act of Congress, the Hoch-Smith Resolution? It would have been necessary for both sides to go into considerable and difficult details, in order to bring out the significance of this decision. 

The Lake Cargo Cases presented a purely economic question. The West Virginia coal fields compete with the 

Pittsburgh and Ohio mines for the lake cargo market. The competition is very keen, so that a difference of a few cents in the price of coal, might throw the business one way or the other. The West Virginia coal fields have always been at a disadvantage due to their greater distance from the Lake Ports, and naturally they could not compete with the Pittsburgh and Ohio mines, unless they had some advantage in transportation or in lower production costs. 

In fixing transportation rates from the West Virginia fields to the Lake Ports, the railroads were therefore obliged to give consideration not only to transportation factors, but also to this economic condition. In fact, although the charge for the entire haul from the West Virginia fields to the Lake Ports has always been higher than from the Pitts- 

4 See p. 57 infra, for text of Resolution. [ 52 ]

burgh and Ohio mines, the rate of transportation from the West Virginia coal fields per ton mile has always been less. 

Partly, the lesser rate was due to the fact that the southern railroads were better equipped to carry coal, but mainly it was due to the fact that if the same rate were maintained, the railroads would get no coal at all to haul from the West 

Virginia fields to the Lake Ports.

Despite the fact that the rates per mile, from the southern coal fields to the Lake Ports were less, as was said, the transportation per ton for the entire haul was higher. In 

1912, the difference stood at 20c. per ton. Then followed a series of increases in rates which, however, had not increased uniformly, until in 1927, the difference stood at 25c. per ton.

It was during that year that the coal operators in the Pittsburgh and Ohio regions filed an application with the Interstate Commerce Commission, asking for a reduction in the freight rates of coal to the Lake Ports. The Commission ordered the northern railroads to reduce the freight 20c. per ton, and this made the differential between the 

Pittsburgh and Ohio mines and the West Virginia coal fields 45c. per ton.

Again, the West Virginia coal fields found themselves. at a disadvantage, and the southern railroads likewise calculated that they might lose the coal traffic on account of that differential. The southern railroads therefore voluntarily reduced their own freight rates 20c. per ton, which made the differential again 25c. per ton. 

The northern coal operators and the northern railroads then again applied to the Interstate Commerce Commission for an order directing the southern railroads to suspend their reduced rates and to charge 20c. per ton more. The 

Commission granted these applications. It ordered the southern railroads to maintain their former rates as mini- [53]

mum rates. This, the Commission did under the authority of the Transportation Act of 1920, empowering the Commission to maintain not only maximum rates, but also minimum rates chargeable by interstate carriers."

The procedure followed by the Commission in reaching this result was responsible for bringing the Hoch-Smith Resolution into the case. The Commission suspended the lower rates and ordered the southern carriers to justify the reduction. The railroads showed that the lower rates would yield a fair return and would not cast any burden on other classes of freight carried by them. The Commission held this was not sufficient; but that the carriers must also show that the new rates would not be unreasonably low as compared with rates on other classes of freight. This, the Commission held was necessary, because in accordance with the Hoch-Smith Resolution, the Commission was directed to institute lower rates on the products of industries suffering from economic depression, and if any reduction in rates was to be effected, those industries should benefit thereby. 

In considering the reduction of rates made by the southern railroads, the Commission took into consideration various transportation factors, but also considered the fact that the coal industry in the north was in a depressed condition, while the southern coal fields prospered, and furthermore, that the cost of production in the southern fields was lower than in the north.

We must digress here for a moment to show how farreaching such factors are and how various classes of persons will take sides on a presumably innocent question, such as freight rates on coal. The cost of production in the West 

Virginia mines is lower, because labor in the southern coal fields is not organized; they operate on a non-union basis and wages there are lower than in the north, where labor 

5 Interstate Commerce Act, Title 49, U. S. C., Sec. 15 (1). 

[54]

is more or less organized. For this reason, when the case came up, labor unions in general took sides on the controversy, although not actively in the litigation before the courts, and favored the northern regions, which were more disposed to their own interests.

When the Commission ordered the southern railroads to suspend their lower rates, the latter brought suit in the statutory three-judge court in West Virginia to set aside this order of the Commission. They argued that the Commission had no power to make or suspend rates as a result of economic considerations or any considerations other than transportation factors.

The Commission took the position that it was empowered by Congress not only to regulate rates, but also to initiate them, and that in the present case it was not possible to regulate rates without taking into consideration economic factors. The Commission argued that it not only had the discretion to take such factors into consideration, but that under the Hoch-Smith Resolution, it was mandatory upon the Commission to do so. 

Judge Parker, who as Circuit Judge, sat in the Special District Court in the case, upheld the contentions of the southern railroads and characterized the action of the Commission as an attempt to equalize industrial conditions through the power of rate regulation. This, he held, the 

Commission could not do, and Congress could not authorize the Commission to do it. The District Court then set aside the order of the Commission and enjoined it from interfering with the reduced rates filed by the southern railroads. 

The Lake Cargo Cases then came to the Supreme Court of the United States. The cases were argued, but before a decision was rendered, the railroads effected an agreement among themselves whereby the difference of 20 cents was cut fifty-fifty. This left the Commission with a bare order [55]

of the District Court which, of course, was no longer binding on the Commission, and the Supreme Court dismissed the cases as having become moot. 

This opinion of Judge Parker was especially important at the time of the debates in the Senate concerning his nomination, for two reasons: (1) It was the first case before the courts involving the validity of the Hoch-Smith Resolution. 

Regarding the Red Jacket decision, Judge Parker's proponents relied on the proposition that he was bound by the decisions of the Supreme Court of the United States in the Hitchman Case and others which followed. In the Lake 

Cargo Cases, however, Judge Parker was not bound by any prior decision on the Hoch-Smith Resolution. (2) The second reason for its importance was that at the time of the debates, the validity of the Hoch-Smith Resolution was pending before the Supreme Court of the United States in the Deciduous Fruit Case. 

It may be said, that the Hoch-Smith Resolution was only indirectly involved in the Lake Cargo Cases. The Commission there did not initiate any rates or order the filing of rates, but merely imposed a burden on railroads to show that their reduced rates were justifiable. The Commission also required the carriers to show that not only would the reduction not be so unreasonably low as to cast a burden on other types of freight, but that the reduction did not result in rates unreasonably low as compared with other commodities carried by these railroads.

Rate Regulation. 

In the Deciduous Fruit Case, the Hoch-Smith Resolution was more directly involved. Here, the Commission reduced the rates on transportation of deciduous fruits from 

6 No. 7-Ann Arbor Railroad Company et al. vs. United States et al. 

Decided June 2, 1930. [56]

the west coast to points east and made this reduction directly under the authority of the Hoch-Smith Resolution. 

This Resolution, passed in 1925 as a farm relief measure, provided as follows:" "That it is hereby declared to be the true policy in rate making to be pursued by the Interstate Commerce Commission in adjusting freight rates, that the conditions which at any given time prevail in our several industries should be considered in so far as it is legally possible to do so, to the end that commodities may freely move. "That the Interstate Commerce Commission is authorized and directed to make a thorough investigation of the rate structure of common carriers subject to the interstate commerce act, in order to determine to what extent and in what manner existing rates and charges may be unjust, unreasonable, unjustly discriminatory, or unduly preferential, thereby imposing undue burdens, or giving undue advantage as between the various localities and parts of the country, the various classes of traffic, and the various classes and kinds of commodities, and to make, in accordance with law, such changes, adjustments, and redistribution of rates and charges as may be found necessary to correct any defects so found to exist. In making any such change, adjustment, or redistribution the commission shall give due regard, among other factors, to the general and comparative levels in market value of the various classes and kinds of commodities as indicated over a reasonable period of years, to a natural and proper development of the country as a whole, and to the maintenance of an adequate system of transportation. 

In the progress of such investigation the commission 743 Stat. 801. [57]

shall, from time to time, and as expeditiously as possible, make such decisions and orders, as it may find to be necessary or appropriate upon the record then made in order to place the rates upon designated classes of traffic upon a just and reasonable basis with relation to other rates. Such investigation shall be conducted with due regard to other investigations or proceedings affecting rate adjustments which may be pending before the commission. "In view of the existing depression in agriculture, the commission is hereby directed to effect with the least practicable delay such lawful changes in the rate structure of the country as will promote the freedom. of movement by common carriers of the products of agriculture affected by that depression, including livestock, at the lowest possible lawful rates compatible with the maintenance of adequate transportation service: Provided, That no investigation or proceeding resulting from the adoption of this resolution shall be permitted to delay the decision of cases now pending before the commission involving rates on products of agriculture, and that such cases shall be decided in accordance with this resolution." 

Over four hundred railroads were affected by the order of the Commission in the Deciduous Fruit Case, and they brought suit in the federal district court in California to set aside the order. They claimed that the resulting rates would compel them to carry this freight at a loss; that they were entitled to receive a fair return on this class of freight independent of earnings on other classes of freight; and that the Commission misconstrued the Hoch-Smith Resolution and exceeded its authority by ordering the reduction, without first making a complete investigation of the entire [58]

rate structure of interstate carriers, in order to ascertain whether such reduction was warranted. 

The District Court denied the relief sought by the carriers, and they appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. Here, the carriers argued that the Commission had no authority to lower rates on agricultural products, if the existing rates were reasonable or otherwise lawful. In this connection, they pointed out that in the original proceedings in the case, the Commission found that existing rates were not unreasonable or unduly prejudicial. 

The carriers argued that the Commission had no authority to reduce the rates on deciduous fruits below a fair return to the carriers, and if the Hoch-Smith Resolution permitted such reduction, it must also be construed as having required that the Commission should, at the same time, increase the rates on other classes of freight to counterbalance the loss in revenue sustained through the proposed reduction on these agricultural products. In other words, they argued, that under the Hoch-Smith Resolution, a reduction of rates cannot take place, unless there is a general proceeding to investigate and revise the entire rate structure throughout the country. If, however, the Hoch-Smith Resolution authorized the Commission to reduce rates below a fair return and without a general revision upwards of other rates, then, the carriers argued, the Resolution must be held unconstitutional. 

The carriers also argued that by reducing the rates, the Commission undertook to regulate rates upon a consideration of industrial conditions rather than conditions of transportation. This, they argued, was not within the power of the Commission to do, nor within the power of Congress to authorize. At any rate, the carriers contended, the rates ordered by the Commission in this case were confiscatory, [59]

because the order compelled them to carry the freight not only without profit, but at an actual loss. 

The United States, the Interstate Commerce Commission and the California Growers' and Shippers' Protective

League argued, in opposition, that there was ample evidence. before the Commission to sustain its finding that the old rates on deciduous fruits were unjust and unreasonable, and that the railroads, in the present case, have not established that the lower rates were confiscatory; that as a matter of fact, the claim of the carriers that they were compelled to operate at a loss under the new rates, was not supported by evidence.

In stating that the existing rates were unjust and unreasonable, the Commission meant that they were unjust and unreasonable in view of economic conditions. It pointed out that the deciduous fruit rates, like all other rates, were subjected to nation wide increases during the war and up to 1920; while the rates on the great body of transcontinental traffic have since been reduced to about 25% above their pre-war levels, the rates on deciduous fruits remained over 50% above the pre-war level; furthermore, while the market prices of deciduous fruits were less than 57% of their peak in 1920, with the prices in 1926 being only 3% higher than those in 1917, the freight rates had been reduced only 10% below their peak, and were over 50% higher than their 1917 level.

The Government further argued that the Commission did not misconstrue or misapply the Hoch-Smith Resolution, but that on the contrary, it correctly interpreted the Resolution to mean that, within the flexible limits of the Commission's discretion in determining the lawfulness of rates, it should require the lowest rates, within those flexible limits, on agricultural products affected by depression; and while it is true that the Commission may not order reduc- [60]

tions of rates solely for the purpose of meeting the supposed necessities of industry, it may, after finding existing rates unreasonable, take cognizance of the economic conditions. in aid of its rate-making functions. 

It is a pity that the Senate, in the debates on Judge Parker's nomination, did not express its views on the Hoch- Smith Resolution, although under our law a legislative interpretation has no binding effect, for the interpretation laid down by the Supreme Court, in effect, nullified that Resolution of Congress. The Court held that the Resolution made no change in the existing law with reference to rate making, and that the Commission could not initiate any changes in the rates without finding that the existing rates were unjust and unreasonable or were discriminatory. 

As to the provision in the Resolution itself, that the Commission is directed to make such lawful changes in the rate structure of the country as will promote the freedom of movement by common carriers of the products of agriculture affected by depression, the Court held that "they are more in the nature of a hopeful characterization of an object deemed desirable if, and in so far as, it may be attainable, than of a rule intended to control rate making." 

The Court declared that these words in the Resolution "should not lightly be disregarded," but at the same time. added that if by those words it was intended to work a change in the existing law, then they "give rise to a serious question respecting the constitutional validity of the paragraph of which they are a part.”

The broad question which confronts us here was whether the Interstate Commerce Commission had the authority to regulate rates giving consideration to economic conditions in addition to transportation factors. Perhaps it is correct to say that the Commission should limit itself to technical questions of transportation, and that it might be dangerous [61]

to entrust an administrative body of Government with farreaching economic powers. One often wonders, however, whether that is not a mere fiction, and whether it is possible for the Commission to decide on rate questions without taking into consideration economic factors. This is especially so in cases where the very existence of the rate problem is but an aspect of an economic controversy. 

This point can be illustrated by the Export Cotton Rate Case recently decided by the Supreme Court. The economic issues in this case were unfortunately not passed on by the Supreme Court, since the decision was based on a jurisdictional question, but the economic problems were there, and sooner or later, may again come to the Supreme Court.

In this case, the Commission equalized the railroad transportation rates chargeable to waterfront and uptown warehouses on export cotton shipped from the southwest to the Gulf ports. This is a complicated problem, requiring some knowledge of the numerous factors in the transportation of cotton bales before, during and after shipment for delivery at shipside, in cities like New Orleans, Houston, Galveston and other Gulf ports.

Prior to 1921, all the cotton warehouses, compresses and facilities at the Gulf ports were located at a distance from the waterfront, and there were but two competing groups of cotton concentrators-"the uptown group" and the "interior" concentrators, that is, those located on the line between the cotton fields and the Gulf ports. The average cost of moving cotton from the uptown warehouses to the wharf, to which it was moved by the railroads in switching service or by other means, was approximately three cents per 100 pounds. In view of this fact, the railroads fixed 

8 No. 19-Alexander Sprunt & Son, Inc. et al. vs. United States et al. 

Decided April 14, 1930. [ 62 ]

the shipside rate at a figure of three cents per 100 pounds higher than the "city-delivery" rate, paid by the uptown warehouses. In other words, if a cotton concentrator in the interior of Texas, western Louisiana, Arkansas and Oklahoma shipped his cotton directly to the ship for export, the rate was three cents per 100 pounds higher than that charged to an uptown concentrator to whom the cotton was delivered at his warehouse, where the cotton was sorted, concentrated and later switched to the docks. For the switching service, the carriers charged three cents, so that ultimately, the charge was the same to both classes of warehouses. 

After 1921, a number of high density compresses were placed near the wharves and these appeared as the disturbing element in the equal rates which theretofore existed. 

These so-called "waterfront" warehouses were charged by the carriers the same rate for export cotton as the uptown warehouses, but the former were not obliged to pay for switching service, since they could deliver their cotton to the ship by means of their own trolleys at a very small cost. 

The uptown warehouses then caused the Interstate Commerce Commission to institute an investigation and, after hearings were held, the Commission found that there was undue preference given to the waterfront warehouses and issued an order requiring the carriers to increase the citydelivery rate one cent per 100 pounds and to reduce the shipside rates, exclusive of wharf or pier terminal charges, equivalent to two cents per hundred weight, to the level of the increased city rates. Thus, whereas formerly both the uptown and waterfront warehouses paid the same charges for the transportation, but the uptown warehouses were obliged to pay in addition three cents for switching and delivery charges to the ship, the new rate reduced the total charge to the same level.

The waterfront warehouses then argued that the new [63]

rates deprived them altogether of the advantages of their location near the wharves. They and the carriers brought suit in the federal district court in Texas to enjoin this order of the Commission. The court dismissed the suit for lack of equity. The railroads did not consider that their own interest warranted taking the case to the Supreme Court of the United States, and the waterfront warehouses alone appealed.

They argued that the decree of the lower court should be reversed on the ground that the Commission was without authority to issue such order, for the finding of the Commission that existing rates resulted in giving an undue preference to the waterfront warehouses was not supported by evidence, that whatever advantages accrued to the waterfront warehouses were due to advantages of location and not due to a difference in rates, and that the Commission had exceeded its authority in that its order was based not on considerations relating to transportation, but on economic factors favoring one set of competitors as against another. 

They also contended that the Commission's order, prohibiting the railroads to make allowances for substituted transportation from their warehouses to the adjacent wharves, was arbitrary and in violation of law, in view of the fact that the Commission, at the same time, approved and authorized allowances for similar substituted transportation under other circumstances. In other words, if instead of switching the cotton from the uptown warehouses, it was conveyed to the wharves by some other means, the railroads could make allowances for this transportation. 

The Interstate Commerce Commission argued that its order was supported by ample evidence, and that it had given consideration to all the testimony on record, upon which it concluded that the disparity in the rates resulted in an undue preference and prejudice, and that its finding [64]

of discrimination in the rates was based solely upon consideration of transportation conditions. As to the contention of the appellants that the order of the Commission was discriminatory, in that it prohibited allowances for substituted transportation to the waterfront warehouses and not to the others, the Commission answered that this was not a discrimination, because the former was in fact an interplant movement, while the latter was an actual movement from the warehouses to the wharves.

As was stated, the Supreme Court did not pass on the merits of the controversy. Apparently, there was no inclination on the part of the Court to pass on complicated questions, which were mainly the business of the Interstate Commerce Commission. Originally, there were two suits brought in this case, one by the railroads and the other by the waterfront warehouses. In the District Court in 

Houston, Texas, where suit was brought, the two cases were consolidated, and when the District Court decided in favor of the Commission, the railroads figured that by taking further part in the controversy, they might offend their shippers, who did not own warehouses on the waterfront.

At the same time, they probably believed there was no economic advantage to be gained by them, and they therefore did not join in the appeal to the Supreme Court. 

In an opinion by Justice Brandeis, the Supreme Court traced the statutes authorizing suits to enjoin the rate orders of the Interstate Commerce Commission, and came to the conclusion that the warehouses had no standing in court, except as intervenors in the suit filed by the railroads. Since the decree rendered against the railroads became final, for lack of appeal, the warehouses had no standing in the Supreme Court, independently of the carriers, because as shippers they do not have such an interest in the orders of the Commission, as would entitle them to relief before the [65]

special three-judge court. If the rates were unjust and discriminatory as to them, they may have an appropriate remedy before the Commission and the courts, and for this reason the Supreme Court ordered that the case be dismissed without prejudice.

Other cases came to the Supreme Court during the term, involving the validity of rate making orders of the Interstate Commerce Commission. The Commission ordered a reduction of rates on wood pulp shipped from the docks at Hoboken, New Jersey, to a paper plant at Garfield, New Jersey. The railroads maintained that the order was invalid, because the shipment was in fact an intra-state shipment. 

On this the Commission disagreed. 

It appeared from the evidence that the course of business which culminated in the delivery of wood pulp at the plant of the paper company was as follows: The company made an offer to a New York broker to buy certain quantities of wood pulp; the broker submitted the offer to a mill abroad; the latter accepted the offer by cable, and this information was conveyed by the broker to the company. 

A contract was then entered into between the New York broker and the company, calling for shipments from abroad of quantities of the described wood pulp, naming the place of delivery as "Ex-dock New York." The foreign mill shipped the quantities ordered, having marked the bales with a brand, but not so as to indicate the broker's individual customer. When the bales were shipped, the mill cabled to the broker, who paid for the pulp as it left the foreign port. 

On arrival of the vessel at Hoboken, he issued an order for the Hoboken Manufacturers' Railroad Company to transport the pulp to the paper company at Garfield, N. J. 

Upon these facts the Interstate Commerce Commission found that the shipment from the foreign port to Garfield, N. J., was continuous, and therefore assumed jurisdiction. [66]

in the case. It ordered the Erie Railroad Company and the 

Hoboken Manufacturers' Railroad Company to publish a rate which should not exceed 10 cents per 100 pounds on the shipment of imported wood pulp in carloads from Hoboken to Garfield, N. J.

The railroads brought suit to set aside the order of the Commission, maintaining that the foreign shipment ended with the delivery of the wood pulp at the Hoboken docks, and that the shipment from Hoboken to Garfield, N. J., was an intra-state shipment over which the Commission had no jurisdiction.

The District Court in New Jersey, where the action was brought, upheld the contentions of the carriers and annulled the order of the Commission, which then appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. This Court reversed the decree, holding that the Commission did have jurisdiction in the matter, and furthermore, that the findings of the Commission should have been conclusive on the District Court.⁹

The most interesting cases involving railroad rates arose out of the intra-state rates ordered by the Railroad Commissions of the states of Montana and North Dakota. 

After the Montana Railroad Commission issued its order reducing intra-state rates on petroleum products, the railroads applied to the Interstate Commerce Commission. to determine whether those intra-state rates would cause an unjust discrimination against interstate commerce. At the same time, the carriers filed a complaint in the Federal District Court in Montana, asking for a temporary restraining order and for an interlocutory injunction to restrain the State Commission from enforcing its order until the matter will have been disposed of by the Interstate Commerce 

9 No. 30-United States and I. C. C. vs. Erie R. R. Co. et al. De cided November 25, 1929. [67]

Commission. A temporary restraining order was granted by Judge Pray. The State Commission, however, filed a motion to dismiss the complaint. That motion came before 

Judge Bourquin, who dismissed the complaint without convening a three-judge court, as was requested in the bill of complaint, on the ground that the affidavits filed by the carriers did not support a conclusion of unjust discrimination against interstate commerce. As already stated,' 10 the Supreme Court of the United States issued a writ of mandamus ordering Judge Pray and Judge Bourquin to convene a three-judge court and to vacate their orders, on the ground that only a three-judge court had jurisdiction in the case.

In the meantime, nothing further was done by the Interstate Commerce Commission. The Commission took the position that it was not obliged to take jurisdiction over rates which had not become effective, since the effectiveness of the rates had been prevented by the temporary restraining order of the district court. The railroads, however, brought suit in the courts of the District of Columbia to compel the Interstate Commerce Commission to take jurisdiction in the case, and the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia issued a writ of mandamus compelling the Interstate Commerce Commission to take jurisdiction. The Commission brought the case to the Supreme Court and argued that should it entertain applications to pass upon rates which are not effective, it would be flooded with such complaints. The 

Supreme Court granted a writ of review," and the case has been advanced for argument for the next term.

A similar situation was created with reference to the rate order of the North Dakota Commission, but there the District Court advanced further than the District Court in 

10 See p. 42 supra. 

11 No. 768-I. C. C. vs. Northern Pacific Railroad Co. et al. Cer tiorari granted, May 5, 1930. [68]

Montana. It actually issued an interlocutory injunction restraining the State Commission from enforcing the intrastate rates ordered by it, having made a finding that the rates, if enforced, would unjustly discriminate against interstate commerce.

The case came on direct appeal to the Supreme Court, and the decision of the lower court was reversed in an opinion by Chief Justice Hughes.12 The Chief Justice. 

reviewed the history of federal control over intra-state rates in so far as they may affect interstate commerce. And he came to the conclusion that the federal courts had no jurisdiction to entertain suits to set aside the rate making orders of state commissions, on the ground that the proposed rates may discriminate against interstate commerce, before the administrative body, charged with the duty of passing on these questions, had acted upon those rates. The Court went further and held that the District Court erred in issuing the injunction, because the intra-state rates were lawful until declared otherwise by the Interstate Commerce Commission.

The Supreme Court has thus avoided an anomalous situation which had arisen through the fact that the District Court in North Dakota did take jurisdiction in this type of case and through the fact that the District Court in Montana, which did not take jurisdiction, was compelled by the Supreme Court to take jurisdiction in such controversy. As the matter stood, an impasse was created by the fact that a federal district court ordered a state commission to refrain from enforcing its order until the Interstate Commerce Commission will have determined whether the intra-state rates might discriminate against interstate commerce, and by the fact that the Interstate Commerce Commission 'took 

12 No. 364-Board of R. R. Comm'rs of North Dakota vs. Gt. North ern R. R. Co. Decided May 19, 1930. See p. 10 supra. [ 69 ]

the position that it will not pass upon those rates, since they had been rendered ineffective by the order of the federal district court.

Terminal Facilities. 

Several cases this term concerned the power of the Interstate Commerce Commission to order the use of union stations. The most important came from Los Angeles, which was anxious that several carriers be compelled to use a union passenger station in that city. And the question arose whether the Interstate Commerce Commission had the power to compel the carriers, against their will, to use such station.

In 1916, Los Angeles applied to the Railroad Commission of California to compel the Southern Pacific Railroad, the Atchison, Topeka and Santa Fe and the Los Angeles and Salt Lake Railroad Company to procure a sufficient amount of land in the Plaza district of Los Angeles, and to erect thereon a union passenger station. 

After investigation, the Railroad Commission of California ordered the construction of the union station. The carriers appealed to the Supreme Court of California to set aside the order of the Commission, and that court granted the relief, holding that the construction and use of union stations would involve the relocation of tracks, etc., which in its opinion was a matter solely within the jurisdiction of the Interstate Commerce Commission. The Railroad Com mission then brought the case to the Supreme Court of the United States, which affirmed that decision, but merely held that the relocation of the tracks required a certificate of approval by the Interstate Commerce Commission, and that this was a condition precedent to the validity of any action by the carriers or any order by the state commission; that until the Interstate Commerce Commission had acted, the [70]

carriers could not be required to provide a new union station. The Supreme Court, in that opinion, did not go to the extent of holding that the Interstate Commerce Commission had exclusive jurisdiction in ordering carriers to use a union station. That decision was rendered in April, 1924.13

Before that decision came down, however, Los Angeles had applied to the Interstate Commerce Commission for a certificate of necessity, covering the relocation of tracks and the construction and use of the union station. The Commission made certain findings as to public necessity, but would issue no certificates or orders, concluding that, although the Commission had jurisdiction to approve of the construction of the union station, it did not have the power to order such construction in any case.

The City then again went to the State Railroad Commission and presented the findings made by the Interstate Commerce Commission. The State Commission added a few more findings of fact, such as, that the cost of the proposed station would be approximately ten million dollars, and that the public necessity required such station, but issued no order requiring the carriers to construct the station. Again the City went to the Interstate Commerce Commission, and again the Commission held that it had no authority to order the construction of such station, although it had made the necessary findings as to facts concerning public necessity. 

The Commission also refused to admit any evidence which would be necessary for the issuance of an order requiring the railroads to construct the union station. 

The City then petitioned the Supreme Court of the District of Columbia for a writ of mandamus, to compel the Interstate Commerce Commission to admit evidence 

13 Railroad Comm'n of Calif. vs. Southern Pacific Co. et al., 264 U. S. 331. [71]

necessary for the order sought by the City. That court dismissed the application, but was reversed by the Court of 

Appeals of the District of Columbia. The Interstate Com merce Commission then brought the case to the Supreme 

Court of the United States.

Here, the Commission argued that Congress had not authorized it to require the construction of new union stations in lieu of existing individual terminals, and that a power of such far-reaching scope should not be read into the law.

The carriers also intervened and filed a brief, arguing, that not only had the Commission no jurisdiction to issue the order sought by the City of Los Angeles, but that Congress had no power to confer such jurisdiction on the Commission. They maintained that such order would violate the due process clause of the Constitution, for it would involve ordering an abandonment of train service along various points where transportation is rendered and also the relocation of tracks and terminals, all of which would involve the carriers into undue expenditures. This, they argued, would constitute a taking of property without just compensation.

The City argued, in opposition, that it is within the power of the Commission to order the construction and use of a union station where the public convenience and necessity require it; that the writ of mandamus was the proper remedy in the case because the Commission had already considered all the evidence and found the facts which warranted the construction and use of such proposed station, and what remained to be done, namely, the issuance of an order, was merely a ministerial function, not requiring the use of discretion. The City therefore maintained that the decision of the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia should be affirmed. [72]

The Supreme Court, in an opinion delivered by Chief Justice Taft, reversed the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia.14 The Court held that the Commission did not have authority to order the construction of the union station, since the Interstate Commerce Act nowhere expressly conferred such authority on the Commission. Nor should such authority be attributed to the Commission by implication, because of the far-reaching economic power the Commission might wield over the carriers and the communities served by them. An order of the Commission to construct a union station would, to a large extent, invade the prerogatives of the states and of the localities served by the carriers. In delivering the opinion, the late Chief 

Justice said: "Without more specific and express legislative direction than is found in the Act, we cannot reasonably ascribe to Congress a purpose to compel the interstate carriers here to build a union passenger station in a city of the size and extent and the great business requirements of Los Angeles. The Commission was created by Congress. If it was to be clothed with the power to require railroads to abandon their existing stations and terminal tracks in a city and to combine for the purpose of establishing in lieu thereof a new union station, at a new site, that power we should expect to find in congressional legislation. Such authority, if conferred in Los Angeles, would have application to all interstate railroad junctions, including the numerous large cities of the country, with their residential, commercial, shopping, and municipal centers now fixed and extablished with relation to existing terminals. It would become a statute of the widest 

14 No. 54-I. C. C. vs. United States ex rel. City of Los Angeles. 

Decided November 25, 1929. [73]

effect and would enter into the welfare of every part of the country. Various interests would be vitally affected by the substitution of a union station for the present terminals. A selection of its site from the standpoint of a city might greatly affect property values and likewise local transportation systems. The exercise of such power would compel the carriers to abandon existing terminals, to acquire new land and rights of way and enter upon new construction, to abandon large tracts and to sell territory of the same extent as no longer necessary for the use of the carriers. "There would have to be tribunals to apportion the expenditures and cost as between the carriers. A proper statute would seem to require detailed directions, and we should expect the intention to be manifested in plain terms and not to have been left to be implied from varied regulatory provisions of uncertain scope. It would be a monumental work and one requiring the most extensive exercise of expert engineering and railroad construction. It would make possible great changes of much importance in the plans of every city and in the re-arrangement and mutations of railroad property and public and private business structures everywhere. We find no statutory preparation for the organization of such machinery. "We cannot agree with the Court of Appeals of the District in its disposition to view Section 3, paragraph 3, as vesting the Interstate Commerce Commission 'with almost unlimited power in the matter of establishing terminals and union stations for the proper interchange of traffic between the converging interstate railroad lines.' The words 'reasonable, proper and equal facilities' are of course comprehensive enough to include not only trackage but terminal facili- [74]

ties described as extending a reasonable distance outside of the terminal, but hardly to give the Commission 'unlimited power' in the building of union stations. "To attribute to Congress an intention to authorize the compulsory establishment of union passenger stations the country over, without special mention of them as such, would be most extraordinary. The general ousting from their usual terminal facilities of the great interstate carriers would work a change of title and of ownership in property of a kind that would be most disturbing to the business interests of every state in the country."

Of course, where the railroads agree to use a union station, they may do so, provided they obtain a certificate of convenience and necessity from the Interstate Commerce Commission. This is what happened in the case of the 

Wheeling & Lake Erie Railroad Company. The Commis sion authorized that railroad to abandon its Ontario Street 

Passenger Station at Cleveland, Ohio, and to use a union station operated by the Cleveland Union Terminals Company. But the Pittsburgh & West Virginia Railroad Company, as connecting carrier of the Wheeling & Lake Erie and also as a minority stockholder of the Wheeling, brought suit in the Federal District Court in Ohio to set aside the order of the Commission.

The facts are stated by the complaining railroad substantially as follows: Prior to 1918, the Van Sweringens were engaged in extensive real estate developments in the City of Cleveland, and they owned and operated an electric railroad for the purpose of furnishing transportation from the various real estate developments to the public square in the City of Cleveland. They also controlled the New York, Chicago and St. Louis Railway, commonly known as the 

Nickel Plate. In 1918, the Van Sweringens organized the [75]

Cleveland Union Terminals Company for the purpose of constructing and maintaining a union station. This was approved by a city ordinance and also by vote of the electors of the city in 1918 and 1919. 

Then the New York Central Railway Company, the 

Cleveland, Cincinnati, Chicago & St. Louis Railway Company, commonly known as the Big Four, and the Nickel Plate, entered into an arrangement whereby they would use the facilities of the Terminals Company. Under this agreement, the Central acquired 71% of the capital stock of the 

Terminals, the Big Four took 22%, and the Nickel Plate 

7%. The railroads agreed that the Van Sweringens shall have the right to elect the directors of the Terminals Company; that the railroads would finance the construction of the depot, and in turn the Terminals Company agreed not to grant the use of the depot except with the approval of the Central, Big Four and Nickel Plate roads. 

In 1921, the Van Sweringens organized the Cleveland Terminals Building Company, to which they conveyed all lands necessary for the construction of the union depot. The 

Building Company and the Terminals Company, with the consent of the three railroads, then entered into a contract which defined the rights of each of the corporations over the lands and buildings in question. 

A few hundred feet from the place of the proposed union depot, the Wheeling and Lake Erie had its Ontario Street Passenger Station site, which had been used by the 

Wheeling as a passenger station. The Van Sweringens and the corporations affiliated in this enterprise tried to get the Wheeling to go into the new station. This was resisted by the Wheeling on the ground that the costs would be too great, but an agreement was reached in proceedings before the Interstate Commerce Commission, that any order made by the Commission would be without prejudice to any [76]

rights of the Wheeling in relation to its then existing 

Ontario Street Passenger Station site. The order of the 

Commission authorizing the construction of the union depot was then issued without affecting the Wheeling's Ontario 

Passenger Station.

That happened on December 6, 1921. Soon afterwards, the construction began and efforts were made to obtain the use of the Ontario Street Passenger Station site during the construction. At the same time frequent attempts were made to induce the Wheeling to use the depot of the Terminals Company when completed. John D. Rockefeller,

Jr., was in control of the Wheeling at that time, and he refused to grant the Terminals Company any right in the Wheeling's propery or to agree that the Wheeling use the proposed union depot, all on the ground that the Wheeling could not afford it.

The railroads then resorted to a different method. The 

Central, Nickel Plate and Baltimore & Ohio, competitors of the Wheeling, purchased from John D. Rockefeller, Jr., a large majority of the stock of the Wheeling and divided it equally among themselves. The Van Sweringens also purchased in open market a large block of preferred and common stock of the Wheeling, and these were likewise divided equally among the three railroads. This enabled the competing railroads, as stockholders of the Wheeling, to elect new directors.

The Interstate Commerce Commission instituted proceedings against these railroads, found that the acquisition of the Wheeling stock was contrary to Section 7 of the Clayton Act, and ordered them to divest themselves of the stock.15

But in the meantime, these railroads had elected new directors of the Wheeling & Lake Erie. They agreed 

15 Act of October 26, 1914; Secs. 18 and 21, Title 15, U. S. C. See also p. 252 infra. [77]

to abandon the Ontario Street Station, to use the new depot and in addition entered into a contract whereby the Wheeling might exercise its option to sell the Ontario Street Passenger Station site to the Terminals Company for the sum of $1,600,000. Thereupon, an application for a certificate of public convenience and necessity to abandon the Ontario site and to use the new depot was made to the Interstate Commerce Commission, and this application was granted. 

The Pittsburgh & West Virginia Railroad Company then brought this suit to annul the order of the Commission, on the ground that the proposed plan was arrived at through an unlawful acquisition of the stock of the Wheeling & Lake Erie. The complaining carrier also relied upon its rights as a stockholder under the law of Ohio, which provides that no aid shall be furnished by one company to its competitor, nor may any purchase or lease be affected between them, until the stockholders of the two corporations shall have assented to such arrangement.

The Statutory District Court in Ohio, where the suit was brought, refused to set aside the order of the Commission, and the Pittsburgh & West Virgnia appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. The case being considered of great importance, it was advanced for argument. Shortly after the argument began, however, the question arose whether the Pittsburgh & West Virginia Railway had any standing in court, as a stockholder of the Wheeling & Lake Erie, to object to an order of the Commission permitting the latter to use the new station or to abandon the old one. This question was apparently not answered to the satisfaction of the Court, and the Court declined to hear the complete arguments in the case.

The Supreme Court affirmed the decision of the District Court dismissing the bill of complaint filed by the Pittsburgh & West Virginia, but not on the merits of the con- [78]

troversy, as was done by the lower court.

16 The Supreme 

Court held that as a connecting carrier the Pittsburgh & West Virginia could not maintain this suit before a threejudge court, because the Pittsburgh & West Virginia did not enter the City of Cleveland and was not affected as a carrier by the Wheeling's use of the union station. Nor did it have any standing in Court, as a stockholder of the Wheeling & Lake Erie, to bring this suit.

The Supreme Court therefore held that the case should have been dismissed for lack of jurisdiction, without prejudice, however, to the right of the Pittsburgh & West Virginia to bring suit in an equity court on the ground that the action of the directors of the Wheeling constituted a breach of trust with the stockholders.

Safety Problems. 

Most of

No less than ten cases came before the Supreme Court this term involving grade crossing accidents. these were not reviewed by the Court, and the decisions of the lower courts in these cases have wrought exceptions into what was believed to be an absolute rule laid down by the 

Supreme Court in the case of Baltimore & Ohio Railway 

Company vs. Goodman, decided during the 1927 term.¹7

We are inclined to disagree with the decision of the Supreme Court in the Goodman case, not so much in the result, as in the judicial process followed in arriving at the result. In that case, there was evidence of negligence on the part of the railroad and the sole question was whether the driver of the automobile was contributorily negligent. 

The standard of care in all such negligence cases is "what would an ordinary prudent man do under the circum- 

16 No. 680-Pittsburgh & West Virginia R. R. Co. vs. U. S. et al. 

Decided May 19, 1930. See p. 26 supra. 

17 Baltimore & Ohio Railroad Co. vs. Goodman, 275 U. S. 66. [79]

stances." The jury, to whom the Goodman case was submitted, applied this standard of due care and found that the driver was not contributorily negligent. The Supreme 

Court reversed the judgment of the lower courts, holding that the matter should have been taken out of the hands of the jury, and the driver held contributorily negligent as a matter of law. In the opinion, rendered by Justice Holmes a standard of care was laid down "once and for all," that when a driver of an automobile goes upon a railroad track without stopping, looking and listening or when he approaches an obstructed crossing and does not get out of the car to look and listen, he is negligent. 

If by arriving at that decision, the Supreme Court laid down a different standard of care than in other negligence cases, then we think that the Court might have left that task to the legislatures of the various states, or to Congress, in its regulatory powers over interstate carriers. If the 

Supreme Court meant to lay down this rule as a correct application of the common law standard, then we think that the Court has permitted its notion of what an ordinary, reasonably prudent man does, to be influenced by what he should do, under the circumstances. 

And what are the circumstances entering into this and similar accidents? In addition to the facts in each particular case, it must be realized that millions of people use automobiles in traveling over public highways for both short and long distances. The speed limits in the states, outside cities are thirty-five, forty and forty-five miles per hour; the roads are usually constructed so as to permit even greater speed, and most of the automobiles are now equipped with four-wheel brakes. The drivers are out to cover distance. Under such circumstances, is it not academic to speak of drivers getting out of their cars, to look and listen when approaching obstructed grade crossings? [80]

It is not surprising, therefore, that modifications and exceptions to the Goodman decision have already developed. Many cases have subsequently come to the Supreme 

Court, in which the carriers, relying on the Goodman case, have maintained that they could not be held liable for the accidents, because the drivers must be held negligent as a matter of law.

In one case,18 the administratrix of an automobile driver brought action to recover damages for his death caused by a grade crossing collision between his automobile and a freight train of the Lehigh Valley Railroad. The evidence showed that the collision occurred on a dark night; that at a short distance from the track there was a white line across the road, but that this had been erased to some extent; that some 430 feet from the crossing and about six feet to the right of the pavement, there was a disk sign containing the word "railroad," which, however, was not very legible because of rust and lack of fresh paint; and that at the crossing there was another sign some 12 to 14 feet high with the words "Railroad Crossing-Look Out for the 

Cars." The driver was a stranger on the highway. When he came on the track, the collision occurred. The District 

Court, where the action was brought, rendered judgment in favor of the plaintiffs, but its decision was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals, on the ground that the driver of the automobile was contributorily negligent in having failed to stop, look and listen.

But in another case,19 although the evidence showed that the driver did not stop, look and listen, the Circuit Court of Appeals held that there was sufficient evidence to go to the jury on the question of the railroad's negligence, and 

18 No. 775-Hattie B. Wilson, Adm'x vs. Lehigh Valley R. R. Co. 

Certiorari denied May 19, 1930. 

19 No. 858-Chicago & Eastern Illinois R. R. Co. vs. Ruby Divine, Adm'x. Certiorari denied, June 2, 1930. [81]

also held that the driver, who was killed in the accident, was not contributorily negligent as a matter of law. 

In still another case,20 the evidence showed that the grade crossing was without guards and without lights, that the driver looked and listened but did not see or hear an approaching train; as he crossed, however, a backing train without lights or lookout collided with the automobile. The trial court permitted the question of contributory negligence to go to the jury. The railroad assigned this as error, maintaining that the driver should have been held guilty of contributory negligence as a matter of law, but the Circuit Court of Appeals held that the question had been properly submitted to the jury. In all these cases, the Supreme 

Court denied application for writs of review.

In most of the other grade crossing cases which came to the Court this term, the evidence showed that the automobile drivers did stop when they approached the tracks, but that they then proceeded without looking in the direction from which the trains came, when the collisions occurred. 

The carriers argued that as a practical matter in the driving of a car, there is no difference between one who does not stop when approaching a crossing and one who does stop, but then proceeds without looking further. In addition, the railroads argued that if the drivers did stop, they must be held to have been contributory negligent, because the physical conditions were such that they could not have avoided seeing the approaching trains and they had ample time within which to cross the tracks, else it was negligence per se to attempt to cross.

In one of these cases," 21 the evidence showed that the plaintiff was driving the car and stopped about three feet 

20 No. 690-Chicago & Eastern Illinois Railway Co. vs. C. L. Mc- 

Coy. Certiorari denied, April 21, 1930. 

21 No. 542-Baltimore & Ohio R. R. Co. vs. Mary Perucca et al. 

Certiorari denied, February 24, 1930. [82]

from the railroad track; she looked about, listened, saw no train approaching and started, when a train going at the rate of fifty miles per hour, came upon her. Assuming the plaintiff's evidence to be true, the railroad showed that the angle of vision from the place she stopped was such that a train could be seen at a distance of some 900 feet, which would have given the plaintiff ample time within which to clear the tracks. The carrier therefore argued that the plaintiff must have been contributorily negligent. 

The railroad carried the argument further and insisted that not only was the driver of the car contributorily negligent as a matter of law, but that a passenger in the car was likewise contributorily negligent, apparently on the theory that the guest could have assisted in looking out for the trains and have caused the driver to stop at the crossing.22 

In other words, under such circumstances, the railroad argued, it was negligence for the passenger not to assume the duties of a back-seat driver. The Circuit Court of 

Appeals overruled the contention that the invited guest was contributory negligent.
