This question of contributory negligence attributable to a passenger in a car can be carried to an absurdity, as was done in another case,23 where the railroad argued that the passenger, a boy of eight, who was riding on the rear seat of the car, was contributorily negligent as a matter of law, on the theory that the negligence, if any, of the driver was also attributable to him. In these cases, also, the Supreme 

Court declined to review the decisions of the lower courts. 

Often the question of the carrier's negligence in such cases is tied up with the validity of local laws imposing duties upon railroads to maintain guarded grade crossings 

22 No. 541-Baltimore & Ohio R. R. Co. vs. Ralph Contrella et al. 

Certiorari denied, February 24, 1930. 

23 No. 859-Chicago & Eastern Illinois R. R. Co. vs. Jesse W. Divine, Jr. Certiorari denied, June 2, 1930. [83]

or to employ safety devices to warn approaching drivers that a train is about to pass. In order to show that the carrier is not negligent in such cases, it must also show that the laws, imposing duties of care, are either contrary to the due process clause or the commerce clause of the Constitution.

Such was the objection raised to the prima facie statute of the State of Mississippi. This law in effect imposed the burden of proof on the railroad, to show that the injury was not caused through its negligence. The carrier argued, on the basis of the Supreme Court's decision in Western and Atlantic Railroad Company vs. Henderson,24 decided the previous term, that the prima facie statute of Mississippi was unconstitutional. Shortly after the argument by the appellant, the Supreme Court dismissed the case without an opinion, for lack of a properly presented substantial federal question.25

A similar objection was raised with reference to the 

Blow Post Law of Georgia, requiring railroads to erect blow posts at grade crossings and requiring engineers to signal when approaching crossings. An action, growing out of a collision in Georgia, was brought in the Tennessee courts against the Nashville, Chattanooga & St. Louis Railway. The Tennessee courts held the Georgia law valid, and the Supreme Court affirmed that decision by an equally divided court.26

In several cases, the validity of ordinances and administrative regulations came into question, without the presence of any accidents. In one case, the railroad attacked the validity of an ordinance of the City of Mayfield, Kentucky, 24 Western & Atlantic R. R. Co. vs. Henderson, 279 U. S. 636. 

25 No. 339-Columbus & Greenville R. R. Co. vs. Buford. Decided 

March 17, 1930. 

26 No. 165-Nashville, Chattanooga & St. Louis R. R. Co. vs. Morgan. 

Decided January 27, 1930. [84]

which prohibited the use of switching and team tracks over certain streets and declared all switching thereon to be a nuisance punishable by a daily fine. The railroad argued that the ordinance was promulgated without authority and was not a proper measure in aid of safety. The Circuit 

Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit upheld the constitutionality of the ordinance, the railroad petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.27 

Another case, involving a safety measure, is apparently giving the Supreme Court considerable difficulty, for it has been pending in the Court over two years, and toward the end of the past term the case was restored to the docket28 for re-argument. Here, the Railroad Commissioners of 

Nebraska ordered a railroad to construct an underground crossing for the convenience of a farmer whose land was intersected by the railroad's right of way. The order required the railroad to pay one-half the cost of construction. 

The carrier contended that since the order was based on the mere convenience of the adjacent property owner and not on considerations for the safety of the general public, the order was without due process of law.

Local governments are not exclusively engaged in promulgating safety measures. Congress passed the Federal 

Boiler Inspection Act and the Federal Safety Appliance 

Act, making interstate carriers criminally liable for failure to comply with the provisions of the law and the regulations of the Interstate Commerce Commission made pursuant thereto. A prosecution was brought by the Interstate Commerce Commission against the officers of the State Belt Railroad of California to recover the statutory penalties for 

27 No. 504-Illinois Central R. R. Co. vs. City of Mayfield. Cer tiorari denied, January 13, 1930. 

28 No. 1-Chicago, St. Paul, Minn. & Omaha R. R. Co. vs. Holmberg. 

Restored for re-argument, May 26, 1930. [85]

violations of the Federal Safety Appliance Act. The facts concerning the violations of the safety measure were not in dispute, but the case raised an interesting question as to the applicability to these federal safety laws to state-owned roads.

The defendants in this case were the Board of State 

Harbor Commissioners of California, which operated the

State Belt Railroad entirely within the state. This road, however, connects with interstate carriers and is therefore subject to the regulations made pursuant to the Federal Safety Appliance Act. The first question was whether this railroad, which operates between the wharves and industrial plants in San Francisco, was a common carrier within the meaning of the Safety Appliance Act; second, whether the 

Act applied to state-owned railroads altogether; third, whether the present suit against the State Harbor Commissioners was a suit against the state itself, and should therefore have been brought in the Supreme Court of the United States, as a court of original jurisdiction; and fourth, if this suit is not against the Harbor Commissioners in their official capacity, but in their private capacity, are the penalties applicable, if the railroad was not owned or personally managed by the Commissioners, but was managed by them. through a superintendent, and the infractions of the Safety Appliance Act were due to no wrongdoing on their part. 

The federal district court in San Francisco held the Commissioners liable. They appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, and that court certified the above questions to the Supreme Court of the United States. The Supreme Court directed the Court of Appeals to certify the entire case to it, and it will be argued during the coming term.29 

29 No. 486-Sherman et al. vs. United States. Case pending. [86]

Damage Suits. 

Several suits were brought against railroads for loss of goods in transportation. One case was brought as a test case to determine the correct ruling as to the proper damages for goods lost in shipment. Coal was shipped from 

Royalton, Illinois, to Minneapolis, Minnesota, and was lost in transit. Action was brought to recover the value of the coal. Both parties agreed that the market value at destination was the measure of damages, but the plaintiff insisted that the retail price of the coal should govern, while the railroad argued that the market value of the coal in the car at destination was the proper measure of damages to be applied. The Supreme Court sustained the argument of the railroad.30

In another case, the plaintiff argued there was delay in the transit of perishable fruits, which resulted in damage. 

The shipper accepted a settlement based on a policy promulgated by the claims agents of the carrier, which policy settled such claims on a fifty-fifty basis. The shipper afterwards brought suit to recover the balance of his claim, on the theory that Section 20 of the Interstate Commerce Act prohibited railroads from contracting in a manner limiting their liability under the bills of lading.31 

The District Court of Appeals in California interpreted Section 20 as applicable only to contracts made prior to shipment, but that it did not apply to contracts of settlement made upon claims. The shipper petitioned the Supreme 

Court of California to review the case, but the petition was 

30 No. 75-Illinois Central R. R. Co. vs. Crail. Decided February 

24, 1930. 

31 Title 49, U. S. C., Sec. 20 (11). 

[ 87 ]

denied. He then petitioned the Supreme Court of the 

United States to review the case, but the Court declined to do so.32

Actions for Freight. 

In two cases, railroads brought actions for freight against shippers. These presented an interesting point as to the setting up of a counterclaim in actions brought by carriers. The first case arose in California, where the Code of Civil Procedure provides that if a defendant omits to set up a counterclaim arising out of the transaction which forms the basis of the plaintiff's suit, he cannot afterwards maintain an action on his counterclaim.

Under the Hepburn Act,33 however, an interstate carrier may not collect a greater or less or different compensation than specified in its tariffs, and the Supreme Court of the United States has previously held that a railroad may not accept a release of damages in payment of transportation charges, nor payment in any other medium than cash.34 

In this case, the Chicago and Northwestern Railway

Company brought action against a shipper to recover freight charges. The shipper interposed his counterclaim for loss suffered by him as a result of the carrier's failure to perform its obligations under the bills of lading on these shipments. 

The railroad maintained that this counterclaim could not be set up, because it was contrary to the Hepburn Act, but the Federal District Court in California, where the action was brought, held that the counterclaim was permissible. 

32 No. 185-Tracy Waldron Fruit Co. vs. Southern Pacific Co. Cer tiorari denied, October 21, 1929. 

33 34 Stat. 587. 

34 Louisville & Nashville R. R. Co. vs. Mottley, 219 U. S. 467; Chicago, Indiana & L. Ry. Co. vs. U. S., 219 U. S. 486. [88]

The railroad appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, which certified the question to the 

Supreme Court of the United States. Here, the shipper argued that in accordance with the Judicial Code, the federal courts must follow the rules of practice maintained by the state courts, except as is otherwise provided for, and that therefore the counterclaim should be allowed. He further maintained that the Hepburn Act did not cover the offsetting of a claim arising out of the same transaction in an action brought by the carrier.

The railroad maintained, in opposition, that the prior decisions of the Supreme Court were clear on the subject, namely, that the railroad may not accept anything but money for transportation, and that since the rules of practice in California were in conflict with the Hepburn Act, as interpreted by the Supreme Court of the United States, and the matter involves a federal question, the state law cannot govern.

In answer to the question certified by the Circuit Court of Appeals, the Supreme Court held that the shipper was not barred by the Hepburn Act from setting up his counterclaim for losses arising out of the shipment; that under that 

Act it was not contemplated that one may not take advantage of his right to set up a counterclaim and thus reduce the amount recoverable by the railroad. This decision was rendered by the Supreme Court on February 24th, 1930.35

While that case was pending before the Court, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit decided a case in which it held that in an action brought by the railroad to recover freight, the defendant could not set up a counter- 

35 No. 193-Chicago & Northwestern Ry. Co. vs. Lindell. Decided 

February 24, 1930. [89]

claim.This case was brought to the Supreme Court for review, the writ was granted, and the case will be argued during the coming term. 

Steamship Companies.

Steamship companies operating in connection with railroads are sometimes subject to the Interstate Commerce Act. 

That law provides that steamship companies shall be subject to the jurisdiction of the Interstate Commerce Commission in those instances in which they act under a common arrangement or management with railroads. 

Prior to December, 1925, the Clyde Steamship Company transported certain shipments of rosin from Brunswick, Ga., to Boston, Mass. These shipments were made. under port-to-port bills of lading, although some of them contained notices that the consignee may later transport the cargoes from the piers at Boston to interior points. 

The Interstate Commerce Commission, upon a complaint received by it, demanded to examine the accounts, records and memoranda concerning this transportation. The demand was refused by the company, on the ground that a suit was then pending before the courts to determine whether the Commission had jurisdiction in this case. 

Those proceedings, however, were dismissed, because the parties had reached a compromise whereby the steamship company agreed to submit the records for examination to 

36 No. 664-Fullerton Lumber Company vs. Chicago, Milwaukee, St. 

Paul & Pacific Ry. Co., 36 F. (2) 180). Certiorari granted March 17, 1930. Had the Circuit Court of Appeals known that this question was pending before the Supreme Court, it might have awaited the Supreme Court's decision, and might have decided its own case in conformity therewith, thus avoiding further consideration of the same question by the Supreme Court. Until a few years ago it was not possible to know all the cases pending before the Supreme Court. Beginning with October 

Term, 1928, the publication of the U. S. Supreme Court Service has made this information readily available to the bench and the bar. [90]

the Commission. It was during these negotiations that the agent of the Commission demanded the books and records, and when the compromise was reached the steamship company turned the papers over to the Commission. 

Notwithstanding these negotiations, the Government instituted an action against the steamship company to recover a penalty of $500 per day from the time the papers were refused on December 16, 1925, until the mandamus proceeding was settled on January 4, 1926. The District 

Court in New York held the steamship company had acted in good faith and that it was not reasonable for the Commission to make a demand while the question was pending in the court. This was reversed by the Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Second Circuit, which held that the good faith was immaterial, and that the company was liable for the penalty, although the controversy was pending in the courts. The Supreme Court denied the company's petition to review that decision.37

Another case which raised the question of the Commission's jurisdiction over a steamship company, was decided by Judge Parker shortly before his nomination to the Supreme Court, and was only casually mentioned but not discussed in the debates in the Senate. In this case, suit for writ of mandamus was brought to compel the Munson 

Steamship Line to file with the Interstate Commerce Commission its schedule of tariffs and rates on property transported partly by railroad and partly by water.

The Munson Line does not accept shipments on through bills of lading from railroads, but requires each railroad to issue a local bill of lading, naming as consignee either the steamship line or the ultimate consignee in care of the steamship line. On arrival of the goods at the dock, for example 

37 No. 716-Clyde Steamship Company vs. United States. Certiorari denied, April 28, 1930. [91]

in Baltimore, the railroad notifies the steamship company and the latter instructs the railroad which vessel will carry the goods to Florida. The goods are delivered on board the vessel, and the steamship company pays the railroad for shipment to Baltimore. It then issues a bill of lading from 

Baltimore to Florida, and when the goods arrive there, it collects from the consignee, the total transportation charge from the point of origin to the point of destination. 

The Interstate Commerce Commission maintained that, although the bookkeeping arrangements appeared to separate the rail shipment from the water shipment, nevertheless the entire shipment was continued under a "common arrangement" within the meaning of the Interstate Commerce Act, subjecting water carriers to the provisions of that Act.

Judge Parker held that the Munson Steamship Line was not acting under a common arrangement with the railroads when it accepted those shipments. The Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, arguing that the mere method of writing out bills of lading did not determine whether the steamship company acted independently or whether it acted under a common arrangement with the railroad. The Supreme Court consented to review that decision, and the case will be argued next term.38 

38 No. 822-United States vs. Munson Steamship Line. Certiorari granted, May 26, 1930. [92]

CHAPTER IV. 

Public Utilities Street Railway Fares. 

Last year the Supreme Court decided the case of the Interborough Rapid Transit Company, in which the Court ruled in favor of the City of New York on the question whether the company was bound by its contract to operate the City's subways on a five cent fare basis.¹ The decision of the Court rested mainly on the proposition that the federal district court in New York had no jurisdiction in the case, because at the time the suit was filed therein, an action had already been commenced in the state court, in which the City sought to compel the Rapid Transit Company to perform its contract. The most significant point of the decision was, however, that the Court disapproved of the use of the federal injunctive powers in order to test the validity of a contract under the laws of the state, when the Rapid Transit Commission of New York had attempted to have these questions of state law determined by the state courts.

Shortly after the decision of the Interborough Case, the City of Los Angeles and the Railroad Commission of California appealed from a decision of the federal district court in that state, which enjoined the Commission and the City from interfering with the Los Angeles Railway Corporation's attempt to institute a seven cent fare. In that suit, likewise, the appellants maintained that the company was 

1 Gilchrist vs. Interborough Rapid Transit Company, 279 U. S. 159. [93]

bound by its agreements, contained in its franchises, to operate the street railways in Los Angeles at the rate of five cents for a single ride. As in the Interborough Case, so in this case, the main question before the Supreme Court was whether under the laws of the state, the City had authority to bind itself by a contract fixing the rate of fare. For, in accordance with the previous decision of the Supreme Court, the test whether there was a binding contract is whether the state has so delegated to the City the authority to enter into such contracts, that an interference with the rates would amount to an interference with the contract.2 

In this case, it was necessary to go into the legislative history in order to determine whether the City of Los Angeles actually had such powers. 

In 1872, an Act was passed by the Legislature of California which provided that no railroad corporation may use the streets in any town, unless the right to use the same was granted by two-thirds vote of the town or city authorities, "from which the right must emanate." In 1891, an Act was passed providing that authority to lay railroad tracks in any city or county may be obtained for a term not exceeding fifty years, "under such restrictions and limitations and upon such terms and payment of license tax, as the city, city and county or town authority may provide." In 1903, California passed an Act providing that the fares on street cars in municipalities of the first class "must not exceed five cents for each passenger per trip of any distance in one direction."

Prior to the passage of the last Act, in 1893, California passed the Franchise Act, which provided for a definite procedure to be followed in the sale of franchises granted to public utilities. This Act was repealed in 1909, and then amended in 1919, providing that cities may grant franchises to public utilities under conditions prescribed in the Act, 2 Hankin: U. S. Supreme Court, 1928-1929, pp. 55-61. 

[94]

but it also contained the following provision: "The grantor may, however, in such franchises impose such other and additional terms and conditions not in conflict herewith, whether governmental or contractual in character, as, in the judgment of the legislative body thereof, are to the public interest." These were the general provisions of law in California with reference to the granting of franchises including those granted to street railway companies. 

Now we come to the provisions affecting specifically the City and County of Los Angeles. This City obtained its charter in 1850. It was amended on various occasions, and the amendment of 1905 provided that no franchise shall be granted for a period longer than twenty-one years, and "every grant * * shall make adequate provision * to secure efficiency of public service at reasonable rates, and the maintenance of the property in good condition throughout the term of the grant." In 1911, the charter was again amended, empowering the City "to fix and determine the rates of compensation to be collected by *** any public utility operated or conducted within the city limits." In 

1915, the state of California amended its Public Utilities Act which provided that no street or interurban corporation shall charge more than five cents for a continuous ride, except upon a showing to the Railroad Commission that such greater charge is justified.

Under these provisions of law, the Los Angeles Railway Corporation obtained some 114 franchises, of which 81 contained a provision that the corporation shall operate on a five cent fare basis, while the remainder contained somewhat different provisions as to the rate of fare. During the war, the Los Angeles Railway Corporation, like every other business, began to feel the rise in prices. In 1918, therefore, the Corporation asked the Railroad Commission of California to make a general investigation into the service and [95]

financial condition of the company and asked for an order which would enable the company so to operate its system, that the income would be sufficient to pay for the cost of the service. In that application, the Corporation expressly disclaimed any desire on its part for an increase in fares. Two years later, however, it amended its application asking for an increase. On May 31, 1921, the Commission issued a report in which it declared that an increase in fares should be granted and allowed the corporation to charge six cents for a single ride or to sell ten tickets for fifty cents. The

Corporation, however, declined the increase, and at its own request the entire proceedings were vacated. 

In 1926, the Corporation again applied to the Railroad Commission of California for an increase in fares. This time the Commission, upon full consideration of the case, refused to authorize an increase in fares, having found that the Corporation was earning an average annual return of over 7 per cent., that it had not been efficiently operated, and that with the introduction of certain economies, the return would be still greater. The Commission concluded that the existing rate was just and reasonable. 

The Corporation then brought this suit to enjoin the order of the Commission, and the City and County of Los Angeles intervened in the case. Upon reviewing the legislative history relating to public utilities in California and the laws relating specifically to the City and County of Los Angeles, the statutory three-judge court doubted, though it did not conclude, that the City of Los Angeles had power to contract for a fixed rate of fare. The court held, however, that if the City ever had such power, the same had been abrogated by the Railroad Commission of the state; that the Corporation was therefore not bound by any contracts as to rates and was entitled to charge a reasonable rate for its services, that is, one which would yield a reasonable [96]

return. Upon the evidence submitted, the district court found that the five cent rate would not yield a reasonable return, that it was therefore confiscatory, and it issued a decree, enjoining the Commission from interfering with the company's new rate of fare. On appeal, this was affirmed by the Supreme Court in an opinion delivered by Mr. 

Justice Butler.³ Justices Brandeis and Stone wrote dissenting opinions, and Justice Holmes joined in the dissent. 

After reviewing the history of legislation on this subject in the state of California, the Court came to the conclusion that the "appellants have failed to sustain their contention that the City was empowered to make such rate contracts.' 

One might ask why the Court should require the appellants, who were defendants below, to sustain their contention, rather than require the Corporation to sustain the burden of proof to the contrary. The reason is aptly stated in the opinion of the Court: "As it is in the public interest that all doubts be resolved in favor of the right of the state from time to time to prescribe rates, a grant of authority to surrender the power is not to be inferred in the absence of a plain expression of purpose to that end. The delegation of authority to give up or suspend the power of rate regulation will not be found more readily than would an intention on the part of the State to authorize the bargaining away of its power to tax."

On this point the dissenting opinions of Justices Brandeis and Stone argued that the case should have been disposed of by remanding it to the lower court for a specific finding, whether the City did or did not have power to contract as to Further, Justice Brandeis maintained that although under the Public Utilities Act of 1915, the Railroad Com- 

3 No. 60-Railroad Commission of California and City of Los Angeles vs. Los Angeles Railway Corporation. Decided December 2, 1929. 

[97]

mission had been given power to regulate rates, the law did not abrogate existing contracts, nor did it purport to take from the City any power to enter into such contract. Said 

Justice Brandeis: 

"Their effect was merely to make any such contract, whether theretofore or thereafter entered into, subject to change by the Commission. Unless and until so changed a contractual fare fixed by franchise remains in full force." 

Moreover, both Justice Brandeis and Justice Stone seemed to regard the question of the power of the City to enter into such contracts as immaterial. The immediate question in this case, according to them, was not whether the City had power to enter such contracts, but whether the company was bound by its undertaking to serve the public on a five cent fare basis. Said Justice Stone: "Here concededly the power to regulate rates is reserved to the state commission and the question preliminary to the whole case is whether the railroad company has bound itself to serve for a five cent fare. I know of no principle of the law of contracts, qua contracts, which would preclude its doing so, even though the city had no power to obligate itself to maintain any particular rate. It has not purported to exercise such power by so contracting. It had power to grant franchises and the grant of the franchise without more would be good consideration for the company's undertaking to maintain a five cent fare."

The majority of the Court went further. Having held that the appellants have failed to sustain that the City had authority to enter into rate contracts, the Court assumed that the City had had such powers and then concluded that the contract had been abrogated by the Railroad Commission, because (1) in 1918 the Commission entertained the [98]

Corporation's application to investigate its service and financial condition and for an order authorizing it to "so operate its system and change its rates that the income would be sufficient to pay the costs of the service;" (2) in 1921 the 

Commission granted a small increase in fares, which increase, however, was not accepted by the Corporation and was revoked in 1926; and (3) later in 1926, the Commission again entertained the Corporation's application for an increase in fares, and after an extensive investigation, denied the same on the merits of the case. By taking jurisdiction. in the case, in order to determine what would be a just and reasonable rate, the contract as to fixed rate was abrogated, though the rates themselves were not disturbed by the Commission.

Justice Brandeis maintained that these actions of the Commission were not sufficient to abrogate the contract as to rates. With reference to the proceedings which took place in 1921, he said: "Obviously this action taken in 1921 cannot be deemed an abrogation or modification of any existing fare provision of the franchises, unless it be held that the mere entry by the Commission upon an inquiry as to the rate of fare, as commanded by the statute, has that effect. Reason and authority are to the contrary."

And with reference to the last order issued by the Commission, Justice Brandeis said: "The Commission may have erred in its judgment, but it is clear that it did not change the rate of fare." And on this point, Justice Stone in his dissenting opinion added: "Even if necessary to decide the question, I would not be prepared to say that the refusal of the Commission to fix a fare different from the contract rate would destroy the contract. By contracting for a five cent fare, the railway company waived the protection of the [99]

due process clause of the Fourteenth Amendment * * *. 

Granting that the contract was subject to the power and duty of the Commission to modify it by changing the rate, that power has not been exercised and the duty is one arising, not under the Constitution and laws of the United States, but is imposed by state statute for breach of which a state remedy alone should be given." 

This brings us to an argument raised by the appellants, but not treated in the opinion. It is that the district court had no jurisdiction in the case, because the company had not exhausted the remedies provided by state statute, namely, to apply for a writ of review to the Supreme Court of California. Obviously, that argument, so far as procedure is concerned, was without merit, but it reflected an attitude. that in such cases a different result might be expected if the matter were first decided by the state courts.

In the Interborough Case, the entire controversy, one might say, hinged on the question whether the state or the federal courts should have the first opportunity to interpret and apply the state statutes involved in the case. In this case, the majority of the Court said: "This court is bound by the decisions of the highest courts of the states as to the powers of their municipalities. Georgia Railway Company vs. Decatur, 262 U. S. 

432, 438. Our attention has not been called to any

California decisions, and we think there is none, which decides that the state legislature has empowered Los Angeles to establish rates by contract. This Court is therefore required to construe the state laws upon which appellants rely."

The case cited by the Court, Georgia Railway Company vs. Decatur, seems to bear out the impression that the result on such question is different when the matter is first decided by the state courts. The contract in the Decatur Case, as it [100]

happened, was again before the Supreme Court this year. 

It appears that the City of Decatur was paving streets on which tracks of the Georgia Power Company, successor of the Georgia Railway Company, were located, and assessed benefits against the company. The Georgia Power Company maintained that the assessment was an undue burden, especially since it received no actual benefit from the paving of the streets, and threatened to cease operating its line. 

This suit was then instituted by the City of Decatur to enjoin the Power Company from ceasing to operate its lines and to compel it to operate at a five cent rate of fare. The 

Georgia Power Company defended on the ground that the franchise under which it operated was not a perpetual franchise, because a municipality possessing the power and authority to make rate contracts can only make them for a limited time, not unreasonable in length. The courts of 

Georgia held in favor of the City, and the company brought the case to the Supreme Court. This Court, in an opinion by Justice Butler, affirmed the decision of the Supreme Court of Georgia. It reviewed the history of the franchises obtained by the predecessors of the Georgia Power Company and came to the conclusion that "it is clear that this franchise and the rate contract of April 1, 1903, are still in force."

The broad question involved in the above cases was, how far a public utility is bound by its contracts to operate at a fixed rate. A different economic problem presented itself concerning increased fares in the City of Baltimore. This was the first case which presented the question, what is a just and reasonable rate for a street railway company, when the loss of revenue is due to the increasing use of the automobile as a means of transportation; or, how far should increases in fares be allowed to meet the needs of those who can least afford to pay them, for obviously those who can [101]

afford to pay more ride in their own automobiles. This question was not considered in the opinion of the Supreme Court, which limited itself to the economico-legal questions in the case, namely, (1) what is the proper method of computing depreciation of property in arriving at the rate base, and (2) what constitutes a fair rate of return under the circumstances of the case.

Since 1919, six increases in fares were granted by the Public Service Commission of Maryland to the United 

Railways and Electric Company of Baltimore. At first, the increase was from five to six cents, then gradually to eight cents cash or two tokens or checks for fifteen cents.

These increases did not remedy the company's financial condition. In 1927 it applied to the Commission for an increase to ten cents for each single ride. The Commission, after an investigation, granted an increase, but not as much as the company applied for. The fare allowed by the Commission was nine cents cash or three tokens for twenty-five cents. In arriving at this rate of fare, the Commission valued the company's property at $75,000,000.00, allowed depreciation on the basis of the original cost of the property, and the net return allowed yielded a rate of 6.26% on the present value of the properties.

The company appealed to the Maryland courts, and the 

Court of Appeals held, (1) that depreciation should be computed on the basis of present costs, and (2) that 6.26% constituted a fair return on the present values of the company's properties. The case was remanded to the Commission for the application of this ruling. This gave the company a ten cent cash fare with four tokens for thirty-five cents. Both the company and the Commission appealed to the Supreme Court. The company maintained that the court below erred in holding that 6.26% constituted a fair rate of return. The Commission argued that the state court [102]

erred in holding that depreciation must be allowed on the basis of present values, rather than original cost. 

The Supreme Court in an opinion by Justice Sutherland upheld the contentions of the railway company on both points. Justices Brandeis and Stone wrote dissenting opinions, in which Justice Holmes concurred.

On the question of depreciation, the Court held that the view adopted by the court below was "plainly right," on the theory that the utility was "entitled to see that from earnings the value of the property invested is kept unimpaired, so that at the end of any given term of years the original investment remains as it was at the beginning." 

Applying this principle, it means that the utility is entitled to "expenditures equal to the cost of the worn out equipment at the time of replacement, and this, for all practical purposes, means present value. It is the settled rule of this 

Court that the rate base is present value, and it would be wholly illogical to adopt a different rule for depreciation." 

This "wholly illogical" view was favored by the dissenting Justices. Justice Brandeis, in an elaborate opinion, entered deeply into the theories and accounting practices relating to depreciation, found that the general practice was to charge depreciation upon actual cost less any salvage, and that this practice has had the approval of such bodies as the Interstate Commerce Commission, the Association of American Railway Accounting Officers, the United States Chamber of Commerce, the Bureau of Internal Revenue, the tax officials of many states, the Bureau of the Census, the Department of Agriculture, the Bureau of Mines, the 

Federal Trade Commission, etc., etc., etc.

Returning the compliment that computation of deprecia- 

4 No. 55-United Railways and Electric Company vs. Harold E. West et al.; and No. 64-Harold E. West et al. vs. United Railways and Electric Company. Decided November 25, 1929. [103]

tion on the basis of cost is "wholly illogical," Justice Brandeis said: "To use a depreciation charge as a measure of the year's consumption of plant, and at the same time reject original cost as the basis of the charge, is inadmissible. It is a perversion of this business device. No method for the ascertainment of the amount of the charge yet invented is workable if fluctuating present values be taken as the basis. Every known method contemplates, and is dependent upon, the accumulation or credit of a fixed amount in a given number of years. 

The distribution of plant expense expressed in the depreciation charge is justified by the approximation to the fact as to the year's plant consumption which is obtained by applying the doctrine of averages. But if fluctuating present values are substituted for original cost, there is no stable base to which the process of averaging can be applied. For thereby the only stable factor involved in fixing a depreciation charge would be eliminated. Each year the present value may be different. The cost of replacement at the termination of the service life of the several units or of the composite life cannot be foretold. To use as a measure of the year's consumption of plant a depreciation charge based on fluctuating present values substitutes conjecture for experience. Such a system would require the consumer of today to pay for an assumed operating expense, which has never been incurred and which may never arise."

Justice Stone likewise insisted that the Supreme Court should not undertake to lay down an a priori rule as to depreciation, but should be guided by general experience and practice, unless it is shown that the general practice is inapplicable to the circumstances of the case at bar. That, [104]

he maintained, had not been shown in this case.

rate, Justice Brandeis stated: 

At any "I know of nothing in the Federal Constitution, or in the decisions of this Court, which should lead us to reject, in determining net profits, the rule sanctioned by the universal practice of business men and governmental departments. For, whether the expense in plant consumption can be more nearly approximated by using a depreciation charge based on original cost or by one based upon fluctuating present values is a problem to be solved, not by legal reasoning, but by the exercise of practical judgment based on facts and business experience."

So much for the question of depreciation. We now come to the second point in the case, namely, whether a rate of return of 6.26% of the present value was confiscatory. 

The Court held that it was. This conclusion was based on evidence before the Commission that to induce investments in the company, or to enable it to compete successfully for money to finance its operations, it should have a net return of 8% on the present values of its properties; it was also shown that the company itself had paid over 7% on money borrowed by it. "In this view of the matter," the Court said, "a return of 6.26% is clearly inadequate." The Court did not lay down any general rule as to what would constitute a fair return. On the contrary, it said: "What will constitute a fair return in a given case is not capable of exact mathematical demonstration." With reference to this case, however, the Court said: "In the light of recent decisions of this Court and other Federal decisions, it is not certain that rates securing a return of 7½ per cent. or even 8 per cent. on the value of the property would not be necessary to avoid confiscation. But this we need not decide, since the [105]

company itself sought from the commission a rate which it appears would produce a return of about 7.44 per cent., at the same time insisting that such return fell short of being adequate. Upon the present record, we are of opinion that to enforce rates producing less. than this would be confiscatory and in violation of the due process clause of the Fourteenth Amendment."

This point likewise evoked much opposition on the part of the dissenting Justices. The figure of 6.26% on present values, in their opinion, was ambiguous and misleading. 

The present value of $75,000,000 found by the Commission included an amount of $5,000,000 for easements, or rights of way, or franchises. Under the law of Maryland the value of easements is part of the valuation of the property. 

The federal rule of valuation excludes easements. And the dissenting Justices maintained that if a utility appeals to the federal courts on the ground that the rate of return is confiscatory, it must show that the rate is confiscatory under the federal valuation, not under any other valuation. Taking the federal rule of valuation, the return allowed by the Commission would actually mean 6.70% of the present values; and if, in addition, instead of the depreciation allowed by the court, the amount is computed on costs, then the return allowed by the Commission was 7.8%. That, they maintained, was clearly not a confiscatory rate. 

Thus the fares in Baltimore were increased to ten cents and now a serious question arises whether this increase will solve the difficulty in that city. For increases in fares cannot be made indefinitely. There is a danger point where increases in fares may mean a lessening, rather than an increase, in revenue. This has been clearly brought out in the case of City of Shreveport vs. Shreveport Railway Company.5 

5 No. 875-City of Shreveport vs. Shreveport Railway Company. 

Certiorari denied, May 26, 1930. [106]

That City passed an ordinance forbidding the use of oneman cars, and the Shreveport Railway Company brought this suit to enjoin the enforcement of the ordinance on the ground that it was arbitrary, unreasonable and oppressive, and resulted in confiscation, in view of the safety devices introduced in the construction of one-man cars and also in view of the financial condition of the company. The matter was referred by the federal district court in Louisiana to a special master, who found that the placing of two men on a car would result in some additional convenience to the public; that the man at the rear entrance could make change, issue transfers, answer questions, help school children on and off the cars, prevent children from meddling with the mechanism of the car, from ringing the gong, from blowing the whistle, from climbing on the back of the cars or through the windows; and that his presence would be of advantage in case of emergency. The master also found, however, that the company was operating at a loss, that the use of one-man cars would effect a saving of some $94,000 a year, and that despite this saving, the company's net return would be under 5%. The special master concluded that the ordinance would result in confiscation, in view of the fact that the street railway company could not raise fares without increasing its loss of revenue.

The recommendation of the special master was adopted by the district court, and its decision was affirmed by the 

Circuit Court of Appeals. The City of Shreveport peti tioned the Supreme Court to review the case, maintaining that the ordinance was a proper exercise of its police powers in aid of public safety. It also maintained that the Circuit 

Court of Appeals erred in holding the ordinance unconstitutional, because in a previous decision, in which the superintendent of the railway company was prosecuted for operating one-man cars, contrary to the provision of the ordinance, [107]

the validity of the ordinance had been sustained by the lower courts and by the Supreme Court of the United States. The 

Supreme Court, however, declined to review that decision. 

Valuation Problems. 

Among the cases involving valuations of public utilities were two relating to the valuations of the properties of water companies. The case of Vincennes, Ind. vs. Vincennes

Water Supply Company was the first valuation controversy which came to the Supreme Court after its decision in the 

St. Louis & O'Fallon Railway Case. The City of Vin cennes maintained that the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit misapplied that decision by requiring the Public Utilities Commission of Indiana to give dominant consideration to the reproduction cost value of the properties of the Water Company, in determining the value for rate making purposes. In valuating the property the Commission took into consideration such elements as (1) the estimated original cost of the property used in normal operations, as well as the property not so used, plus additions from the date of the estimate to the date of the order; (2) the total outstanding securities; (3) the sale price of the common stock; (4) the valuations in the company's tax returns; (5) the valuation of the property claimed by the company as a rate base in 1921 and the valuation fixed upon that claim; (6) the additions since the last valuation to the date of the order; (7) the cost of the original plant and franchise in stocks and bonds; and (8) property and plant accounts carried on the company's books. Taking these elements into consideration, the Commission found that the company's properties were worth $725,000. 

Sullivan vs. City of Shreveport, 251 U. S. 169. 

7 St. Louis & O'Fallon Railway Co. et al. vs. United States et al., 279 U. S. 461. [108]

The company then brought this suit to enjoin the Public Utilities Commission from enforcing the rates based on that valuation. It was stipulated by the parties that 7% was a fair return to be received by the Water Supply Company, and the controversy reduced itself solely to the question of valuation. The Circuit Court of Appeals held that the 

Commission erred in failing to give consideration to the reproduction costs of the water works as of the date of its order. In rendering its decision, however, the Circuit Court of Appeals also held that any valuation below a certain amount must be deemed confiscatory. 

The City of Vincennes and the Public Utilities Commission of Indiana then petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision on the question of valuation, and it also maintained that the Circuit Court of Appeals usurped the legislative functions of the Commission by setting a fixed replacement value on the company's properties, instead of remanding the case to the Commission for the purpose of ascertaining the proper value of those properties. After the petition was filed the Circuit Court of Appeals modified its opinion, stating that it did not intend to bind the legislative actions of the Commission, but merely found the value of the company's property to be $1,032,000, as against the figure found by the Commission, as a preliminary step in determining that the schedule of rates ordered by the Commission resulted in confiscation. The Supreme Court denied the petition to review that decision.Ⓡ 

The second case pertained to the properties of the Washburn Water Works Company in Wisconsin. This case likewise came from the Seventh Circuit, but not from the Circuit Court of Appeals. It was a suit brought in the statutory three-judge court in Wisconsin to enjoin the order 

8 No. 197-City of Vincennes vs. Vincennes Water Supply Co. Cer tiorari denied, October 21, 1929. [109]

of the Railroad Commission of the state, denying the application of the receiver of the Washburn Water Works for an increase in rates. The question hinged on the valuation of the company's properties and on the proper expenditures to be allowed in computing the rate base. 

The Commission found that the reproduction cost of the property was $87,000, but that the value of the property for rate making purposes was only $75,000. The Commission arrived at this result by giving consideration to the fact that property values in the city had suffered a general decline and that there was no prospect of values increasing in the near future. In computing the company's proper operating costs, the Commission held that savings could be effected by installation of an electric pump and of meters, and therefore the Commission refused to take into consideration the actual operating expenses incurred by the company. In its order, the Commission found that the company should have a seven per cent. return on the value of $75,000. 

The District Court in Wisconsin enjoined this order on the ground that the Commission had no evidence before it to support the value of $75,000. The court made no findings of fact nor conclusions of law, and from this bare order the Commission appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States.

It maintained that the cost of reproduction less depreciation is not the sole or dominant element of valuation, but that all elements entering into a particular case should be considered; that evidence of the economic conditions in the community in which the utility is located is relevant to fixing the values of the utility's property; that in considering property values in the city of Washburn, the shrinkage in population, the scarcity of industries in the city, the fact that the water works constructed were larger than required were all material factors affecting the valuation of the [110]

utility's property for rate making purposes; and that the value of $75,000 placed on the property by the Commission. was not unreasonable or confiscatory. The Commission further argued that a return of seven per cent. on a value of $75,000 was not confiscatory, if it was proper to take into consideration, as it did, the fact that the costs incurred by the company were excessive, that the legal expenses in the suit between the company and the Commission should not be considered, and that the installation of certain devices would result in a decrease of operating costs. 

The receiver of the Water Works Company argued in opposition that the Commission could not ignore the actual operating expenses and substitute its own estimate of what the proper operating expenses should be, without evidence to support the finding that the expenses would be decreased by a definite amount. He also argued that the only evidence as to valuation before the Commission pertained to reproduction costs, and that therefore the valuation order should have been based entirely on reproduction costs. 

In a per curiam decision which, we understand, was written by Chief Justice Hughes, the Court reversed the order of the district court and remanded the case to that court for specific findings of fact and conclusions of law." 

The Court stressed the necessity and importance that in a suit of this character the district court state the grounds of its decision. The Court said: "The importance is even greater where the decree enjoins the enforcement of a state law or the action of state officials thereunder. For then, the respect due to the State demands that the need for nullifying the action of its legislature or of its executive officials be persuasively shown." 

9 No. 301-Railroad Commission of Wisconsin vs. Maxcy, Receiver of Washburn Water Works. Decided March 12, 1930. 

[111]

An important valuation case came to the Supreme Court toward the end of the term and has been carried over to the next term.10 This was the case involving the valuation of the Illinois Bell Telephone Company in which the statutory district court in Illinois enjoined the rate making order of the Illinois Commerce Commission, on the ground that the rates ordered were based on an insufficient valuation of the company's properties. The original cost of these properties was about $95,000,000. The company claimed a reproduction cost of $139,560,000, the Commission's valuation was about $115,000,000, while the district court held that any valuation of these properties less than $125,000,000 must be considered confiscatory. This case also involves other important problems in rate making. For example, the 

Commission ruled that a fund maintained by the company as a reserve for depreciation should be deducted from the valuation of the properties used as a rate base. This, the court held could not be done. Also in computing the earnings of the company, the Commission found that the compensation it received from the American Telephone and Telegraph Company (the only investor of the Illinois Bell Telephone Company) for the use of the facilities for long distance service was inadequate. The court held, on the contrary, that the compensation received by the Illinois Bell Telephone Company was fair and reasonable. These and other questions will probably make this case one of the outstanding of the coming year. 

Cooperatively-Owned Utilities.

We have already touched on the Oklahoma Cotton Gin Law and on the First Cotton Gin Case when we discussed the role which economic factors play in the judicial process 

10 No. 845-Illinois Commerce Commission vs. Illinois Bell Telephone 

Company. Probable jurisdiction shown, June 3, 1930. [112]

of the Supreme Court." As was said there, the Court held that the Oklahoma Cotton Gin Law was unconstitutional, in so far as it required privately owned cotton gins to show public convenience and necessity, as a condition to obtaining a license to operate, without imposing such requirement on cooperative cotton gins. The reason for the decision was that one who is subjected to this requirement has a right to be free from competition on the part of those who are not subjected to it. 

This term another Oklahoma cotton gin case came to the Supreme Court. It was a suit brought on the basis of the first cotton gin decision, and for a while it threatened to exterminate all cooperative gins in the State. This was a suit brought to enjoin the Corporation Commission of Oklahoma from granting a license to a cooperative cotton gin to operate in competition with the complainant, a privately owned cotton gin. The essence of the complaint was that under the laws of Oklahoma cotton gins are public utilities, and the rates for ginning cotton are regulated by the Corporation Commission of the State, which means that the charge must be the rate prescribed and the cotton gin may not give rebates from the prices charged. 

By the laws of Oklahoma authorizing cooperative cotton gins to do business, the cooperatives must provide a reserve fund, may fix dividends for the members of the association, and may distribute the remainder of the income ratably among its patrons on the basis of the amount of business done with the cooperative. The complainant argued that the laws governing cooperative associations in effect permit them to give rebates and to render ginning service at a lesser rate than that prescribed by the Commission, thus putting the privately owned cotton gins at an unfair disadvantage. It argued that this is an arbitrary discrimination, 11 See p. 19 supra. [113]

contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution. 

The argument was based directly on the first cotton gin decision.¹2

The United States District Court in Oklahoma sustained the contentions of the privately owned cotton gin, and the Corporation Commission appealed to the Supreme Court. 

It argued that the distinction made by the legislature of Oklahoma between privately owned cotton gins and cooperative gins was not so arbitrary and unreasonable as to be contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution; that on the contrary, laws encouraging and favoring cooperative enterprises have been passed by many states since 1865, but that Oklahoma, because of the peculiar conditions of the cotton gin industry, was the first state to declare the operation of cotton gins as affected with a public interest. 

It argued that should the Supreme Court sustain the decision of the district court, it would mean the end of cooperative enterprises in fields which are declared public utilities. 

With reference to the cooperative cotton gins in Oklahoma, the Commission argued that such decision would affect adversely some 20,000 stockholders and farmers who have invested over $2,000,000 in these cooperative enterprises. 

The case was argued in the Supreme Court at the time when the Senate was debating the nomination of Judge 

Parker. True to the slogan that "Judge Parker is only on incident, the Supreme Court is the issue," one of the newspapers in the Capital challenged the consistency and correctness of the Court's decisions with reference to cooperative enterprises. After discussing the Court's decision in the 

First Cotton Gin Case, the editorial called attention to the fact that two years before the Court upheld the Kentucky Cooperative Marketing Act as a valid exercise of police power in order to prevent night riding and other forms of 

12 Frost vs. Corporation Commission of Oklahoma, 278 U. S. 515. 

[114]

violence to which Kentucky farmers had resorted.13 The editorial concluded by the question whether it was the Court's theory that farmers must resort to violence in order to have valid protection against private corporations. 

The answer to the question is obvious. It is not the task of the Supreme Court to shape the legislative policies of the various states so that they will all resort to a systematic program of farm relief. Yet the difference in the result reached by the Court in the Kentucky Case and the First Cotton Gin Case, perhaps, does indicate a weakness in the judicial function of our federal courts with reference to state legislation, in so far as questions of policy are decided on constitutional grounds. The editorial was, of course, intended to arouse interest in the results of the Second Cotton Gin Case, for it was supposed that after the decision in the First Cotton Gin Case, the Supreme Court could not but affirm the decision of the district court. 

The Supreme Court, however, reversed the decision of the lower court, and thus saved the Oklahoma cooperative cotton gins.¹ Chief Justice Hughes, delivering the opinion of the Court, held that the distribution of net profits does not constitute a giving of rebates in the ordinarily accepted meaning of the term, that in accordance with the law of Oklahoma there was nothing to prevent a privately owned cotton gin from distributing its net profits, hence there was no discrimination between the two types of cotton gins and no denial of the equal protection of laws. 

We think that the opinion was an ingenious method of saving the cooperative cotton gins from the consequences of the first cotton gin decision, but we cannot agree with the reasoning in the opinion. Assuming that a privately owned 

13 Liberty Warehouse Company vs. Burley Tob. Growers' Cooperative 

Association, 276 U. S. 71. 

14 No. 454-Corporation Commission of Oklahoma et al. vs. William 

Lowe. Decided May 19, 1930. [115]

cotton gin does distribute its net earnings to its patrons, it remains at an economic disadvantage as compared with the cooperative cotton gins. Let us take a simple hypothetical case. A cooperative cotton gin has a paid up capital of $100,000 and a surplus of $50,000,15 or a total of $150,000, and that its net earnings for the year is $20,000. Of these, $8,000 are distributed as dividends,16 and $12,000 are distributed among the patrons, who in most instances are also the stockholders. In this case, the stockholders, therefore, receive on the average a minimum of 5%% and a maximum return of 13% on the entire investment, depending on how many of the patrons are not stockholders. If, on the other hand, a privately owned cotton gin, having a total investment of $150,000 and net earnings of $20,000, distributes $12,000 among its patrons, then the return to the stockholders on their entire investment is only 5%%, with the additional fact that most of the net earnings are received by strangers to the business enterprise.

There is a further difference in that the patrons of a cooperative gin may transact business with the expectation that they will receive a pro rata share of the net profits, whereas a patron of a privately owned gin can have no such expectation. This, of course, puts the privately owned gin at a disadvantage in obtaining patronage, and in addition puts it at a disadvantage in obtaining investments, or, to use the phrase of Justice Sutherland in the United Railways

Case, it is at a disadvantage in its task "to induce the 17 15 Under the law of Oklahoma, not less than ten per cent. of the net earnings and profits accruing since the last apportionment must be set aside. in a surplus or reserve fund until such fund equals at least fifty per cent. of the paid up capital stock. The hypothetical case is simplified by assuming that the cooperative cotton gin has a surplus equal to fifty per cent. of its paid up capital. 

16 Under the law, the directors of the cooperative associations may, in their discretion, declare dividends at the rate not to exceed 8% per annum upon the paid up capital stock. 

17 See p. 105 supra. [116]

investment of capital in the enterprise or to enable the company (in this case the privately owned cotton gin) to compete successfully in the market for money to finance its operations."

The decision furthermore overlooks the obvious fact that it is not within the nature of a private business to distribute its net earnings or profits to its patrons. Premiums, profit sharing coupons, trading stamps and similar devices may be used in the nature of advertising in order to attract more business. But the distribution of profits is not the object of a private enterprise, whereas it is the object of a cooperative enterprise. When the privately owned cotton gin invoked the protection of the Fourteenth Amendment, it did so in aid of its right to do business as a private enterprise. To say that it may also distribute its net earnings means that it may also do business as a cooperative, with the additional burden of supplying its own capital. That is not equal protection as between private and cooperative enterprises. The result of the decision can be supported only upon a recognition of a fundamental difference between these two types of economic units, and if that is the case, then it would have been simpler for the Court to have reversed its decision in the First Cotton Gin Case, and to have adopted the dissenting opinion written by Justice 

Brandeis in that case. Radio.

We now come to the youngest of public utilities,-Radio Broadcasting. We call it a public utility, because under the law one cannot operate a broadcasting station except upon a showing of public convenience and necessity and under a license of the government; also because the operation of a broadcasting station involves a monopolistic control of the "ether," within some particular channel of the [117]

broadcasting band. The first radio case which came to the 

Supreme Court involved the operation of Station WGY at Schenectady, N. Y. The owner of the station, the General 

Electric Company, questioned the constitutionality of the Federal Radio Act of 192718 and also of the General Orders issued by the Federal Radio Commission. 

The General Electric Company sought the renewal of its license issued to it on November 1, 1927, to operate 

Station WGY upon 790 kilocycles and with a power of 50 kilowatts as a full-time station. This license was originally granted for a period of two months, was successively extended for short periods, but it finally expired on November 11, 1928. On October 12, 1928, the Commission granted a renewal of the license for the same frequency and substantially the same power, but with hours of operation reduced, to what is defined by the regulations of the Commission, as a limited time station. This license was issued on October 

17, 1928, it covered the period from November 11, 1928, to January 31, 1929, but it was rejected by the General Electric Company, which, however, continued to operate the station under a "stay order" from the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia. The General Electric Company appealed under the provisions of the Federal Radio Act which provides that licensees may appeal from the decisions of the Commission either to the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia or to the district court of the United States for the district within which the broadcasting station is located. 

The State of New York which had intervened in the proceedings as guardian of the people of the state, likewise appealed, when overwhelming protests were filed throughout the state, against the closing of station WGY as a fulltime station. 

18 Act of 1927, c. 169, 44 Stat., pt. 2, 1162, as amended in 1928, c. 

263, 45 Stat. 373, and in 1929, c. 701, 45 Stat. 1559. [118]

The Radio Commission filed its motion to dismiss the appeals on the grounds, among others, that none of the appeals were taken within twenty days after the effective date of the Commission's decision, as required by Section 16 of the Radio Act; that the State of New York had no standing in the appeal, because it was not interested in the broadcasting station; and furthermore, that the Court of Appeals lacked jurisdiction as to the subject matter of the appeal. 

The Court of Appeals overruled the motions of the Commission and held that the General Electric Company was entitled to operate Station WGY as a full-time station.

In its appeal the General Electric Company questioned the constitutionality of the Federal Radio Act, in so far as it terminated the right to broadcast except under a license issued by the Federal Radio Commission. The company maintained that it had acquired a vested right in the station and in the use of it, and that this right could not be taken away without just compensation, as provided by the Fifth 

Amendment to the Constitution.

The Company also questioned the validity of General 

Order No. 40, adopted by the Commission on August 30, 1928, and made effective on October 1, 1928. This order was promulgated by the Commission for the purpose of carrying out the mandate of Congress to clear up the chaotic interferences in broadcasting and to equalize broadcasting facilities. The Order classified the 96 channels in the broadcasting band and placed restrictions on each of them with respect to the number of stations which may operate on them simultaneously, and the power which such stations might use. This Order set aside 40 of the 96 channels, known as "cleared" channels to be used by stations of substantial power, namely, from 5 to 50 kilowatts, with a restriction that only one station might use such channel at any time during the night hours. These 40 channels were [119]

allocated eight for each of the five zones. The channel of 

790 kilocycles was designated as a cleared channel and was assigned to the fifth zone for exclusive operation in the evening without interference.

Having adopted General Order No. 40, the Commission made a tentative allocation of all broadcasting stations and issued a list of new assignments to every licensee, including the General Electric Company. This allocation beginning 

November 11, 1928, showed Station WGY as having been assigned 790 kilocycles with 50 kilowatt power as a limited time station; and Station KGO at Oakland, California, also owned by the General Electric Company, was assigned to full time operation, with 10 kilowatt power on the same channel. As applied to Station WGY this meant that it would have to close down when the hour of sunset arrived at Oakland, unless the Oakland station would submit to a slight curtailment of its time.19

The General Electric Company maintained that it was entitled to full time operation on 790 kilocycles, which under General Order No. 40 was a cleared channel assigned to the fifth zone. The Commission maintained that there were eight other channels designated as cleared channels and assigned to the first zone, all of which were available to the General Electric Company. The application of the company also asked for a power of 150 kilowatts, which was 100 kilowatts in excess of the maximum permitted by General Order 42. The Commission denied the application, 19 The interference range of radio waves of two stations on the same channel is considerably less during daylight hours than after dark. Therefore, if Station KGO would not submit to any curtailment, the Commission's ruling meant an average daily closing time for Station WGY at 

9 P. M. at Schnectady, N. Y., varying, however, from 10.30 P. M. in

June to about 8 P. M. in December. The Commission proposed an arrangement whereby Station WGY might operate until 10 P. M. on the condition that KGO close down between sunset and 7 P. M. during those months when sunset at Oakland occurs prior to 7 P. M. [120]

because it was in conflict with the regulations, and all this was done without a hearing. 

When the General Electric Company appealed to the Court of Appeals, it filed evidence contained in documents attached to its notice of appeal and also evidence which was contained in the application of the company for a renewal license. The Commission maintained that the evidence in the record clearly pointed to the conclusion that there would be serious interference arising from simultaneous evening operations of WGY and KGO, and that such interference would reduce the service range of both stations to a very small area around the transmitter of each station. The 

Court of Appeals, as was said, held in favor of the General Electric Company on the ground that the public convenience and necessity required the operation of Station WGY as a full time station.

The Commission then filed two cases in the Supreme Court, one was an original action for a writ of mandamus or prohibition to compel the Court of Appeals to vacate its order and to abstain from taking further jurisdiction in the case. The second was a petition for a writ of certiorari to review the decision of the Court of Appeals. The reason for this double proceeding was that it did not seem clear whether the Court of Appeals was acting in an administrative or in a judicial capacity in reviewing this order of the Commission. Acting in an administrative capacity, the 

Court of Appeals undertook to find that the broadcasting station had a right to broadcast as a full time station, instead of remanding the case for further proceedings before the Commission. In order to arrive at this finding, the Court also considered evidence which had not been presented to the Commission, instead of requiring that such evidence be first introduced before the Commission. On the other hand, the Court also acted in a judicial capacity, in that it issued a [121]

"stay order," under which the station was operating pending the appeal before the court.

If Thus an interesting jurisdictional difficulty arose. the Court of Appeals in such cases acted in an administrative capacity, then its decisions were not reviewable by the Supreme Court on certiorari. If it acted in a judicial capacity, then its decisions were reviewable only on certiorari. 

Since the exact function of the Court of Appeals was not known, the Commission attempted both remedies. The petition for writ of review was granted, and the Court postponed the petition for writ of mandamus or prohibition until the hearing of the case on its merits. Shortly before the argument, however, the Commission became convinced that the proper remedy was by petition for writ of certiorari, and it withdrew its petition for a writ of mandamus or prohibition. When the argument began, this jurisdictional question came up, but apparently the Supreme Court was immediately convinced that the Court of Appeals acted in an administrative capacity, for it declined to hear the arguments on the merits of the case. 

While this case was pending for decision, several other interesting radio cases came to the Supreme Court. In one of these, the City of New York had appealed to the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia from an order of the Radio Commission, limiting the operation of the municipal station WNYC to a limited time station. The city's station was first licensed by the Department of Commerce on July 2, 1924, and this license was subsequently renewed and extended by the Department, always as a full time station. 

After Congress passed the Radio Act of 1927, and the Radio Commission promulgated its general orders, Station WNYC was allocated for limited time operation to broadcast on alternate days with Station WMCA, operated by the Y. M. C. A. of New York. [122]

The city maintained that the Federal Radio Commission erred in limiting the time of Station WNYC, because the public convenience and necessity required its full time operation, particularly in view of the educational programs broadcasted. It also added the arguments concerning the validity of the Federal Radio Act under the Fifth Amendment of the Constitution. Its most significant argument, it seems to us was, that the Commission had no jurisdiction to limit the operation of Station WNYC, because the station was used for governmental purposes and the Commission had no power to interfere with a governmental function of a state or its political subdivisions. The Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia sustained the order of the Federal Radio Commission, and the Supreme Court denied the city's petition to review that decision.20

Much speculation arose as to the reasons for the Court's denial of the petition for writ of certiorari in this case, but the reason became apparent only after the Supreme Court decided the WGY case.21 It held, in an opinion delivered by Justice Van Devanter, that the Supreme Court had no jurisdiction to review the decisions of the Court of Appeals, because, in the appeals from the Federal Radio Commission, that Court was acting in an administrative capacity. 

Then various comments appeared concerning the effect of the WGY decision. One newspaper declared that the decision made the rulings of the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia, on radio matters, the supreme law of the land. On the other hand, it may also be suggested that if the Court of Appeals acts in an administrative capacity in reviewing the orders of the Federal Radio Commission, then the Commission need not acquiesce in those decisions 

20 No. 612-City of New York vs. Federal Radio Commission. Cer tiorari denied March 12, 1930. 

21 No. 122-Federal Radio Commission vs. General Electric Company. 

Decided May 19, 1930. [123]

altogether. And, if an owner of a broadcasting station chooses to abide by the decision of the Court of Appeals, rather than by that of the Commission, the latter may bring suit for violation of its order, upon which the validity of the order would be judicially determined. Likewise, where the 

Court of Appeals sustains the order of the Commission, the owner can disobey, making himself subject to prosecution, in which case he will have his day in court to show that the decisions of the Commission and of the Court of Appeals were erroneous.

While the WGY case was still pending for decision, a bill22 was introduced in Congress amending Section 16 of the Radio Act, giving the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia authority to review the orders of the Commission in a manner analogous to the review exercised by the Circuit Court of Appeals over the orders of the Federal Trade Commission. The amendment limited the review to questions of law, and provided that the findings of fact by the Commission, if supported by substantial evidence, shall be conclusive, unless it should clearly appear that the findings were arbitrary or capricious or that the action of the Commission constituted an abuse of sound discretion. The amendment also provided that the court's judgment shall be final, subject to review by the Supreme Court of the United States upon writ of certiorari. This bill passed the House of Representatives on April 30, 1930.23 24 

On May 24, 1930, another petition for certiorari came to the Supreme Court, to review a radio decision of the 

22 H. R. 11635, 71st Cong., 2nd Sess. 

23 Cong. Rec., 71st Cong., 2nd Sess., pages 8352, 8353,8357. 

24 No. 944-Agricultural Broadcasting Co. vs. Great Lakes Broadcast ing Co. et al. [124]

Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia. The peti tioner maintained that its case differed from the WGY case, because the lower court ordered it to give up part of its broadcasting time to another station, contrary to the findings of the Commission. It maintained that this property right could be taken away only if the lower court acted in a judicial capacity; therefore, the Supreme Court had jurisdiction to review that decision. The proposed amendment to the Federal Radio Act, however, had not become law when the petition came up for consideration, and, as has already been stated,25 the petition was dismissed on the authority of the WGY decision.

Thus the main constitutional questions raised in the WGY case remained undecided, but, it is expected, not for long, since another case came to the Supreme Court raising the same questions. The Court may be able to avoid final decision on these questions, but not on jurisdictional grounds, for this case came on certificate from the Circuit 

Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit.26

This was a suit brought to enjoin the United States District Attorney in Chicago from enforcing an order of the Federal Radio Commission. The complainant was operating Station WCRW in Chicago on a frequency of 1340 kilocycles and a power of 500 watts under a license from the Federal Radio Commission. On May 25, 1928, the Commission issued its General Order 32 in which it required broadcasting stations to show that the public interest, convenience and necessity would be served by granting their applications for renewal of licenses. Upon consideration 

25 See p. 32 supra. 

26 No. 573-Clinton R. White vs. George E. G. Johnson. Case still pending. [125]

of Station WCRW, the Radio Commission ordered the owner to reduce the power of his broadcasting station to 100 watts, which, the evidence showed, would necessitate reconstructing and replacing a substantial portion of the apparatus. The district court denied the application for an injunction, and the Circuit Court of Appeals certified a number of questions to the Supreme Court, going into the validity of the Federal Radio Act and of the rules and practices of the Commission. [126]

CHAPTER V. 

Insurance
