Insurance problems considered by the Court during this term may be roughly divided into those pertaining to the regulation of insurance companies, those involving problems of taxation, and those dealing with interpretation of insurance contracts.

Regulation of Insurance. 

The outstanding cases dealing with regulation of insurance companies concerned the rate making orders of the 

Superintendents of Insurance of the States of Kansas and 

Missouri. We have already seen what happened to the 

Kansas case in the Supreme Court during this term.¹ In the 

Missouri case, the State fared somewhat more successfully in the Supreme Court.

This controversy arose when the Superintendent of Insurance of Missouri in 1922 ordered a reduction of 10 per cent. on insurance rates charged by the stock fire insurance companies doing business in the State. This order was based on the earnings of the companies during a test period of five years. In computing the earnings, the Superintendent of Insurance took into consideration the premiums received and the expenses or losses paid out by the companies. The latter argued that this method was erroneous and that the earnings should be computed on the basis of premiums earned less expenses and losses incurred during those five years. There was quite a difference in the results 

1 See p. 43 et seq. [127]

arrived at by the use of these two methods of computation. 

While the premiums received by the insurance companies during this period amounted to $83,500,000, and the losses and expenses paid out were $80,000,000, thus showing a net income of $3,500,000, a computation on the basis of premiums earned and expenses incurred during this test period showed a loss of $7,000,000. The 155 stock fire insurance companies doing business in the state brought suit to enjoin the enforcement of this rate order, arguing that the reduction of 10 per cent. was confiscatory and contrary to the Fourteenth Amendment of the Federal Constitution. In that case, the Supreme Court of Missouri upheld the validity of the order. The companies brought the case to the Supreme Court of the United States on a writ of error. After argument, however, the writ of error was dismissed for lack of a substantial federal question, in an opinion delivered by Mr. Justice Butler.

The Court did not go into the merits of the controversy,3 but held that while each of the companies might have complained that the order of the superintendent resulted in confiscation as to each of them, they could not combine and claim that the order was confiscatory as to all of them. In other words, as was aptly stated by Justice Butler, the Fourteenth Amendment, prohibiting taking of property without due process of law, was not a guarantee against free competition among the insurance companies. 

These 155 insurance companies then instituted separate suits in the federal district court in Missouri to enjoin the enforcement of the rate order. Each company filed substantially the same complaint, and substantially the same answer was filed by the superintendent of insurance. The superintendent contended that, since the companies had 

2 Aetna Insurance Co. vs. Hyde, 315 Mo. 113. 

3 Aetna Insurance Co. vs. Hyde, 275 U. S. 440. [128]

elected the state courts as their forum, and since the state courts had already ruled on the question of confiscation, and their judgment had become final, the companies could not institute proceedings anew in the federal court, raising the same questions; in other words, that they were estopped by the final judgment reached in the previous suit. The federal district court overruled this defense and held that the reduction ordered by the superintendent of insurance was based upon an unsound theory, that it was capricious, arbitrary and oppressive, and resulted in confiscation. The district court enjoined the superintendent from enforcing the rate in 41 of the 155 cases. In the remaining 114 cases the district court refused to grant an interlocutory injunction for reasons stated below.

The National Fire Insurance Company of Hartford was among these unsuccessful companies. It complained that the order was confiscatory, because during the test period in question its outgo exceeded its income by $305,000 computed on premiums earned less insurance losses and expenses incurred during that period. It failed, however, to disclose what its net loss or net gain might have been, if computed on the basis of premiums received less losses and expenses paid. 

When the superintendent first promulgated his order, the companies, including the National Fire Insurance Company, filed suit in the Circuit Court of Cole County, Missouri, to restrain that order. A stipulation was then entered into between the Attorney General of the State and the attorneys for the insurance companies, whereby it was agreed that the superintendent would make further investigations and further findings of facts, and that if the companies are then aggrieved by the order of the superintendent, they might institute a new suit to restrain the order; that pending the final determination of that suit the companies would collect premiums in accordance with the old rates; [129]

that the excess premiums collected by them would be deposited into a separate fund; and that should the final determination by the court of last resort be against them, they would refund to their policyholders any excess premiums collected by them. As we have seen, the Supreme 

Court of Missouri upheld the validity of the order, and the Supreme Court of the United States declined to go into the merits of the controversy, thus leaving the judgment of the Supreme Court of the State as the judgment of last resort. 

When this suit was brought by the National Fire Insurance Company, the superintendent of insurance maintained that the company was not entitled to the injunctive relief sought by it, because it had refused to comply with the provision in the stipulation to return the excess premiums, in other words, because it came into equity with unclean hands. 

This contention was upheld by the district court, and that was the reason why the injunctive relief was denied to this and the other 113 companies which had entered into the stipulation.

The companies argued that it would be unfair to require them to comply with this provision in the stipulation, because the matter was still to be disposed of by the federal courts, and should they return the excess premiums collected by them, there would be no way of collecting these again, should it afterwards be finally determined that the rate order was confiscatory. On this, the district court held : "It is probable that such recovery would be impossible, or at any rate highly improbable. Such result is no reason for granting the injunction." Upon this question the case came to the Supreme Court of the United States. The appeal was filed on May 22, 1929.

While the case was pending in the Supreme Court, to wit, on August 19, 1929, the National Fire Insurance Company and some 113 other fire insurance companies doing [130]

business in the state voluntarily and of their own accord put the 10 per cent. reduction into effect. The superintendent of insurance therefor contended, in addition to his arguments on the merits of the controversy, that this appeal should be dismissed, because the controversy had become moot. The insurance company argued in opposition that the voluntary reduction in rates did not render the case moot, because in this suit the insurance company sought to enjoin the superintendent from cancelling the company's license, from prosecuting it for having collected premiums in excess of those prescribed in the order, and from refusing to renew its license because of the fact that it had collected such premiums. Therefore, it maintained, the voluntary reduction in rates for the future did not render this controversy moot. Undoubtedly, the insurance company was right on this point, and in its opinion which finally disposed of this controversy, the Supreme Court did not even refer to this argument.

The Supreme Court decided the case against the National Fire Insurance Company, on the ground that the appellant had not shown that the lower court abused its discretion in failing to grant the interlocutory injunction.* 

In the course of the argument, the insurance company maintained that it did not obligate itself absolutely to refund the excess premiums; that it had agreed to refund the excess premiums collected, upon the superintendent's promise to institute an investigation as to the necessity of a rate reduction, at the conclusion of which he would make certain findings of fact, and, if based on such findings he would order a reduction in rates, then these reductions should apply alike to all classes of risks. The insurance company claimed that the superintendent failed to make the specified son. 

4 No. 104-National Fire Insurance Company of Hartford vs. Thomp- 

Decided Apil 14, 1930, [131]

findings and therefore relieved the insurance company from any obligation under the stipulation. 

Upon examination of the record in the previous suit brought before the Court, Justice Butler said: "The order is there fully set forth. It states that the companies refused to furnish the superintendent the necessary facts, and that accordingly such findings could not be made. Clearly plaintiff's showing is not sufficient to require the Court to find that the superintendent was not excused by the companies' refusal to furnish information as agreed." For these reasons, the Supreme Court affirmed the decree of the statutory district court.

Another problem of regulation related to insurance agencies. Under the Insurance Law of Oregon, it is provided that when an insurance company maintains more than two agents in a city having a population of 50,000 or more, the company must pay a license fee of $500 for each additional agent in that city. A man named Karl von Herbring made application for a license as insurance agent in the City of Portland. His application was denied, because his insurance company had not paid the $500 fee, and it already had two agents in that city. Herbring appealed to the state courts, maintaining that the statute was contrary to the privileges and immunities clause of the Federal Constitution, and that it was an unreasonable and unwarrantable interference with his rights as an individual to engage in his legitimate business of insurance agent. He argued that the $500 fee was not a fee imposed on the insurance company, for it already had obtained a license to do business in the state, but an unwarranted limitation on himself.

The courts of Oregon sustained the validity of the statute and the denial of the license, and Herbring appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. Here the case was argued for only a few minutes, the Court having de- [132]

clined to hear further, and later it affirmed the decision of the Oregon courts in an opinion delivered by Justice Sanford. This Court sustained the validity of the agency fee provision in the Insurance Law of Oregon, on the ground that it was not directed against the individual insurance agents and imposed no restrictions upon them, but merely required the insurance company itself to pay a $500 fee as a condition precedent to its right to maintain an additional agent in the city. Unless an insurance company obtained a license for an additional agent, no license could be issued to anyone applying to represent that company.

The insurance agent also argued in the Supreme Court that if the statute is construed as imposing an obligation upon the insurance company rather than upon the insurance agent, then it must be held unconstitutional, as arbitrary and capricious, and cannot be sustained under the police powers of the state. On this the Court held, without passing on the merits of the question, that since the insurance company itself was not heard to insist that the statute constituted an impairment of its own right, this question raised by the agent was not properly before it for decision. 

Later in the term another statute regulating insurance agencies came to the Supreme Court from the state of New Jersey. This law forbade insurance companies to pay any agent greater premiums than it paid to any other agent in the state. In other words, it in effect prescribed the maximum compensation to be paid to insurance agents. An insurance agency which operated in the state under contracts with the Phoenix Assurance Company and the Hartford Fire Insurance Company, brought suit to enjoin the enforcement of this law, on the ground that it was in violation of the contracts, and also on the ground that the statute was without due process, as a price fixing measure. 

5 No. 17-Herbring vs. Lee. Decided November 25, 1929. [133]

The Court of Errors and Appeals of New Jersey upheld the validity of the statute as being a proper measure in aid of the state's power to regulate the insurance business. Since the state can regulate insurance rates, that Court held, it can also regulate the commissions paid to insurance agents as an incident to rate regulation. The insurance agency appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. 

Previously, when the Supreme Court had occasion to pass on minimum wage laws, one of the arguments attacking the constitutionality of such statutes was that if a state can prescribe minimum compensation it can also prescribe maximum compensation, which is untenable. In this case, the question of the right of a state to prescribe maximum compensation is more directly presented, but it so happens that it comes up in connection with a field of activity over which the state legislatures have wide powers. The case was argued in the Supreme Court, but shortly afterwards the Court ordered the case restored for re-argument, which will take place during the coming term." Taxation.

Insurance companies have for a long time been regarded as a special class for taxation purposes. In Missouri, insurance companies were taxed on the following basis: Each insurance company organized under the laws of the state was required to file a return listing (1) all real estate held or controlled by it, and (2) "the net value of all its other assets or values in excess of the legally required reserve necessary to reinsure its outstanding risks and of any unpaid policy claim, which net value shall be assessed and taxed as the property of individuals ***." In other words, in the ***” 

6 Adkins vs. Children's Hospital, 261 U. S. 525. 

7 No. 458-O'Gorman & Young, Inc. vs. Hartford Fire Insurance Co., and No. 459-O'Gorman & Young, Inc. vs. Phoenix Assurance Co. 

Restored to Docket, May 26, 1930. [134]

taxation of the insurance companies, the tax was imposed on the net value of the corporation less the legal reserve and unpaid policy claims, which were thereby declared taxexempt.

The Missouri Insurance Company had personal property totalling some $448,000, of which $94,000 were United States bonds. The legal reserve and unpaid policy claims amounted to $333,000. In its return, the insurance company deducted the amount of the bonds from the total value of personal property, and then deducted the exemption allowed by law, namely, the legal reserve and the unpaid policy claims, which left a net taxable amount of about $21,000. 

The Board of Equalization declined to accept the return, and assessed the company's taxable property at $50,000. 

This was supposedly the difference between the total personal property, less the legal reserve and unpaid policy claims, without deducting the United States bonds, but in reality no one knows how that amount was arrived at. 

The company appealed to the Supreme Court of Missouri, which held, however, that the legal reserve and unpaid claims should be apportioned between the taxable and non-taxable property according to their respective amounts, that as thus apportioned 79 per cent. of the legal reserve and unpaid claims was to be allocated to the taxable property and that amount should be deducted from the value of the taxable personal property. Since the result exceeded the amount fixed by the Board, the Supreme Court of the state refused to disturb the assessment. 

From this decision the insurance company appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States, maintaining that the statute, as applied, amounted to a tax on United States Government securities. The Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Butler, upheld the contentions of the insurance 

8 No. 222-Mo. ex rel. Mo. Ins. Co. vs. Gehner. Decided April 14, 1930. [135]

company mainly on the authority of the Court's decision in the case of National Fire Insurance Company vs. United 

States, rendered two years ago. The Court held that "a state may not subject one to a greater burden upon his taxable property merely because he owns tax-exempt government securities." Since these securities were not taxable, the company was not allowed its full deduction for legal reserve and unpaid policy claims, and the ownership of the securities was made "the basis of denying the full exemption which is accorded to those who own no such bonds." This, the Court concluded, amounted to an infringement of the guarantee that the securities would be free from taxation. "It is clear that the value of appellant's government bonds was not disregarded in making up the estimate of taxable net values. That is in violation of the established rule." 

This decision of the Court evoked a strong dissenting opinion on the part of Justice Stone, with whom Justices Holmes and Brandeis agreed. This, however, will be discussed later.10

Insurance Contracts. 

We now come to the construction or validity of insurance contracts. Various types of insurance contracts were represented in the cases considered by the Court during this term.

Life, Accident and War Risk. 

Life, accident and war risk insurance constituted the largest number of insurance cases. The most important of the problems under life insurance contracts related to the double indemnity clauses in case of accidental death. One of these will appeal to those interested in aviation. For in 

9 277 U. S. 508, 519. 

10 See p. 226 infra. [136]

that case, the policy provided that the double indemnity clause shall not apply if death resulted from aeronautic operations. The evidence showed that the insured was a passenger in an airplane, that when the plane rose, he unbuckled the strap which held him in the seat of an open cockpit, that he then crawled out on the wing, and jumped off to his death. His widow brought this action on his life insurance policy.

The details as to the cause of the accident were in dispute. It seems that shortly after the plane left the ground, the deceased became panicky. The pilot becoming aware of this, started to return to earth and when the plane was near the ground the deceased got out of his seat and jumped, sustaining a broken neck. The plane landed, but was wrecked. After this evidence, the federal district court gave judgment for the insurance company, but its decision was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals. This Court held that the condition in the policy excluding double indemnity in case of death resulting from aeronautic operations did not preclude recovery for the death of a casual passenger in an airplane. The Court further held that this death could have resulted either from accidental means or from self-destruction, and therefore remanded the case to the district court to put the question of self-destruction to the jury. The insurance company petitioned the Supreme 

Court to review this decision, but the petition was denied.¹¹ 

Another problem of life insurance and double indemnity involved the operation of a somewhat safer means of transportation, the automobile. Here suit was brought on a double liability life insurance policy alleging that the insured had been accidentally burned to death in his automobile. The evidence showed, however, that both the auto- 

11 No. 172—New York Life Insurance Co. vs. Gits. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. [137]

mobile and the insured were so badly burned that there was nothing to identify the insured, except a bunch of keys, a watch charm, a knife, a pair of cuff links and two dental bridges. In the course of the trial, counsel for the insurance company, thinking that the deceased was not sufficiently identified, intimated that the insured might have absconded so that the insurance could be fraudulently collected. The insurance company, however, did not introduce evidence attacking the good reputation of the insured. Thereupon, the widow of the insured immediately introduced evidence in support of the insured's good reputation. The jury found for the plaintiff. The insurance company assigned as error the trial court's refusal to exclude evidence as to the reputation of the insured, and appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals. That court, however, affirmed the decision of the trial court, and the Supreme Court denied the insurance company's petition to review that decision.¹2

With recent welfare legislation in the various states protecting beneficiaries of insurance policies against claims of creditors of the insured, an interesting constitutional question was raised in the Supreme Court this year. The law of Florida provides that proceeds from life insurance policies shall inure to the surviving spouse of the deceased or to his children, or to the beneficiary named in the policy, and that it shall not be subject to attachment, or garnishment, or any other legal process by creditors, unless the policy declares that it was effected for the benefit of creditors. It so happened in this case that the insured had purchased goods from Tiffany & Company, and after his death his creditors sought to collect the amounts owing to them out of the proceeds of his life insurance policies, which aggregated over $200,000. The decedent's will distributed 

12 No. 862-Fidelity & Insurance Co. vs. Howe. Certiorari denied 

June 2, 1930. [138]

the property and made provision for his widow, but she renounced the will and elected to take her dower instead. 

The question arose how the estate should be distributed. 

The courts of Florida held that the widow was entitled to her dower out of the proceeds of the insurance policies, but that the creditors were not entitled to these proceeds. 

Tiffany & Company then petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, maintaining that the decision amounted to an arbitrary discrimination among creditors, for the widow then was also a creditor of the estate, and that this discrimination constituted a denial of the equal protection of laws, contrary to the Fourteenth Amendment of the Constitution. 

The Supreme Court, however, declined to go into this matter.13

After insurance premiums are paid and the insured dies, the insurance company sometimes seeks to avoid the insurance contract because of misrepresentations contained in the application for insurance. And often difficult questions arise as to whether the specific facts incorrectly stated in the application constitute warranties or merely misrepresentations. These troublesome questions have caused a number of states to adopt laws limiting the defense of misrepresentation in actions brought on insurance policies. For example, the Missouri Misrepresentation Statute provided that no misrepresentation in obtaining a policy of insurance shall be deemed material or render the policy void, unless the matter misrepresented actually contributed to the death, and the question whether it did so contribute should in all cases be one for the jury.

A physician, in his policy for insurance, represented that he had never received treatment in any hospital, sanitarium or asylum, that he had never had any disorders of the heart or blood vessels, or of his respiratory system, or any general 

13 No. 450-Tiffany & Co. vs. Davis. Certiorari denied November 

25, 1929. [139]

disease, or any diseases of joints, bones or spine, and that in the past seven years he had not consulted any physician.

Shortly after obtaining a policy of insurance, the physician died of general heart impairment. Action was brought on the insurance policy. The evidence showed that a few years before the application, the physician had been confined for several weeks in a Kansas City hospital. While he was there, not for any heart trouble, the two attending physicians found evidence of serious impairment of the heart and blood vessels.

The insurance company therefore requested the court to instruct the jury that the innocence of the insured in making the false statements was immaterial, if the fact misrepresented actually contributed to the death; and if the matters which were misrepresented actually contributed to the death, then recovery could not be had on the insurance policy. The federal district court in Missouri, where the action was brought, refused these instructions. Instead, the jury was instructed that no misrepresentation by the insured would void the policy, unless it concerned a matter which actually contributed to the death and at the time of the application the insured either knew such matters to be false or that these matters were reasonably within his personal knowledge. The court also instructed the jury that the statements in the application concerning previous hospital treatment did not void the policy, even if the treatment was for an ailment which actually contributed to the death, unless the applicant knew that he had consulted the physician about that particular ailment. 

Upon these instructions, the jury brought in a verdict for the beneficiary of the insurance policy, judgment was entered on the verdict and the Circuit Court of Appeals affirmed that judgment. The insurance company petitioned the Supreme Court of the United States to review that [140]

decision, arguing that the Circuit Court of Appeals had misapplied and misinterpreted the Missouri statute, but the 

Court declined to go into the matter.¹¹

Also in an accident insurance policy, the insured falsely stated that no life, health or accident company had ever cancelled his insurance or rejected his application or refused to renew or reinstate it. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit held that the misrepresentations were not material as a matter of law, and allowed recovery on the policy.15

A different result obtained in an action brought on an accident insurance policy in the federal district court in Virginia, where the laws of the state do not undertake to protect beneficiaries of insurance policies to the same extent as in Missouri. Here, in the application for an accident insurance policy, the insured misrepresented that he had never received indemnity for accident or sickness, that he had never been disabled and that he had never received medical or surgical attention. An accident occurred, and the insured brought action against the insurance company. 

The company defended on the ground that recovery could not be had, because of the misrepresentations, and the trial court submitted to the jury whether the misrepresentations were material to the policy. The jury held that they were not, and awarded a verdict for the plaintiff. 

On appeal, this was reversed by the Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Fourth Circuit, on the ground that under the law of Virginia the question of the materiality of the misrepresentation was one for the court and not for the jury. 

The Circuit Court of Appeals then found, as a matter of law, that the false statements in the application were mate- 

14 No. 714-Security Life Ins. Co. vs. Brimmer. Certiorari denied 

April 28, 1930. 

15 No. 400-Business Men's Assurance Co. of America vs. Campbell. 

Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [141]

rial and precluded recovery. The insured petitioned the 

Supreme Court to review the case, arguing that the Circuit Court of Appeals had misinterpreted and misapplied the law of Virginia, but the Supreme Court declined to disturb that decision.16

Sometimes it looks as if one may be able to collect on an accident insurance policy, provided one can catch the insurance company. In an action against an insurance company on a $5,000 accident insurance policy, the company had the case removed to the federal district court in Missouri on the ground of diversity of citizenship. Then the company moved to quash the summons on the ground that it was not doing business in Missouri and was therefore not subject to process in that state. Under the law of Missouri, process may be served on a foreign insurance company not licensed to do business in the state, by delivering a copy of the summons to any person who solicits insurance or who adjusts any losses for the company in the state. In this case, process was served on a physician, who, it was alleged, was acting as agent and adjuster for the company in St. Louis. The company denied that the physician was its agent, or that he had ever adjusted any losses in that state, and argued that the process was void.

The evidence showed that the insured had been induced to take out the insurance by a member of the insurance company in Missouri, that he was furnished with a blank application which he filled out and mailed to New York, where it was accepted, and then he received a policy sent to him. from New York. The evidence also showed that the physician on whom the summons was served had made several examinations of the insured, for which he was paid by the company, and that in these examinations he had asked questions which bore no relation to the medical facts or to the 

16 No. 287-Dodd, Abner F. v. Union Indemnity Co. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [142]

medical examination. On previous occasions, it was testified, the physician had advised claimants to accept less than their original claims. The plaintiff in the case, therefore, argued that the physician really acted in the capacity of an adjuster. 

Both the District Court and the Circuit Court of Appeals held that the insurance company was not doing business in Missouri, and was not subject to process in the state. The plaintiff in the case petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.¹

The war risk insurance policies considered by the Court were somewhat similar to other life insurance contracts, except for the differences resulting from the fact that the Government was the insurer. In fact, in two cases the question presented to the Supreme Court was whether, in so far as the Government undertook to insure, it entered into the private business of insurance and therefore divested itself of sovereignty to that extent. In one of these,18 action was brought against the Government, and the District Court held that the Government was obligated to pay installments due on the war risk insurance policy, together with interest an such installments from the time they became due to the time that the judgment was rendered, also interest on the judgment from the time it was rendered until it was paid. 

This was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, which held that the Government was not obliged to pay any interest.

In the other case,19 the District Court likewise allowed interest at 7 per cent. on the judgment obtained by a beneficiary of a veteran's war risk insurance policy, and in addition allowed 10 per cent. for attorney's fees and also interest 

17 No. 850-Rausch, etc. et al. vs. Comm. Trav. Mutual Accident 

Ass'n. Certiorari denied May 26, 1930. 

18 No. 463-Jackson vs. United States. Decided April 14, 1930. 

19 No. 548-United States vs. Worley, Admn'x. Decided April 14, 1930. [143]

on deferred insurance payments which were to be made monthly. In this case, the Government appealed to the 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit, which certified the questions to the Supreme Court of the United States in the following manner: "Whether the United States as a matter of law stands in the position of one who has gone into the business of insurance and must therefore be assumed to have accepted the ordinary incidents of suits in such business; secondly, whether the judgment entered against the 

United States may include amounts of insurance installments maturing after the actions were instituted; thirdly, whether interest was allowable against the United States upon the monthly installments from the date they were found to be due; and fourthly, whether costs may be taxed against the United States on the condition that they be paid from the accumulated funds of the Veteran's Bureau, if any such funds were available for that purpose." 

After these cases were argued, the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Butler, held that the costs and interest were not taxable against the United States, because in the absence of a statute specifically providing for such payments, costs and interest are not taxable against a sovereign. 

In the second case, which was certified by the Circuit Court of Appeals, the veteran enlisted in the army on April 2, 1917, and was discharged on March 18, 1918. He obtained insurance for $10,000, payable in the event of death or total disability at the rate of $57.50 a month. Later he presented a claim for permanent and total disability from the date of his discharge, but this was rejected on December 29, 1926. 

He died on January 7, 1927. On April 23 of that year, his mother, as administratrix, brought this action in the United States District Court in Nebraska to recover on account of his disability from the date of his discharge to the time of [144]

his death, and later she intervened as beneficiary to recover the installments maturing after his death. 

Upon verdict rendered in favor of the plaintiff, judgment was entered in her favor as administratrix for $6,095 and as beneficiary for $3,905. Later, the District Court entered a supplemental judgment dividing the $3,905 into two parts-$1,265 on account of the installments falling due before the judgment, and the balance, $2,640, to be paid at the rate of $57.50 per month, commencing November 1, 1928. We have already stated that interest and costs were taxed against the Government and that this was held to be erroneous.

The Supreme Court held further that the District Court erred in giving judgment for the installments which had not yet become due, for under the law it is provided that if the designated beneficiary does not survive the insured, or dies prior to receiving all of the 240 installments, the value of the monthly installments thereafter payable shall be paid to the estate of the insured. "A judgment for the designated beneficiary for all installments thereafter to mature did not protect the United States against a claim by the estate of the insured for any installments falling due after the death of the beneficiary."

The difference between war risk insurance and ordinary life insurance was also brought out in a case in which the issue was one of fact. This was an action brought to reinstate a veteran's insurance policy which had lapsed, the Veterans' Bureau having refused to reinstate the policy, because at the time of the veteran's application he was permanently and totally disabled through pulmonary tuberculosis. It appeared that the veteran had a policy in the amount of $10,000, insuring him against death and permanent or total disability, while serving in the army during the world war. On February 1, 1920, he converted $3,000 [145]

of this insurance into a twenty-payment life policy and allowed the remaining $7,000 to lapse. On February 21, 1923, he made application for reinstatement and for the conversion of the lapsed $7,000.

At the time of this application he was suffering from tuberculosis which he claimed was of a degree less than permanent disability. The Veterans' Bureau, however, denied this application for reinstatement, on the ground that at the time he was permanently and totally disabled. The veteran then brought this action in the federal district court in Colorado to compel the reinstatement of the policy and for payment of installments of $40.25 a month, beginning with August 3, 1923, from which time he claimed he had become permanently and totally disabled. 

The District Court granted the relief sought, on the ground that the findings of the Director of the Veterans' 

Bureau were erroneous and were wholly unsupported by evidence. On appeal, however, the Circuit Court of Appeals reversed the decision, holding that the trial court had no jurisdiction in the matter at all, because the point in controversy was purely one of fact, upon which the findings of the Veterans' Bureau were final and conclusive. This was the view finally adopted by the Supreme Court of the United 

States, in an opinion delivered by Mr. Justice Sutherland.20

The laws relating to war risk insurance policies also contain provisions limiting the rights of creditors. But in a case which came from South Carolina, the Supreme Court of the state held that the insurance fund was subject to claims of creditors under the circumstances therein involved. 

In this case, a soldier had borrowed money with which to buy real estate, and gave his creditor a mortgage on the property as security. After the soldier's death, the mortgaged premises were sold, but the mortgage note was not 

20 No. 269-Meadows vs. United States. Decided April 14, 1930. 

[146]

fully satisfied, and the creditor obtained a judgment for the balance against the soldier's estate. The veteran's mother, who was his beneficiary, collected several installments of the war risk insurance, then she died, and the balance of the insurance was paid to the administratrix of the veteran's estate. The creditor of the veteran tried to collect his judgment from the estate, but both the probate court and the circuit court in South Carolina held that such funds were not subject to the debts of the veteran. The Supreme Court of the state, however, reversed that decision. 

This case raised a further question as to assignability of war risk insurance. A brother of the soldier likewise was in debt. He executed and delivered to his creditor an assignment of his interest in the veteran's war risk insurance. The administratrix of the estate likewise refused this payment, and the Supreme Court of South Carolina reversing the judgment of the lower court, held that the brother of the veteran could assign his share in the fund in the same manner as any other personal property. The administratrix of the veteran petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.21 

Fire Insurance. 

The next type of insurance contract which involved the largest number of controversies was that of fire insurance. 

These cases dealt mainly with the construction of the insurance contracts. In one case, a sugar house was insured and the owner warranted that he would operate the business during the grinding season. After a fire occurred and action was brought on the policy, the question arose what was meant by the words "grinding season." The trial court submitted that question to the jury, which found that the in- 

21 No. 100-Jones vs. Whaley. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. 

[147]

sured complied with the conditions of the policy. The insurance company appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals, maintaining that the words were so clear that the court should have passed on the question without submitting it to the jury, but the Circuit Court of Appeals upheld the decision of the trial court, and the Supreme Court declined to go into the matter.22

In another case, the question arose whether the foundation walls of a building constituted part of the building, in determining "total loss" within the meaning of the fire insurance policy. The trial court instructed the jury that the foundation walls of the building should not be taken into consideration in determining total loss, since the foundation walls were not within the contemplation of the parties to the insurance contract. Here the Circuit Court of Appeals held that the instruction was erroneous, but that it was harmless. The insurance company, however, petitioned the 

Supreme Court to review the case, and this petition was likewise denied.23

Liability Insurance. 

Automobile liability insurance likewise figured in several cases. In one of these the question arose as to the constitutionality of a rule adopted by the Supreme Court of Nebraska permitting attorneys for the plaintiff to inquire of the defendants, on cross-examination, whether or not they carried liability insurance. This case arose out of an automobile accident where the defendant was asked such question and the insurance company, one of the appellants in the case, maintained that its rights were prejudiced by the rule 

22 No. 380-North River Insurance Co. vs. Becnel. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. 

23 No. 471-St. Paul Fire & Marine Ins. Co. vs. Eldracher. Cer tiorari denied December 2, 1929. [148]

permitting the disclosure before a jury that the defendant was insured. The appeal to the Supreme Court of the 

United States, however, was dismissed for lack of a substantial federal question.24

An interesting question of automobile liability insurance came from Ohio where the age limit for the operation of motor vehicles is not regulated by statute, but by municipal ordinances. Action was brought for indemnity on a liability insurance policy, which provided that it should not cover any liability of the insured, if an accident occurred while the automobile was being operated by any person under the age fixed by law, or under the age of sixteen.

The insured was a resident of Cleveland. One of his employees, aged seventeen, was operating his automobile in a neighboring town and an accident occurred. The injured person sued the insured and recovered damages. The insurance company refused to indemnify the insured, claiming that there was no liability on its part because a municipal ordinance of the town, where the accident occurred, made it unlawful for the owner of any motor vehicle to permit any minor under the age of eighteen to operate such vehicle. 

The insured sued the insurance company in the federal district court in Cleveland, which held that the phrase "fixed by law" in the policy meant the age fixed by state statute. 

Since the insurance contract was drawn by the insurance company, it was to be construed favorably to the insured. 

This decision was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit.

The insurance company brought the case to the Supreme Court, maintaining that the construction put on the policy by the lower court was erroneous. It also argued that since there was no state statute fixing the age limit for operating automobiles and the state expressly conferred this power 

24 No. 90-Harrington vs. Sloan. Decided October 28, 1929. 

[149]

upon municipalities, the age limits set by the municipal ordinances constituted the age limits "fixed by law." This view was sustained by the Supreme Court in an opinion by Justice Sanford, in which he held that the clause in the insurance policy used the term "law" in a generic sense as meaning "the rules of action or conduct duly prescribed by controlling authority, and having binding legal force; including valid municipal ordinances as well as statutes."25 

An interesting case came to the Court this year, which did not deal directly with liability insurance, but indirectly presented a problem with which liability insurance companies are much concerned. To them one of the most distressing types of cases is where action is brought against the driver of an automobile by a friend who was injured while riding in his car. If the friend alleges negligence, and the driver practically admits it, then he must pay damages and becomes entitled to indemnity from the insurance company. 

In this case, an action for personal injuries was brought by a wife against her husband, alleging that she was injured in an automobile accident while riding in her husband's car in Connecticut. Under the law of that state, an invited guest in an automobile may not recover damages for injuries sustained on account of the negligence of the driver, unless such accident is intentional on the part of the owner or operator of the vehicle, or is caused by his heedlessness or his reckless disregard of the rights of others. When the wife brought action against her husband, she maintained that this statute was contrary to the equal protection clause in the Fourteenth Amendment to the Constitution, because while the law permits an invited guest generally to recover for the negligence of the host, an arbitrary and unreasonable dis- 

25 No. 179-United States Fidelity & Guaranty Co. vs. Guenther. 

Decided February 24, 1930. [150]

crimination is made against guests, who are injured through the host's negligence while operating an automobile. 1126 The statute was held constitutional by the Supreme Court of Connecticut with some of the judges dissenting, and the case was appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. This Court likewise upheld the validity of the statute on the ground that "the use of the automobile as an instrument of transportation is peculiarly the subject of regulation. The Court took judicial notice of the fact that there is an increasing frequency of litigation in which passengers carried gratuitously in automobiles, often casual guests or licensees, bring actions to recover large sums of money for injuries alleged to have been due to negligent operation. In view of this and other facts, the Court held that the legislature may use its discretion in regulating such matters. "In this day of almost universal highway transportation by motor car, we cannot say that abuses originating in the multiplicity of suits growing out of the gratuitous carriage of passengers in automobiles do not present so conspicuous an example of what the legislature may regard as an evil, as to justify legislation aimed at it, even though some abuses may not be hit. *It is enough that the present statute strikes at the evil where it is felt and reaches the class of cases where it most frequently occurs." 

Burglary Insurance.

Of the cases involving burglary insurance, the most interesting was one in which the insurance company defended that the alleged burglary was only a simulation and a fraud. 

The case was tried four times. Three times the juries disagreed and on the fourth time they found for the plaintiff. 

26 No. 24-Mae Silver vs. Benj. Silver. Decided November 25, 1929. [151]

The testimony was conflicting. Several witnesses, including an officer of the Safe and Lock Squad of the New York City Police, testified that the burglary was actual and not simulated, and a lock expert was also of the opinion that this was not an inside job. The insurance company introduced evidence to the contrary.

When the jury found for the plaintiff, the defendant company appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals, and that court reversed the judgment which was rendered on the verdict. The plaintiff then petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case. He maintained that the Circuit Court of 

Appeals erred in reversing the judgment, because it had no jurisdiction to review the facts on appeal in an ordinary action at law, which was tried by a jury, and furthermore, since the evidence was conflicting, it was not within the province of the Circuit Court of Appeals to reverse the judgment of the trial court, in the absence of a certificate that the bill of exceptions contained all the evidence which was adduced before the trial court. The Supreme Court, however, declined to go into the matter.2

Marine Insurance. 

27 Finally, we come to the interpretation of a marine insurance contract, which was in the form of an insurance clause in a repair contract between the United States and a ship building company. In this case, the Government brought suit to recover damages caused by a fire to the vessel AMERICA, and the defense was that the insurance provision in the contract absolved the ship building company from liability. The following were the facts: 

27 No. 616-Leidesdorf vs. Union Indemnity Co. Certiorari denied 

March 12, 1930. [152]

The Government advertised for bids to repair the steamship AMERICA, and the Newport News Shipbuilding and

Drydock Company submitted the lowest bid. All the bids were rejected. In the specifications for that bid it was provided that the contractor was to insure the hull of the vessel, in the amount of $2,000,000, while it was undergoing repairs. The cost of such insurance was $5,000. In the specifications for the second bid, that provision was modified so that the Government was to continue the insurance it had upon the vessel, but the contractor was to place builders' risk at its own expense to cover the amount of the contract and Government material removed from the vessel or placed in storage at the contractor's plant. 

The Newport News Shipbuilding and Drydock Company was again the lowest bidder, and the amount of the bid was about $100,000 less than the first bid. The repair contract was then entered into and some of the provisions of the contract related to the insurance carried by the Government on its own vessels and to the precautionary measures against fire to be adopted by the company. 

While the vessel was undergoing repairs (the repairs having been 99½ per cent. complete), a fire broke out on the vessel, and the amount of the damage was over $2,000,000. 

This fire was caused by a lighted open flame furnace, used by a plumber in one of the staterooms.

The Government filed a libel against the company to recover the amount of the damage. Two main questions arose in the course of the suit: (1) Was the burden of proof on the Government to show that the damage was caused through the negligence of the shipbuilding company, and (2) assuming that the shipbuilding company was negligent, was it absolved from liability by the insurance carried by the [153]

Government with the United States Lines covering the hull, machinery and equipment of the vessel. 

The Government maintained that since the company contracted to repair the vessel and return it in a state of repair, the burden was upon the contractor to show that it had exercised due care in the performance of the contract. 

The company, on the other hand, maintained that since its liability depended on negligence, it was the Government's burden to prove that the damage was caused through the negligence of the company. On this question, the District 

Court in Norfolk held in favor of the Government, and on appeal, this point was sustained by the Circuit Court of Appeals.

On the second question, whether the insurance carried by the Government absolved the company from liability, the District Court also held in favor of the Government, but was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals. The court held that the provision in the contract, that the Government should carry insurance, at the same time imposing duties of care on the shipbuilding company, constituted an ambiguity which must be explained by the evidence concerning the negotiations and purposes relating to the question of insurance. The Circuit Court of Appeals, after reviewing such evidence, came to the conclusion that the purpose of absolving the company from carrying insurance. on the vessel was to absolve it from such liability; that the 

Government undertook to insure its own vessel, and therefore must look to its own insurer for indemnity for the damage to the vessel; the fact that the Government insured the vessel through its own system of insurance, namely, through the "United States Lines," the court held, did not affect the situation.

This was the opinion of two of the judges of the Circuit Court of Appeals, but the third, Judge Parker, dissented [154]

from this view. He took the position that in accordance with the provision of the contract, the shipbuilding company was liable for negligence in failing to comply with the fire precautions clause of the contract. Had the Government insured its vessel through an insurance company, the dissenting opinion held, the Government would have been entitled to the proceeds of the insurance, and the insurance company would have been entitled to recover the amount of the damage from the shipbuilding company; therefore, the fact that the Government was its own insurer simply meant that the shipbuilding company was liable directly to the Government.

The Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, but its petition was denied.28 

28 No. 431-United States vs. Newport News Shipbuilding & Drydock 

Co. Certiorari denied November 4, 1929. [155]

CHAPTER VI. 

Banks and Banking

The most important banking decision of the year concerned the power of the Federal Farm Loan Board to assess stockholders' liability upon insolvency of Joint Stock Land 

Banks. The specific question presented was whether the receiver of a joint stock land bank had powers similar to those of a receiver appointed by the Comptroller of the Currency, in assessing double liability on stockholders of insolvent national banks. The Supreme Court answered that question in the negative.¹ 

In the case of J. R. Wheeler vs. Howard Greene, Receiver of the Bankers Joint Stock Land Bank of Milwaukee, the Federal Farm Loan Board had appointed a receiver to take charge of the assets of the bank. After an investigation the Board ordered an assessment of 100 per cent. on the shares of the stockholders. One of the stockholders refused to pay the assessment, claiming that the receiver had no power to enforce it, and the receiver sued the stockholder for the amount assessed. The United States District Court in Wisconsin sustained the stockholder's contention for the reason that this extraordinary power was not expressly conferred upon the receiver by the Federal Farm Loan Act.2

That decision was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit, which held that the receiver derived such power, by implication, from the general powers conferred upon the Board. 

1 No. 39-Wheeler vs. Green, Receiver. Decided November 4, 1929. 

2 39 Stat. 374; Sec. 812, Title 12, U. S. C. 

[156]

When the case came to the Supreme Court, the stockholder argued that although the Federal Farm Loan Act was modeled after the National Banking Act, it specifically omitted any reference to the power of the receiver to enforce such assessments in an action at law, and that an action to recover the stockholder's liability can be brought only by means of a creditor's bill in equity. The receiver argued in opposition that the Federal Farm Loan Act gave the Board wide general powers, analogous to those of the Comptroller of the Currency under the National Bank Act, and that the construction of the Act, urged by the stockholder, would only involve duplication of receiverships together with increased expenses and delay.

The Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Holmes, held that the situation was not analogous and sustained the contentions of the stockholder. He reasoned that under the 

National Bank Act, the Comptroller of the Currency is given such wide powers, because national banks issue notes which constitute an important part of the currency of the country; upon a bank's refusal to pay these notes, the Comptroller of the Currency appoints a receiver, and the authority to enforce stockholder's liability adds security to the national circulation. Joint Stock Land Banks, however, do not issue notes. They are created merely for the purpose of making loans on farm mortgages to members of an association, within a limited territory, and are relatively local in character. The bonds issued by such banks are required to be secured by mortgages, therefore in case of insolvency there is not the same immediate need to dispose of stockholder's liability in a summary manner, rather than by the usual proceeding, a bill in equity.. For in the usual proceeding the stockholder can be heard, and instead of his 

3 R. S. 5234; Sec. 192, Title 12, U. S. C. 

[157]

assessment being 100 per cent. as a matter of course, it can be adjusted to the specific case in which the stockholders are held equally and ratably liable. 

Stockholder's Liability in National Banks. 

This brings us to two cases which involved various phases of the provision for stockholders' liability under the National Bank Act. In the first case, a receiver was appointed for a national bank, who assessed the stockholders' liability upon one who was no longer a stockholder of record when the receiver was appointed, but held the stock as trustee for his minor children. The district court, in which the receiver brought suit to enforce the stockholder's liabil· ity, held that when the stockholder transferred the stock to himself as trustee, this relieved him of stockholder's liability. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit affirmed this decision. The receiver petitioned the Supreme 

Court to review that decision, but his petition was denied.* 

In the second case, shares of stock in a national bank were bought from the registered holder of the shares. The purchaser received a certificate endorsed in blank by the registered holder. At this time, the purchaser had no knowledge of the failing condition of the bank, and he purchased the shares solely for the purpose of giving the stock to his minor children. After having received the certificates endorsed in blank, he delivered them to the bank with verbal instructions to register the stock and issue new certificates in the names of his minor children. This was done, and the new certificates were retained in the custody of the bank. It was agreed by the parties concerned that all this was done in good faith and without intent to avoid the stockholders' liability. 

4 No. 162-McNair vs. Darragh. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. 

[158]

The bank became insolvent, and the Comptroller of the Currency appointed a receiver, who assessed the double liability on the stockholders, including the father of the minors. The federal district court, where the receiver brought action to recover the stockholder's liability, held that the stockholder was not liable. The receiver appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals, which certified the question to the Supreme Court, and this Court held the father liable." 

The Court held that neither the intent of the purchaser to buy the stock for his minor children, nor the fact that by his direction the transfer was made to them upon the books of the bank and certificates issued in their names, relieved him from liability. The transferees, the Court held, being minors, were without legal capacity to assume the obligation; upon coming of age they would have an election either to affirm or to avoid the entire transaction, and in the meantime, the transfer of the stock having resulted to their disadvantage, the law will avoid it for them, thus leaving the liability of their father unaffected. In this case, it was also argued that the father might be considered as having held the stock as trustee for the minors, but the Supreme Court regarded the argument without merit. Since he purchased the stock with his own money, he became the owner of it, and when he transferred the same to his minor children, that was an ordinary gift and did not create a trust. 

The question of stockholder's liability also arose with reference to state banks. One case involved the validity of the banking law of North Carolina. Under this law, when a state bank becomes insolvent and the Corporation Commission takes over the control of the bank for liquidation, the Commission may levy an assessment on the shares of stock, file a copy of the levies in the office of the clerk of the 

No. 133-Early, Receiver vs. Richardson. Decided February 24, 1930, [159]

superior court, and have it recorded and indexed as a judgment. If it is not paid, then execution may be issued against the stockholder at the instance of the Corporation Commission. The stockholder, however, may appeal to the superior court from the levy of the assessment and raise all the questions which he might otherwise have raised against the assessment itself.

A stockholder of the Farmers Bank & Trust Company of Wallace, N. C., which became insolvent and was taken over by the Corporation Commission, maintained that this law was unconstitutional, since no notice was given to him before the assessment was entered as a judgment against him. The stockholder further maintained that a judgment can be issued only by a court after a trial and that although the statute provides for an appeal, in which the issues concerning the validity of the assessment may be raised, the statute is still invalid because no appeal can be taken except from a judgment. He also argued that the law was unconstitutional because by giving the assessment the force of a judgment, the legislature invaded the judicial power of the state.

The courts of North Carolina sustained the validity of the Act, and the stockholder appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. Here the Court affirmed the judgment of the North Carolina courts without an opinion, on the basis of its previous decision in the case of Coffin 

Brothers & Co. vs. Bennett," in which a similar question had been raised with reference to the validity of the Georgia Banking Law, having a provision somewhat similar to that of the North Carolina statute.

The Georgia Banking Law, however, also came up for consideration during this term. Here, a stockholder ac- 

No. 184 Murphey vs. Corporation Commission of North Carolina. 

Decided January 20, 1930. 

7 277 U. S. 29. [160]

quired shares of stock in the Citizens Bank of Waynesboro,

Ga. This occurred prior to 1919. The statutes of Georgia at that time provided that if a bank examiner found the capital stock of a bank impaired or reduced, he should require the bank to make good the impairment by transfer from surplus or undivided profits, then by an assessment upon the stockholders. There was no provision of law for levying such assessments. They were purely voluntary, and the penalty for failure to restore the impairment of the capital was the forfeiture of the bank's charter. 

In 1919, Georgia amended this Act by providing that when the Superintendent of Banks determines that the capital stock of a bank has been impaired as much as 10 per cent., he should require a transfer from the surplus or undivided profits to restore the impairment; if these are insufficient to restore the capital, the superintendent is required. by law to notify the bank to make good the impairment within sixty days by an assessment on the stockholders. The law further provided that the directors and officers of the bank, upon receipt of this notice, should call a special meeting of the stockholders for the assessment of amounts sufficient to cover the impairment of the capital. The law further provided that the bank could sell so much of the stock of any stockholder at no less than par, as would pay the assessment levied upon him, or the bank might sue the stockholder to recover the amount of the assessment. 

The stockholder was a resident of Florida, and when the Superintendent of Banks notified the bank that it shall restore the capital through an assessment, the directors sent a notice of a special meeting to him, as they did to the other stockholders. It does not appear from the record whether the stockholder received the notice. He did not appear at the meeting, and the bank then filed action against him to recover the amount of the assessment. He defended that [161]

the statute, if applied to him as a purchaser of stock prior to the 1919 amendment of the Georgia Bank Act, must be held unconstitutional, as being without due process of law. 

He also maintained that the statute was unconstitutional because it did not provide specifically for any form of notice to stockholders of the special meeting required to be held to levy the assessment. The Georgia courts sustained the validity of the Act, and the Supreme Court of the United States affirmed the decision. The Supreme Court, however, did not pass on the validity of the Act itself, but affirmed the decision on the ground that the appellant did not sustain the burden of proof; first, as to whether the notice was actually not received by him; second, that the notice which was sent was not a reasonable one on the face of the record. The Court did not go into the question of the retroactive application of the 1919 amendment to those who purchased the shares of stock before its enactment, apparently regarding that point as being without merit.

Liability of Bank Officers. 

Stockholders are not alone in their discomfiture when a bank becomes insolvent. Officers and directors of banks often share their troubles when criminal and civil prosecutions are instituted against them for the breach of their trust. 

This year several interesting cases came up with reference to the criminal liability of bank officers. In only two cases, however, has the Supreme Court gone into the merits of the controversies, and these did not involve so much a question of guilt, as a question of criminal procedure.

Two prosecutions were brought against an officer of the 

Union Bank & Trust Company of Batesville, Ark., a mem- 

8 No. 485-Toombs vs. Citizens Bank of Waynesboro. Decided May 

26, 1930. [162]

ber of the Federal Reserve System, charging him with having made a false entry in a certain book of the bank, in violation of the Federal Reserve Act. His defense was that he had already been tried and acquitted under an indictment which charged him with having made the same false entry in a different book of the bank. The federal district court in Arkansas, where the prosecution was brought, sustained the plea of double jeopardy and held that there could be but one prosecution for false entries based upon a single draft or check passing through a bank, even though several entries were made in the various books of the bank. 

From this decision the Government appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States, and the decision was affirmed. The Court, in an opinion by Justice Holmes, held that the statute did not contemplate "that the mere multiplication of entries, all to the same point and with a single intent, should multiply the punishment in proportion to the complexity of the bookkeeping," and that having been prosecuted for having made a false entry with reference to the same item, the defendant cannot be prosecuted again. 

In the second case, the same defendant was indicted for having made a false entry in a report concerning the condition of the bank, showing that there were due from banks, other than federal reserve banks, the sum of $138,000 instead of the true sum of $91,000. Again the defendant interposed a plea of double jeopardy, because he had been previously prosecuted for having made these false statements in the books of the bank, and on which charge he had been acquitted.

In this case, the lower court also sustained the defense and quashed the indictment, but on appeal the Supreme 

Court reversed that decision.10 Justice Holmes held that 

9 No. 281-United States vs. Adams. 

10 No. 282-United States vs. Adams. 

Decided April 14, 1930. 

Decided April 14, 1930. [163]

the plea should have been overruled, because the former prosecution charged the defendant with having made a false entry concerning the condition of the bank with intent to defraud the bank or the examiners, whereas in this case he was charged merely with having made a false entry. He might have been acquitted in the previous case, even if the entry were false, but on the ground that the intent to defraud was lacking, whereas in this case the essence of the offense was merely the false entry in the report.

All other cases in which bank officers were prosecuted for violations of law were not reviewed by the Supreme Court. The president of a bank in West Virginia fared rather badly when he overdrew his account with his own bank. He was prosecuted for embezzlement, was convicted, and sentenced to imprisonment for ten years. The evidence showed that the president had overdrawn his account by only $2,800, deposited that check with another bank where he had an account, and subsequently drew a check on a third bank in favor of the first in order to make good his overdraft. In the meantime, his bank failed and the check had not been paid. The evidence also showed that it was customary for depositors in the bank to overdraw their accounts by small amounts without the bank taking exception to such overdrafts. 

In the prosecution of the president for having embezzled the sum of $2,800, the defendant maintained that there was not sufficient evidence to support the charge of embezzlement and he moved for a directed verdict. This motion was overruled, he was found guilty and was sentenced. The 

Supreme Court of West Virginia sustained the judgment of the lower state court. The president of the bank petitioned the Supreme Court of the United States to review that decision, arguing that he had been deprived of due process because the motion for the directed verdict was made, [164]

argued and decided without the presence of the defendant or of the jury. As the case was presented, there was no substantial federal question involved, and the petition was denied.¹¹

In one case, a cashier of a national bank was charged with having made a false entry in a report to the Comptroller of the Currency, in violation of law. He was prosecuted by the United States, and he maintained that the indictment was fatally defective because it failed to state specifically that the report pertained to a federal reserve bank and did not show wherein those entries were false. 

He also argued that since the report was made in the presence of an examiner, this should have been considered as a mitigating circumstance, whereas the trial court instructed the jury that the presence of the examiner was not relevant to the consideration of the question whether the cashier unlawfully made a false entry.

The cashier was convicted on March 31, 1925, and the case was continued for sentence to June 7, 1926. Then, without further assignments, the case was continued from term to term until May 26, 1928, when he was finally sentenced. After appeal to the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, where the judgment of the district court was affirmed, the defendant petitioned the Supreme Court to review his case, mainly on the question whether the court could continue the case for sentence for several terms. This petition, however, was likewise denied.12 

In two cases, persons not connected with a bank were prosecuted for aiding and abetting an employee of a bank, a member of the federal reserve system, in unlawfully abstracting bank funds. The evidence showed that these two men had been engaged in placing bets on horseraces. 

11 No. 871-Frantz vs. State of West Virginia. Certiorari denied 

June 2, 1930. 

12 No. 576-Caldwell vs. U. S. Certiorari denied March 3, 1930. 

[165]

They were in contact with the employee of the bank, who apparently desired to place bets with them. The employee was less than 23 years of age, he received a salary from the bank of about $30 per week, but within four months he turned over to the defendants about $50,000 in money and a number of checks. The evidence further showed that during this period the employee had lost about $250,000. 

About $100,000 of this represented winnings, and the balance represented misapplied funds of the bank. All this money was turned over to the defendants either in the bank or on various street corners.

The two defendants were sentenced to serve five years and one of them was in addition fined the sum of $2,000. 

They petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit, upholding the conviction and sentence, on the ground that the indictment charged them with aiding and abetting in the abstracting of funds of a member bank, but did not specify that it was a federal reserve bank. On this the lower courts held that the statute making such acts an offense applied to any member bank, whether it was a state bank or a national bank.13

The civil liability of bank directors came up in only one case during this term. It was in a suit brought by stockholders of the Northwestern National Bank of Portland,

Oregon, to recover damages for having permitted the officers of the bank to cause its insolvency. In 1922 this bank had deposits aggregating $29,000,000. By 1927 it was sold to the First National Bank and to the U. S. National 

Bank of Portland, Ore., because it could not continue to do business. In its complaint, the stockholders alleged that the officers, employees and agents of the bank had misapplied 

13 No. 105-Morgan vs. United States; No. 106-Hust vs. United 

States. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. [166]

the funds with the knowledge and assent of the directors, and notably, that the president of the bank had been convicted and sentenced to imprisonment for ten years for having misapplied $1,000,000 of the bank's money. The evidence further showed that there were repeated warnings by the Comptroller of the Currency that the bank was making excessive loans and that its capital was being impaired. 

The Circuit Court of Appeals ruled against the stockholders, holding that the plaintiffs failed to show that these instances of misconduct on the part of the officers had been made with the actual assent of the directors. The stockholders, on the other hand, contended that the evidence clearly pointed to the conclusion that the misapplication of the funds were made with the knowledge of the directors, and that the assent would be inferred. They petitioned the 

Supreme Court to define the duties and obligations of directors of the national banks under such circumstances, but the Supreme Court declined to go into the matter.¹ 

Commercial Paper. 14 The insolvency of a bank often has its effect on the rights and obligations of persons who deal in commercial paper through such bank. This was the largest class of banking cases which came before the Supreme Court during this term. In one of these the question presented was whether a clearing house had a right to use the deposit balance of a bank to pay off checks which came in after the bank closed its doors. At the time the Farmers & Merchants National 

Bank of Lake City, S. C., closed its doors, it had on deposit a balance of $22,000 with the Federal Reserve Bank of Richmond. The receiver appointed by the Comptroller of the Currency demanded the amount in order to make a 

14 No. 841-Burckhardt vs. Northwestern National Bank. Certiorari denied June 2, 1930. [167]

ratable distribution of the bank's funds among creditors, in accordance with the provisions of Sections 5234 and 5236 of the Revised Statutes. The Federal Reserve Bank, however, insisted that it had a right to apply the deposit balance for the benefit and protection of depositing banks which had used its clearing house system to collect checks on the failed bank. The receiver brought this action against the 

Reserve Bank, and the district court held that the bank was under obligation to turn over the deposits to the receiver of the failed bank. This was reversed by the Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Fourth Circuit. 

The receiver then brought the case to the Supreme Court of the United States. He argued that the decision of the lower court amounted to giving a preferred claim to banks using the Federal Reserve Clearing House System as against the other creditors of the insolvent bank. The Federal Reserve Bank argued, in opposition, that the clearing house system was put into effect for the purpose of facilitating the presentation of checks for collection; that in order to make this system workable, it was necessary to make available at the bank a sufficient amount of money to meet the checks which go through the system at any given time, with agreements that the amount of deposits shall be used for that purpose, and that therefore the collection items sent to the Federal Reserve Bank should be honored, though the bank on which the checks were drawn had become insolvent. 

In an opinion by Justice Holmes, the Supreme Court upheld the contentions of the Federal Reserve Bank.15 In holding that the Reserve Bank had a right to apply the deposit balance, the Supreme Court stressed the agreements which constituted the basis of the transactions between the two banks. The Federal Reserve Bank had issued the fol- 

15 No. 226-Early vs. Fed. Res. Bank of Richmond. Decided March 

12, 1930. [168]

lowing circular: "Checks received by us drawn on our member banks will be forwarded in cash letters direct to such banks, and each member bank will be required either to remit therefor immediately available funds or to provide funds available to us to meet such cash letters within the agreed transit time to and from the member bank. Therefore, the amount of any cash letter to a member bank is chargeable to available funds in the reserve account of such member at the expiration of such transit time, which charge will be shown on each cash letter. The right is reserved, however, to charge a cash letter to the reserve account of the member bank at any time or in any particular case we deem it necessary to do so." 

In this case, the transit time or time allowed for collection was three days, and this time had not expired when the South Carolina bank closed its doors. The Court therefore held that the right of the South Carolina bank to draw against its reserve account was subject to the Richmond bank's lien on the deposit. 

The failure of the Fourth National Bank of Macon, Ga., gave rise to several cases which involved somewhat analogous problems. Here the Fourth National Bank had a deposit with the Federal Reserve Bank at Atlanta. When the Fourth National closed its doors, the question arose whether the Federal Reserve Bank should pay the deposit balance to the receiver of the Fourth National, or to the 

Farmers National Bank of Monticello and others who had drawn checks on the Fourth National, which checks reached the Federal Reserve Bank on the day when the Fourth 

National closed its doors.

In this case, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit held that in accordance with the agreement between the Fourth National Bank and the drawer banks, the latter's checks drawn on the Fourth National and sent to the Fed- [169]

eral Reserve should be immediately credited by the Federal Reserve to the drawers' accounts and immediately charged to the reserve account of the Fourth National Bank. This constituted an equitable assignment which entitled the drawers to the fund in the hands of the Federal Reserve Bank. Here the receiver petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, but the petition was denied.16 

On the other hand, in a somewhat different case, the same Circuit Court of Appeals held in favor of the receiver of the Fourth National Bank. In this case, a corporation sent several drafts, with bills of lading attached, to the Fourth National Bank for collection. Each of the drafts contained the following: "This draft is a cash item and is not to be treated as a deposit.
