Funds obtained through its collection are to be accounted for to the drawer and are not to be comingled with the other funds of the collecting bank." But that was not the procedure followed by the 

Fourth National Bank. After collecting on the drafts, the bank undertook to remit to the company its cashier's check drawn against an account with the Guarantee Trust Company of New York. Before the check was presented for payment, the Fourth National became insolvent and the 

Guarantee Trust Company of New York refused payment, because of insolvency.

The drawee of the drafts had paid the same to the Fourth National Bank, by its check drawn on the Fourth National itself, the drawee had a balance at the bank in excess of the amount of the check, and the bank had in its vaults at that time several hundred thousand dollars. The corporation which presented the draft for collection then made a claim against the receiver of the Fourth National Bank, claiming that it was entitled to a preference, on the theory that the 

16 No. 622-Pearson vs. Farmers National Bank of Monticello. Cer tiorari denied March 17, 1930. [170]

Fourth National, acting as a collecting agent for the corporation, came into possession of a trust fund belonging to it. In this case, the Circuit Court of Appeals held that the corporation was not entitled to a preferred claim, because the proceeds of the draft became mingled with the general mass of unidentified bank funds, although that was done contrary to the instructions in the drafts. The Court therefore held that the corporation could not come in as a preferred creditor, but had to share in the insolvent estate with other general creditors.¹7

Still another case arose out of the same insolvency, in which the Court held against the receiver. In this case, a partnership drew a draft on X and sent it to the Fourth National Bank of Macon for collection. On the next day, X gave his check drawn on the Citizens & Southern National Bank of Macon, Ga., in payment of the draft. On the 26th of November, the Fourth National deposited the check in the Macon Clearing House, and would have received credit for it, except for the fact that it failed on that day, and because of this failure the Fourth National did not make remittance to the partnership, the drawer of the draft. 

In the final settlement of accounts between the Macon Clearing House and the Citizens & Southern, there was a balance of about $29,000 in favor of the Macon bank, and the Citizens & Southern bank paid the amount to the receiver. The partnership then brought this suit to recover the amount of the draft, on the theory that the proceeds of the draft were traceable into the balance which the Citizens & Southern paid to the receiver, and that the receiver came into possession of an identified fund which belonged to the plaintiff, the partnership. This was the view adopted by the 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit. This time 17 No. 685-Early & Daniel Company vs. Pearson, Rec. Certiorari denied April 14, 1930. [171]

the receiver petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, but the petition was likewise denied.18 

When a bank fails, there are many accidental facts which enter into determining whether one person or another is entitled to the money. Rights of parties may depend on when a certain letter is received or when the envelope containing the letter is opened. This happened in the case of a railroad company which had accounts in two banks, the Citizens & Southern National Bank of Savannah and the First National Bank of Dublin, Ga., and the latter had an account with the former. The railroad asked the Dublin bank to send $4,000 to the Citizens and Southern National Bank for its, the railroad's, account. This request was made on September 19.

On September 21, the Dublin bank mailed its check to the Citizens bank, but the envelope, though received earlier, was not opened until after 9:00 A. M. on September 24.

In the meantime, the Dublin bank became insolvent, and a receiver was appointed by the Comptroller of the Currency with instructions to do no business, pay out no money, keep assets intact, etc. Before the envelope containing the check was opened by the Citizens bank, it was notified of the appointment of the receiver. Had the Citizens and Southern National Bank been more prompt in opening its mail, it could have credited the check to the account of the railroad company. As it was, a question arose whether the receiver of the Dublin bank or the railroad company was entitled to the amount of the check. This question was decided in favor of the receiver. The railroad company argued that it was entitled to the money, because in the instructions telegraphed by the Comptroller of the Currency to the receiver, there was no instruction to stop pay- 

18 No. 738-Pearson, Rec. vs. Sumney & Tolson. Certiorari denied 

April 28, 1930. [172]

ment on checks, and on this point it petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision of the lower court. The petition, however, was denied.19 

Regulation of Banking. 

The statutes passed by the various states regulating the banking business always yield a number of cases questioning the validity of such laws. This year the Small Loan Act of 

Georgia was attacked as being contrary to the due process clause of the Constitution. This Act forbids any person to engage in the business of making any loans of money or credit in an amount less than $300 and charge more than 8 per cent. interest, without first obtaining a license from the State Bank Examiner, except as authorized by the Act. 

Violation of this provision is punishable as a misdemeanor. 

The appellant in this case was indicted on two counts, charging him with violation of this provision of the Georgia law. He was arrested on a bench warrant, and he brought habeas corpus proceedings, on the theory that the provision violated the due process clause of the Constitution, because it fixed the price at which credit may be sold; in other words, because it was a price fixing measure; and also because as a criminal statute it was not sufficiently definite, since it failed to define the term "loan of credit." The 

Supreme Court of Georgia held the Act constitutional and remanded him to the custody of the sheriff, but he appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. After argument was commenced, the Supreme Court declined to hear further, and afterwards dismissed the appeal for want of a substantial federal question in the case.² 20 Another banking law whose constitutionality was ques- 

19 No. 751-Wrightsville & Tennille R. R. Co. vs. Citizens & Southern 

National Bank et al. Certiorari denied May 5, 1930. 

20 No. 235-Morgan vs. Georgia. Decided March 3, 1930. 

[173]

tioned was the law of Nebraska, which created the Depositors' Guarantee Fund and imposed assessments on banks for the benefit of the fund. This suit was brought by 559 state banks to enjoin the governor and other officers of the state from collecting this special assessment of one-quarter of one per cent. of the average daily deposits in the banks during 1928. They claimed that this was a taking of property without due process of law. 

The Supreme Court of Nebraska, however, sustained the validity of the Act as a reasonable regulation of the banking business, in view of the bank failures and other financial conditions, and also held that the statute was not an unreasonable measure taken for the protection of depositors. From this decision the banks appealed to the Supreme 

Court of the United States, attacking the constitutionality of the statute. They filed a statement showing that the Court had jurisdiction in the case, but the Court postponed the consideration of the jurisdictional question until the hearing of the case on the merits.21 This case will be argued during the coming term of Court. 

In another case involving the banking statutes of

Nebraska, the Supreme Court held that it had no jurisdiction in the appeal. This suit came under the Nebraska law, which provides that banks and trust companies shall be regulated by the Department of Trade and Commerce of the state, which may take over the business of such corporations whenever they violate any law of the state, or any order of the department, or operate in an unsafe manner, or have their capital impaired.

A trust company was taken over by the Department of Trade and Commerce because its capital was impaired, and a liquidating agent was appointed. Shortly afterwards the trust company filed a voluntary petition in bankruptcy. It 

21 No. 750-Abie State Bank vs. Weaver, Governor. Case pending. [174]

was adjudicated a bankrupt, and a receiver was appointed. 

The receiver applied to the state court to have the liquidating agent turn the property over to him, but this application was denied. The receiver then instituted summary proceedings in the federal district court to take over the property of the trust company.

The defense was that after the company had been taken over by the Department of Trade and Commerce, it could not file a voluntary petition in bankruptcy; furthermore, that the company was a banking corporation and was therefore exempt from the Federal Bankruptcy Act. The liquidating agent further argued that the properties demanded by the receiver were actually not properties of the trust company, but were held by it as agent, trustee, guardian, executor or administrator, and that therefore the property should not be turned over to the receiver. 

On all these questions, the federal district court in Nebraska ruled against the liquidating agent, on the theory that the Department of Trade and Commerce and the liquidating agent were merely operating the trust company as agents, and therefore they stood in no better position than the trust company itself. It also held, contrary to the contentions of the liquidating agent, that the properties in question were subject to summary proceedings under the 

Bankruptcy Act.

This was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit, and the liquidating agent appealed to the Supreme Court, maintaining that the lower court in effect nullified the state statute. The Supreme Court, however, declined to take jurisdiction in the case.22 

22 Uo. 852-Gamble vs. Daniel, Rec. Decided June 3, 1930. [175]

CHAPTER VII. 

Federal Taxation* 

The largest single class of cases which came to the Supreme Court this term arose under the internal revenue laws. These dealt with intricate questions beginning with the filing of returns and ending with the bringing of suits for refunds of alleged overpayments. We find not only problems concerning income and excess profits taxes, but also the special taxes imposed by the revenue laws, such as the estate tax, the capital stock tax, taxes on automobile parts and accessories, etc.

Exemptions From Taxation. 

Before going into the problems concerning the factors of taxation, the question arises whether the particular person, association or corporation is subject to the internal revenue laws altogether. For example, state officers or employees are exempt from paying any tax on income, but the question often arises whether the particular person is an officer or an employee of the state within the meaning of the Revenue Acts. This question came up with reference to an attorney who represented the City of Houston, Texas, under a contract to defend it in certain suits. The attorney received several thousand dollars for his services, which were paid from the revenues of the city. He argued that in *There were about 175 cases under the various internal revenue laws in the Supreme Court during the term under discussion. Space limitations. make it impossible to cover all the cases. Those who are specifically interested will find an analysis of all the cases and their disposition in the 

U. S. Supreme Court Service, October Term, 1929. 

[176]

this respect he was in the same position as any officer of the state, and that these fees were not taxable by the federal government. But the Supreme Court held the income taxable and that the tax on the fees did not constitute a burden on an instrumentality of the state, contrary to the Constitution.¹

While this case was pending for decision, a similar case came to the Supreme Court from Pennsylvania, in which Senator Reed had been engaged as counsel for the state in an inheritance tax suit. Senator Reed likewise argued that he was engaged and acted as an employee or officer of the state, and that his fees were not taxable. But the Supreme 

Court held otherwise,2 disposing of the question without an opinion, on the authority of the above case from Texas. 

A pilot, licensed under the laws of the state of Virginia, took the position that his income was not subject to tax by the federal government, because he was a state officer. The 

Government, however, assessed and collected a tax on his income, and the pilot brought suit in the Court of Claims to recover the amount. That court held that his income was subject to tax, because such tax would in no way interfere with the state exercising its powers of government under the Constitution. The pilot petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, maintaining that the Court of Claims erred in applying such test to the term "state officers" as used in the Revenue Acts, but the petition was denied.³ 

Indians, as special wards of the United States, are generally exempt from taxation, and it is conceded that income from tribal mineral leases, received by incompetent Indians, is not subject to income tax. The question arose whether the same exemptions extended to such income received by a competent Indian or by a white heir at law of a deceased 

1 No. 52-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Howard. Decided December 9, 1929. 

2 No. 462-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Reed. Decided May 5, 1930. 

3 No. 694-Bew vs. United States. Certiorari denied May 5, 1930. [177]

Osage Indian. In both of these cases the Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Tenth Circuit held that the income was taxable. In the former case, the Supreme Court granted a writ of review, and the case will be argued next term. In the latter the writ of review was denied."

Sometimes the question of exemption attaches not to the person but to some particular class of income. This term a writ of review was granted on the question whether profits derived from the sale of municipal bonds were subject to federal taxation. This case will be heard early next fall. 

Filing of Returns.

We now come to the filing of the income and excess profits tax returns, and the first question is, who must file the return? Where a husband and wife have separate sources of income, they may file separate income tax returns, in which case each is entitled to one-half of the statutory exemptions, together with other deductions allowed by law in determining net taxable income. The question came up, however, whether a husband and wife, having only one source of income, which was the husband's salary, may enter into an agreement to hold all their property as joint tenants and then file separate returns. The filing of separate returns makes quite a difference in the total tax due, especially since the surtax is higher as the income is greater. The 

Supreme Court held that the entire income was taxable to the husband, and not one-half to each of them."

In the above case, there was an agreement between the 

4 No. 815-Choteau vs. Lucas, Comm'r. Certiorari granted May 19, 1930. 

5 No. 816-Pettit vs. Lucas, Comm'r. Certiorari denied May 19, 1930. 

6 No. 535-Wilcutts, Collector vs. Bunn. Certiorari granted January 

27, 1930. 

7 No. 99-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Earl. Decided March 17, 1930. 

[178]

husband and wife in the state of California, which is a community property state. Toward the end of the term, two similar cases came from other community property states, Louisiana and Arizona. These cases will be heard next term in the Supreme Court.8

Similar problems arise concerning corporations which are so closely affiliated, that the law regards them as one and requires them to file a consolidated return. The provisions in the internal revenue laws prescribing when corporations must or may file consolidated returns are complicated and vary with the various revenue acts. A national bank owned the capital stock of a corporation operating a cotton mill. 

The bank caused a reorganization of the corporation, reduced the capital stock and issued bonds to itself. The corporation argued that it was entitled to file a consolidated tax return, which would have reduced the tax some $20,000. 

This was denied by the courts, and the Supreme Court refused the corporation's petition for a review of that decision."

The Taxable Year. 

An income tax return covers a period known as the taxable year. This is usually a calendar year, but those keeping their accounts on a fiscal year basis file their returns to cover such fiscal years. One point of difficulty often is whether a given item of income was received by the taxpayer during one taxable year or another. A taxpayer received dividends through the mails on January 2, 1923, which had been issued by the corporation a few days before the close of the year 1922. The lower court held that the income was taxable for the year 1922. The taxpayer argued 

8 No. 882-Goodell, Collector vs. Koch. Case pending. No. 823Bender, Collector vs. Pfaff. Certiorari granted May 26, 1930. 

⁹ No. 150-Montgomery Cotton Mills, Inc. vs. United States. Cer tiorari denied October 14, 1929. [179]

that the income, having been received in 1923, was taxable for that year. But the Supreme Court declined to go into the question.10

It is not always easy to tell when one's dividends are received. In imposing the tax on income received as dividends from corporations, the law provides that the dividends shall be presumed to have been declared out of the most recently accumulated earnings and profits of the corporation, and shall be taxed at the rates prevailing during that time. A stockholder of the Ford Motor Company argued that this presumption as to the "most recently accumulated earnings and profits," should not apply where the distribution of profits was ordered by a court to be made out of earlier earnings. The Circuit Court of Appeals, however, held that the dividends in question must be treated as being derived from the very latest earnings available for distribution, and the Supreme Court refused to disturb that decision.11

The question of the specific taxable period when an item of income is received, is not limited to dividends. This year, the Supreme Court held that profits derived from the sale of real estate accrue as income for federal tax purposes during the year when the conveyance is perfected.12 The taxpayer entered into a contract for the sale of land in 1916, and the conveyance was perfected in 1917. The Commissioner and the Board of Tax Appeals ruled that the income belonged to the taxable year of 1917, but the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit overruled their decision and held that the income accrued in 1916. In the Supreme 

10 No. 608-Bingham vs. Comm'r. Int. Rev. Certiorari denied March 

12, 1930. 

11 No. 213-Kales vs. Woodworth. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

12 No. 92-Lucas, Comm'r vs. North Texas Lumber Co. Decided 

February 24, 1930. [180]

Court the Government argued that since the income was derived from the conveyance, and not from the contract to convey, such income accrued in the year when the conveyance was made; also that in accordance with the best accrual methods, income on the sale of real estate is credited when the conveyance is made. The Supreme Court adopted this viewpoint.

Sometimes the question arises, not whether a given item of income belonged to the particular taxable year, but how much of it belonged to the taxable year. A manufacturer of condensed milk brought this question to the Supreme Court. Since condensed milk is sold in competition with fresh milk, and since prices of fresh milk vary in accordance with the season, the taxpayer adopted the policy of selling its goods on a tentative price, adjusting that price upon the happenings of such contingencies as the raising or lowering of the prices of fresh milk. It so happened that the adjustments of price in this case came within the taxable year succeeding the one in which the goods were originally sold. Under such circumstances the Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Third Circuit held the return for the earlier year must include the total tentative price charged without deductions on account of the later adjustments in the price. 

The taxpayer lost its petition in the Supreme Court to review that decision.13

This difficulty as to when the financial transaction takes place, arises not only with reference to items of income, but items of expense, and sometimes the situation becomes further complicated by the fact that the taxpayer keeps his books on an accrual basis. Where a corporation kept its books on an accrual basis, and in 1920 the directors voted extra compensation to be paid to the president and treasurer 

13 No. 506-Highland Milk Condensing Co. vs. Phillips. Certiorari denied January 13, 1930. [181]

for services rendered during prior years, these amounts were held deductible from the gross income of the corporation in 

1920.14 Chief Justice Hughes, in delivering the opinion of the Court, held that the payments in question were actually made in 1920, that the expenses represented by these payments were incurred in that year, for there was no prior agreement or legal obligation to pay the additional compensation.

Items of Income. 

From the discussion of the returns and the periods they cover, we now turn to the various items of income. This problem resolves itself mainly into two questions: (1) Does the particular receipt of assets constitute taxable income to the taxpayer, and (2) how should the net taxable income be computed? 

1. The most important case under the first question was that of Corliss vs. Bowers. 15 Here the Court held that where a revocable trust was created before the passage of the Revenue Act of 1924, and the income of the trust was received by the beneficiary, the person creating the trust must pay a tax on this income under the provisions of the 1924 Act. The taxpayer argued that since this trust was created before the 1924 Act and none of the income of the trust was received by the taxpayer, but by the beneficiary, the trustee was not subject to the tax. Justice Holmes, delivering the opinion of the Court held, however, that "taxation is not so much concerned with the refinements of title as it is with actual command over the property-the actual benefit for which the tax is paid," and that here the income was 

14 No. 250-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Ox Fibre Brush Co. Decided April 

14, 1930. 

15 No. 344-Corliss vs. Bowers. Decided April 28, 1930. 

[182]

subject to the grantor's command and was taxable to him as income, whether he saw fit to enjoy it or not. 

The Cleveland Railway Company argued that its "interest fund" was not taxable. This company operates the street railways in Cleveland, Ohio, under the "Tayler Plan," whereby the railroad agrees to furnish transportation at "cost." "Cost," under this plan, includes operating and maintenance expenses, and allowances for interest on indebtedness and taxes, and a 6 per cent. return to the stockholders. 

This plan further obligates the company to start with a socalled "interest fund" of $500,000, with a provision that if the revenues over and above "cost" will exceed the interest fund by $200,000, the fares should automatically be lowered, and if the "interest fund" should fall $200,000 below the $500,000 mark, the fares should be automatically increased. 

The Commissioner of Internal Revenue and the Circuit 

Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit held that this "interest fund" constituted taxable income. The railway company petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but its petition was denied.16

An interesting question in this connection arose when a taxpayer recovered a judgment from the United States for a sum equal to the amount by which its expenditures during the years 1913-1916 exceeded the compensation received under a contract with the Government. This judgment was recovered in 1920, and the Commissioner of Internal Revenue ruled that the amount of the judgment should be included in the taxpayer's gross income for that year. The 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit held, how ever, that this amount was not income, but merely reimbursement for expenditures which had been incurred during the prior years. The court ordered that this amount should 

18 No. 710-Cleveland Ry. Co. vs. Comm'r Int. Rev. Certiorari denied April 21, 1930. [183]

be excluded from the taxpayer's income for 1920, and that the deductions for losses allowed during the prior years should be decreased by that amount. The Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, maintaining that the Court below erred in holding that the amount of the judgment was not taxable income, and also that it exceeded its authority in remanding the case to the Board of Tax Appeals for the purpose of reducing the assessment and revising the amount of tax owed for the prior years. The Supreme Court granted a writ of review," and the case will be argued during the coming term of the Court. 

Another interesting question came up in connection with annuities received by a beneficiary under a will. It is a settled rule that where a beneficiary receives an annuity out of an estate, or receives a portion of the estate outright, the amount is not taxable income. Where, however, the beneficiary receives not a portion of the estate, but a portion of the income of the estate, the amount received by the beneficiary is taxable. In a case which came to the Supreme 

Court toward the end of the term, a beneficiary received an annuity under a will, and the executors paid the annuity out of the income of the estate, though the will itself did not specify that the annuity be paid out of the income. The court below held, contrary to the contention of the Government, that this income was not taxable. The Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, but the petition has not yet been submitted for the Court's consideration.18

In three cases the Commissioner and the lower courts. held that corporate reorganizations resulted in tax liability. 

In the first case,19 a reorganization was affected under the 

17 No. 579-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Sanford & Brooks Co. Certiorari granted, March 3, 1930. 

18 No. 911-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Whitehouse. Case pending. 

19 No. 249-Lonsdale vs. Comm'r Int. Rev. Certiorari denied October 

21, 1929. [184]

"Chicago Plan," by the National Bank of Commerce of St.

Louis, when that bank wished to engage in the business of a trust company. The bank could not do this under its charter as a national bank and it could not do it through a branch bank, because that was contrary to the law of Missouri. It therefore adopted the following plan: It passed a resolution issuing a cash dividend of 10 per cent. to all the stockholders, and the resolution provided that each stockholder might, with such dividend, pay his proportionate share in a new corporation to be formed for the purpose of engaging in the business of trust company. This was declared to be the object of the dividend. The dividends were then paid to a committee, who used the money to pay the interest of each stockholder in the new corporation. The Commissioner of Internal Revenue ruled that this dividend constituted taxable income to the stockholders, and this was the view taken by the Board of Tax Appeals and by the Circuit 

Court of Appeals.

In the second case,20 a stock insurance company took over the business of a mutual insurance company. The Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia held that when this occurred the surplus of the latter in the amount of $105,000 constituted a premium paid to the stock company for reinsurance, rather than one of the purchased assets, and that this item was therefore taxable income under the revenue acts. 

In the last case, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit held that a certain exchange of property for stock in a subsidiary corporation resulted in taxable income.2 A company had transferred all of its real estate and insurance business to its subsidiary in exchange for 350 21 

20 No. 286-Hoosier Casualty Co. vs. Comm'r Int. Rev. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

21 No. 740-Insurance & Title Guaranty Co. vs. Lucas, Comm'r. 

Certiorari denied April 28, 1930. [185]

shares of stock at a par value of $35,000. Thereupon, the company reduced its capital stock from 700 shares of par value of $70,000 to 350 shares of the par value of $35,000 and distributed to its stockholders 350 shares of stock of its subsidiary. The Commissioner held that the difference between the par value of the subsidiary stock and the value of the real estate and insurance business constituted taxable income. The company, however, maintained that the transaction was exempt from taxation as an intercompany transaction between affiliated corporations, or as constituting a reorganization within the meaning of Section 202 

of the Revenue Act of 1918. 

In all these cases the Supreme Court denied the taxpayers' petitions to review the decisions of the lower courts. 

One of the most interesting questions concerning taxability of income came up with reference to a declaration of dividends, made prior to August 6, 1917. The Revenue

Act of 1917 imposed taxes on dividends, but provided that this should not apply to any distribution made prior to August 6, 1917, out of earnings and profits accrued prior to

March 1, 1913. The question arose whether dividends declared prior to August 6, 1917, but paid out after August 6th, were subject to the tax. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit held they were, and the taxpayer failed to obtain a review of that decision by the Supreme 

Court. 22 

2. The second question is, how should the income be computed for tax purposes. Last year, the Supreme Court held that in computing the gain or loss on the disposition of property received as a gift, the taxpayer must deduct from the sales price, the value of the property to the donor.23 

22 No. 134-Harbison vs. Llewellyn. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. 

23 No. 23-Taft vs. Bowers, 278 U. S. 470. [186]

This year the question came up with reference to a similar situation, but where the gift was made and the property disposed of by the donee in 1921, but prior to the passage of the Revenue Act of 1921. The Supreme Court held that the same rule applied, though both the gift and the sale took place prior to the effective date of the law.24

In one case an estate, consisting of personal property and real estate, was left to four residuary legatees. Prior to the probate of the will in 1918, the residuary legatees entered into an agreement for the distribution of a part of the personal property among themselves. This distribution was made in 1918, and in 1919 the legatees received income from the portion of the estate thus distributed. During that year, the amounts of the Federal Estate Tax exceeded the income from the estate and, therefore, the executor paid no income tax. Under the agreement of distribution, each of the legatees contributed one fourth of the Federal Estate Tax, and the amount contributed by each exceeded the amount of his income either credited to him by the executors or collected by him from the portion of the estate distributed.

The Commissioner of Internal Revenue ruled that the income, from the personal property distributed, represented income to the beneficiary and not income to the estate in the process of administration, and that therefore the beneficiary was not entitled to deduct the Federal Estate Tax thus paid from his income. He further ruled that the income from the real estate could not be considered as income from the estate, for the reason that under the laws of Illinois, where this occurred, real property passes directly to the heirs, and therefore he ruled that the taxpayer was not entitled to the deduction of the Federal Estate Tax on that income. The Commissioner did allow a deduction 

24 No. 93-Cooper vs. U. S. Decided February 24, 1929. 

[187]

as to the income which was collected directly by the executors and credited to the legatees.

Here the first question to be determined was whether the income of the legatees dated from the time of the testator's death, or from the time of the agreement, or from the time of the actual distribution ordered by the probate court. The Commissioner in effect ruled that the taxpayer (one of the beneficiaries) had already received part of the estate for tax purposes, or that the receipt of the property did not depend upon a further act of distribution. After having paid the tax, the taxpayer brought suit in the Court of Claims to recover the amount alleged to have been overpaid. The Court of Claims sustained the rulings of the 

Commissioner, the taxpayer petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, but the petition was denied.25 

That case did not affirmatively answer the preliminary question, when is property received under a will for tax purposes. This question, however, was answered by the 

Supreme Court in another case. A residuary legatee, sold the stock he acquired under the will and paid the tax imposed by the Commissioner, on the theory that he acquired the stock as of the date when the decedent died. The taxpayer brought suit in the district court to recover a portion of the tax, on the theory that upon the testator's death, the personal propery not specifically bequeathed passed to the executor and not to the legatee, and that the legatee did not acquire it until the Surrogate Court in New York ordered the property distributed.

The federal district court in New York, where the action was brought, held in favor of the taxpayer, but was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit, which upheld the Commissioner. This was affirmed by 

25 No. 259-Matthiessen vs. United States. Certiorari denied October 

21, 1929. [188]

the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Butler, who held that under the Revenue Acts of 1918 and 1921 the proper basis for computing gain or loss on the sale of securities received by a residuary legatee is the value of the securities as of the time of the testator's death, not as of the time when the probate court orders the distribution of the estate.26

Market conditions and methods of accounting sometimes combine to make difficult the determination of the basis for computing gain or loss for income and excess profits for tax purposes. This is best illustrated by a number of cases decided by the Court during this term. Several can companies kept their accounts on an accrual basis and they paid their taxes for 1917 on that basis. During that year it will be remembered, prices jumped sky-high, and the can companies took advantage of these price changes of their inventories in computing the amounts of profit. The Commissioner rejected their inventory values on the ground that these values were abnormal and therefore did not accurately reflect the income of the corporations. The Commissioner imposed additional assessments, and the can companies, having paid the same, brought action to recover a refund of the taxes. They claimed that since the Commissioner rejected their inventories, they were entitled to have the entire tax computed on cash receipts and disbursement basis. This made a difference of several million dollars in the taxes for that year.

The Government maintained that in this action the can companies must prove that the accrual basis did not reflect the true income of the companies, before recovery could be had by them in this action. The Government, furthermore, denied that the Commissioner rejected the accrual basis, but that he merely corrected the returns on the same basis 

26 No. 61-Brewster vs. Gage. Decided January 6, 1930. 

[189]

on which they were made.

The District Court in New Jer sey and the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Cir cuit held in favor of the taxpayers. This decision was reversed by the Supreme Court in an opinion by Mr. Justice 

McReynolds. The Court held with the Government that the Commissioner had merely corrected an item in the tax return, and that this did not constitute a rejection of the basis upon which the return was made.27 

It was suggested in the case, that since there was so much. difficulty concerning the inventory values, these might be omitted altogether in computing the profits of the corporations. On this the court said: "We need not discuss the question whether under any circumstances the taxable income of a manufacturing or mercantile corporation can be ascertained without reference to inventory values. Certainly, in most instances, where the taxpayer carries on an extensive business this cannot be done."

This question was more directly involved in another case in which the Supreme Court held that certain inventories must be considered. It was the case of the Kansas 

City Structural Steel Company, which fabricates steel and other material for specific structures, that is, it does not manufacture or deal in steel, but gets contracts for structural steel work and then goes out into the open market and buys what steel it needs, according to its contracts. In order to prevent delays in obtaining the necessary steel, it keeps 

27 No. 128-United States vs. American Can Co.; No. 129-United

States vs. Missouri Can Co., and No. 130-U. S. vs. Detroit Can Co. De cided February 24, 1930. Later in the term, four similar cases came from the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit, whose decision on the same question was in conflict with that of the Third Circuit and was in accord with the decision later rendered by the Supreme Court. The 

Supreme Court denied the companies' petitions for writs of certiorari. 

These were No. 676-American Can Co. vs. Bowers; No. 677-Missouri 

Can Co. vs. Bowers; No. 678-American Can Co. of Mass. vs. Bowers, and No. 679-American Can Co. of Utah vs. Bowers. Certiorari denied 

March 17, 1930. [190]

on hand a reasonable amount, which it calls an emergency supply, and whenever the company is forced to use some of the steel in this reserve supply, it replaces the same and charges it to the contract on which such steel was used, at replacement cost. In its 1918 and 1920 returns, the company treated this reserve supply as part of its fixed equipment and carried it at cost. 

The Commissioner, upon auditing the tax returns, inventoried the entire emergency steel supply at the market price of the steel most recently purchased. This resulted in a higher tax. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth 

Circuit held that the emergency supply of steel should not have been considered by the Commissioner at all in determining the taxable income of the company; that in view of the character of the business of this company, and the purpose for which it kept this supply of steel, it did not add a dollar to the profits which the taxpayer made on any contracts it had ever executed. 28 This was reversed by the Supreme Court in an opinion. by Mr. Justice Brandeis, who regarded this emergency supply of steel as clearly affecting the profits of the company. 

The Court held that the reserve stock should have been included in the inventories in computing the net taxable income, and the fact that the company was not a dealer, manufacturer or producer, but rather a contractor or builder, was immaterial.

Deductions. 

After having determined the amount of gross income, the taxpayer is entitled to various deductions. The most important of these may be grouped as follows: (1) Depletion, depreciation, absolescence, exhaustion, wear and tear, 28 No. 323-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Kansas City Structural Steel Co. De cided April 14, 1930. [191]

(2) business losses, which are not compensated for by insurance or otherwise, (3) expenses regularly incurred in any trade or business, (4) interest on borrowed money, and (5) taxes paid during the taxable year. (1) In the first group of deductions we find a decision on the proper method for computing the depletion of a mine. The taxpayer maintained that the depletion was de terminable by the market value of the ore mined and sold during the year for which the return was made. The Government maintained that the depletion was determinable by the ratio between the amount of ore withdrawn and the whole reserve of the mine, multiplying this ratio by the taxpayer's interest in the mine, based on cost or March 1, 1913, value. This question arose when the taxpayer brought suit to recover a refund of taxes alleged to have been overpaid, because of this difference in method of computing depletion. The Circuit Court of Appeals upheld the taxpayer's method. The Supreme Court reversed that decision on the ground that the taxpayer had not sustained her burden to show that the taxes were illegally exacted.29 "The real point is whether respondent established her claim for a refund by adequate evidence, and we think she did not.' 31 Two cases came to the Court as applications of its decision in Weiss vs. Miller,30 in which the Court had held that a lessee of property was not entitled to deductions for exhaustion, wear and tear." But the most important question relating to this class of deductions during this term was whether an owner of a brewery or distillery was entitled to deductions for the depletion, exhaustion, wear and tear of the good will, labels, trade-marks, and physical properties 

29 No. 59-Reinecke vs. Spalding. Decided January 6, 1930. 

30 279 U. S. 333. 

31 No. 348 Ohio Clover Leaf Dairy Co. vs. Lucas, Comm'r. Cer tiorari denied October 21, 1929. No. 705-Belt Railway Co. vs. Comm'r. 

Certiorari denied April 21, 1930. [192]

of the business which had been proscribed by prohibition legislation. In two cases the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Holmes, held that the words "exhaustion" or "absolescence" in the Revenue Acts were not intended to cover losses due to the adoption of an amendment in the Constitution or to the enactment of a law. Justice Holmes said: "It seems to us plain that when a business is extinguished as noxious under the Constitution, the owners cannot demand compensation from the Government, or a partial compensation in the form of an abatement of taxes otherwise due." 32 Shortly after this decision, rendered on January 27, 1930, two similar cases came to the Supreme Court. In one of these the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit allowed a deduction for absolescence of the tangible property of a brewery, although the property continued to be used in the manufacture of near beer after National Prohibition became effective.33 In the second case the Court of 

Appeals of the District of Columbia allowed deductions for losses sustained through the abandonment of beer vats and two floors in the cellar of a brewery building.³ In both of these cases the Supreme Court granted the Government's petitions to review the decisions of the lower courts.

2. The second class of deductions, i. e. losses incurred in the trade or business, gave rise to some interesting questions. In one case the question involved was whether losses in intercompany transactions should be allowed as deductions. This was a suit brought by the Utica Knitting Mills to recover about $30,000 of the excess profits tax it paid for the year 1917. It appeared that in 1912, the Utica Knitting Mills purchased for $100,000 the capital stock of another corporation. In June, 1915, the directors of the subsidiary company resolved to liquidate, and empowered the officers to take the necessary steps. Before 1917, that company had gone out of business, but continued its corporate existence for the purpose of winding up its affairs. By December 31, 1917, when the subsidiary company was wound up, it owed the Utica Knitting Mills some $26,000 on an open account, and the Utica Knitting Mills charged off as a loss on its books the original investment of $100,000 and this indebtedness.

The Commissioner allowed a deduction of $127,000 on the company's income tax return, but disallowed this deduction in the company's consolidated profits tax return for 1917, on the ground that the loss was an intercompany transaction which could not be eliminated. This ruling was sustained by the Court of Claims in the action brought by the Utica Knitting Mills to recover the amount of the tax paid. 

The Utica Knitting Mills petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision raising the question whether the two corporations could be said to have been affiliated in 1917, since the subsidiary corporation transacted no business, but merely existed for the purpose of winding up its affairs. 

Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia, though rendered nine days after the Supreme Court's decision contained no reference to that decision. 

Through the use of the U. S. Supreme Court Service the Court could have informed itself of that decision on the morning following its rendition. [194]

The Supreme Court, however, declined to go into the matter.36

Another case involved the question whether certain contributions made by stockholders constituted deductible losses. Here the taxpayers, who were the principal officers and stockholders, returned part of their salaries to the corporation. They claimed that the amounts paid back into the corporation were reductions in salary and were therefore losses deductible from gross income. The Commissioner of Internal Revenue, the Board of Tax Appeals, and the Circuit Court of Appeals held that those were not salary reductions, but were contributions to the capital of the corporation, and therefore were not losses deductible under 

Section 214 of the Act. The Supreme Court likewise de clined to disturb that decision.³ 37 The most interesting case in this connection was the case of Lucas, Commissioner vs. American Code Company.38 

This company had entered into a contract of employment with one Farquahr for a term beginning January 3, 1919, and ending February 1, 1937. The compensation of the employee was to be on a commission basis. On May 1, 1919, the Company discharged Farquahr from its employment, and the latter began action in the courts of New York to recover commissions due him for April, 1919, and for breach of contract of employment, claiming damages in the amount of $100,000. The American Code Company defended this suit, denying its liability, but on its books which were kept on accrual basis, it set up a reserve of some $14,- 000 computed on the amount of commissions for that year. 

The action resulted in favor of the employee in 1922, and 

36 No. 611-Utica Knitting Mills vs. United States. Certiorari denied 

April 21, 1930. 

37 No. 175-Burns vs. Lucas. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

38 No. 67-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Amer. Code Co. Decided February 

24, 1930. [195]

on appeal the judgment of the lower court, in the amount of $21,000, was affirmed by the Court of Appeals in New 

York in 1923.

In 1920 the American Code Company increased its reserve for liability under the contract to $32,000. In 1922 it increased the reserve to $47,000, when the reserve was again adjusted to $21,000, which was the amount of the final judgment.

In filing its income tax return for the year 1919, the American Code Company claimed a deduction for that year in the amount of the judgment. This claim was denied by the Commissioner of Internal Revenue, and his decision was affirmed by the Board of Tax Appeals. On appeal, the 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit reversed that decision, holding that since the books were kept on an accrual basis, the entire amount of the judgment was deductible in the year in which the action was brought. This was reversed by the Supreme Court on the ground that the loss did not actually accrue upon the breach of contract, for the reason that recovery under the contract or the amount of recovery was contingent upon future events. 

3. The cases involving deduction for expenses centered mainly about the question whether they were incurred during the particular taxable year or whether the particular expenditures were actual expenses, or what might be termed as capital expenditures. A corporation had paid a sum of money to bankers, for which the latter agreed to purchase the stock of the corporation which had not been taken by stockholders. Under this agreement, about $240,000 was paid to the bankers in 1921, but in 1922 the entire issue of stock was called and retired. The corporation sought a deduction of the amount of the money paid to the bankers from its gross income for 1922. This was disallowed by the Commissioner, on the ground that the expenditure was [ 196]

a non-deductible capital expense, and not an expense regularly incurred in trade or business. His decision was affirmed by the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia, and the Supreme Court denied the taxpayer's petition to review the case.39 

4. The deductions for interest paid on borrowed capital involved an interesting constitutional question. It was a case in which the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit held that in allowing deductions for interest paid, Congress could not discriminate against interest paid for money borrowed and used for trading in municipal bonds. 

In this case the taxpayer, in 1922, received $60,000 as interest from municipal bonds, and during the same year he paid $78,000 as interest on money borrowed to buy and carry municipal bonds. In his income tax return he excluded from his income the amount of the interest received by him from the municipal bonds, and deducted the interest paid by him on the money borrowed. The Commissioner disallowed this deduction. His decision was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals and the Supreme Court granted the Government's petition to review that decision.¹º

Toward the end of the Court's term another interesting question came up involving deductions for interest. Here interest was paid by a joint stock land bank on its farm loan bonds, the proceeds of which were used to make loans to farmers under the provisions of the Federal Farm Loan Act of 1916. The Commissioner and the Court of Claims held that this was not deductible in computing the net taxable income of the bank under the Revenue Act of 1921. The bank petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, maintaining that the denial of the deduction for interest was 

39 No. 703-Corning Glass Works vs. Lucas. Certiorari denied April 

21, 1930. 

40 No. 719-Nauts, Coll'r vs. Slayton. Certiorari granted April 28, 1930. [197]

a tax burden on joint stock land banks, contrary to the in tent of the Federal Farm Loan Act. The Supreme Court granted a writ of review in the case.¹¹

Corporation Taxes. 

The above questions concerning gross income and deductions relate to individuals and corporations alike, except where we discussed reorganizations of corporations and their effect on the taxability of income received. The Internal Revenue Laws often make a distinction between individuals and corporations, but the line of demarcation between them is not as clear in these laws as it is in the general corporation law. Thus, under some circumstances unincorporated associations are treated as if they were corporations, while some corporations are treated as individuals. 

The latter are known as "personal service corporations," that is, corporations where the income-producing factor is the service rendered by the stockholder or stockholders of the corporations. Since the taxes imposed on corporations are heavier than on individuals, many corporations are apt to claim that they are personal service corporations. 

In a suit in equity brought by the United States to recover from stockholders taxes due from the corporation, after the latter had been dissolved and the assets distributed among the stockholders, the latter maintained that the taxes were not due, because the corporation was a personal service corporation. On this they were overruled by the Circuit 

Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit, because the corporation had borrowed money both from the stockholders and from banks, in order to make it possible to carry on the business of commission merchants of the Sioux City Live 

41 No. 905-First Nat. Bank of Chicago vs. U. S. Certiorari granted 

June 2, 1930. [198]

Stock Exchange. The court held that the capital of the corporation was a substantial income-producing factor, and that the income was not produced solely through the efforts of the individual stockholders."

In another case a corporation had fourteen stockholders, all of whom were exclusively engaged in the executive conduct of the business, and they held their stock under an agreement that the stock would be transferred only to the corporation in case of a stockholder's death, resignation or discharge for any cause whatsoever. Here the same court held that the corporation was not a personal service corporation, because its income was not ascribable primarily to the principal owners or officers, since its employees performed executive duties, and the work of the employees was as indispensible to the conduct of the business as that of the principal stockholders. In both cases the Supreme 

Court declined to disturb the decisions of the court below. 

The main difference between the taxes imposed on corporations and on individuals lay in the fact that during the war, and for a number of years afterwards, the corporations were obliged to pay war profits and excess profits taxes. 

Since the excess profits taxes were imposed on income over and above the normal income, and the normal income depended upon the amount of invested capital, the greater the invested capital, the greater was the allowance for normal profits and the less the excess profits tax. Numerous problems therefore arise concerning methods of computing invested capital. The most troublesome question in the computation of excess profits taxes arises in connection with those corporations where, by some peculiarity of stock ownership or of borrowed capital, the excess profits tax, if com- 

42 No. 614-Hatch vs. United States. Certiorari denied March 12, 1930. 

43 No. 688-Meinrath Brokerage Co. vs. Comm'r. Certiorari denied 

April 14, 1930. [199]

puted on the same basis as that of other corporations, might result in great hardship. Congress, therefore, enacted the "special assessment provisions," in which the Commissioner, upon finding certain facts concerning the particular corporation, may impose an excess profits tax on it, equal to the average tax-rate paid by corporations engaged in the same line of business. The statute gives the Commissioner wide powers of discretion, and it has been previously held by the Supreme Court that the findings of the Commissioner in this respect are subject to review by the Board of Tax Appeals," but not by the courts." 44 45 Still suits are being brought in the courts seeking a review on the Commissioner's determination under the special assessment provisions, and several of such cases were dismissed by the Court of Claims on the authority of the Supreme Court's decision in the Williamsport Wire Rope Company Case. Of course, the Supreme Court declined to review those cases. 46

In one case a taxpayer thought that it would be sufficient to allege that the Commissioner acted wrongfully, unlawfully and fraudulently in denying the claim for special assessment, in order to obtain a review of the Commissioner's findings under the special assessment provisions. The facts disclosed no fraud or other misconduct, and the Circuit Court of Appeals held that the Commissioner's ruling on the question of special assessment was not reviewable. 

In this case likewise the Supreme Court denied the taxpayer's petition for a writ of review.""

For some inexplicable reason it has also been seriously maintained in a number of cases that having determined the 

44 Blair, Comm'r. vs. Oesterlein Machine Co., 275 U. S. 220. 

45 Williamsport Wire Rope Co. vs. United States, 277 U. S. 551. 

46 No. 291-Clinton Corn Syrup Refining Co. vs. U. S., and No. 292 -National Candy Co. vs. U. S. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

47 No. 652-Live Stock National Bank vs. U. S. Certiorari denied 

May 26, 1930. [200]

taxpayer's liability under the special assessment provisions, the Commissioner was precluded from redetermining the tax liability, either on the basis of special assessment or on the basis of statutory capital. The court below did not agree with the taxpayers' contentions and the Supreme Court declined to go into the question.48 

Period of Limitations. 

The filing of the tax return and the payment of the tax on the income reported do not always end the transaction. 

After paying the tax, the taxpayer may discover that he has paid too much, and may apply for a refund. This he can. obtain, provided he sufficiently specifies the reasons for the claim, and provided he files his claim within the statutory period for the filing of such claims. A claim for refund may be filed within a given time after payment of the tax. 

Ordinarily it is not difficult to tell when payment was made. 

Sometimes, however, payment is made by means of a credit allowed by the Commissioner of Internal Revenue. Then the question arises when, or at what point in the proceedings, the credit was allowed. 

In one case, on February 9, 1923, the Commissioner, in accordance with an established practice of the department, forwarded to the Collector of Internal Revenue a Schedule of Over-assessments in which was included an over-assessment of income and excess profits taxes for 1918 against the taxpayer. On July 25, 1923, the Collector returned to the 

Commissioner a Schedule of Refunds and Credits showing what portions of the over-assessments were overpayments and how the overpayments should be distributed between credits and refunds. On September 6, 1923, this Schedule of Refunds and Credits was signed by the Commissioner. 

48 No. 641-Austin Co. vs. Lucas, Comm'r. Certiorari denied March 

17, 1930. [201]

The Government maintained that the credit was made when the Commissioner found an over-assessment for 1918, which meant that the taxpayer's claim for refund was filed more than four years afterwards, since it was filed on September 3, 1927. The taxpayer maintained that the credit was made when the Commissioner signed the Schedule of Refunds and Credits, which made the claim for refund within the four-year period. The Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, which petition was granted.¹9

There is an exception to the general rule that claims for refunds must be filed within a given time, and that is where a refund is filed because of a decrease in invested capital on account of the taxpayer's failure to take sufficient deductions in the previous years. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit has, however, put a limitation on this provision in Section 281 (c) of the Revenue Act of 1924. 

In this case the Commissioner of Internal Revenue had ruled that the provision in Section 281 (c) was applicable only to cases where there had been a decrease in the invested capital, and such decrease was due to the fact that the taxpayer failed to take adequate deductions in the previous years. In this case, however, although there was a decrease in invested capital due to the fact that insufficient deductions were taken in the previous years, there have also been increases in the invested capital for other reasons, with a resulting increase, rather than a decrease, in invested capital.

The Commissioner, therefore, held that Section 281 (c) was not applicable and that the claim for refund was barred by the period of limitations. This ruling was sustained by the District Court in Pennsylvania and by the Circuit Court 

49 No. 700-United States vs. Swift & Co. Certiorari granted March 

17, 1930. [202]

of Appeals for the Third Circuit. The taxpayer petitioned the Supreme Court of the United States to review that decision, but the petition was denied.50 

Of course, if the claim for refund is not allowed, the taxpayer may file suit for a refund, but this suit must be filed within two years after disallowance of the claim. Here again an interesting question came before the Court as to when the two-year period begins. A taxpayer filed a claim for a refund on February 14, 1924. In 1925 he was notified that his claim for a refund would be rejected, and that the rejection would officially appear on the next schedule to be approved by the Commissioner. The taxpayer, however, was not notified until June, 1928, that the claim had been rejected on September 2, 1925. He then brought suit to recover the amount of income tax overpaid, and the Government argued that this suit was barred by the period of limitations, because it was brought more than five years after the tax had been paid and more than two years after disallowance of the claim for refund. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit overruled the contention of the Government, holding that since the action was instituted within two years after the taxpayer was notified that the claim had been rejected, this action was timely brought. 

The Supreme Court granted the Government's petition to review this decision.51

A claim or suit for refund does not always end the controversy. The Commissioner may determine that the taxpayer should have paid more and may assess an additional amount. This, however, must likewise be done within the statutory period "after the return is filed." This phrase, though innocent in appearance, has given rise to a great 

50 No. 441-Southwestern Oil & Gas Co. vs. United States. Cer tiorari denied November 25, 1929. 

51 No. 812-United States vs. Michel. Certiorari granted May 19, 1930. [203]

many difficulties. For example, what happens, if the return does not comply as to form, or is merely a tentative return, will they start the period running? The Court held that where a tentative tax return is filed, and the Commissioner extends the time for filing the final return, the period of limitations for assessment and collection of taxes begins to run from the date of final return.52

Other questions concerning the validity of assessment arose in cases where taxpayers kept their books on a fiscal year basis and filed the returns on a calendar year basis. 

A taxpayer filed his return for the calendar year ending December 31, 1918, while its books were kept on a fiscal year basis ending January 31st. The Commissioner assessed an additional tax, which was done beyond the period of limitations, if the date of the filing of the return governed, but within the period, if the fiscal year be taken as the basis for the running of the period of limitations. The Circuit 

Court of Appeals held that the return on the calendar year basis, when the taxpayer kept its books on the fiscal year basis, was not sufficient in form and substance to start running the statute of limitations. It therefore sustained the validity of the assessment. The Company petitioned the 

Supreme Court to review that decision, and its petition was denied.53

But in another case of a similar nature a different result was reached in the Court below Here the taxpayer kept its books on a fiscal year basis ending on May 31st, but made its tax returns on a calendar year basis. In 1924, the 

Commissioner required the taxpayer to amend its returns for the years 1919, 1920 and 1921 to conform to its methods of accounting, namely, the fiscal year basis. On March 15, 52 No. 118-Florsheim Bros., etc. vs. United States. Decided February 

24, 1930. 

53 No. 406-Paso Robles Mercantel Co. vs. Lucas, Comm'r. Cer tiorari denied October 21, 1929. [204]

1926, the Commissioner made jeopardy assessments for the years ending May 31, 1920, and May 31, 1921. The taxpayer maintained that the assessment was barred by the five-year period of limitations, since the returns were filed on March 15, 1921, and March 15, 1922, respectively. The

Commissioner held, however, that the period did not begin to run until the filing of the last calendar year return embracing a portion of the particular fiscal year. This was sustained by the Board of Tax Appeals. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit held, however, that the period of limitation began to run from the date of the filing of the return, and furthermore that the date of the filing should be included in the computation. The Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, and its petition was granted.54

If the assessment is properly made, the taxpayer may either pay the additional tax and then sue to recover the same, or he may appeal to the Board of Tax Appeals for a redetermination of the tax. If the Board decides in favor of the Commissioner (or if the Board decides in favor of the taxpayer and the Commissioner does not acquiesce in the decision), the Commissioner may proceed to collect the tax. This proceeding can be either by a suit to collect the tax, or by distraint. Usually a demand on part of the collector will bring results, since no suit will lie to restrain him from collecting the tax by distraint.

This is a well settled proposition, but some people seem to be under the impression that this does not apply in cases where one invokes the constitutionality of the tax. The Commissioner of Internal Revenue imposed a tax on stockholders of a corporation, which had become dissolved and had distributed its funds. This direct assessment on the 

54 No. 683-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Willingham Loan & Trust Co. Cer tiorari granted April 14, 1930. [205]

stockholders was made under the provisions of Section 280 

of the Revenue Act of 1926. The stockholders maintained that this provision in the 1926 Act was unconstitutional as being without due process of law and as denying them a trial by jury as provided by the Seventh Amendment of the Constitution, and they sought to enjoin the collection of the tax assessed. Of course, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit held that an injunction could not be issued because Section 3224 R. S. prohibits suits for the purpose of restraining the collection of a federal tax, notwithstanding the contention that the tax itself may be unconstitutional. The Supreme Court declined to go into the matter.55

The collection of the tax, however, must likewise be made within the period of limitation prescribed for collection of taxes. Under the Revenue Acts of 1918 and 1921 the periods of limitation for assessment and for collection of taxes were the same. Beginning with the Revenue Act of 

1924, the Commissioner had six years within which to collect the tax after assessment. There is a general exception, in that where no return is filed, or a fraudulent return is filed, the Commissioner may assess and collect the tax at any time. The court has interpreted this provision to mean, however, that having assessed the tax, the Commissioner may collect the same only within six years." 56 In this case a corporation was dissolved in August, 1917, after having filed its income and excess profits tax returns through June 30, 1917. The corporation filed no return under the Act of October 3, 1917, and the assets of the corporations were distributed among the stockholders. The 

Commissioner assessed additional taxes in 1920, but no suit 

55 No. 634-Tyroler vs. Routzahn, Coll'r. Certiorari denied March 

17, 1930. 

56 No. 340-United States vs. Nelson B. Updike. Decided May 19, 1930. [206]

or proceeding for collection of the additional taxes was instituted until 1927, when the Government filed suit in equity against the stockholders of the corporation on the trust fund theory. 

The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit held that the suit was barred by the six-year period of limitations for collection of the tax after assessment. The Government brought the case to the Supreme Court arguing that the suit for collection of the tax was not barred by limitations, because the corporation had never filed a return under the Act of October 3, 1917, though that Act was passed after the corporation had been dissolved. The Supreme Court, in an opinion of Justice Sutherland, affirmed the decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals.

The procedure is often more complicated than desrcibed above. Claims for refunds are followed by allowance of the same, by redeterminations of the taxes, by proceedings for collections, and while these matters are being considered the time is extended from year to year by waivers. Then for the first time the question arises whether the waivers were valid, or what is their effect if they are valid. In several cases before the Court during this term the question arose, whether waivers filed after the expiration of the period, within which the Commissioner could assess the tax, were valid. The Supreme Court granted writs of review in the cases, which will be argued during the coming term of the Court.57

In a number of other cases the question arose whether a waiver filed to extend the period for the assessment of the tax also operated to extend the period for collecting the In one of these cases the Circuit Court of Appeals same. 

57 No. 554-Stange vs. United States. Certiorari granted March 3, 

1930; and No. 777-Aike, Adm'x. vs. Lucas, Comm'r. Certiorari granted 

May 5, 1930. [207]

for the Second Circuit held that since the period for collection was the same as the period for assessment in the particular case, there was no need for two separate waivers and that the one waiver covered both steps in fixing the liability of the taxpayer. The Supreme Court declined a writ of review in the case.58 In another case the same question was involved together with a question whether the tentative or final return of the taxpayer started the period of limitations running. The Court held that a waiver filed extending the period of assessment also extended the period for collection of the tax, so that if, before the expiration of the period for assessment under the 1918 Act the period is extended for one year, so that the period expires after the enactment of the 1924 Act, and the assessment is made within that period as extended, the Commissioner may collect the tax within six years after the assessment.59 

In the Revenue Act of 1926, Congress contemplated eliminating some of the difficulties in the periods of limitation by the enactment of Section 1106 (a) of the Act. This provision apparently intended to wipe out both the remedy and right to assess or collect the tax or to bring suit to recover operpayments when the period of limitations had run. Shortly after the enactment of this provision questions arose as to the meaning of it, and it appeared that even those who sponsored the provision were not clear as to its meaning. 

Then in the Revenue Act of 1928, Congress enacted Sections 607 and 611 which were supposed to settle all these difficulties. Section 607 provides that any tax assessed or paid after the expiration of the period of limitations properly applicable, shall be considered an overpayment and 

58 No. 116-Loewer Realty Co. vs. Anderson, etc. Certiorari denied 

October 14, 1929. 

59 No. 118-Florsheim Bros. vs. United States. Decided February 24, 1930. [208]

shall be credited or refunded to the taxpayer, if he makes a claim for it within the period of limitation for filing such claims. But Section 611 provides that if any tax was assessed within the period of limitations prior to June 2, 1924, and if a claim in abatement was filed, on account of which the collection was stayed, then the collection of such tax made before or within one year after the enactment of the Revenue Act of 1928 should not be considered an overpayment within the meaning of Section 607 of the Act. 

60 These provisions have already given rise to additional problems. In one case, the taxpayer had filed his income tax return for 1916, in February, 1917. In November, 1920, he executed a waiver of the three-year period of limitation in the 1916 Act and the Commissioner made an additional assessment in October, 1921. In November, 1921, the taxpayer filed a claim in abatement which was allowed in part and rejected in part in 1923. Then the additional assessment was sustained by the Committee on Appeals and Review in August, 1923, after which a certificate of assessment was issued. In May, 1924, the Commissioner made a formal demand on the taxpayer for the amount of tax due in accordance with the additional assessment, less the amount allowed on the claim for abatement. The tax was paid by the taxpayer in 1927 under protest, and he filed a claim for a refund of the amount so paid, which claim was rejected. 

The taxpayer then brought this suit to recover the amount paid.

He claimed that the amount of the tax collected was unlawfully collected, because it was done after the period of limitations had expired and also after the waiver had expired. The Court of Claims held, however, that the tax was not barred by the period of limitations in the Revenue Act of 1916, but by the period of limitations in the Revenue 

60 No. 761-Magee vs. United States. Certiorari granted May 19, 1930. [209]

Act of 1921. Assuming, however, that at the time the tax was collected the period of limitations had run, the Court of Claims held that this did not constitute an overpayment in accordance with Section 611 of the Revenue Act of 1928, because the assessment and collection of the tax was delayed on account of the taxpayer's claim for abatement. 

In another case,61 the taxpayers paid their 1917 tax under duress in 1924, after the period of limitation for collection, and brought suit to recover the amount paid. The 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit held that, since the collection was stayed by a claim for abatement filed by the taxpayers, this case came within the provision of Section 611 of the Revenue Act of 1928, and the taxpayers could not recover the amount of tax paid, although it was paid after the period of limitations. The taxpayers maintained that Congress did not intend that Section 611 of the Act should operate retroactively so as to deprive a taxpayer of a right which he had gained when the statute of limitations had run, namely, the right not to pay the tax. He claimed that, as applied by the Circuit Court of Appeals, Section 611 must be considered contrary to the Fifth 

Amendment of the Constitution. The Supreme Court con sented to review both of these decisions. Interest.

With the collection of a refund by the taxpayer, or with the collection of additional taxes by the Government, the unsuccessful party must pay interest. Since the rates and conditions for the payment of interest are different under the various revenue acts, the question often arises from what date and under what Act interest is to be computed. Several cases came to the Supreme Court on the question when the overpayment was made, if the overpayment was in the 

61 No. 589-Graham vs. Goodcell. Certiorari granted March 3, 1930. 

[210]

form of a credit allowed by the Commissioner. The question which arose, when a credit was made, in order to determine whether a claim for refund was filed in time,' also arose in another case where it was necessary to determine whether interest should be computed under the Rev enue Act of 1921 or under the Revenue Act of 1924. Prior to the passage of the Revenue Act of 1924, the Commissioner signed a certificate allowing an overassessment and directing the Collector to apply the overpayments to taxes due from the taxpayer for other years. After the passage of that Act, the Commissioner, upon a return from the Collector, signed another certificate to make payments out of the amounts refundable. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the First 

Circuit held that the signing of the second certificate constituted the granting of the credit, and since that was done after the passage of the 1924 Act, interest on the overpayment should be computed under that Act. The Government petitioned the Supreme Court for a writ of review and the writ was granted.63 The Court also granted a writ of review in a case involving a similar question, which came from the Court of Claims.64 Two other cases on this question came from the Court of Claims toward the close of the term, which the Court has not yet had an opportunity to consider.65 

The Estate Tax. 

The Estate Tax again figured prominently this term in the tax cases before the Court, and the question most frequently raised referred to the computation of the gross 

62 See p. 202 supra. 

63 No. 632-United States vs. Boston Buick Co. Certiorari granted 

March 17, 1930.

estate. Several cases dealt with the more specific question whether the widow's interest or share should be included in the gross estate. The reason for not including her share was either that she and her husband were tenants by the entireties, or because the widow's interest, under the laws of her state, does not pass to her by descent and distribution, but is hers in her own right, or because by virtue of an antenuptial agreement, the wife was entitled to a portion of the estate upon the death of her husband.

The Supreme Court held, in an opinion by Justice Sutherland, that where a husband and wife hold property as tenants by the entireties, in a state where such tenancies are recognized by law, the value of the property so held must nevertheless be included in the estate of the husband, if he dies first, and that it was the evident and legitimate aim of Congress to prevent an avoidance of the estate tax by creating estates of the entireties during lifetime.66 

Where the executor argued that the widow's interest, under the laws of the state, did not pass to her by descent or distribution, but was hers in her own right, the Supreme

Court refused to go into the question.67 Neither did the 

Supreme Court care to disturb the ruling of the Commissioner in the case where the executor argued that the widow's interest should not be included in the gross estate for federal estate tax purposes, since there was an antenuptial agreement providing the wife with a certain portion of the estate upon the husband's death.68

The remaining questions concerning computation of the 

66 No. 428-Tyler, Jr. vs. U. S.; No. 546-United States vs. Provi dent Trust Co. of Penna.; and No. 547-Lucas, Comm'r vs. Girard 

Trust Co. Decided May 19, 1930. 

67 No. 85-Wilhite et al., Trustees vs. U. S. Certiorari denied Octo ber 14, 1929; and No. 181-Henshaw, Exec. vs. Lucas. Certiorari denied
