gross estate dealt with specific items of assets, rather than with some particular person's interest in the estate. One of the problems was whether the proceeds of life insurance policies are part of the gross estate of the decedent. In this case the decedent took out insurance on his own life in favor of his estate, but the policies were taken out before the passage of the Revenue Act of 1918, and before the passage of any estate tax law. The question arose whether under the Revenue Act of 1918, the proceeds of the life insurance must be included in the gross estate of the decedent. The 

Court of Claims held that it must be. The executor argued that as interpreted by the Court of Claims, the provision in the Revenue Act of 1918 imposing an estate tax, is without due process, and he petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, but the petition was denied."⁹ 

Another problem was whether an irrevocable trust created by the decedent was part of the gross estate. The decedent created a trust which provided for the payment of the income from the trust to her husband for life, then to herself for life, and after the death of both, it provided, the property shall be distributed among her children. She predeceased her husband, and the Commissioner ruled that the value of this trust property should be included in the gross estate, because the trust was created in contemplation of death and was intended to take effect at or after her death. 

This ruling was sustained by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit. The executors brought the case to the Supreme Court, which reversed that holding in an opinion delivered by Mr. Justice McReynolds." In reversing the judgment of the lower court, the Supreme Court held that this transfer was not made in contemplation of death 

69 No. 163-Minnaugh, Exec. vs. U. S. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

70 No. 311-May vs. Heiner, Coll'r. Decided April 14, 1930. [213]

within the legal significance of that term. It was not testamentary in character and was beyond recall by the decedent. 

At her death the decedent had no interest in the property, title to which had been definitely fixed by a trust deed. The amount of the property should therefore not have been included in the net taxable estate. 

A third problem came up specifically with reference to real estate in the state of Missouri. The Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Eighth Circuit held that the real estate should not be included, because, under the law of Missouri, real estate is not subject to the payment of administration expenses, and therefore the property does not come within the provision of Subdivision (a) of Section 402 of the Revenue Act of 1918. The Government petitioned the Supreme 

Court to review the decision, maintaining that the Circuit Court of Appeals erred in permitting the local rule as to property to disturb the uniformity of the general provisions of the estate tax law. The Court granted a writ of review in the case, which will be argued during the coming term."¹ 

Federal Gift Tax.

This year the Supreme Court upheld the validity of the Federal Gift Tax of 1924. Two years ago the constitutionality of this Act was before the Court in three cases. 

In the first of these,72 the Court was equally divided on the question presented; in the other two cases the Court, in a decision of five to three, held that the Federal Gift Tax was unconstitutional, if applied to gifts made prior to the effective date of the Act.78 Then came this case, in which it was 

Certiorari granted Feb- 

71 No. 556-Crooks, Coll'r. vs. Harrelson. ruary 24, 1930. 

72 Blodgett vs. Holden, 275 U. S. 142. 

78 Untermeyer vs. Anderson, 276 U. S. 440. 74 

74 No. 27-Bromley vs. McCaughn, Coll'r. Decided November 25, 1929. [214]

argued that a gift tax is a direct property tax and therefore must be held unconstitutional, because it was not apportioned among the states. But the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Stone, upheld the validity of the tax, on the theory that a gift tax is not a direct property tax, but is an excise impose on the right to transfer property. "It is a tax laid only upon the exercise of a single one of those powers incident to ownership, the power to give the property owned to another." 

Justice Sutherland, with whom Justice Van Devanter and Justice Butler agreed, dissented from this view. Justice 

Sutherland pointed out that a tax cannot be imposed on a separate element or incident of ownership without imposing a tax on the property itself. "The right to give away one's property is as fundamental as the right to sell it or, indeed, to possess it." And if a tax imposed on possession of property is a direct tax, there is no reason why a tax imposed on the right to give away one's property is an indirect tax. He said: "To me it seems plain that a tax imposed upon an ordinary gift, to be measured by the value of the property given and without regard to any qualifying circumstances, is a tax by indirection upon the property, as much, for example, as a tax upon the mere possession by the owner of a farm, measured by the value of the land possessed, would be a tax on the land. To call either of them an excise is to sacrifice substance to a mere form of words." 

We think that Justice Sutherland was right. The courts (the Supreme Court, the lower federal courts and the state courts) have long in this connection set up distinctions where there are no differences. In matters of taxes imposed upon transfers whether upon death or inter vivos, the courts have set up differences between estate taxes, that is where [215]

the tax is imposed on the property transferred; transfer taxes, in which it is said that the tax was imposed not upon the property, but upon the right to transfer it; and inheritance taxes, in which they distinguished from the other two by saying that the tax was imposed upon the receipt of the property; whereas in all cases the tax was taken out of the property itself. In this respect the courts have played the role of the gravedigger in Hamlet, who said that "an act hath three branches; it is, to act, to do, and to perform."" 

It may well be that Congress should not be hampered by a constitutional provision that direct taxes shall be apportioned among the states, and we think that the provision should be stricken out of the Constitution. Whatever might have been the reason for the adoption of that provision and whatever might have been its applicability at the time when the country consisted of thirteen independent states, more or less equal both economically and politically in the Federal Union, this provision in the Constitution is wholly inapplicable to the country as it is at present. It would be utterly absurd for Congress to impose direct taxes and apportion them equally among such states as New York and 

Idaho, or North Dakota, or New Mexico, etc. That, however, does not mean that we must set up such spurious distinctions as a tax upon the right to receive and a tax upon the right to transmit, when the two are mere aspects of the same transaction, or to set up such differences as saying that a tax upon the right to sell property is a direct tax, whereas a tax on the right to transmit the same property by gift is an excise, which belongs to a different class of taxation." 

75 Act V, Scene 1. 

76 76 I disagree with the view expressed above by the senior author. 

Whatever may be the logic of the situation, it is well settled that neither the economic problems nor the legal problems of our social order follow the laws of logic. (See holmes: The Common Law, chap. 1). For many years it has been recognized that such rights, incident to ownership, as the [216]

Special Taxes. 

Several cases came to the Supreme Court concerning taxes on automobile parts and accessories. No less than six such cases came from the Court of Claims, questioning whether storage batteries, speedometer parts, replacement parts for bumpers, gas strainers, window anti-rattlers, etc., constituted automobile parts within the meaning of the revenue acts imposing taxes on the sale of such products. In those cases, in which the Court of Claims had made a specific finding that the products in question were intended to be used for automobiles, the Supreme Court held that the Court below was right in considering them as automobile parts and accessories within the meaning of the Act." 

Where the court below had not made such specific findings, the Supreme Court remanded the cases with directions that the court make that finding, for the test of the tax liability lies in the purpose for which the specific product is used.78 

Capital Stock Tax. 

Another special tax considered by the Court was the capital stock tax imposed on corporations. In one case, the 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit held that the Bank of North Dakota was obliged to pay the capital right to transfer property has been peculiarly the subject of taxation, and there is no reason why Congress should not impose a tax on such transfers. 

If the tax on gifts should be held unconstitutional, then the Estate Tax should likewise be held invalid, and by the same mode of reasoning many other laws which have been upheld through a liberal interpretation of the Constitution, would share the same fate. Society moves more rapidly than the stable laws of logic, and the Constitution should be interpreted to make it applicable to these ever changing conditions.-C. A. H. 

77 No. 275-Vesta Battery Corp. vs. U. S.; No. 351-Stewart Mfg.

Corp. vs. U. S., and No. 352-Gemco Mfg. Co. vs. U. S. Decided May 

26, 1930. 

78 No. 127-Universal Battery Co. vs. U. S.; and No. 350-Bassick 

Mfg. Co. v. U. S. Decided May 26, 1930. [217]

stock tax, though all the stock in the bank was owned by the state. In answer to the state's argument, that the tax must be held unconstitutional, as one imposed on an instrumentality of the state, the court held that in so far as the State of North Dakota engaged in the business of banking, it engaged in a private enterprise, and to that extent divested itself of its sovereignty. The State appealed to the Supreme 

Court from this decision, but the appeal was dismissed for lack of jurisdiction, since the state had appealed the case instead of having petitioned for a writ of review.79 

It is too bad that this very important economic question was thus left undecided. For what constitutes a private enterprise, when a state engages in it? And does a state really divest itself of its sovereignty by engaging in a private enterprise* It is hoped, however, that these questions will be clarified by the Supreme Court in the case of Alabama vs. United States, which will be argued during the coming term of Court.80

Since the capital stock tax is imposed only on corporations "carrying on or doing business" and not on inactive or holding companies, it was maintained in a number of cases that the corporations were not carrying on or doing business within the meaning of the capital stock tax provision. 

The activities of one corporation consisted in the acquisition of additional shares of stock of its subsidiary, in advising its subsidiary concerning the purchase of patents and real estate, in authorizing the subsidiary to mortgage some of its property, in paying dividends on its own stock and in paying its organization expenses. The corporation did not lend money or its credit to its subsidiary, nor did it enter into contracts with its subsidiary, nor did it conduct any 

79 No. 435-North Dakota vs. Olson. Appeal dismissed December 9, 1929. 

80 See p. 229 infra. [218]

operations for profit. The Court of Claims held that the corporation was the directing brain and nerve of its subsidiary, and that therefore it was doing business within the meaning of the capital stock provisions.81

Several corporations organized for the purpose of engaging in coal mining claimed that they were not doing business during the taxable years in question within the meaning of the capital stock provisions of the Revenue Acts. One of these did not operate the coal mines owned by it, but merely owned the lands subject to mortgages on which payments were made by an affiliated company. It owned stock of another corporation, for which it paid part cash and part notes, but which notes were paid by the affiliated corporation. It also bought some real estate for the affiliated corporation and held directors' meetings, elected officers and maintained its corporate expenses." Another corporation maintained its corporate existence, held lands for sale or development, loaned or borrowed money on which it paid or received interest, paid taxes and legal expenses, sold securities and bonds held by it, and purchased certain coal lands.83 A third corporation maintained its corporate existence, owned the stock of its subsidiaries, paid taxes and legal expenses, paid notes and expenses of its subsidiaries and bought and sold securities.84 82 In all these cases the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit held that the corporations were subject to the capital stock tax, and the Supreme Court denied their petitions to review those decisions.

14, 1929. 

82 No. 513-Harmar Coal Co. vs. Heiner. Certiorari denied January

CHAPTER VIII. 

State Taxation 

Inheritance Taxes.

Inheritance tax laws enacted by the states aroused an unusually great amount of public interest. This public interest was due not so much to the specific problems involved, although they were important, but to the fact that the Supreme Court reversed one of its own decisions. That also was not very startling, for the Supreme Court reverses itself more frequently than is generally supposed. This time, however, the Court expressly reversed itself, and it did so on the basis of a principle, upon which, in the opinion of the minority, it had no business to rely. 

Up to this time, it has generally been the rule that hardship of taxation in itself is not a constitutional objection, and that even double taxation is not contrary to the Fourteenth Amendment.' The question has come up on previous occasions, whether a state may impose an inheritance tax on a debt owed by a resident to a non-resident decedent, and it was held that such tax may be imposed.2

On the other hand, within recent years the Supreme Court has extended the doctrine that tangible personal property may be taxed only where the property is located, so that when a state attempted to impose an inheritance tax on tangible property located outside of its jurisdiction, the tax was held unconstitutional.3 That, however, is not the law 

1 Coe vs. Errol, 116 U. S. 517. 

2 Blackstone vs. Miller, 188 U. S. 189. 

3 Frick vs. Pennsylvania, 268 U. S. 473. [220]

with reference to intangible property such as stocks, bonds, notes, etc., where the property consists in a right which one person has against another. A state may impose a transfer tax on such property, though that property is not and has never been within the jurisdiction of the state of the decedent. The reasoning in such cases is supported by a structure of legal fictions. It starts out with the assumption that every item of property has a situs or place. We say this is a fiction, because if property is intangible, i. e. does not exist in physical space, then it cannot be said that it has a situs. However, a further fiction is indulged in that in the case of intangible property the situs is where the owner ismobilia sequuntur personam.

And then the question arises, where is the owner? For purposes of taxation, Mr. Justice McReynolds pointed out,5 there are four theories of the situs of intangible property: (1) the domicile of the owner, (2) the domicile of the debtor, (3) where the evidences of the indebtedness are found, and (4) where the owner has caused the property to become an integral part of a localized business. With each state adopting its own view as to the situs of intangible property, the same property could be taxed at four places at the same time.

The State of South Carolina imposed an inheritance tax on dividends due from a South Carolina corporation to a stockholder, resident in Illinois. The validity of the tax was argued in the Supreme Court in the 1928 term, it was carried over to the 1929 term, and toward the end of this term the Court ordered a reargument to be held early next fall in the 1930 term."

Shortly afterwards came the Minnesota inheritance tax 

4 Blodgett vs. Silberman, 277 U. S. 1. 

5 No. 26-Farmers Loan & Trust Co. vs. Minnesota. Decided Janu ary 6, 1930.

No. 3-Beidler vs. South Carolina. Case pending. 

[221]

law, in which the Court reversed itself. A resident of New 

York died, owning bonds of the State of Minnesota, of the cities of St. Paul and Minneapolis and certain certificates of indebtedness of the state. In accordance with the statutes of Minnesota, an inheritance tax was imposed on the transfer of these bonds: The Minnesota courts upheld the tax, and the executor appealed to the Supreme Court, arguing that Minnesota had no jurisdiction to tax the transfers at the death of the non-resident and that the statutes in question were contrary to the due process clause of the Constitution. 

The state argued that since the debts represented by the bonds were given validity and protection by the laws of the state, the situs for taxation purposes was in that state. This was the rule laid down by the Supreme Court in Blackstone vs. Miller, 188 U. S. 189, rendered some twenty-five years ago.

Now the Supreme Court flatly reversed its former decision, on the ground that the bonds never came within the territorial jurisdiction of the State of Minnesota, after the non-resident decedent had acquired them. Justice Mc- 

Reynolds, who delivered the opinion of the court, said: "Blackstone vs. Miller no longer can be regarded as a correct exposition of existing law, and to prevent misunderstanding, it is definitely overruled." With reference to the four views pertaining to the situs of intangible personal property, and the taxability of it in four places at the same time, the Court said: "Such a startling possibility suggests a wrong premise."

The wrong premise, it seems to us, is the assignment of a situs to intangible property. But this premise, it appears, is still recognized by the law and is supported by another fiction, that of mobilia sequuntur personam (movable prop- 

7 No. 26-Farmers Loan & Trust Co. vs. Minnesota. Decided Janu ary 6, 1930. [222]

erty follows the person), which is buttressed up with another fiction that in cases of debts the creditor is the person or owner.

That was not the premise set aside by the Supreme Court. It attacked only one of the manifestations of this wrong premise, that is, double or multiple taxation. And the reason for the step taken by the Court, it seems, is that intangible property has become widely used, it has become a large source of revenue to the states, and therefore the taxation has become more burdensome. The Court said: "Primitive conditions have passed; business is now transacted on a national scale. A very large part of the country's wealth is invested in negotiable securities whose protection against discrimination, unjust and oppressive taxation, is matter of the greatest moment."

And so, while previously the due process clause of the 

Fourteenth Amendment did not protect one from the burden of double taxation, the Court now interpreted the clause differently, because of a change in economic conditions. 

This evoked a dissenting opinion on the part of Justice 

Holmes, who was the author of the opinion in Blackstone vs. Miller. He adhered to the view formerly expressed that though the bonds in question were taxable at the domicile, of the owner, they were also taxable by the State of Minnesota, because the debt or obligation represented by the bonds exists by virtue of the laws of Minnesota. He said: "The right to tax exists in this case because the party needs the help of Minnesota to acquire a right, and that State can demand a quid pro quo in return." With reference to the application of the Constitution, Justice Holmes said: "A good deal has to be read into the Fourteenth Amendment to give it any bearing upon this case. The Amendment does not condemn everything that we may think undesirable on economic or social grounds." [223]

This was only a beginning. In the Missouri Inheritance 

Tax Case, a resident of Illinois died, leaving all her property to her son, also a resident of Illinois. At her death she owned certain intangible property in the form of bank accounts, government bonds and promissory notes due both from residents of Missouri and from non-residents. The evidences of all this property were discovered in the State of Missouri. The Supreme Court of Missouri held that the state could impose an inheritance tax on this property, since the evidences thereof were found in the state, in other words, the situs of the property was within the state. But the Supreme Court of the United States, relying on its 

Minnesota decision, reversed that ruling and held the tax invalid. The Court held that the inheritance tax could not be imposed on this property, because the situs of the debt for taxation purposes was at the domicile of the creditor, which was in Illinois. 

Here Justice Holmes delivered a strong dissenting opinion which may be regarded as a continuation of his dissent in the Minnesota case. Since the bonds, notes and bank accounts were within the power and received the protection of the State of Missouri, he argued, the state was justified . in imposing the tax. He cited a number of decisions, but added: "I suppose that these cases and many others now join Blackstone vs. Miller on the Index Expurgatorius but we need an authoritative list." He concluded by saying: "It seems to me to be exceeding our powers to declare such a tax a denial of due process of law."

Justice Stone, who concurred in the result reached in the Minnesota case on the theory that there it was not necessary to invoke the power of the state to effect a transfer of the property, now joined Justice Holmes. He dissented on the ground that he who invokes the help of the state in con- 

8 No. 417-Baldwin vs. Missouri. Decided May 26, 1930. [224]

tinuing the existence of the debt also subjects himself to the state's power to tax, and the state is not deprived of the power merely because the taxpayer had in one manner or another also subjected himself to the taxing power of another state. To hold otherwise, Justice Stone pointed out, would open the door to tax evasion. "Under the law as it has been, no one need subject himself to double taxation by keeping his securities in a state different from his domicile, or by seeking the protection of its laws for his mortgage investments. 

But it is a practical consideration of some moment that taxation becomes increasingly difficult if the securities of a non-resident may not be taxed where located, and where alone they may be reached, but where the courts are not open to the tax gatherers of the domicil."

Among the other less controversial inheritance tax cases, a question was raised whether a state may impose an inheritance tax on the transfer of property, if the decedent died before he came into possession of the property. The 

Court declined to go into the matter. Another case involved the retroactive application of the Massachusetts Inheritance Tax Law. This case will be heard next term.10 

Corporation Taxes. 

Inheritance laws were not alone in creating dissention among the members of the Supreme Court. The State of 

Missouri imposed a special tax on insurance companies. 

As we have seen," the basis of the tax was the net worth of the corporation, including all property, real and personal, less the legal reserve and unpaid claims. The question was whether United States Government bonds owned by the 

11 See p. 135 supra 

10 No. 583-Coolidge vs. Long. Case pending. 

11 See p. 135 supra. [225]

corporation should be included in the basis of the tax. The 

Supreme Court of Missouri held that the deductions (legal reserve and unpaid claims) should be apportioned between the taxable and non-taxable assets, that is, between the United States bonds and the other assets. This was reversed by the Supreme Court of the United States, holding that the company was entitled to a full deduction of the tax-exempt securities, irrespective of other deductions allowed by the state law.12 For to include these securities among the assets which are not taxable, would mean to deprive the owner of the benefits attaching to the tax-exempt character of such securities, which would in reality constitute a burden on such securities and be unconstitutional. Chief Justice 

Hughes concurred in the result reached by the Court on the basis of a previous decision. Justice Stone wrote the dissenting opinion, in which Justices Holmes and Brandeis concurred.

Within recent years the Court has gone a long way in protecting instrumentalities of the Federal Government from taxation by the states. In its recent decision in the case of Macallen Company vs. Massachusetts,13 the Court held that a state may not impose a franchise tax on a corporation, basing the tax on the net income of the corporation, including income from tax-exempt securities. In that case, Justice Stone also dissented and Justices Holmes and Brandeis agreed with him, because the tax was not one on the income, but on the right to do business and was merely measured by the income; furthermore, they held, the tax was measured by the net income, which meant that the tax had no direct or proportionate relation to the income from tax-exempt securities. 

12 No. 222-Missouri ex rel. Mo. Ins. Co vs. Gehner. Decided April 

14, 1930. 

13 279 U. S. 620. [226]

In the Missouri case, the tax was measured by the net worth of the insurance company, and the tax-exempt securities were as much part of the net worth as any other assets. 

They, like the other assets, were income producing factors and, in the opinion of the minority, should therefore participate in the payment of the liabilities of the company, including liabilities to the state. To exempt such securities from participating in the tax, Justice Stone argued in his dissenting opinion, would mean to confer a benefit, rather than to refrain from imposing a tax liability on such securities, which the state is not obliged to do. "If the constitutional inhibition is not directed against the imposition of burdens, but affirmatively compels the annexation of such benefits to the ownership of government bonds as will increase their currency and stimulate the market for them, even though those privileges are extended at the expense of the constitutional powers of the states, it is difficult to see what the limits of such a doctrine may be. I suppose that the sale and market value of government bonds would be materially increased if we were to say that the Constitution sub silentio had forbidden their seizure for debts, or rendered their possessor immune from the various forms of state taxation to which this Court has said he is subject." 

We suppose Justice Stone meant that carrying the recent decision a step farther, the Court may hold, contrary to its previous decisions, that tax-exempt securities should not be included in computing franchise taxes paid by corporations for the right to do business in the state, or in computing transfer or inheritance taxes.

Various other corporation taxes were attacked on the theory that they were imposed on instrumentalities of the Federal Government. States may tax shares of national [227]

banks only in the manner prescribed by the National Bank Act, and in no event may a state discriminate against a national bank in favor of other moneyed capital competing with national banks. This was the complaint of a bank from the City of Lynn, Massachusetts. But this appeal was dismissed as not having presented a substantial federal question.¹4

A national bank in Kansas, however, was far more successful, even to the extent of compelling a state agency to pay a judgment. In this case, the bank paid the tax imposed on national bank shares by the State of Kansas and then sued to recover the amount on the ground that the tax discriminated against national banks and was contrary to the National Banking Act. The county officials, against whom the action was brought in the federal court, maintained that the bank had no standing in court, because the tax was imposed on the shareholders and not on the bank, and furthermore that the federal courts had no jurisdiction on the ground of diversity of citizenship because each shareholder had a separate and distinct cause of action which did not amount to the required jurisdictional sum of $3,000. That suit resulted in favor of the bank, and execution of the judgment was ordered against the County Treasurer and the 

Board of County Commissioners. 

These officers then brought this suit to enjoin the execution of the judgment. Instead of staying the judgment, the 

District Court issued a writ of mandamus ordering the County Commissioners to issue a warrant for the payment of the judgment and ordering the County Treasurer to pay the judgment out of the county treasury. On appeal, this action of the District Court was sustained by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit. The county offi- 

14 No. 173-Central National Bank vs. City of Lynn. Decided Octo ber 28, 1929. [228]

cials petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but their petition was denied.15 

What appears to be the most flagrant example of a tax imposed on the federal government came to the Supreme Court toward the end of the term, and will be considered during the coming term. The State of Alabama imposed a license tax on the federal government for engaging in the production and sale of hydroelectric power at Muscle Shoals, on the theory that the federal government engaged in a private enterprise and therefore to that extent divested itself of its sovereignty. Of course, the tax was not paid, and the state brought suit in the Court of Claims to recover the tax, together with a 15 per cent. penalty for failure to pay the tax. The Court of Claims dismissed the suit, and the 

Supreme Court granted a writ of review in the case.16 The 

Government argued, and we think rightly, that the Court of Claims had no jurisdiction in the case, and that it erred in dismissing the case on the merits. It should have dismissed the case for lack of jurisdiction. The Government also argued that in producing and selling the power, the United States was not engaged in a private enterprise, because in accordance with previous decisions of the Supreme Court the United States cannot constitutionally engage in private business. In this argument, we fear, the Government begs the question. 

We have already mentioned the case of North Dakota vs. Olson," in which the Circuit Court of Appeals for the

Eighth Circuit followed the same line of reasoning as the State of Alabama, in holding that the Bank of North Dakota was obliged to pay a capital stock tax. We confess we are 

15 No. 797-Wright, etc. vs. Central Nat'l Bank of Topeka. Certio rari denied May 19, 1930. 

16 No. 817-State of Alabama vs. United States. Certiorari granted 

June 3, 1930. 

17 See p. 218 supra. [229]

puzzled by the reasoning both of the Circuit Court of Appeals and of the State of Alabama, despite the decision of the Supreme Court in South Carolina vs. United States,' in which this Court held that the dispensation of whiskey by the state, though as an incident to its police power, did not render such dispensation free from the federal tax. We fail to see why banking or production of electric power should be regarded as a private enterprise when either a state or the federal government engages in it. It can be argued with equal force that when an individual engages in banking or power production, he engages in a public enterprise. The line of demarcation between banking or power production on the one hand, and insurance, transportation, education or police protection on the other, is very indistinct, except in the length of time in which states have been engaged in such enterprises. Assuming that the difference lies in the fact that here the state or the federal government engages in this enterprise for profit, if there is any, then we see no reason why a state or the federal government should be taxable on this method of raising revenue, and not on the method of raising revenue through taxation.

The State of Alabama and the Circuit Court of Appeals also argue that in so far as the federal government or the state engages in a private enterprise, it divests itself of its sovereignty. We think this is a non-sequitur. It is well possible for sovereigns, qua sovereigns, to engage in private enterprises. We are cognizant of the legal fiction which obtains in the courts of law, that when the government engages in litigation, it divests itself of its sovereignty and stands before the court in the capacity of a private litigant. 

That is only another way of saying that the same rules of substantive and procedural law will be made applicable to both parties. This rule also has its exceptions; for example, 18 199 U. S. 437. [230]

in the taxation of costs, payment of interest, limitations of actions, etc. But we see no reason why this fiction should be drafted in aid of a tax which otherwise might be considered improper.

The remainder of the corporation taxes were brought before the Supreme Court on the argument that they imposed undue burdens on interstate commerce. The most important of these was the franchise tax of New Jersey, levied by the state on telephone companies and similar corporations doing business in the state. It w a gross receipts tax based on the business done within the state, but it also provided that where the service is rendered beyond the state lines, then the gross receipts tax should be paid on that portion of business done in interstate commerce, as the length of their lines in New Jersey bears to the length of their lines over which their business is done. 

Two arguments were advanced by the New Jersey Bell Telephone Company against the validity of the tax. The company argued (1) that a gross receipts tax imposed on corporations engaged in interstate commerce cannot be valid, unless it is imposed in lieu of all taxes payable by the corporation, whereas here the corporation was not relieved from the payment of all other taxes, but only of taxes on intangible property, and (2) that the tax as imposed constituted a burden on interstate commerce. The highest court of the state overruled both contentions and sustained the validity of the tax. The Supreme Court did not pass on the first point, but held that in view of the fact that the property itself had already been taxed, a tax of $3,200 per mile of lines for the mere right to use the streets was wholly disproportionate to the value of the right taxed. The Court held that this tax based on gross receipts from interstate commerce was a burden on such commerce, hence uncon- [231]

stitutional.19 Justice Holmes, with whom Justice Brandeis agreed, dissented in the case on the ground that the state had a right to impose a gross receipts tax on the use of its streets, and that the apportionment of gross receipts between the intrastate and interstate service in long distance calls was clearly reasonable.

The corporation tax law of Illinois came up for consideration in two cases. This law requires each corporation doing business in the state to pay an annual license fee of five cents for each $100 on the proportion of its issued capital stock, represented by business transacted and property owned in the state. A foreign corporation, doing business in the state, argued that the interstate transactions, represented by sales made in Illinois to other states, should not be included in the basis of the tax. But the Supreme Court, affirming the decision of the Illinois courts, held that the tax was not a burden on interstate commerce.20

This provision in the corporation tax of Illinois also provides that in no event shall the amount of such license fee or franchise tax be less than that required of corporations having no property or doing no business in the state. 

In the case of corporations having no property located in the state, and transacting no business therein, the Act provides a graduated scale of franchise taxes, and corporations which have issued more than twenty million dollars of capital stock must pay a license fee of $1,000. 

The St. Louis-Southwestern Railway Company is a 

Missouri corporation having an authorized capital stock of $130,000,000, of which over $36,000,000 was actually issued and outstanding. The total value of the property was about $75,800,000, and the total business done during the year etc. 

19 No. 254-New Jersey Bell Telephone Co. vs. State Board of Taxes, 

Decided January 6, 1930. 

Decided May 

20 No. 375-Western Cartridge Co. vs. Emmerson. 

19, 1930. [232]

amounted to over $18,000,000. The value of the railroad's property in Illinois was about $786,000, and the business transacted by the railroad in the state in 1926 amounted to over $2,008,000. Computing the license fee or franchise tax on the basis of the property located or business done in the state, the tax amounted to only $135.21. Pursuant to the provision of the corporation tax, however, the Secretary of State of Illinois assessed the required minimum fee of $1,000.

The railroad brought suit to enjoin the collection of the tax on the ground that it would place an unreasonable burden upon interstate commerce, and deprive the railroad of its privileges and immunities guaranteed by the Constitution; also that the tax was without due process of law, and contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution. 

The District Court in Illinois dismissed the bill of complaint, but its decree was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit, which held that the tax as imposed was an unlawful burden upon interstate commerce, and was depriving the railroad of property without due process of law.

From this decision the State of Illinois appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States. It argued that the minimum fee provisions of the Illinois Corporation Act were reasonable in amount, and that therefore the statute did not impose a burden upon the interstate commerce of the railroad. The case was argued, but before the close of the term the Court ordered that the case be restored to the docket for reargument. 21 In a case which came from North Carolina, a corporation complained of the tax imposed on its "corporate excess," which included stock held by it in a foreign corpora- 

21 No. 98-Stratton, Secy. vs. St. Louis Southwestern Ry. Co. Case pending. [233]

tion. It argued that the tax law arbitrarily discriminates against corporations, because individual owners of shares of stock in foreign corporations are not subject to tax. This case, however, was dismissed without consideration of the merits of the controversy. The corporation had failed to appeal from the decision of the Board of County Commis sioners to the State Board of Assessment. The Supreme 

Court of North Carolina dismissed the case for that reason, and on appeal the Supreme Court dismissed the case for lack of jurisdiction, because the judgment of the court below was based on a non-federal ground sufficient to support it.22 

A similar procedure was required of a Missouri corporation which complained that its shares of stock were discriminated against by the taxing authorities, and the controversy was brought to the Supreme Court. The company had brought suit to enjoin the collection of twenty-five per cent. of the taxes levied against its shares of stock on the ground that the taxing authorities of the county had deliberately, intentionally, systematically and arbitrarily assessed that property at one hundred per cent. of its true value, whereas they assessed all other property in the county at amounts not exceeding seventy-five per cent. of their true value and, in addition, omitted from the assessment roll such classes of property as poultry and sucking animals. It claimed that the tax, as applied, was arbitrarily discriminatory and therefore contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution.

This case was dismissed by the highest court of the state on the ground that the company had not pursued its remedies before the county and state boards of equalization. It held that had the company applied to the State Tax Commission before the tax books were turned over to the collector, the 

22 No. 349-Garysburg Mfg. Co. vs. Board of Commissioners of Pen der County. Decided October 28, 1929. [234]

commission might have removed the discrimination complained of in the imposition of the tax. In a prior decision rendered by the Supreme Court of Missouri in a similar case, however, that court had held that the State Tax Commission had no power to give such relief. In reliance upon that decision, the company brought this suit, but here the Missouri Supreme Court expressly overruled its former decision.

The Supreme Court of the United States held that the company could not thus be denied an opportunity to defend its substantive rights and accordingly reversed the decision of the lower court and remanded the case for further proceedings.23 Mr. Justice Brandeis, delivering the opinion. of the Court, said: "We are of opinion that the judgment of the Supreme Court of Missouri must be reversed, because it has denied to the plaintiff due process of law-using that term in its primary sense of an opportunity to be heard and to defend its substantive right."

A similar problem of discrimination came from North Carolina. But here the corporation applied to the federal courts for an injunction to restrain the collection of the tax, and relief was denied on the ground that the corporation had an adequate remedy at law.

The complaint here was that the assessment was intentionally and arbitrarily discriminatory, that the property in question was assessed at its full value as provided by statute, whereas all other property in the county was assessed at 60 per cent. of its value. The federal district court in North 

Carolina dismissed the bill of complaint, holding that the taxpayer had an adequate remedy at law by paying the tax and suing to recover the amount overpaid, although by pro- 

23 No. 464-Brinkerhoff-Faris Trust & Savings Co. vs. Hill, Treas- 

Decided June 2, 1930. urer. [235]

ceeding in the state courts, the taxpayer might have had standing in a court of equity. This was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit, and the taxpayer brought the case to the Supreme Court of the United States. This Court sustained the decision of the court below.24 Chief Justice Hughes, delivering the opinion of the 

Court, held that "Where a state statute creates a new equitable right of a substantive character, which can be enforced by proceedings in conformity with the pleadings and practice appropriate to a court of equity, such enforcement may be had in a federal court provided a ground exists for invoking the federal jurisdiction. *** But the enforcement in the federal courts of new equitable rights created by States is subject to the qualification that such enforcement must not impair any right conferred, or conflict with any inhibition imposed, by the 

Constitution or laws of the United States. * * * A state statute of a mere remedial character, such as that which the petitioner invokes, can not enlarge the right to proceed in a federal court sitting in equity, and the federal court may, therefore, be obliged to deny an equitable remedy which the plaintiff might have had in a state court."

Property Taxes. 

The last three cases discussed above, strictly speaking, belong to the discussion of personal property taxes. We have included them in the discussion of taxes imposed on corporations, because corporations are peculiarly interested in guarding their shares of stock against burdensome and 

24 No. 270-Henrietta Mills vs. Rutherford County, N. C. Decided 

April 14, 1930. [236]

discriminating taxes. We now come to other personal property taxes considered by the Court. 

In one case the Court held that states may not impose taxes on property situated on federal reservations, because such property is without their sovereign jurisdiction, while in another case such ruling was made by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, and the Supreme Court declined to review that decision.

In the first case, the State of Arkansas imposed a personal property tax on blankets sold as surplus property by the Government to a private corporation. On the tax day the property was still at Camp Pike, a military reservation. 

The Court, after tracing the history of the purchase of this area by the United States, with the consent of the State Legislature, came to the conclusion that jurisdiction vested solely in the United States, exclusive of all state authority. 

The state therefore could not tax property within the reservation.25 

In the second case, a tax was imposed by the County of Gallatin, Montana, on personal property situated in the Yellowstone National Park, but owned by a Delaware corporation. When the Court below held that the property could not be taxed by the state, because the area was ceded by it to the United States, the county petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision on the ground that the legislature of the state could not have ceded that area relinquishing its taxing powers. This, it maintained, would be contrary to the provision in the Montana constitution that the power to tax corporations or corporate property shall not be relinquished or suspended. The petition, however, was denied.26 

25 No. 2-Surplus Trading Company vs. Haynie. Decided June 3, 1930. 

26 No. 96-County of Gallatin et al. vs. Yellowstone Park Transpor tation Co. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. [237]

Another personal property tax, which was held to be without due process, because the property was without the taxing jurisdiction of the state, came from Virginia. Here a resident of Massachusetts left property, consisting of securities of an Illinois corporation, to a Maryland corporation in trust for the benefit of his son who was a resident of Virginia, and the securities themselves were never brought into the State of Virginia. The state imposed a personal property tax on the theory that the beneficial owner of the property was a resident of the state. The Supreme Court, in an opinion by Mr. Justice Holmes, held the tax invalid.27

This decision proved a boomerang to the author of the opinion, for when the Minnesota Inheritance Tax Case was decided, Mr. Justice McReynolds relied on this opinion and applied the principle expressed therein to inheritance taxes. 28 The remaining property taxes considered were in the nature of assessments of benefits, or, as a preliminary thereto, in the nature of laws relating to bond issues for the purpose of effecting improvements.

In a case involving assessment of benefits the question arose whether the Government may keep the money assessed without instituting the contemplated improvements. This happened in the District of Columbia. In 1912, Congress passed an act authorizing the extension of a certain street. 

The Commissioners acquired the necessary land for the extension of the street by condemnation proceedings, and then assessed the benefits against the abutting properties. 

In 1921, the plaintiff in this case paid her share of $200 to the Collector of Taxes of the District of Columbia. In 

1927 she brought action for a return of the $200, because 

27 No. 20-Safe Deposit & Trust Co. of Baltimore vs. Virginia. De cided November 25, 1929. 

28 No. 26-Farmers Loan & Trust Co. vs. Minnesota; No. 417Baldwin vs. Missouri, supra. [238]

the District had failed to extend that street and had abandoned the project. The question was submitted to a jury which found in favor of the plaintiff, judgment was entered, and the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia affirmed that judgment.

The District of Columbia brought the case to the Supreme Court, maintaining that, since the extension of the street was authorized by an Act of Congress, the Commissioners of the District could not be held to have abandoned the project, because it was not within their power to act contrary to the law of Congress. The Supreme Court affirmed the decision of the lower courts. It held, in an opinion delivered by Justice Sanford, that the consideration for the assessment was the construction of improvements, and since the improvements were in fact abandoned, the District was under obligation to return the money collected.29

The remaining cases challenged the validity of statutes creating sanitary districts, drainage districts, water supply districts, road districts and conservancy districts. The state laws creating the districts were attacked either because they did not provide for proper notice and hearing to be given to property owners on the proposed improvements, or because they attempted to delegate legislative powers to nonelective officers, or executive powers to judicial officers, or because the assessments provided for would result in arbitrary discrimination contrary to the equal protection clause of the Fourteenth Amendment. 

It is interesting to compare the equal protection argument in two of these cases. In the one the appellant argued that the statute violated the equal protection clause, because it provided for a different rate of taxation on different 

29 No. 44-District of Columbia vs. Thompson. Decided February 

24, 1930. [239]

classes of property in the same district (presumably the difference in rates related to the amount of benefit).30 In the other, the appellant argued that the statute was defective, because it imposed the same rate of taxation irrespective of the benefit derived by the taxpayers.31 

Taxes on Automobiles and Fuels. 

The extensive use of the automobile as a means of transportation over public highways has necessitated large road construction programs throughout the states. Recently ordinary macadamized or tarred roads were considered adequate to meet the demand of this mode of transportation. 

Soon, however, these roads became insufficient, and now many states are widening and reconstructing the roads so as to allow four cars to pass abreast. Together with the great costs involved in the construction of these roads, there are also costs of upkeep, of policing, of administration, etc. 

Since these roads are constructed mainly for the convenience of those using automobiles, and since the main wear and tear is due to automobiles, the states have within recent years adopted the policy of raising their revenues for this purpose from the owners of motor vehicles and from the sales of motor fuels. One can readily see that the old method of assessing the benefits of road construction against the abutting property owners in sparsely populated districts would prove disastrous where millions of dollars are required to construct a road.

In imposing taxes or fees on the use of the public highways, the states have classified the motor vehicles by weight, by the types of tires (solid or pneumatic), and by the use 

30 No. 544 Gutierrez vs. Middle Rio Grande Conservancy District. 

Certiorari denied January 13, 1930. 

31 No. 538-Johnson vs. State Highway Comm'n. of South Carolina. 

Decided March 3, 1930. [240]

made of the vehicles. The first two classifications are predicated upon differences in the wear and tear of the roads. 

But when the State of California imposed additional registration fees for motor vehicles, including trailers and semi-trailers depending on the weight of the vehicles, and provided that these fees be used for construction and maintenance of highways, it was claimed that the Motor Vehicle Act of the state was unconstitutional as imposing "tolls" for the use of highways, contrary to the Federal Highway Aid Act. This contention was overruled by the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Mr. Justice Stone, who said:32 "The present registration fees cannot be said to be tolls in the commonly accepted sense of a proprietor's charge for the passage over a highway or bridge, exacted when and as the privilege of passage is exercised. *** The fact that registration fees are imposed generally upon all residents who use motor vehicles within the state, without reference to any particular highways or to the extent or frequency of the use, and that, as in California, they are not exacted of non-resident automobilists passing through the state (1923 California Statutes, c. 266, Sec. 47), marks them as demands of sovereignty, not of proprietorship, and likens them to taxes rather than tolls. The fact that they may have been held justified, in other connections, because of their similarity to 'tolls for the use of highways' affords no basis for saying that the present fees are prohibited tolls within the meaning of the Federal 

Highway Act."

The third classification, depending on the use of motor vehicles, in effect imposes franchise taxes for the various uses of the public highways; whether for pleasure transpor- 

32 No. 86-Carley & Hamilton vs. Snook; and No. 267-Cottingham vs. Snook. Decided February 24, 1930. [241]

tation, private commercial use, or transportation of persons or property for hire. These three classifications are not mutually exclusive. And so, in the former cases, the validity of the Motor Vehicle Act was attacked, because in imposing the additional registration fees dependent on weight a distinction was made between pleasure vehicles and those used for transportation "of passengers for hire or for transportation of property.' And in another case the statute was assailed because a further differentiation was made between public motor carriers operating over regular routes and fixed termini, and other public or private carriers. The 

Court held,33 in an opinion by Mr. Justice McReynolds: 

"The power of a State in respect to classification has often been declared by opinions here. We are unable to say that there was no reasonable basis for the one under consideration."

Similar cases came from the State of Iowa, and were disposed of on the authority of the California cases.34

The State of California also imposed a tax on the sale of motor fuels. The law licensed the distribution of motor fuels, imposed a tax of three cents per gallon, and provided that the revenue thus received shall be used for construction and maintenance of roads. Suit was brought to enjoin the collection of this tax not by the dealers who were taxed, but by their purchasers of motor fuels. These argued, as in the above case, that the tax amounted to a "toll" imposed for the use of highways aided by the federal government, contrary to the Highway Aid Act (39 Stat. 355; 42 Stat. 212). 

33 No. 13-Bekins Van Lines, Inc. vs. Riley. Decided November 25, 1929. 

34 No. 65-Iowa Motor Vehicle Association vs. Board of R. R. 

Comm'rs of Iowa; and No. 69-Hawkeye Stages, Inc. vs. Board of R. R. 

Comm'rs. Decided January 6, 1930. [242]

The Supreme Court held that the appellants had no standing to attack the validity of the law.35 

The State of Mississippi likewise imposed a tax on gasoline, and the application of the tax was assailed by a dealer who adopted a rather novel method of selling gasoline, which he maintained constituted interstate commerce, but which the State of Mississippi maintained was merely a scheme for evading the tax.

The Superior Oil Company imported gasoline from outside the state and sold most of it to fishermen in Louisiana. 

The course of its buisness was as follows: At Biloxi, Miss., there are a number of manufacturing plants where raw shrimp and oysters are packed for commercial purposes.

The supply of shrimp and oysters comes from Louisiana. 

They are taken by gasoline propelled boats and sold exclusively to packers in Biloxi, Miss. Whenever a fisherman needs gasoline, he informs one of the packers at Biloxi, and the latter orders it from the Superior Oil Company. The oil company then draws the gasoline from its storage tanks into 50-gallon drums, which are delivered to the master of the packer's boat, and are consigned to the packer at a place in Louisiana. The boat transports the gasoline to Louisiana and there it is delivered to the fisherman who ordered it. 

Payment is made to the oil company by the packer under a monthly contract, and the packer subsequently collects from the fisherman.

The taxing authorities of Mississippi imposed a tax on these sales of oil, as sales of oil in Mississippi, for title to the oil passed upon delivery to the boat of the packer. They also held that the method of shipment and the form of the bill of lading, which purported to transfer the title after the boats reached Louisiana, constituted a scheme to evade the Mississippi tax. The Superior Oil Company resisted the 

35 No. 12-Williams vs. Riley. Decided November 25, 1929. 

[243]

tax, for the recovery of which the Attorney General of the State brought this action. The trial court in Mississippi sustained the contentions of the oil company, but its decision was reversed by the Supreme Court of the state, which held that these sales were subject to the gasoline tax. This decision was affirmed by the Supreme Court in an opinion by 

Mr. Justice Holmes.36

Gasoline taxes are only one class of taxes imposed on motor fuels. There are a number of taxes preceding the final sale to the consumer. The taxes in fact begin with the extraction of the crude oil from the ground, and this year the Court had occasion to consider the Oklahoma Oil Production Tax and the Louisiana Severance Tax. 

The first of these involved the question whether that tax may be applied to royalties received by an Indian out of his allotted lands. This question was before the Supreme 

Court in another case in the 1927 term, but in that case the complainant sought to enjoin the state auditor from collecting the tax, and the Supreme Court of Oklahoma held that an injunction was not the proper remedy in accordance with the laws of the state. The Supreme Court of the United 

States dismissed the case, because the decision of the state court was based on a non-federal ground sufficient to support the conclusion reached."

In the present case the Indian paid the tax and sought to recover the same, but the Supreme Court of Oklahoma held that the tax was properly collected. This was reversed by the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Mr. Justice Stone, on the ground that in accordance with the agreement between 

36 No. 28-Superior Oil Co. vs. State of Mississippi. Decided February 

24, 1930. 

37 McCoy vs. Shaw, 277 U. S. 302. [244]

the United States and the Choctaw and Chickasaw Tribes the allotted lands are not taxable.38 

The State of Oklahoma argued that the production tax statute imposed a tax not on the lands, but on personal property after it was taken from the land, and this was the basis of the decision in the court below. The Supreme Court held, to the contrary, that the exemption from taxation extended to any legal interest in the allotment. 

The State of Louisiana was more successful with reference to its production tax. Under the Severance Tax law of the state, the tax on crude oil or petroleum is imposed on the basis of specific gravity of the oil extracted from the land. 

Thus, on oil of 28 degrees gravity and below, the tax is 4 cents per barrel; on oil having a gravity between 28 degrees and 31 degrees, the tax is 44 cents, and so on, until on oil above 43 degrees gravity the tax is 11 cents per barrel. The

Ohio Oil Company brought suit to enjoin the collection of the tax imposed upon it, maintaining that the tax, as imposed, was arbitrarily discriminatory, since the taxes imposed on the various types of oil are not in direct proportion to their value.

The Federal District Court in Louisiana, where the suit was brought to enjoin the collection of this tax, sustained the validity of the act on the ground that the state had a right to tax oil on the basis of quality rather than on the basis of value.

The Ohio Oil Company appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States, which affirmed the decree of the District Court, in an opinion delivered by Chief Justice Hughes. In affirming the decision, the Supreme Court 39 

38 No. 50-Carpenter vs. A. S. J. Shaw. 

39 No. 440-Ohio Oil Co. vs. Conway. 

Decided January 6, 1930. 

Decided April 14, 1930. [245]

held that the state had wide discretion in the imposition of taxes. It is not limited to ad valorem taxation, but may impose different taxes and vary the rates of excises upon various products, so long as the classification rests upon some ground of difference having a fair and substantial relation to the object of the legislation, so that all persons similarly circumstanced shall be treated alike. A classication according to gravity of oil cannot be regarded as palpably arbitrary, especially in view of the fact that the oil industry resorts to the factor of gravity in fixing the schedule of prices for such oils. The state, however, is not required to grade its tax strictly in accordance with the value of the oil. [246]

CHAPTER IX. 

Trade Regulation Restraint of Trade. 

Trade practices in the moving picture industry came before the Supreme Court shortly before the end of the term. These were suits in equity brought by the Government under the Sherman Anti-Trust Act¹ to restrain the

Famous Paramount Lasky Corporation, the First National 

Pictures, Inc., and a number of other moving picture producers from continuing their restraint of interstate commerce. These violations, the Government contended, were effected by means of certain contracts entered into by the producers and the theatres exhibiting moving pictures. 

Judge Thatcher, now Solicitor General of the United States, presided over the cases. He held that one group of defendants, among them the Paramount Famous Lasky Corporation, was guilty of violating the Sherman Act and he issued an injunction. The producers appealed to the 

Supreme Court.2 The main question they raised was whether the "Standard Exhibition Contract" and the "Rules of Arbitration" employed by them constituted conspiracies in restraint of interstate commerce; also whether these are not prima facie lawful, being part of the Trade Practices approved by the Federal Trade Commission.

In the case of the First National Pictures, Inc., and several others, the Government charged that the operations of 

1 Act of July 2, 1890; 26 Stat. 209. 

2 No. 820-Paramount Famous Lasky Corp. vs. United States. Juris diction shown June 3, 1930. [247]

the "Credit Committees of the Film Board of Trade" con stituted an unreasonable restraint of trade, contrary to the 

Sherman law. Here, Judge Thatcher disagreed with the contentions of the Government and dismissed the bill of complaint. From this decision the Government appealed.³

The provision in the Clayton Anti-Trust Act* concern ing "interlocking directorates" was considered by the Supreme Court in several cases this term. This provision forbids the acquisition of stock of one corporation by another, where the effect might be substantially to lessen competition or to tend to create a monopoly in any line of commerce.

The Federal Trade Commission had ordered the International Shoe Company to divest itself of the stock and properties of the McElwain Company, acquired in violation of the Clayton Act. In prior decisions by the Supreme 

Court, it was held that where a corporation acquires the stock of another corporation in violation of the Clayton Act, and then, by virtue of its ownership of stock in the newly acquired corporation, it also acquires the assets of that corporation, and then the Commission institutes proceedings under the Clayton Act, the Commission cannot order the corporation to divest itself of the property and stock. In this case, the International Shoe Company acquired the stock of the McElwain Company, the Commission then instituted proceedings, and immediately afterwards the 

International, by virtue of its ownership of stock, also acquired the assets of the McElwain Company. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the First Circuit held that under such circumstances, the Commission had the power to order the company to divest itself of the property and stock. 

3 No. 856-United States vs. First National Pictures, Inc. Jurisdic tion shown, June 3, 1930. 

4 38 Stat. 730; Sec. 18, Title 15, U. S. C. 

5 Thatcher Manufacturing Co. vs. Federal Trade Commission, 272 U. 

S. 554. [248]

This left the state of the law pretty much in the same position as feeding powders to a horse, where the success of the operation depends upon who acts first. Thus, if the company acquires the property first, then the Commission. is powerless; if the Commission gets ahead and files a complaint, then the company is out of luck. 

This was the real question to be settled by the Supreme Court. But in the course of the argument, the International 

Shoe Company also maintained that Section 7 of the Clayton Act really did not apply, because there was no substantial lessening of competition due to the stock acquisition; that the McElwain Company had been in a failing condition and therefore could not compete with the International, even if it tried to; furthermore, that the McElwain Company sold a different type of shoe, therefore there was no competition altogether, and hence there could be no lessening of competition.

The Federal Trade Commission argued, in opposition, that there was ample evidence on the question of competition, and that this finding of fact was final and conclusive. 

On this the Commission had already been sustained by the 

Circuit Court of Appeals. But the Supreme Court in an opinion delivered by Justice Sutherland reversed that decision, holding that there was no evidence to sustain the finding of a substantial lessening of competition between the two companies.

The Court thus set aside the findings of the Commission, despite the provision in the Clayton Act that the Commission's findings as to facts, if supported by testimony, shall be conclusive. In doing so, the Court relied on its previous 

6 No. 42-International Shoe Company vs. Federal Trade Commis sion. Decided January 6, 1930. 

7 38 Stat. 730. Sec. 21, Title 15, U. S. C. See also Hankin: Con clusiveness of Federal Trade Commission's Findings as to Facts. Michigan Law Review, January, 1925. [249]

decision in Federal Trade Commission vs. Curtis Publish ing Company, in which Mr. Justice McReynolds delivering the opinion of the Court said: "If there be substantial evidence relating to such facts from which different conclusions reasonably may be drawn, the matter may be and ordinarily, we think, should be remanded to the Commission-the primary fact-finding body-with direction to make additional findings, but if from all the circumstances it clearly appears that in the interest of justice the controversy should be decided without further delay the court has full power under the statute so to do."

It is interesting to note that in the Curtis Company case, Chief Justice Taft had written a "doubting" opinion, in which he said: "If this means that where it clearly appears that there is no substantial evidence to support additional findings necessary to justify the order of the commission complained of, the court need not remand the case for further findings, I concur in it. It is because it may bear the construction that the court has discretion to sum up the evidence pro and con on issues undecided by the commission, and make itself the fact-finding body, that I venture with deference to question its wisdom and correctness. *** I only register this doubt because I think it of high importance that we should scrupulously comply with the evident intention of Congress that the federal commission be made the factfinding body, and that the court should, in its rulings, preserve the board's character as such, and not interject its views of the facts where there is any conflict in the evidence." 

8 260 U. S. 568. [250]

In the present case, however, Chief Justice Taft sided with the majority, while Mr. Justice Brandeis, who concurred in Chief Justice Taft's "doubting" opinion, now agreed with Mr. Justice Stone who wrote the dissenting opinion in the International Shoe Case. 

In commenting on the provision of the Clayton Act concerning the conclusiveness of the Commission's findings as to facts, Justice Stone said: "Congress has thus forbidden the substitution of the judgment of courts for that of the commission where it is founded upon evidence. Conforming to this requirement, I cannot say that its conclusions here lack the prescribed support. Even without such statutory limitation this Court will not set aside the findings of an administrative board or commission, upheld, as in the present case, by a unanimous court below, unless the record establishes that clear and unmistakable error has been committed."

Mr. Justice Stone then proceeded to show that there appeared abundant evidence upon which the Commission. had based its conclusions. 

Two other cases involved the application of the above provision in the Clayton Act. Both cases concerned the same parties and the same acquisition of stock. The Pittsburgh & West Virginia Railway Company, as stockholder of the Wheeling & Lake Erie Railway Company, sought to prevent the latter from using the union station of the Cleve land Union Terminals Company. The Pittsburgh & West 

Virginia charged that the arrangements to use the new station had been brought about through an unlawful acquisition of the Wheeling stock by the New York Central, the 

Nickel Plate and B. & O. Railroads, competing carriers of the Wheeling; that this enabled the competing carriers to elect new directors, who then entered into a contract for the [ 251]

use of the union station at Cleveland. The Interstate Commerce Commission declared this acquisition of stock unlawful, but before this ruling of the Commission was rendered, the new directors had entered into the above contract. The 

Commission later granted a certificate of convenience and necessity for the use of the union station. 

The Pittsburgh & West Virginia, as stockholder of the Wheeling & Lake Erie, sought to enjoin the execution of this contract, on the ground that it had been made possible only through the unlawful acquisition of the stock, but the 

Circuit Court of Appeals held the Pittsburgh was not entitled to an injunction, and the Supreme Court declined to review that decision." 

In the second case, the Pittsburgh & West Virginia, as connecting carrier of the Wheeling & Lake Erie, sought to enjoin the enforcement of the order of the Commission granting the certificate of public convenience and necessity for the use of the station. Here the lower court refused to grant the injunction, and the Pittsburgh & West Virginia appealed to the Supreme Court. But the Court held, in an opinion by Justice Brandeis, that the Pittsburgh's suit must be dismissed on the ground that neither as stockholder nor as connecting carrier of the Wheeling & Lake Erie, could the Pittsburgh & West Virginia bring suit to enjoin the order of the Commission.10 9911 

The provision in the Clayton Act prohibiting "tying contracts," ,"11 was also considered by the Supreme Court during this term. This provision prohibits any person from selling goods in interstate commerce on the condition that

the buyer also purchase or lease another product from the seller. Ever since the enactment of the Clayton Act, there has been much speculation as to whether this provision was applicable to patented devices, as for example, where the patentee licenses the manufacture of some parts of the patented structure, on the condition that the licensee would purchase other parts from the patentee. On this question the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit last year held that this condition in a contract was contrary to the Clayton Act, and the Supreme Court denied a writ of certiorari in the case.¹2 In other words, this much debated question was not settled by the Supreme Court. 

This year a similar question came to the Supreme Court from the Second Circuit and the Court granted a writ of review.18 The American Patents Development Corpora tion has a patent on a method for refrigerating foodstuffs by means of solid carbon dioxide. The licensee of the patent, the Dry Ice Corporation of America, sells solid carbon dioxide to be used with this method, on the condition that the buyer will use its solid carbon dioxide. The Carbice 

Corporation sold its solid carbon dioxide to a user of the patented device, and the present suit was instituted by the owner of the patent to enjoin the Carbice Corporation from thus infringing the patent. The District Court in New 

York dismissed the complaint on the ground that the contract in question was a violation of Section 3 of the Clayton Act, prohibiting tying contracts, but the decision was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals, which held the patent infringed.

In several cases, private suits were instituted by corporations to enjoin their competitors from engaging in restraint

Unfair Competition.

An important phase of trade regulation is the prohibition of unfair competition. This year, the Federal Trade 

Commission again received a setback in its efforts to prevent unfair competition, when the Supreme Court decided the "Shade Shop" case. In this case, the Commission proceeded against a partnership doing business in the District of Columbia, and ordered it to cease and desist from using the name "Shade Shop," which was the name of an established competitor, thus misleading the purchasing public into believing that they were dealing with the competitor. 

The Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia held that there was no evidence of unfair competition, and the case came to the Supreme Court. This Court held that there was not sufficient public interest in the case for the Commission to have instituted proceedings under Section 5 

of the Federal Trade Commission Act.17 17 No. 8-Federal Trade Commission vs. Alfred Klesner. Decided 

October 14, 1929. [255]

This section prohibits unfair methods of competition in interstate commerce, empowers the Federal Trade Commission to prevent persons, partnerships or corporations from using such methods, and provides: "Whenever the 

Commission shall have reason to believe that any such person, partnership or corporation has been or is using any unfair method of competition in commerce, and if it shall appear to the Commission that a proceeding by it in respect thereof would be to the interest of the public, it shall issue and serve upon such person, partnership or corporation a complaint 

In the course of the argument, the members of the Supreme Court raised the question whether this proceeding was to the interest of the public. The Commission took the position that this was an administrative question, wholly within the discretion of the Commission and not subject to judicial review, especially since the statute provides “if it shall appear to the Commission," etc.; that the object of the law was to vest this discretion in the Commission, so that it might proceed in such matters as it deemed advisable in view of such limitations as appropriations, personnel, etc., and that in this respect the Commission was very much in the same position as any other public prosecutor, who is charged with the task of using his discretion when to institute proceedings.

The Supreme Court, in an opinion by Mr. Justice Brandeis, took a different position in this case, on the ground that the Commission, when instituting such proceedings, acts not only as a prosecuting agency, but also in a quasi-judicial capacity. Therefore, the Court concluded, the requirements of public interest should be passed on by the courts, and if the case appears to them not to involve a sufficient amount of public interest, they should vacate the order of the Commission without going into the merits of the controversy. [256]

We cannot agree with the result reached by the Supreme Court. First, we confess to an inability to understand why a different rule of law should apply to an agency which has both prosecuting and quasi-judicial functions than to a prosecuting agency. We see no logical connection between this difference in types of prosecuting agencies and the extent of the discretion to be exercised by them in instituting proceedings. It may well be that in passing the Federal 

Trade Commission Act, Congress might have limited the Commission's discretion in determining when a proceeding by it would be to the interest of the public. But Congress chose to vest the discretion with the Commission, as the language of the Act clearly indicates. And we do not see why the courts should take upon themselves to substitute their own judgment for that of the Commission in determining when a proceeding is for the good of the public, especially since the Commission is charged with the duty of acquainting itself with conditions prevailing in the trade and is therefore, presumably, in a better position to judge on this question.

Second: Assuming that this preliminary step in the proceedings instituted by the Federal Trade Commission is subject to judicial review, by what rules or principles of law will the courts pass on the Commission's determination?

Should the Commission take testimony in order to find evidence to that effect? Testimony of individuals may show that they were misled by the unfair methods employed, or that the innocent competitor suffered financially, for which damage the law may or may not provide a remedy, but it is hardly evidence that a proceeding by the Commission would be of public good. Such testimony would have to be supplemented by opinion evidence, in which case the question arises again, why should the Commission rely on the opinions of witnesses, when the statute in effect authorizes the [257]

Commission to rely on its own opinion in the matter. Sometimes when the public is aroused, the public interest becomes evident. But public concern is not easily aroused in specific instances of unfair competition, unless they are flagrant violations of law or decency. With reference to unfair trade practices in general, however, public indignation has sufficiently evidenced itself, when Congress passed the Federal Trade Commission Act and the legislatures of a number of states passed similar acts to prohibit unfair methods of competition.

Third: We think the Commission was right in its judgment that the unfair practice in the "Shade Shop" case warranted a proceeding by it. Commercial piracy is prevalent throughout the country. Let one through painstaking efforts engage in a lawful occupation and make it profitable, and immediately there will be others who by various and diverse unfair methods will attempt to deprive him of the fruits of his labors. One need only look through the reports of the Federal Trade Commission to see the methods to which some business men resort in their quest for gold. The methods range from misleading advertising to causing collision with a competitor's truck in order to prevent delivery of goods on time.18
