Fourth: Much stress was laid on the fact that in the "Shade Shop" case the primary controversy was between the specific competitors. That undoubtedly is so, not only in this case, but in most cases of unfair competition. There is where the unfairness converges and is felt most. Each member of the purchasing public comes in contact with an isolated manifestation of the unfair method and therefore he rarely feels the effect. The cumulative effect of all the 

18 For an analysis of unfair practices considered by the Commission through 1923, see Hankin: Functions of the Federal Trade Commission, Illinois Law Quarterly, April, 1924; see also Henderson: Federal Trade Commission. [258]

acts of the unfair method is felt by the competitor. But that is a situation which obtains in other fields in which the discretion of proceeding against the offender is entirely with the public prosecutor. For example, in case of assault or wilful destruction of property, the injured party is the one who suffers; he may have a cause of action against the offender, but that will not preclude prosecution, if the public prosecutor considers it to the interest of the public. 

The fact that the prosecution is conducted at public expense is likewise no obstacle in such matters. 

Fifth: It seems that the real reason for the Court's decision in the "Shade Shop" case, was that the controversy was mainly between the competitors, and that the aggrieved competitor can resort to the courts of law or equity for redress and should not resort to the Commission for a remedy at public expense. It has been felt, however, that litigation in the courts was too rigid, too costly, and too protracted to be adequate. This was indeed well illustrated in several cases which came to the Supreme Court during this term.

Cheney Brothers had been engaged in the manufacture and sale of silk fabrics since 1838. It acquired a reputation for excellence both with the trade and the public, and sales depended, in a great measure, upon the novelty and attractiveness of the designs. To meet demands of changing fashions, the company established a special designing department with an art director in Paris, where new designs were constantly developed. 

Cheney Brothers alleged that the Doris Silk Corporation copied the designs and sold the silks in competition with them. Cheney Brothers brought suit for unfair competition in the federal district court in New York, to enjoin further simulation of the designs. The court refused the injunction on the ground that the designs were not copyrighted and [ 259]

were perhaps not within the copyright provisions of the law. The Circuit Court of Appeals affirmed the decision on the ground that the unfair method of competition must be weighed against the danger of encouraging monopolistic prices, should injunctions be issued in such cases. The 

Supreme Court of the United States refused to review the case. 19

The Supreme Court also denied a writ of review to the 

Maytag Company, a manufacturer of washing machines.20

The company claimed that its competitor, the Meadows Manufacturing Company, had copied the design, shape and collocation of colors adopted by the Maytag Company, so as to confuse and mislead the public. The Maytag Company brought suit in the courts for damages and for unfair competition, but the courts held that the company had no redress because the designs, shapes and colors had not been patented. 

On the other hand, the Supreme Court did grant a writ of review to the Wisconsin Electric Company, which had brought suit against the Dumore Company to enjoin the use of its trademark "Dumore."21 The Wisconsin company had adopted the name "Dumore" and registered this as its trademark for the company's electrical appliances. Subsequently the Dumore Company began manufacturing "Dumore" electric washing machines. The Wisconsin Electric Company brought suit to enjoin the use of this word on the machines and also as part of the corporate name. The district court enjoined the use of the word "Dumore" in the corporate name, and the corporation changed its name to Dayton Washer Company. The district court, however, 19 No. 601-Cheney Bros. vs. Doris Silk Corporation. Certiorari de nied March 21, 1930. 

20 No. 687-Maytag Company vs. Meadows Manufacturing Co. Cer tiorari denied March 17, 1930. 

21 No. 673-Wisconsin Electric Company vs. Dumore Company. Certiorari granted, April 14, 1930. [260]

refused to enjoin the use of the word "Dumore" on the washing machines, because the products manufactured by the two concerns did not possess the same descriptive properties. On appeal this was modified by the Circuit Court of Appeals, which held that the defendant may use this word "Dumore" on the machine if accompanied by the new corporate name. 

If the real reason for the decision in the "Shade Shop" case was that the Commission should not conduct private litigation, then it is indeed puzzling why the Supreme Court declined to review the case of the Royal Baking 

Powder Company.22

In 1920, the Federal Trade Commission issued a complaint against the Royal Baking Powder Company, charging it with certain unfair methods of competition by advertising widely that its baking powder had a phosphate base, that it contained no alum, implying that alum had a deleterious physiological effect. Some eighty hearings were held before an examiner of the Commission. The proceedings lasted five years. In 1925, the examiner reported that the complaint was without merit, and that there was no violation of the Federal Trade Commission Act. After hearing arguments on the report of the examiner, the Commission, in March, 1926, entered its final order dismissing the complaint.

On July 7, 1926, the Commission entered an order vacating the order of dismissal and reopening the case for consideration of charges other than those which were the main basis of the original complaint. It appeared that in addition to the public docket in the case, the Commission also kept a secret docket, and that the order to reopen the case was based on a motion to which were attached exhibits con- 

22 No. 221-Royal Baking Powder Company vs. Federal Trade Com mission. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [261]

tained in the confidential docket, but which were not disclosed to the Royal Baking Powder Company. These exhibits, however, were considered by the Commission in ex parte conferences attended by counsel of competing companies. Later a petition was filed by seven alum-using competitors of the Royal Baking Powder Company that the order to reopen the case be amended so as to include evidence concerning a booklet published by the Royal Baking Powder Company. This booklet contained a statement of the history and purpose of the controversy together with the trial examiner's report and the order of dismissal. 

The Royal Baking Powder Company took the position that the Commission after having entered its final order of dismissal had no authority to reopen the case, but on this it was overruled by the Commission. In addition, it appeared that one of the Commissioners made the Royal Baking Powder Company the subject of comment in public addresses. 

The company began to feel that the proceeding had turned into a systematic and limitless persecution. 

The company, therefore, applied to the Supreme Court of the District of Columbia for a writ of certiorari, or in the alternative, for an injunction to restrain the Commission from proceeding with the case. The company complained of numerous examples of arbitrary and unreasonable action on the part of the Commission, such as failure to give notice, secrecy, etc. The petition for certiorari was denied in the district court on the ground that the Federal Trade Commission was not a judicial body. The case then proceeded on the question whether the company was entitled to an injunction. Both the Supreme Court and the 

Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia dismissed the suit for injunction on the ground that the company had an adequate remedy at law, namely, that should the Commission issue an order against the company, then in accordance [262]

with the Federal Trade Commission Act it may appeal to the Circuit Court of Appeals to have that order set aside. 

The company petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, maintaining that under the circumstances, the acts of the Commission in reopening the case were arbitrary and capricious, contrary to the provisions of the Federal Trade Commission Act, with reference to the procedure prescribed therein, and without due process of law. It argued that the 

Court of Appeals erred in holding that the company had an adequate remedy at law in view of the protracted proceedings, the injuries caused by the revocation of the order of dismissal and by the irregularity employed in revoking that order. But the petition was denied.

That occurred one week after the Court rendered its opinion in the "Shade Shop" case. The Royal Baking

Powder Company asked for a reconsideration of the petition for writ of review, arguing that the principles of law announced by the Court in the "Shade Shop" case were applicable to its case. The petition for reconsideration, however, was likewise denied. 

Packers and Stockyards.

In the matter of trade regulation the packers and stockyards have been favored with a special Act, which subjects them to the jurisdiction of the Secretary of Agriculture.28

This Act contains a provision that all rates or charges for any stockyard services furnished by any stockyard owner or agency shall be just, reasonable and non-discriminatory. 

The Act empowers the Secretary of Agriculture to regulate rates and charges on public stockyards.

Acting under these provisions of the Packers and Stockyards Act, the Secretary of Agriculture issued an order fix- 

23 Packers and Stockyards Act, August 15, 1921; 42 Stat. 159, 163168; Secs. 201-217, Title 7, U. S. C. 

[263]

ing rates chargeable by commission merchants at the Union 

Stockyards at Omaha, Nebraska. The commission mer chants, fifty-eight in number, brought suit to set aside the order of the Secretary, on the ground that the Packers and Stockyards Act gave him no such authority, and that the Act as construed by the Secretary was contrary to the Fifth Amendment of the Constitution, depriving the commission merchants of liberty and property without due process of law.

They argued that their work was like that of a wageearner, and that the Secretary had no more power to regulate their fees than he could fix the prices at which a laborer shall work. They also maintained that the rates fixed by the 

Secretary were without due process, in that they were manifestly unjust, unreasonable, non-compensatory and con fiscatory. 

The statutory district court in Nebraska sustained the validity of the Secretary's order, and on appeal the Supreme Court affirmed that decision in an opinion delivered by Mr. 

Justice Brandeis.24 He upheld the Secretary's construction of the Act as authorizing him to regulate commission rates. 

To the argument that the regulation as applied to the work of commission merchants was without due process, Justice Brandeis answered that on account of the manner in which the work of commission merchants was conducted, in conjunction with the services of the stockyard owner, it was affected with a public interest, and the charges for the service were therefore subject to regulation. He said: "The Union Stockyards are owned by a separate 

24 No. 45-Tagg Bros. & Moorhead et al. vs. United States et al. 

Decided February 24, 1930. Compare this case with the one in which the validity of the New Jersey Insurance Law was questioned. See p. 134 supra. [264]

corporation in which the plaintiffs have no interest and which has no interest in the commissions charged by them. But each agency occupies a certain space in the yards and Exchange building, for which an annual or monthly rental is paid. When a producer wishes to sell his live stock on the Exchange, he ships it by rail or motor truck, or drives it on foot, to an agency at the stockyards. After the stock is unloaded, it is driven by the agency to its pens, sorted, watered, fed and offered by it for sale. The feed is provided by the stockyards corporation on order of the agency and a separate charge is made therefor by the corporation. 

When a purchaser is found, the stock is driven to the yard scales and weighed. Responsibility passes to the purchaser, or to the agency acting for him, as the stock is taken off the scales. Shipments are generally sold on the day of delivery and payments are made on the same day of the next morning. The agency remits the proceeds to the shippers at once, after deducting its commissions, freight, yardage, feed, inspection and other charges. 

"It is true that performance of the specific work done by the plaintiffs does not require them to invest extensive capital. But it is essential that they employ the valuable property of the stockyards corporation, for which a charge is ultimately made to the shipper or buyer. The mere division of the stockyard services between the stockyards corporation and the market agencies does not deprive Congress of a power of regulation which it otherwise would have had. "Plaintiffs perform an indispensable service in the interstate commerce in live stock. They enjoy a substantial monopoly at the Omaha Stock Yards. They [265]

had eliminated rate competition and had substituted therefore rates fixed by agreement among themselves, without consulting the shippers and others who pay the rates. They had bound themselves to maintain uniform charges regardless of the differences in experience, skill and industry. The purpose of the regulation attacked is to prevent their services from thus becoming an undue burden upon, and obstruction of, that * * * There is here no attempt to fix anyone's wages or to limit anyone's net income. Differences in skill, industry and experience will continue to be factors in the earning power of the several plaintiffs. For, the order fixes only the charges to be made in individual transactions." commerce. [266]

CHAPTER X. 

Labor Problems-Problems of Organization 

The application of the anti-trust acts, passed to prevent restraints in interstate commerce, has given rise to a serious controversy in the field of labor problems. Section 20 of the Clayton Anti-Trust Act,' after providing for suits in equity to prevent restraints in interstate commerce, declared that no injunction shall be granted in any case growing out of a dispute concerning terms or conditions of employment, unless necessary to prevent irreparable injury to property or to a property right. Since the enactment of the law, organized labor has relied on this provision as a limitation on the powers previously exercised by the federal courts in issuing injunctions in labor disputes. 

In the case of Texas and New Orleans Railway Company vs. Brotherhood of Railway and Steamship Clerks,2 known as the Railway Clerks Case, this provision in the Clayton Law was for the first time invoked by an employer, rather than by employees. The railroad maintained that the courts exceeded their authority in granting the injunction against it and its officers, because of this limitation. In the courts below, the question arose whether this objection could be raised by the employer, or whether this provision was passed solely for the benefit of the employees. This question was not answered by the lower courts, which held that the employees had an ample property right, which could be protected by injunction. And when the case came to the 

1 Title 29, U. S. C., Sec. 52. 

2 No. 469-Texas & New Orleans Railroad Co. vs. Brotherhood of 

Railway and Steamship Clerks et al. Decided May 26, 1930. [267]

Supreme Court, Chief Justice Hughes, delivering the opinion, likewise failed to pass on that question, but held that in accordance with the previous decisions of the Court, Section 20 was merely declaratory of the existing law. He said: "It may be doubted whether Section 20 can be regarded as limiting the authority of the court to restrain the violation of an explicit provision of an act of Congress, where an injunction would otherwise be the proper remedy. It is not necessary to pass upon this point, for if it could be said that it was necessary in the present instance to show a property interest in the employees, in order to justify the court in granting an injunction, we are of the opinion that there was such an interest, with respect to the selection of representatives to confer with the employer in relation to contracts of service, as satisfied the statutory requirement." 

Company Unions.

The controversy in the Railway Clerks Case began with a demand for higher wages. After the company had refused to grant the demands of their clerical employees, the clerks brought the matter to the Board of Mediation under the provisions of the Railway Labor Act of 1926. These employees were represented before the Board by the Brotherhood of Railway and Steamship Clerks, one of the standard national railway brotherhoods. While the controversy was pending before the Board, the Brotherhood brought suit in the United States District Court in Houston, Texas, to enjoin the Southern Pacific Lines in Texas and Louisiana in its efforts to break up the Brotherhood's organization, which had represented the railroad clerks since 1918, and to re- 

3 Duplex Printing Press Company vs. Deering, 254 U. S. 443, 470. 

4 44 Stat. 577; U. S. C., Title 45, Secs. 151-163. 

[268]

strain the company from coercing the employees to join 

"The Association of Clerical Employees-Southern Pacific 

Lines," which was a "Company Union." This, the clerks argued, was contrary to Section 2 of the Railway Labor Act of 1926, which provides that representatives of both the carriers and the employees shall be designated "without interference, influence or coercion exercised by either party over the self-organization or designation of representatives by the other."”

This was the gravamen of the complaint. In reality the controversy was more fundamental, for organized labor has always regarded company unions not as bona fide organizations, but as subterfuges whereby employers seek to prevent independent labor organization, and as instruments whereby the employers themselves play the role of "labor leader," at the same time thwarting collective bargaining on the part of the employees. For this reason it was expected that the Brotherhood of Railway and Steamship Clerks would engage in a bitter fight in this particular case. 5 The Brotherhood also produced evidence of a letter, written by a vice-president to the president of the company, showing that the company was planning to get rid of the Brotherhood as the representative of the clerical employees in the then pending wage dispute, and that if the company could eliminate the Brotherhood, it would obtain a settlement of that wage dispute at a cost of about $75,000 a year, whereas any settlement with the Brotherhood would result in an increased payroll cost of about $340,000 a year. The railroad company defended that the "company union" did in fact represent the majority of its employees, and that therefore it had a legal right to deal with that association, rather than with the Brotherhood. 

5 Dunn, Robert W.: Company Unions. 

[269]

The trial court issued a temporary injunction, forbidding the company to influence or coerce members of the Brotherhood in their activities for their own union, or to coerce them to join the company union, and also to prevent them in any way from selecting their own representatives in all matters dealing with employment. The court also held that the injunction should not be construed as giving the company authority or permission for any of its officers or agents to influence such employees on the ground that the influence was being done by an individual acting on his own behalf and not for the company.

Shortly afterwards, the Brotherhood filed an information that the company had violated the temporary injunction. They complained that the carrier had recognized the company union and refused to deal with the Brotherhood; that a number of officials of the Brotherhood had been dropped from seniority rosters, their passes called in and their insurance and sick benefits terminated; that the company had docked the pay of employees for time used in the service of the Brotherhood, while others working for the company union were allowed to do so on the company's time without deduction in pay.

On the basis of this evidence, the court adjudged the railroad guilty of contempt, and imposed the following conditions for the company to purge itself of this contempt: 

The company was to cancel its recognition of the company union and to recognize the Brotherhood, reinstate the discharged employees and restore the officers of the Brotherhood to their previous status. At the same time, the court made its injunction permanent. The company purged itself of contempt, but appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals.

Here the company argued that the injunction had been erroneously issued, because (1) there was no "property [270]

right" in question and therefore the injunction was forbidden by the Clayton Act; (2) that the injunction was contrary to the decision of the Supreme Court in Pennsylvania 

Federation vs. Pennsylvania Railroad Company, and (3) that if the Railway Labor Act is interpreted to mean that a carrier cannot advise its employees concerning the selection of representatives, the Act must be held unconstitutional as depriving the railroad of an inalienable right. The company furthermore argued that the contempt order had been erroneously issued, because the recognition of the company union had not taken place until the company had evidence showing that the "association" did represent the majority of its employees.

The Court of Appeals overruled the railroad company on all these contentions and affirmed the decree of the district court. The case then came to the Supreme Court on a writ of certiorari. Here the railroad company maintained that the findings as to facts made by the lower courts were without support of evidence, that in fact the railroad did not coerce or influence the clerks in the selection of representatives, but merely advised them to support the "association" which had been in existence for a long time, but which in recent years has been dormant. Counsel for the 

Brotherhood argued, in opposition, that there was evidence to support the findings of the lower courts that the company did coerce and influence the employees, contrary to the provision of Sec. 2 of the Railway Labor Act, and argued that since both lower courts arrived at the same conclusions of fact upon evidence, this Court would not review those facts in order to set aside their findings. To the railroad's statement that the company union was an organization of long standing, though dormant, the Brotherhood observed that 

6 Pennsylvania Railroad System and Allied Lines Federation No. 90 

vs. Pennsylvania Railroad Company, 267 U. S. 203. [271]

the Association was not "dormant," but that it was "dead," and that the railroad did not awaken that association, but resuscitated it for the very purpose of interfering with the employees in their representation before the Board of Mediation. On this point the Supreme Court held, with the employees, that since there was evidence to support the conclusions of fact reached by the lower courts, this Court will not go into those facts. It therefore turned to the questions of law involved in the case.

To the railroad's argument that the decision of the lower courts was in conflict with the Supreme Court's decision in 

Pennsylvania Federation vs. Pennsylvania Railroad, the

Brotherhood answered that the rule in the Pennsylvania case did not apply to the present controversy, because in that case it was merely held that under the statute creating the old Labor Board,' the regulations of that Board could not be judicially enforced, whereas in the present controversy the question was whether Congress had a right to give the employees certain rights in their selection of representatives when mediating disputes before the Board of Mediation. 

Assuming, however, that under the Pennsylvania Case the railroad company was at liberty not to deal with the employees' union, the Brotherhood argued that the rule was no longer applicable, because in passing the Railway Labor

Act of 1926, creating the Board of Mediation, Congress intended to cure the very weaknesses of the old Act as interpreted in the Pennsylvania decision.

Counsel for the Railway compared the Labor Act of 

1926 with the provisions in the Transportation Act of 1920, creating the old Labor Board, and showed that in neither of those Acts did Congress provide for any obligation on the part of the carriers to submit to arbitration or mediation. Since there was no such mandatory provision in the 

7 Chapter 91, 41 Stat. 456, 469. [272]

earlier act, this Court held in the Pennsylvania case that the courts were without power to impose a mandatory injunction upon the Railroad to comply with a decision of that Board, and that in this respect the present Railway Labor Act of 1926 was similar to the old law. 

Chief Justice Hughes in his opinion distinguished the present case from the Pennsylvania Case. He reviewed the history of the legislation on this subject, pointing out the inadequacies of the prior law, showing how this very problem became an issue in the presidential campaign of 1924, wherein both the Republican and Democratic parties indicated dissatisfaction with the provisions of the Transportation Act relating to labor, and adopted platforms to cure these defects. He showed how later in the 69th Congress this act was finally enacted. While adhering to the policy of the old statute in providing for amicable adjustment of labor disputes and for voluntary submissions to arbitration as opposed to a system of compulsory arbitration, Chief Justice Hughes pointed out that "Congress buttressed this policy by creating certain definite obligations." He said: "The outstanding feature of the Act of 1926 is the provision for an enforceable award in arbitration proceedings. The arbitration is voluntary, but the award pursuant to the arbitration is conclusive upon the parties as to the merits and facts of the controversy submitted. *** Thus it is contemplated that the proceedings for the amicable adjustment of disputes will have an appropriate termination in a binding adjudication, enforceable as such."

Thus, while the Act itself did not provide for compulsory arbitration, the parties, having entered into the arbitration, were bound by the award, and in order to provide for fairness in the proceedings before the Board of Mediation, Congress also provided that "representatives for the [273]

​purposes of this Act shall be designated by the respective parties *** without interference, influence or coercion exercised by either party over the self-organization or designation of representatives by the other." 

It is true, Congress prescribed no penalties for the violation of this provision nor did it provide specifically that violation thereof shall be subject to injunctive relief. Chief 

Justice Hughes, after commenting on the necessity of free representation by each party to the proceedings, said: "The absence of penalty is not controlling. The creation of a legal right by language suitable to that end does not require for its effectiveness the imposition of statutory penalties. Many rights are enforced for which no statutory penalties are provided. In the case of the statute in question, there is an absence of penalty, in the sense of specially prescribed punishment, with respect to the arbitral awards and the prohibition of change in conditions pending the investigation and report of an emergency board, but in each instance a legal obligation is created and the statutory requirements are susceptible of enforcement by proceedings appropriate to each. The same is true of the prohibition of interference or coercion in connection with the choice of representatives. The right is created and the remedy exists."

The company further argued that the Railway Labor Act of 1926, if interpreted to mean that the carrier cannot advise and suggest to its employees concerning the selection of representatives, must be held unconstitutional, as depriving the railroad of an inalienable right. To this the 

Brotherhood answered that it is competent for Congress, under its powers to regulate interstate commerce, to make provision for the settlement of disputes between interstate [274]

carriers and their employees with reference to wages, rules and working conditions, to the end of preventing disputes resulting in the interruption of interstate commerce of passengers or freight. To this Chief Justice Hughes said: "We entertain no doubt of the constitutional author ity of Congress to enact the prohibition. *** Exer cising this authority, Congress may facilitate the amicable settlements of disputes which threaten the service of the necessary agencies of interstate transportation. 

In shaping its legislation to this end, Congress was entitled to take cognizance of actual conditions and to address itself to practicable measures. The legality of collective action on the part of employees in order to safeguard their proper interests is not to be disputed. 

It has long been recognized that employees are entitled to organize for the purpose of securing the redress of grievances and to promote agreements with employers relating to rates of pay and conditions of work. 

Congress was not required to ignore this right of the employees, but could safeguard it and seek to make their appropriate collective action an instrument of peace rather than of strife. Such collective action would be mockery if representation were made futile by interferences with freedom of choice. Thus the prohibition by Congress of interference with the selection of representatives for the purpose of negotiation and conference between employers and employees, instead of being an invasion of the constitutional right of either, was based on the recognition of the rights of both. * ** As the carriers, subject to the Act, have no constitutional right to interfere with the freedom of the employees in making their selections, they cannot complain of the statute on constitutional grounds." [275]

Union Rules.

Several cases involved suits to enjoin the enforcement of rules promulgated by labor unions, in which the workers, rather than the employers, undertook to dictate the terms of employment.

The Brotherhood of Painters, Decorators and Paper Hangers of America had promulgated a rule, that where an employer performs work outside of his home city, he must pay the wage scale, either of his home town or of the place where the work is conducted, whichever is higher. 

The Barker Painting Company, a New York corporation with headquarters in New York City, obtained a contract to paint a hospital at Somerville, N. J., and contracted with the local union of painters to do the work at the prevailing wage rate at Somerville, which was $8 per day. 

The prevailing rate of wages in New York City, the home town of the corporation, was $10.50 per day. When about one-third of the work had been completed, the business manager of the local union called this rule to the attention of the company, and demanded the higher wage scale for the job. The company refused, and the men left work. 

The company obtained a temporary restraining order in the U. S. District Court in New Jersey, enjoining the officers of the local union from calling the strike in violation of the contract and compelling the men to return and finish the job. Under the protection of this injunction the work was completed at the local wage rate of $8 per day. When the case came up for final hearing the company argued that the above mentioned rule of the International Union was illegal as constituting an unlawful restraint of trade, an illegal interference with interstate commerce and a conspiracy in violation of the anti-trust acts. 

The company therefore sought to have the injunction made [276]

permanent. The district court, however, dismissed the case as having become moot, since the work, for which the temporary injunction was issued, had already been completed. The company then appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit. This court affirmed the decree of dismissal of the lower court, without going into the merits of the controversy. The company then petitioned the Supreme Court of the United States to review the case, which petition was granted. 

In the Supreme Court, the company argued that the above rule constituted a boycott, a conspiracy illegal at common law and a violation of the policy enunciated in the Anti-trust Acts against combinations and conspiracies; that the rule in question was unreasonable and bore no reasonable relation to the wage scale; and that the attempt of the union to enforce the rule by combination was an unlawful invasion of the rights of the company. 

The union went fully into the circumstances and conditions which brought about the adoption of that rule. They showed that the rule had been enacted after tremendous difficulties had arisen. Outside contractors were in the habit of bringing their own men to out-of-town jobs, in which case the outside men were paid the prevailing rate of wages in their home town, while the local men on the job were paid the local rate; if the prevailing rate for the former was higher than the latter, where the work was done, the local men became very much dissatisfied, since they were doing the identical work at a lower rate. A compromise was finally reached whereby the union adopted the rule that all the workers be paid the same rate, which was to be the higher rate prevailing at the time the work was performed. For these reasons, the union argued that the rule was reasonable and not unlawful. [ 277 ]

Both parties were anxious for a final determination of this question, for on several previous occasions the Barker Painting Company had instituted suits to enjoin the Brotherhood of Painters, Decorators and Paper Hangers from enforcing this rule, and had been unsuccessful in the lower courts, and the Supreme Court denied a writ of review in each of these cases. For this reason the Barker Painting 

Company was anxious for a determination of this question by the Supreme Court. In this controversy, although the decision of the lower courts resulted in favor of the union in form, the union lost its case in substance, since the employees were compelled to finish the work at the lower wage, under the temporary injunction. For this reason the 

Brotherhood was likewise anxious for a final determination on the validity of the union rule. Although in his final brief counsel for the union argued that the lower court was right in having dismissed the case as moot, in his oral argument in the Supreme Court he joined with opposing counsel in requesting that the merits of the controversy be determined. 

Justice Holmes delivered the opinion of the Court, affirming the decision of the lower courts. The Court declared that the lower courts were right in having dismissed the case as moot and that the corporation had no right to force upon the courts a consideration of a mooted controversy. The Supreme Court likewise did not pass upon the merits or demerits of the union rule. Justice Holmes said: "Both sides desired that the Court should go farther afield. But a Court does all that its duty compels when it confines itself to the controversy before it. It 

8 Barker Painting Co. vs. Brotherhood of Painters, Decorators and 

Paperhangers et al., 273 U. S. 747; 276 U. S. 631. 

9 No. 477-Barker Painting Company vs. Local No. 734, Brotherhood of Painters, Decorators and Paperhangers of America et al. Decided May 

19, 1930. [278]

cannot be required to go into general propositions or prophetic statements of how it is likely to act upon other possible or even probable issues that have not yet arisen."

There were two other cases before the Court this year, involving the rules of labor unions. In one of these a rule of the Actors' Equity Association was questioned. Suit was brought to restrain the officers of the Association from promulgating and enforcing their rule, forbidding members from entering into contracts with any booking agent, unless he was licensed by the Association. This rule was adopted by the Association to protect its members from certain existing abuses in the profession. The licensing was in the form of a contract between the booking agent and the Association, whereby the agent was required to guarantee to each artist, with whom he contracted, twenty weeks of employment in each year at a salary not less than the average salary paid to such artist for three years prior to the agreement. The agent was to receive not more and not less than 10% of the artist's salary. The agent could not contract with an artist for a period greater than three years. 

The permit or license could be revoked by the Association after a hearing, but the procedure for such hearing was not provided for, and the right to revoke by the Association was to be absolute and non-reviewable. The Association reserved the right to change, modify or add to the provisions of the permit. A revocation of the permit put an end to the contract between the booking agent and the artist. The contract of employment could be changed upon the request of the artist. Any or all disputes between the agent and the members of the Association were to be arbitrated by the Association, and the decision of the arbitrators was to be final, with the right to amend, modify or suspend any agreement, apportion commissions, and determine any adjustments. [ 279]

The Association took active steps to enforce this resolution, and a booking agent brought suit in the federal district court in New York against the Association and its officers to enjoin them from enforcing this resolution. The suit against the Association itself was dismissed for lack of jurisdiction, but the District Court granted an injunction against the officers of the Association. The Circuit Court of Appeals reversed that decision, holding that the rule adopted by the Association was reasonable in view of the conditions which had previously existed in the profession; that the policy adopted by the Association was aimed at improving the members' positions and strengthen their bargaining power in the economic struggle; and that there was no compulsion on the part of the booking agent to deal with members of the Association. The Court held that the members of the Association, under settled law, had the right collectively to secure the benefit of a standard rate of charges and a uniform agency contract.

The booking agent petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision. We quote the manner in which he presented the question to the Supreme Court: "There is presented squarely the question whether the respondents, who represent an organization in complete control of an economic field, may so dictate to the petitioner that he must either submit to virtual slavery or give up his lawful occupation." It was apparent that the question as presented was somewhat extravagant, and the Supreme Court declined to go into the matter.¹ 10 The second union rule which came up before the Supreme Court related to jurisdiction over particular trades.

Jurisdictional differences among various unions sometimes affect employers and others as innocent bystanders. With- 

10 No. 489-William Edelstein vs. Frank Gillmore et al. Certiorari denied January 6, 1930. [ 280 ]

in recent years there have been quite a number of cases in which neither union will concede that the other has jurisdiction over a particular type of work, and if an employer gives the job to one class of workers the others go on strike. 

In this case a controversy arose among the building trade unions in St. Louis as to whether "Rockwood," or plaster board, was within the province of the carpenters or bricklayers. On one occasion, while the carpenters were installing "Rockwood," an agent of the Bricklayers' Union appeared and insisted that the bricklayers should install the "Rockwood." The result was that the carpenters withdrew and the bricklayers proceeded with the work, using a different material. Thereafter builders, architects and contractors in St. Louis refrained from specifying "Rockwood" in construction work, fearing labor difficulties. 

The Rockwood Corporation then brought suit against the Bricklayers' Union, alleging conspiracy to boycott its materials in restraint of interstate commerce, and sought to enjoin the union from asserting jurisdiction over this work. 

Both the district court and the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit found that the agent of the Bricklayers' Union had acted on his own initiative and without the knowledge of the union, and that the union had not approved or ratified his actions. The Circuit Court of Appeals further found that the evidence was insufficient to connect any person or organization with restraint in interstate commerce, or with calling any strike, or with doing any other acts which might be in violation of the federal antitrust acts. The Court took the position that even if there was a conspiracy, then the purpose was not to interfere with interstate commerce, but merely to bring about a result whereby bricklayers, rather than carpenters, would install "Rockwood"; also that any injury to interstate commerce was indirect, remote and unintended. The Court thus dis- [281]

tinguished this case from the Bedford Cut Stone Case,¹¹ on which the Rockwood Corporation relied. The Rockwood 

Corporation petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, but the petition was denied.12 

In one case the courts were called upon to use their equity powers in determining the rights of an international union over a local union. The International Typographical 

Union was going to put into effect a proposition whereby it was intended to put an end to the Trade District Unions, and the members of the Mailer's Trade District Union brought suit in the federal court to enjoin the International Union from putting that proposition into effect. The local unions argued that the proposed plan would affect their vested property rights, because the International Union had for many years received payments from members of the district unions and had permitted the district unions to elect executive officers of the International Union.

The injunction was granted, and this was affirmed by the 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit. The In ternational Union petitioned the United States Supreme Court to review this decision, maintaining that under the right to amend its constitution the International could abolish the District Unions. This petition was likewise denied.13 

11 Bedford Cut Stone Company vs. Stonecutters' Association, 274 U. 

S. 37. 

12 No. 251-Rockwood Corporation of St. Louis vs. Bricklayers' Local 

Union. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

13 No. 246-Charles P. Howard and International Typographical 

Union vs. Walter J. Weissman et al. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [282]

CHAPTER XI. 

Labor Problems-Compensation for Injuries Workmen's Compensation Acts. 

Most of the labor problems which came to the Supreme Court during the term, related to compensation for injuries sustained in the course of employment. Of these, the most significant case questioned the validity of the provision of the Workmen's Compensation Act of New York, directing that in case of death of an employee, where compensation cannot be had under the law, the employer shall pay two sums of $500 each into funds maintained by the state for vocational training of injured employees and for additional compensation to those who are totally disabled after they had been partially disabled.

In this case, an employee was killed through the negligence of the Staten Island Rapid Transit Company. The widow brought action against the company, and the claim was settled for an amount greater than she would have been entitled to under the Compensation Act of the state. This precluded the widow from compensation under the Act from the employer, who was thus obliged to pay $1,000 to the state.

The law, however, also provides that in such cases the employer may recover the amount from the party through whose negligence the death occurred. The employer's insurer paid the required sums to the state and brought suit against the railroad company for $1,000. The New York courts held that the state was entitled to the amounts paid by the insurance company and that the insurance company was [283]

entitled to recover the same from the railroad company. 

The railroad company then brought the case to the Supreme Court, arguing that the compensation act, as applied, was without due process of law and contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution, because it allowed double recovery for the same cause of action.

In an opinion by Chief Justice Hughes, the constitutionality of this law was upheld.¹ The Court held that in providing for the recovery of damages by the dependents or next of kin, the State had not exhausted its authority to provide redress for the wrong. The State may permit recovery of punitive damages, provide for recovery by the employer, or impose penalties for wrongful death. The manner in which these may be enforced, and the disposition of the amount collected, are matters within legislative discretion. In this case, therefore, the State of New York could provide for payment by the employer's insurer, giving him a cause of action against the wrongdoer and disposing of the amounts paid by the insurer by establishing the two funds.

To the railroad's argument that the Act was contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution, the Court held that a special provision for cases where there are no persons entitled to compensation was not an unreasonable classification; nor is the right of recovery by the insurer against the wrongdoer contrary to the equal protection clause, since it operates uniformly in all cases where compensation may not be had under the Act. 

Several cases which aroused no little public interest related to the conflict between state and federal jurisdiction. 

A blacksmith helper was ordered to paint angle irons in the hold of a ship, lying in navigable waters of the United 

1 No. 307-Staten Island Rapid Transit Railway Co. vs. Phoenix In demnity Co. Decided March 17, 1930. [ 284]

States, at Philadelphia. While so engaged, he was blinded by an acetylene torch used by another worker. The Pennsylvania courts sustained an award for compensation made by the State Industrial Commission. The insurer of the employer argued that the Compensation Law of the State could not apply, since the injury was sustained in the ship, while on navigable waters. It brought the case to the 

Supreme Court. The insurance company, maintained that if the Compensation Act is interpreted to apply to such injuries, it must be held unconstitutional as interfering with the exclusive admiralty jurisdiction of the federal government. The Court, in an opinion by Mr. Justice McReynolds, sustained this argument, and held that the state compensation law could not apply in this case.2 

Justice Stone, with whom Justices Holmes and Brandeis agreed, was of the opinion that the decision of the Pennsylvania courts should have been sustained, because the employee was not a seaman and the contract of employment was non-maritime, since the employee was engaged as a painter generally, and it was only accidental that the injury occurred on navigable waters.

A similar problem arose in Texas and concerned an employee of the Ford Motor Company's assembling plant at Houston. The Ford Motor Company had two vessels which it operated for the purpose of transporting its automobile parts and accessories from Detroit to various branch plants, including that located at Houston. The greater portion of the employee's duties were in the assembling plant, but he was ordered to unload the vessel. While he was in the hold, and while the vessel was anchored in navigable waters, he was injured, and later died from those injuries.

Suit was brought by the widow under the compensation 

2 No. 62-John Baizley Iron Works vs. Abraham Span. Decided 

April 14, 1930. [285]

act of Texas to recover 360 weeks' compensation at the rate of $20 per week, but the insurer of the employer (under the 

Workmen's Compensation Act) resisted recovery because the injury occurred on navigable waters, and the remedy, if any, was under the maritime law of the United States. The 

U. S. District Court in Texas held in favor of the widow, and its decision was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit.

The insurance company brought the case to the United States Supreme Court. It relied on a recent decision of the 

Supreme Court in the Strand Case, in which it held that when a stevedore was killed in the hold of a vessel in the Great Lakes while unloading coal, his widow could not recover compensation under the Workmen's Compensation 

Act of the State of Wisconsin. In opposition, the respondent maintained that this case was different, because a stevedore is a seaman whose remedy is under the Jones Seamen's Act, whereas in this case the employee did not come under the provisions of that law.

Again Justice McReynolds delivered the opinion of the Court, sustaining the contentions of the insurance company and reversing the judgment of the lower courts. The Court held that since the injury occurred while the employee was working in the ship, the fact that his employment there was an exception made no difference. 

Justice Stone concurred in the result in this case on the authority of the Strand Case, because the employee here was acting as a seaman.

The decisions in these cases, it seems to us, carries the idea of maritime jurisdiction to an extreme. It has been previously held that the mere fact that the injury occurred on navigable waters does not of itself preclude the injured 

3 Northern Coal & Dock Co. vs. Emma Strand, 278 U. S. 142. 

4 No. 81-Employers Liability Assurance Corp. vs. Myrtis Cook. De cided April 14, 1930. [286]

employee from recovering compensation under the Workmen's Compensation Acts. It has been previously held by the Supreme Court that in order to exclude jurisdiction under the state acts, not only the tort, but also the contract of employment must be maritime in character, and then only if recovery under the state act would become a burden on navigation or disturb the uniformity of the general maritime jurisdiction of the United States."

In these cases, we think, the contracts of employment were non-maritime. The painter who was blinded was not hired to work on ships. He was engaged to paint wherever he was directed to. That was his contract of employment, and it is under this contract that recovery was sought under the Pennsylvania Compensation Act. The fact that in this given instance he was painting on a ship did not make his contract of employment a maritime contract. Likewise, with the employee in the Ford assemblying plant. 

The Court proceeded on the theory that it will not look to the nature of the employment in general, but to the specific employment at the moment of injury, and the Court held that "repairing a completed ship lying in navigable waters has direct and intimate connection with navigation. and commerce." We think this is "making little fishes talk like whales." While the employer of the painter was engaged in repairing a completed ship, the employee was engaged in painting angle irons. His connection with commerce and navigation was very indirect and far removed. 

Nor do we think that allowing recovery in this case would become a burden on navigation. Under the law of 

Pennsylvania, the insurance company was directly liable to the employee for the injuries. The insurance company was 

5 Grant Smith-Porter Ship Co. vs. Rhode, 267 U. S. 469; Miller's Indemnity Underwriters vs. Braud, 270 U. S. 59.

Sultan Railway Co. vs. Department of Labor, 277 U. S. 135. 

[287]

not engaged in navigation, and the insurance contract was not a contract of marine insurance. The costs to the employer had already been incurred and paid upon payment of the premium on the policy. Therefore, so far as this case was concerned, the payment of the award made by the Industrial Commission would not have affected commerce and navigation at all.

It may be argued that allowing an award in this case may have its effect on commerce and navigation in the future. This probable effect can be calculated. One can ascertain the amount by which the premiums on such employment insurance policies would be increased, if made to cover accidents on navigable waters, and then calculate how much of this increase would reflect itself in the charges for repairing vessels. We venture a guess that the effect on commerce and navigation would be negligible, if at all. 

Nor do we think that the allowance of compensation in such cases would disturb the uniformity of the maritime jurisdiction, for in suits brought against insurance companies, the question of maritime jurisdiction need have no application. 

One case presented an interesting question of conflict of laws as between states. An employee of a Tennessee corporation was sent into Texas to work, where he was injured. 

Action was brought in Texas under the Compensation Act of that state, against the insurer of the employer, who insured the latter under the Compensation Act of Tennessee.

The insurance company defended that it was not bound under the Texas law, because there was nothing in the complaint or the evidence to show that the employer had insured himself under the Texas Act, or that the contract of employment was made in Texas or that the employee had chosen to be bound by the provisions of the Texas law. 

The insurance company further argued, that although by [ 288]

bringing action under the Texas law the employee might be said to have elected to be bound by that law, in fact the employee did not choose to be so bound, because he also brought an action in Tennessee under the Compensation Law of that state.

The federal district court in Texas held that the employee was entitled to compensation under the law of that state; furthermore, that the employee had a right to bring an action in the Tennessee courts under the compensation act of that state in order to protect himself against the running of the statute of limitations, which otherwise would have barred his action under the Tennessee law, had it been determined that he could not sue under the Texas law. This decision was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit, and the Supreme Court denied the insurance company's petition to review that decision." 

Although in most cases the compensation acts operate automatically and eliminate many difficulties, uncertainties and litigation costs such as result under the employers' liability acts or actions at common law, there are some instances of dissatisfaction, both on the part of employers and employees. Generally the dissatisfaction concerns some specific finding of the compensation board. 

Two such cases were brought to the Supreme Court this term. Application for compensation was made to the Industrial Accident Commission of California, and later application for additional compensation was made on the ground that the employer had been guilty of wilful misconduct, in having failed to protect certain dangerous machinery. The Commission awarded the compensation, but denied the application for the additional compensation. The 

California courts denied a writ of review, because under the 

7 No. 769-Amer. Mutual Liability Ins. Co. vs. McCaddrey. Certio rari denied May 5, 1930. [289]

law the findings of the Commission on questions of fact are final and conclusive, and not subject to review. The employee petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, arguing that as applied in his case, the Compensation Act deprived him of due process of law, but the petition was denied.8

In the other case, the employer and his insurer were dissatisfied with the rulings of the state compensation board. 

An employee of a smelting company in Utah lost the sight of an eye, and the vision of the other was seriously impaired. The Industrial Accident Commission made a finding that the employee suffered total and permanent disability and awarded compensation accordingly. Under the 

Utah law, the award of the Commission may be reviewed by the Supreme Court of the state, which may pass on whether the Commission acted within its powers and also whether the findings supported the award. To avoid this procedure, the company brought suit in the federal courts to enjoin the enforcement of the award, arguing that the Commission had acted outside its jurisdiction and had made the award contrary to evidence. The district court dismissed the bill of complaint, holding that the case in the federal court must be limited to the question whether the State Commission had jurisdiction. This decision was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals, which also held that one cannot resort to the federal courts to attack collaterally an award of a state commission. And the Supreme

Court denied the company's petition to review this decision."

Other petitioners brought their cases to the Supreme Court objecting to the operation of the compensation acts, 8 No. 545-Hunter vs. Baash-Ross Tool Co. et al. Certiorari denied 

January 13, 1930. 

9 No. 718-U. S. Smelting, Refining & Mining Co. vs. Evans. Cer tiorari denied April 28, 1930. [290]

but their main dissatisfaction was merely with the amount of their compensation, although this was not the reason assigned in the petitions. In one case, an injured painter brought suit against his employer, a mining company, for damages sustained. The courts held that his injury came within the provisions of the compensation act of Idaho and that the courts had no jurisdiction." 

In the other case, an injured worker rejected the award under the compensation law of Arizona and brought suit in the federal court, maintaining that the law was unconstitutional because it provided for no trial by jury, nor for the production of witnesses, etc. The employee appealed his personal injury case directly to the Supreme Court from the district court, and, of course, the appeal was dismissed.¹¹

It may be that the amounts awarded under the various compensation acts are indeed inadequate. But when one considers the costs involved to both employers and employees and to the state under the employers' liability acts or at common law, and what are the final benefits, one wonders whether the difference in the verdicts obtained makes up for the losses involved.

Federal Employers' Liability Act. 

The outstanding example of inadequacy regarding compensation for injured employees in a modern industrial system is the Federal Employers' Liability Act.12 This term, a comparatively small number of cases under this Act were considered by the Court, yet there were 43 such cases, which is indeed a large number, considering that they are personal injury cases and have no business before the Su- 

10 No. 321-Aschenbach vs. Sullivan Mining Co. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. 

11 No. 429-Ortega vs. Magma Copper Co. Decided December 9, 1929. 

12 35 Stat. L. 65. 45 U. S. C., Sections 51-59. 

[291]

preme Court altogether. This number of cases is also an index of the large amount of litigation under the Act in the lower federal and state courts. The very fact that there is so much litigation under the Federal Employers' Liability Act is in itself an indication of its inadequacy. 

Within the past number of years the Supreme Court has granted writs of review in a comparatively large number of cases arising under this law. Yet there were few among them in which the Court had occasion to or did promulgate new principles or rules of law, and in many of them the Court concerned itself with mere questions of fact. As one reads through these cases considered by the Court within the past few years, one gets the impression that the Court merely acts as a counterbalancing influence to the tendency in the trial courts to award compensation in all cases. It is interesting to note, however, that since Chief Justice Hughes took office, in February, 1930, the Court has had occasion to pass on thirteen petitions to review the decisions of the lower courts in Federal Employers' Liability Act cases, and all of these petitions were denied. We do not know whether this indicates a change of policy in the Court. At any rate, it is a hopeful sign.

The Federal Employers' Liability Act, itself, is replete with ancient doctrines, wholly inapplicable to a modern industrial system and especially to the operation of a railroad system. In passing that Act, Congress gave railroad employees or their administrators a right to recover damages for injury or death, if caused through the negligence of the employer or his servants. In this respect the Act merely abrogated the fellow servant doctrine of the common law. It retained, however, the doctrine of assumption of risk and of contributory negligence, except where it is also shown that the railroad violated the Federal Safety Ap- [292]

pliance Act 13 or the Federal Boiler Inspection Act.14 These features, alone, are sufficient to cause more difficulties than is desirable. But in addition, the cases often involve the ever troublesome questions whether the employee was engaged in interstate commerce, whether the action was timely brought, whether the employee himself was the cause of the injury, whether the injury occurred in the course of employment, whether the damages awarded were excessive, and whether the action was barred by a previous settlement, and many other questions.

Course of Employment. 

The first condition for recovery under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, as under any other employers' liability or workmen's compensation act, is that the injury or death occur in the course of employment. In one case the question arose whether a flagman was injured in the course of his employment, if the injury occurred on his return homeward from work. It appeared that the employee was riding on free transportation of the railroad to the station. nearest his home. When he reached his station, he left the train by the rear car, and stepped on a plank at the end of the abutment of a bridge. His intention was to go down the abutment and reach the sidewalk, rather than go by way of the station platform, which was a longer route. The plank gave way, the employee fell to the sidewalk and was injured. 

He brought action under the Federal Employers' Liacility Act in the courts of New York, where he recovered a judgment against the railroad for $15,000. On appeal, this was reversed by the Appellate Division, but its judg- 

13 Act of April 14, 1910; 36 Stat. 298; U. S. C. A., Title 45, Sec. 11. 

14 Act of February 17, 1911, Chap. 103, Sec. 2, as amended March 4, 1915, 38 Stat. 1192, U. S. C. A., Title 45, Sections 11-30. 

[293]

ment was in turn reversed by the Court of Appeals, which affirmed the judgment of the trial court. The railroad company petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, arguing that the employee should not recover damages, because he was not injured in the course of employment, but the petition was denied."

An interesting case arose in Virginia where an action was brought to recover damages from the railroad for the wrongful death of an employee. This action was not brought under the Federal Employers' Liability Act but under the Virginia Wrongful Death Statute, on the theory that at the time of the assault, the deceased was no longer in the employ of the railroad company. It appeared from the evidence that the employee, a section man, had quarreled with his foreman, who on Saturday had told him that he would have no more work for him. On the following 

Monday, however, the section man came back on his job, and the foreman definitely discharged him. Immediately thereafter, and before the man had moved from the place where he stood when dismissed, the foreman drew a revolver, shot and fatally wounded him.

Under a prior decision of the Supreme Court 16 the employee's administrator would have had no remedy under the Federal Employers' Liability Act. The railroad there fore argued that the Federal Act applied; that since prior to his discharge, the section man was engaged in interstate commerce, he continued to be in interstate commerce until he had a reasonable time in which to leave the premises, and that therefore, the rights of the administrator must be governed by the Federal Employers' Liability Act. The administrator, on the other hand, was anxious to prove that 

15 No. 447-N. Y., N. H. & Hartford Ry. vs. Hendricks. Certiorari denied November 25, 1929. 

16 Atlantic Coast Line R. R. Co. vs. Southwell's Admr., 275 U. S. 64. [294]

the federal law did not apply, and he introduced evidence to show that the discharge had actually taken place on Saturday, and that, therefore, the section man was no longer in the employ of the railroad when he returned on Monday. 

To this evidence, the railroad demurred.

Under the state practice, a demurrer to evidence is considered an admission of the facts demurred to. The Virginia courts therefore held that the section man was no longer in the employ of the company at the time of the shooting, and that the state statute applied in the case. The administrator thus recovered a judgment of $8,000, and the railroad brought the case to the Supreme Court. Since the decision of the state courts was based on a non-federal ground sufficient to support it, the petition of the railroad would ordinarily have been denied. But it appears the 

Supreme Court was under the impression that there was no doubt but that the deceased was employed in interstate commerce up to the moment immediately preceding his death. 

The Supreme Court therefore issued a writ of review, but after having heard arguments in the case, it dismissed the writ as having been improvidently granted." 

Just as the question arises how long after the employee is through with his work, does he continue in his employment, so the question arises whether an employee is injured or killed in the course of his employment, if the accident occurs before he begins his work. In a case which came to the Supreme Court this term, a man was sent to a railroad by an employment agency upon free transportation of the railroad, to work as a member of an extra gang in repairing the main line of the interstate carrier. Shortly after he arrived at the place of employment he was run over while walking along the tracks. His administratrix brought ac- 

17 No. 113-Chesapeake & Ohio Railway Co. vs. Bryant. Decided 

February 24, 1930. [295]

tion under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, and one of the defenses entered by the railroad was that the deceased was not killed in the course of his employment, since he was killed before his employment had begun. This defense was overruled by the trial court and by the Circuit Court of Appeals, and the Supreme Court likewise declined to review that decision.18 

Interstate Commerce.

The Federal Employers' Liability Act gives a remedy to those employees of interstate carriers who are engaged in interstate commerce. This is the second Federal Employers' Liability Act passed by Congress. The first Act 19 was declared unconstitutional, because it attempted to embrace all employees of interstate carriers.20 It was a five to four decision. Justice White, who delivered the opinion of the 

Court, held the Act unconstitutional, because it was applicable to all employees, including those working in the local offices or in the repair shops. Justice Moody was of the opinion that the Act was constitutional and should be interpreted to embrace all employees of interstate carriers, except those engaged wholly within the state; Justice Holmes favored an interpretation which would make the Act applicable to interstate carriers while they (the carriers) were engaged in interstate commerce; Justices Harlan and Mc- 

Kenna favored a still narrower construction of the statute to save its constitutionality; they maintained that the Act should be interpreted as applying only to employees engaged in interstate commerce at the time of the injury.

It is interesting to note that when the second Federal 

18 No. 342-Chic., Mil., St. P. & Pa. R. R. Co. vs. Kane. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

19 Act of June 11, 1906, 32 Stat. 232. 

20 Employers Liability Cases, 207 U. S. 463. 

[296]

Employers' Liability Act was passed, the interpretation of Justices Harlan and McKenna was adopted as the proper interpretation of the statute. To be entitled to compensation for injuries under this Act, the employee must be engaged in interstate commerce at the moment of the injury.

This imposes a great hardship on the very employees whom the Act was intended to benefit, and introduces an element of chance into the application of the Act, since railroad employees constantly shift from intrastate to interstate operations. Some illustrations may be thought unbelievable, but they are nevertheless true. Two years ago, there came to the Supreme Court the case of an electric welder who was electrocuted while welding a boiler with an electric torch. The engine, which was being repaired, had been taken out of operations for what is known as "running" repairs, which meant that the engine was still in interstate commerce, as opposed to "classified" repairs, which are more serious in nature and indicate that the engine has been withdrawn from interstate commerce. While the engine was undergoing "running" repairs, it was discovered that the engine also needed some "classified" repairs. The question arose then, whether the welder was electrocuted before or after it was discovered that the engine required the classified repairs. It so happened that the need for the classified repairs was discovered shortly after the employee was killed. 

The courts therefore held that he was engaged in interstate commerce when electrocuted, and recovery was allowed for his death under the federal law.21 Thus the recovery here depended on such an accidental fact, as the moment when the need for classified repairs was discovered. Had these been discovered a second before the welder was killed, his dependents would have had no remedy under the Federal 

Employers' Liability Act. 

21 St. Louis, San Francisco Railroad Co. vs. Oglesby, 277 U. S. 587. 

[ 297 ]

Let not the reader think that we are supercritical in pressing this case to an absurdity. We are not dealing with hypotheses, but with facts. For this term there came an analogous case to the Supreme Court, where the classified repairs were discovered shortly before the injury occurred, and the employee was held to be without remedy under the Federal Employers' Liability Act. A machinist was working on an engine undergoing light repairs in the railroad's roundhouse. The engine came from an interstate operation, and its future destination was not determined at the time of the injury, which meant that the engine was still in interstate commerce. While working on this engine, the machinist noticed that the driving boxes were slightly sprung. 

He then went into the machine shop, used for classified repairs, in order to consult with his superior. While discussing the condition of the driving boxes, his leg was crushed by a wheel which fell from a dismantled engine. Upon these facts, the Circuit Court of Appeals held that at the time of the injury, the employee was not engaged in interstate commerce, and therefore could not recover damages under the federal act. The employee petitioned the Supreme 

Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.22 

When an engine is taken out of interstate commerce is indeed a puzzle. For example, in one case this term, the lower court held that an engine had been taken out of interstate commerce when a firehose test was made. The injured employee was a member of a switching crew, whose work required them to supply both interstate and intrastate passenger trains with necessary equipment. The employee was oiling an interstate engine when the yardmaster instructed him to make a test on the firehose of the engine. Instead of sending cold water through the hose, scalding water and 

22 No. 325-Richardson vs. Louisville & Nashville Railway Co. Cer tiorari denied October 21, 1929. [298]

steam were caused to go through, with the result that the hose burst, and the employee was scalded and severely burned. It was conceded that the damages were caused through the negligence of the railroad. But in the action brought by the employee for his injuries, the railroad defended that he could have no remedy under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, because at the time of the injury he was not engaged in interstate commerce. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit agreed with the railroad.

This employee likewise petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.23 24 It seems to us that in determining the question of interstate commerce in cases under this Act, the courts have adopted a much narrower view than in other types of cases, in which the term "interstate commerce" is involved. For example, in cases involving the effect of intrastate rates on interstate commerce, or in cases involving restraint of trade under the anti-trust acts,25 or even in cases involving the validity of state taxes,26 which are claimed to be a burden on interstate commerce. The test in the former is not whether the employee is generally engaged in work which aids the interstate business of the carrier, but whether the particular work done by the employee at the moment of injury, is in itself interstate commerce.27

After having studied hundreds of cases under the Fed- 

23 No. 711-Olney vs. Lehigh Valley Railroad Co. Certiorari denied 

April 21, 1930. 

24 Shreveport Case (Houston & Texas Ry. vs. U. S.), 234 U. S. 342. 

25 Bedford Cut Stone Co. vs. Stonecutters' Ass'n., 274 U. S. 37. 

26 Carson Petroleum Co. vs. Vial, 278 U. S. 560. 

27 Compare this Act with the Railway Labor Act of 1926 which makes provision for the settlement of wage disputes between interstate carriers and their employees irrespective of whether the employees are or are not engaged in interstate commerce. And see the decision of the Court in 

Texas & New Orleans Railroad Co. vs. Brotherhood of Railway & Steam ship Clerks, in which the Act was upheld as a proper measure incident to the powers of Congress over interstate commerce. See p. 267, et seq., supra. [299]

eral Employers' Liability Act, we must confess an utter inability to make a logical differentiation between those acts of employees constituting interstate commerce and those constituting intrastate commerce. One can group the decisions of the courts into classes, and on that basis state that one or another accident occurred while the employee was engaged in interstate or in intrastate commerce, but one cannot, on the basis of those decisions, arrive at general rules which would serve as a guide for the future. 

Let us take a few cases and see whether the distinctions warrant a difference in remedy for injuries in the same general line of employment by interstate carriers. When a switchman is at work, he cannot stop to examine every cut of cars to see what may be his remedy in case of injury. His rights are determined only after the accident occurred. A switchman, who was injured while moving empties in a switching yard, was held by the Supreme Court of Michigan not to have been engaged in interstate commerce, though two of the empty cars had come from outside the state, and the practice obtained to return empty cars to their home state without further instructions.28 One might well ask how many "interstate cars" must there be in order that the switchman be held engaged in an "interstate switching."

Apparently, not all of them, for in another case where a switchman was crushed between the couplers of two freight cars while engaged in a switching operation in the railroad's yards, he was held by the Supreme Court of Missouri to have been engaged in interstate commerce, though the two cars were "bad order cars" and were destined for the repair shop.29 And in both cases the Supreme Court declined to review the decisions of the lower courts. 

28 No. 84-Rogers vs. Canadian National Railways Co. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. 

29 No. 359-Chic., Rock Is. & Pa. R. R. Co. vs. Stottle. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [300]

The question of interstate commerce often arises in connection with work done on equipment used for the movement of interstate trains. Thus a switchman was injured while repairing a turntable used in repairing out of service engines, and he was held to have been engaged in interstate commerce, because some of the engines were sent to the shop from out of state.30 But in another case an employee was engaged in repairing an apparatus used to supply sand to engines, both interstate and intrastate, and his work was held to be "as a matter of law" too remote from interstate transportation to bring him within the benefits of the Fed eral Employers' Liability Act.31 

What constitutes interstate transportation is another puzzling question. Does the term depend merely on the route of the train or does it depend also on the character of the freight the train carries? If the latter is the significant factor, it may be difficult to explain the decision of the Supreme Court of Illinois, which held that a brakeman was not engaged in interstate commerce under the following circumstances.

In this case, the employee of the railway, a brakeman, was injured while working on a train which operated from Chicago to points west within the state. The train carried shipments of the American Express Company, sent from the Pacific Coast, and destined for points west of Chicago. 

In accordance with the practice of the railroad, such shipments came directly to Chicago and then were reshipped by local trains to their points of destination. It was on the "return" trip that the brakeman was injured. It cannot be denied that the goods on the train were still in interstate commerce, and if the train carried interstate shipments, it may 

30 No. 171-Cleveland, Cin., Chicago & St. Louis Railway Co. vs. 

Kepner. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

31 No. 330-Phillips vs. Chic. & Northwestern Railway. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [301]

well be supposed that to that extent the train was used in interstate commerce and that the brakeman on the train was aiding in the interstate transportation. The Supreme 

Court, however, declined to review the employee's petition to review that decision.32

Period of Limitations. 

If the injury or death occurs in the course of employment and while the employee is engaged in interstate commerce, then the suit to recover damages under the Federal Employers' Liability Act must be brought within two years after the accident.
