In this connection it may be mentioned that when death follows an injury sustained by an employee, the Federal Employers' Liability Act actually gives two separate and distinct remedies. One to the employee himself for his injuries, or if he dies, his administrator can recover for whatever suffering the employee experienced prior to the actual death, and second, the administrator may recover damages for the benefit of the employee's dependents, as provided in the Act. 

An employee was injured while helping to load a shipment. He filed an action in the courts of Indiana under the employers' liability act of that state and won a judgment in the trial court. While the case was pending on appeal, the employee died from his injuries, and his widow was appointed administratrix. The judgment was then affirmed by the Supreme Court of Indiana, and the railroad took the case to the Supreme Court of the United States. Here the judgment of the Indiana courts was reversed on the ground that the deceased at the time of his injury was engaged in interstate commerce and that suit should have been brought 

32 No. 668-Spencer vs. Chic. & Northwestern Ry. Certiorari denied 

March 17, 1930. [302]

under the Federal Employers' Liability Act.33 The case was remanded to the Indiana courts. Then the administratrix amended the complaint, in which she claimed damages to the deceased during his lifetime and to herself for his death. But this amendment to the complaint was not filed until more than three years after the death of her husband, since it took all this time to get through the courts. 

The railroad maintained that the amendment to the complaint was barred by the statute of limitations, but this objection was overruled by the Indiana courts. 

The case again came to the Supreme Court, which again reversed the judgment of the Supreme Court of Indiana, holding that the amendment to the complaint was barred by the two-year period of limitations, prescribed in the Federal Employers' 

Liability Act.³4 

In another case pertaining to the statute of limitations, the administratrix amended the name of the defandant, the Director General of Railroads, more than one year after the predecessor in that office resigned and more than two years after the cause of action arose. The Supreme Court granted a writ of review in the case, but by that time the Director General, whose name was substituted, had also ceased to hold that office, and he was succeeded by Andrew 

W. Mellon. Counsel for the Director General made a motion to substitute the name of Mellon for that of his predecessor, but that motion was made more than three months after the judgment below had become final, in other words, more than three months after Mellon, as Director General of Railroads, could have applied for a writ of certiorari. 

The motion to substitute the name was therefore denied and the writ of certiorari was dismissed.35 Thus an attempt to 

33 B. & O. S. W. R. R. vs. Burtch, 263 U. S. 540. 

34 No. 87-Baltimore & Ohio Southwestern Ry. Co. vs. Carroll. Decided February 24, 1930. 

35 No. 188-Davis vs. Preston. Decided February 24, 1930. 

[303]

upset a judgment on account of one technicality was averted by a ruling on another technicality. 

After these preliminaries, which are, broadly speaking, of a jurisdictional character, we come to the substantive allegations in the complaint and the substantive defenses. 

Employers' Negligence. 

In order to recover damages under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, it must be alleged and proven that the damage resulted from the negligence of the railroad or its employees. There are three questions which the plaintiff in the case must satisfy in the proof of negligence. First, is there evidence of negligence. Second, do the acts or omissions of the carrier or its employees constitute negligence. Third, was the negligence of the railroad the proximate cause of the injury or death. 

1. The requirement of the sufficiency of evidence of negligence on the part of the railroad sometimes fails to take into account the fact that many accidents occur without eye witnesses, and if an employee is killed, then, it is apt to remark that "dead men tell no tales." A conductor had performed a switching operation, after which his mutilated body was found between the rails at a derail. Various allegations of negligence were made to show that the death resulted through the fault of the railroad or its employees, but the railroad argued that, since no one saw how the accident occurred, there was no evidence to show that the negligent acts complained of caused the accident. In the trial court, the jury brought in a verdict for $22,750, upon which judgment was entered, and the judgment was affirmed by the Supreme Court of Kansas. This decision was reversed by the United States Supreme Court in an opinion by Mr. [304]

Justice Stone.36

Although there was evidence that the switching movement was carried out without signals and without having placed a flagman or a light on the cars, there was no evidence that these omissions by the railroad were the cause of the employee's death. Justice Stone said: "But proof of negligence alone does not entitle the plaintiff to recover under the Federal Employers' Liability Act. The negligence complained of must be the cause of the injury. The jury may not be permitted to speculate as to its cause and the case must be withdrawn from its consideration unless there is evidence from which the inference may reasonably be drawn that the injury suffered was caused by the negligent act of the employer. *** Even though we assume that in all the respects alleged the petitioner was negligent, the record does not disclose any facts tending to show that the negligence was the cause of the injury and death."

An employee, working as floorman in a grain elevator owned by a railroad, was discovered dead in one of the storage bins. No one saw the accident, but his widow brought action under the Federal Employers' Liability Act. 

It appeared from the evidence that experiments were conducted there on a preparation for extermination of boll weevil in grain. The experiment consisted of mixing a powder with the grain, which on account of the heat and moisture of the grain produced a gas saturating the grain, and this gas was intended to kill the weevil. There was evidence that this gas killed the complainant's husband. In the state courts of New Jersey, the action resulted in favor of the plaintiff, but that decision was likewise reversed by 

36 No. 303-Atchison, Topeka & Santa Fe Ry. vs. Toops. Decided 

April 14, 1930. [305]

the United States Supreme Court.37 Justice Sutherland, delivering the opinion of the Court, held: "We are of the opinion that there must be a reversal because the evidence fails to establish negligence on the part of the railroad company. *** In any view of the matter, the respondent (plaintiff), upon whom lay the burden, completely failed to prove that the accident was proximately due to the negligence of the company. 

It follows that the verdict rests only upon speculation and conjecture and cannot be allowed to stand."

Of course, the question of the sufficiency of evidence is one for the lower courts, and the Supreme Court will usually not review cases on that question, if there are any facts at all on which the jury could have arrived at its verdict. It is interesting to note, however, in what precarious condition are the rights of the parties, both of the railroads and the employees, when one searches into "sufficiency of evidence" concerning an accident which happened months and sometimes years before the trial and which occurred during a complicated railroad operation. 

These questions relate generally to some specific fact in the evidence which constitutes a link in the chain of circumstances upon which the jury must base its findings. For example, evidence as to the safety of the roadbed, or the tools which the railroad supplies to the employee, or evidence as to whether there was a custom to give warning or whether the required warning was given, and also evidence as to the manner in which trains were operated. 

Thus in the case of a brakeman who was injured as he climbed on a refrigerator car to set the brake and stop it, after the car had been switched on to a side track, it was alleged that after several attempts to make the brake work, 37 No. 73-New York Central R. R. Co. vs. Ambrose. Decided Feb ruary 24, 1930. [306]

the employee braced himself on the top of the car and strained to get the brake to work. In the meantime, the car was rapidly increasing in speed, going downhill, it gave a lurch, the brake suddenly gave way, and the brakeman was thrown around the brake and twisted in such a manner as to cause permanent injuries in the lower part of his back. This action resulted in recovery of a judgment for $17,500, and the railroad petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision on the ground that there was not sufficient evidence of the inefficiency of the brake or of the fact that the inefficiency of the handbrake was the proximate cause of the injury. The Supreme Court declined to go into the matter. 

In another case, the question arose whether a scaffold upon which an employee was painting an interstate railroad bridge was defective and resulted in the death of the worker. 

It was alleged that the rope on the scaffold was defective, that it had a knot which had to be undone, to permit the painter to raise or lower the board on which he was sitting, that while manipulating the knot the rope parted and the painter fell into the water and drowned. The railroad defended that there was not sufficient evidence of negligence on its part to go to the jury. But the Arkansas courts, where the action was brought, held in favor of the administrator. 

The railroad petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.39

In several cases the sufficiency of evidence related to the question whether there was a custom or practice to give warning to employees working about the railroad yards and whether the warning was actually given. Here, likewise, the railroad maintained that the evidence was not sufficient. 

A conductor, in charge of a work train and crew, was re- 

38 No. 117-International Gt. Northern Ry. vs. Hailey. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. 

39 No. 376-Chic., Rock Is. & Pa. Ry. vs. Garrett. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. [307]

turning from repair work done along the right of way of the railroad's main line. The train stopped near a block signal and the conductor alighted to throw a switch. The train started backwards, ran over the conductor and killed him. The widow brought action under the federal act, claiming that the accident occurred because the employees in charge of the engine and the train started the train without sounding a warning bell and that this was in violation of an established practice. The railroad's defense was that there was not sufficient evidence either as to the existence of such practice or that no warning was given. The jury brought in a verdict of $35,000 for the plaintiff, which, however, was reduced by the trial court to $25,000, and this was affirmed by the Supreme Court of Missouri. The railroad's application to review the decision was denied by the 

Supreme Court.40 

In another, action was brought under the Act for the death of an employee, alleged to have occurred through the negligence of a switching crew when they kicked a cut of cars onto a gondola car which the employee was repairing. 

The plaintiff claimed no warning was given. Again the carrier argued that the evidence was insufficient to support a conclusion of its negligence in failing to give proper warning of the switching operation. The judgment resulted in favor of the plaintiff and the railroad's petition to review that decision was denied.41 

2. The second problem in the proof of negligence is to show that the particular act or omission by the railroad or its employees constituted a breach of duty on the part of the railroad. Usually this is a question of fact for the jury. 

A temporary foreman was directing the loading of pianos 

40 No. 297-Missouri Pacific R. R. vs. Koonse. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. 

41 No. 582-Cinc., New Orleans & Tex. Pac. Ry. vs. McGill. Cer tiorari denied March 3, 1930. [308]

from the freight platform to the freight cars. Leaning against one of the crates was a large wheel weighing about one thousand pounds. It was necessary to move this wheel before moving the crate. The foreman directed his men to move the wheel while he grasped the wheel at the hub. 

They straightened up the wheel so that it rested on its rim, and while in this position one of the helpers released his hold, the wheel fell upon the foreman and injured him.

Upon this evidence the foreman recovered damages in an action against the railroad under the Federal Employers' Liability Act. The railroad maintained that there was absolutely no evidence to show that a wheel of this size and resting on its rim required the combined efforts of three men to hold it in balance while another man was shifting his hold, that without such evidence no negligence was shown and that the case should not have been allowed to go to the jury. The Missouri courts, where the action was brought, sustained the verdict, and the railroad's petition to review that decision was denied by the Supreme Court.42 

Other plaintiffs complained that the railroad was negligent in supplying unsafe tools. A car cleaner climbed to the top of a car in order to fill tanks by means of a water hose. The hose parted, he fell off the car and was killed. 

Among the defenses set up by the railroad was that the supplying of this waterhose did not constitute an act of negligence. The Kansas courts, where the action was brought, however, ruled in favor of the plaintiff.43 

Suit was brought by a boy, twenty years old, who was injured when he was thrown off a motor car. He alleged that the railroad was negligent in failing to provide a cover for the toolbox on the motor car, that because of this failure, 42 No. 204-Missouri Pac. R. R. Co. vs. Wheeler. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. 

43 No. 255-Mo. Pac. R. R. vs. Crouch. Certiorari denied October 

21, 1929. [309]

one of the tools fell off in front, the car ran over it and threw him off, seriously injuring him. This action was brought in the Minnesota courts, where the jury brought in a verdict for the plaintiff for about $27,000. The railroad appealed on the ground that failure to provide a cover for the toolbox was not an act of negligence. The Supreme Court of Minnesota, however, sustained the judgment of the lower court. 

In both cases the railroads petitioned the Supreme Court to review these decisions, but the petitions were denied. 44 In two cases the question came up whether failure to give notice or warning constituted negligence. A fireman sat down on the track at the rear of his train. The engineer backed up, and the fireman was run over. The evidence showed that there was a custom to give warning upon moving a train, and the jury found that the engineer and the flagman violated this custom in starting the engine toward the fireman while he was sitting on the rail.45 In the other case, a brakeman left his freight train in order to throw a switch. There he saw a "light" engine about to enter the track. He stopped it and then walked toward his own train. 

The "light" engine, however, after having stopped, proceeded and ran over the brakeman, severely injuring him. 

He brought action under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, and the court submitted to the jury the question whether the railroad was negligent in the failure to give notice or warning and in failing to keep a proper lookout ahead. The jury found in favor of the employee, and the railroad petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, maintaining that there was no obligation on the part of the engineer in charge of the light engine to give notice or warning with- 

44 No. 606-Chic., Mil. & St. Paul R. R. vs. Garedpy. Certiorari denied March 12, 1930. 

45 No. 209-Louisville & Nashville Ry. vs. Bush. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. [310]

in the railroad yard. The Supreme Court, however, declined to review the cases.

In cases of this character, a collateral question arises whether a breach of duty makes the negligent party liable, or whether the breach must be specifically toward the party who is injured or killed. There is a conflict of authority among the various jurisdictions on this point, but under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, it appears, the Supreme Court favors the latter point of view. In a case, this term, a section man was provided with a house near certain team tracks, where he had lived for a number of years. He used a small speeder in the performance of his duties around the railroad's right of way. On his way to the speeder, he crossed the team tracks and climbed over the top of one of a number of coal cars standing there, when he was struck by cars shunted on to the track by means of a "flying switch" operation.

The section man brought action under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, claiming that the railroad was negligent in shunting the cars with unusual force and violence, and in failing to give notice of the intended switching operation. The railroad defended that the shunting was not done with any unusual force and that the section man's work was so irregular that those performing the switching did not know of his presence on the track. There was evidence, however, of a practice to give notice of switching operations to those working on unloading cars. held that the railroad was negligent and sustained the verdict of $20,000 awarded by the jury. This judgment was reversed by the Supreme Court of the United States in an opinion delivered by Justice Sutherland." The Court held that the practice to warn those unloading and loading cars 

46 No. 240-Penn. R. R. Co. vs. Lutton. Certiorari denied October 

21, 1929.

The Illinois courts 

47 No. 21-Ches. & Ohio Ry. vs. Mihas. Decided November 25, 1929, [311]

did not extend to warning this particular section man, though the testimony showed that the injured man saw there were men unloading cars and that he knew of the practice to give warning. "If there was a violation of duty, therefore, on the part of the railway company, it was not a duty owing to Mihas; and that the rule is well established that it is not sufficient for a complainant to show that he has been injured by the failure of another to perform a duty or an obligation, unless that duty or obligation was owing to the complainant."

But in the case of the conductor who was killed by the backing train after he had thrown a switch, where it was shown that there was a failure to give warning, the railroad also maintained that there was no evidence to show that it was the customary practice to sound a warning bell for the benefit of the conductor in charge of the train and in control of the train's movements and upon whose own signals the train would start or stop. As we have seen, however, the courts below sustained the verdict awarded by the jury, and the Supreme Court did not disturb their decision.48 

3. The third problem in the proof of negligence is to show that the act or omission, constituting the negligence, was the proximate cause of the injury. We have already seen what the Supreme Court said in the case of the floorman whose body was found in the grain elevator and where recovery was denied for lack of evidence. The Court there stressed the necessity of proving that the negligent act of the railroad was the cause of the injury.* 49 A more complicated situation arose in another case. A brakeman was riding on the front end of a gondola car which was being pushed from the rear by a locomotive. His 

48 See p. 307 supra. 

49 See p. 306 supra. [312]

duty was to watch out and signal the engineer. A cow came on the track in front of the gondola car, and was struck by the right front wheel of the car. The car was derailed, it veered to the right and sideswiped one of the cars standing on a sidetrack, crushing the brakeman to death between the two cars.

The widow brought action under the Federal Employers' Liability Act and recovered a judgment for $30,000. 

She alleged that the railroad was negligent in having failed to repair the fences and cattle guards, which had burned; that due to this failure, the cow was able to come on the tracks, and that this was the proximate cause of the injury. 

The railroad argued that the failure to repair the fences and cattle guards was not the proximate cause of the injury; that on the contrary, the proximate cause was the failure of the brakeman to warn the engineer. The Supreme Court of Texas, however, sustained the decision of the lower court on this question, and after arguments of the case in the Supreme Court of the United States, the judgment of the 

Texas courts was affirmed without an opinion,50 on the authority of a decision rendered last year.51 In that case a fireman was knocked from the running board of an engine, thrown against the uprights of a bridge and killed. The 

Supreme Court held that the negligence of the railroad in failing to repair the roadbed at that point caused the train to sway and therefore was the proximate cause of the injury. 

An engineer was killed while operating an express train and his widow brought action under the Act. The complaint was that the train was derailed, because of the company's failure to repair or replace old, worn out and unsafe tracks and ties. The company introduced evidence that near the tracks, where the accident occurred, a crowbar 

50 No. 89-Texas & Pac. Ry. vs. Guidry. Decided January 20, 1930. 

51 Western & Atlantic Ry. Co. vs. Hughes, 278 U. S. 496. 

[313]

was found, which indicated that the track had been moved by some miscreant, and that there was no evidence to show that the worn condition of the tracks had caused the derail. 

On this question the jury found for the plaintiff. The railroad appealed to the Supreme Court of Arkansas and after the judgment rendered on the verdict was affirmed, it petitioned the Supreme Court for a writ of review, maintaining that the negligent condition of the roadbed was not the proximate cause of the injury, but the writ was denied.52 

We now come to the affirmative defenses usually set up in actions brought under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, as opposed to a mere denial of negligence. The defenses usually are that the accident was caused through the employee's own negligence, or that he was contributorily negligent, or that he had assumed the risk which resulted in his injury or death. 

Negligence of the Employee.

The most frequent defense is that the employee was injured or killed through his own negligence. On looking at these cases from the standpoint of the modern development in industrial compensation, one might well ask the question, what of it? Few employees, if any, go into railroad work for the purpose of committing suicide. Yet it will be noticed that as soon as the employee has become acquainted with his work, he becomes less cautious, takes more risks and is to that extent more or less negligent. In fact, the efficiency in the operation of a railroad, in some respects, depends upon the negligent conduct or the risks taken by employees. It may be negligence to board a moving train, yet switching operations might be entirely tied up 

52 No. 599-Missouri Pac. Ry. vs. Bushey. Certiorari denied March 

12, 1930. [314]

if every employee engaged in it were to wait until the train or locomotive came to a full stop before getting on or off. 

The taking of these risks is a condition not only in the operation of railroads, but in any line of work; and in fact, in most walks of life. For example, anyone who, for the first time, sees a worker operating a paper cutting machine will probably shudder as he sees the knife come so near the fingers of the operator. Yet little could that operator accomplish if he carefully laid the paper on the machine and kept at a safe distance from the sharp blade. An illustration of the same general principle outside of industry may be observed in the manner in which people in a busy city go through the revolving doors of an office building. Instead of waiting for the doors to stop revolving, people will constantly run through and cause them to revolve still more rapidly for the next comer. The busier the people, the more risks they take.

Offhand, it is academic to maintain that those who are engaged in a hazardous occupation like railroading, should not place themselves in a position of danger. Sometimes it may not be good judgment to place oneself in such a position, but the judgment of employees is likewise a condition. of the industry. In most instances, it is probably easier to look back upon an accident and say that the employee had placed himself in a position of danger, rather than appraise the situation as a participant in the railroad operation at the time of the accident.

Whether the employee placed himself in a position of danger is the most frequent test of the defense that the employee was the cause of his own misfortune. Carriers have argued that the fireman, who sat upon the track and was run over by the train, had put himself in a position of danger;53 that the employee who was injured when he fell 

53 See p. 310 supra. [315]

from the abutment of the railroad bridge to the sidewalk, was himself the cause of the accident because he had taken a short cut rather than the usual route.54 

Whether under a given set of circumstances, the accident occurred through the employee's negligence in that he had placed himself in a position of danger, is in most instances a question of fact to be determined by the jury. And in all cases this year, in which this particular type of employee's negligence was raised as a defense, the juries found in favor of the plaintiffs, the judgments of the trial courts were affirmed by the appellate courts and the Supreme Court denied the railroads' petitions to review these decisions. In previous years, however, juries have found otherwise, or the courts below held in favor of the railroads, or the Supreme Court reversed the decisions of the lower courts, and held that no recovery could be had under the Federal Employers' Liability Act because the employee had placed himself in a position of danger and was therefore the cause of his own negligence. 55 We said that the negligence of the employee is a condition of the railroad industry, as it is of any other industry. 

Of course, to some extent, accidents can be diminished through the promulgation of safety rules, just as they can be diminished through the institution of safety devices. The employee, however, will violate rules. That is likewise a condition of the industry. But when an employee is injured or killed through his disobedience of a company rule, neither he nor his administrator can recover damages under the Federal Employers' Liability Act. Thus in the case of the floorman of the grain elevator, discussed above, who was killed by poison gas resulting from the experiment, the administrator most probably could not have recovered dam- 

54 See p. 293 supra. 

55 Atlantic Coast Line Railroad Co. vs. Davis, 279 U. S. 34. 

[316]

ages though the death had resulted from the railroad's negligence, because by going into the bins the employee violated a rule to the contrary." 56

An engineer met his death when his train went down a steep grade and turned over at a point where the rail had been removed. The country was mountainous with many sharp curves and grades. Evidence showed that a "steel gang" was at work on the line laying rails, and that the engineer had been informed about this. But his widow brought action under the federal law, maintaining that the railroad was negligent in having failed to give the customary warning to engineers, by sending out a flagman, if any rail was actually torn up; that in this case the foreman actually did send out a man to give this special warning, but that this was done too late to save the train. 

The company maintained that the engineer came to his death entirely through his own negligence in failing to keep the train under control, after he received warning that the gang was at work in that territory. The trial court submitted to the jury the question whether sufficient warning was given, and the jury returned a verdict for the widow in the sum of $14,000. On appeal, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit reversed the judgment, holding that it was the engineer's duty to hold that train in control over the line, and that his failure to do so was the sole cause of the accident. The Supreme Court declined to disturb this decision.57

The same result ensued when an engineer was injured in a head-on collision between his passenger train and a freight train. A rule required the engineer to take his train to a siding at Bridgewater, N. Y., and wait for the freight train 

56 See p. 305 supra. 

57 No. 724-Hylton vs. Southern Ry. Co. Certiorari denied April 28, 1930. [317]

to pass. It appears that the station agent received a message for the engineer and conductor of the passenger train that they await the freight train, but the message had never been delivered. The conductor signaled the engineer to proceed, which the latter did, and about one-quarter of a mile from the station the collision occurred. The conductor was killed and the engineer was injured. In the suit brought by the administrator of the conductor, the Supreme Court held, last year, that the conductor was killed through his own negligence, because he deliberately violated the rule of the company by signaling to the engineer to proceed without waiting for the freight train.58 This was contrary to the printed instructions. The opinion rendered by Justice 

Holmes laid stress on the fact that the conductor was in command of the train, and that his signal to proceed was equivalent to his being the prime factor in the movement of the train. That decision overruled the decision of the Court of Appeals of New York which had held that the conductor was only contributorily negligent, since the message in this instance was not delivered to the conductor or the engineer. 

Later, when the engineer brought his suit, the lower courts ruled against him, on the ground that he, too, was responsible for the accident, and the Supreme Court refused to review that decision.59

However, it is only occasionally that an employee cannot recover damages for injuries because he has disregarded a rule. If a company rule is consistently disregarded, then it is presumed that the company has acquiesced in this disregard, and it is said that the rule has been abandoned. When a railroad defended that a car inspector was killed through his own negligence, because he failed to use a blue light on 

58 Unadilla Valley Ry. Co. vs. Caldine, 278 U. S. 139. 

59 No. 176-Dibble vs. Unadilla Valley Ry. Co. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. [318]

the car which he was repairing, in accordance with the rules, the answer was that the rule had been abandoned." 

In another case, the railroad maintained that the employee was killed through his own negligence because he went on the tracks contrary to orders, but evidence showed that the rule was honored more in the breach than in the observance.61 

Contributory Negligence. 

Sometimes the defense that the employee was negligent goes only part way. The railroad does not maintain that the employee was injured or killed solely through his own negligence; the carrier does not deny its own negligence, but it maintains that the employee was likewise negligent and that acting upon the negligent situation created by the employee, the railroad inflicted the injury or death complained of.

At common law no recovery could be had by a plaintiff who was contributorily negligent. But a difficult question sometimes arises, whether the negligence of the employee might be considered sole negligence or contributory negligence. And the line of demarcation between the two is by no means simple. For example, in the case of the engineer, supra, who was injured in a head-on collision when he disregarded the printed rules, but obeyed the signal of his conductor, he was held to have been solely negligent rather than contributorily negligent, on the authority of a previous decision by the Supreme Court, in the case of this conductor, who was killed. Regarding the conductor there might be some reason for saying that he was solely negligent although the question is indeed debatable. But the engineer's disobedience of the written instructions only contributed to the 

60 No. 582-Cin., New Orleans & Tex. Pa. Ry. vs. McGill. Cer tiorari denied March 3, 1930. 

61 No. 212-N. Y. Central R. R. vs. Marcone. Decided April 14, 1930. [319]

negligent situation created by the conductor, who signalled for him to start, and the conductor's negligence, so far as the engineer was concerned, was the railroad's negligence. 

In some jurisdictions the doctrine of contributory negligence is supplanted by a doctrine of last clear chance. That is, where it is shown that the plaintiff was contributorily negligent, it does not preclude him from recovering damages, but the question then arises who had the last chance to avoid the accident. And in the case of the employee who was killed before entering upon his employment,62 the trial court advised the jury that the doctrine of last clear chance was applicable. In petitioning the Supreme Court to review the decision, the carrier also raised the question whether the last clear chance doctrine was applicable at all in suits brought under the Federal Employers' Liability Act. 

Most of the defenses of contributory negligence are used in connection with other defenses, and merely to minimize the amount of damages. For the Federal Employers' Liability Act abrogated the contributory negligence rule of common law to the extent of substituting a rule of comparative negligence, that is, where it is shown that the employee was contributorily negligent, the damages should be assessed in inverse proportion to the negligence of the employee. If an employee was 25 per cent. negligent he should recover 75 per cent. of the loss suffered by him; if he was 90 per cent. negligent he should recover only 10 per cent. of his loss. There is only one difficulty about this rule, that there is no way of determining the proportion of negligence. We take it, however, by this rule Congress merely meant to say that if an employee is contributorily negligent, then let the jury give him something anyhow. This simply shows in what a slipshod manner such important legislation is enacted. 

62 See p. 295 supra. [320]

Generally, whenever we find the defense that the employee was negligent, we also find the defense that he was contributorily negligent. In the case of the employee who fell off the car when the water-hose parted, and it was shown that the railroad was negligent in supplying a defective hose, the railroad maintained that the employee was also contributorily negligent in having failed to inspect the hose before using it.63 

Since accidents resulting from negligence of employees is a condition of the industry, it may be argued that when an employee enters upon the employment, he knows or should know that he must take the consequences of his own. negligent conduct. This presupposes a single factor resulting in the accident, that is, the conduct of the employee. 

That supposition, however, is contrary to fact. Many factors are brought together which influence the conduct of the employee, and we cannot pick out one of these and regard it as the cause or even as the proximate cause of the injury or death. If accidents due to the negligence of employees constitute a condition of the industry, then the cost of this condition should be borne by all those who benefit by the industry. 

In other words, it should be one of the factors to be taken into consideration in computing the total costs of the railroad service.

Assumption of Risk. 

The same principles apply to the second defense often set up in actions brought under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, that the employee assumed the risk of injury or death as a risk of his employment. In other words, the theory is that when one accepts a job on a railroad he assumes the risks ordinarily incident to that employment.

This is another remnant of the common law, which is 

63 See p. 309 supra. [321]

wholly inapplicable to our modern giant industrial undertakings. What are risks ordinarily incident to employment on a railroad? Are not all risks incident to the employment, including those resulting from the gross negligence of the fellow employees or even of the officers of the railroad? Of course, in a given case the question may be submitted for decision to twelve men who are utterly devoid of knowledge concerning railroad work, but that is not a solution of the problem.

One might also ask why should a worker entering an employment assume the ordinary risks? A reference to the common law supplies a historical background for the existing condition, but hardly a reason for it. It may with equal reason be argued that those who employ men in a hazardous occupation should stand the losses sustained on account of risks incident to that employment.

It may fairly be supposed that in seeking to impose the burden of such costs two questions must be met. First, who benefits by these accidents? Second, where would the imposition of the costs produce least hardship? If the risks are incident to the employment, then they are a condition of the industry and should enter into the cost of operation and hence into the rates chargeable to those who use the railroads. In other words, the burden should primarily be borne by shippers and passengers, and if any part of these additional costs cannot be shifted on to the rates, then the remainder should go to the lessening of dividends paid to stockholders. As thus distributed, the loss would work by far less hardship than if it were allowed to fall, as it is, on the employee alone, simply because he engages in work which is dangerous, but which is necessary for our economic structure.

Under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, as enacted by Congress and as interpreted in the decisions of the courts, [322]

the employee assumes the risks incident to the employment, and, in addition, he assumes two other types of risks. First, risks which are not ordinary, but which are the result of a negligent situation created by the railroad, but of which the employee has or should have knowledge, and second, other extraordinary risks, which the employee knows or should know in the light of his experience with railroad work. 

1. The assumption of risks ordinarily incident to the employment is best illustrated by the case of a switchman who after throwing his switch to enable a cut of cars to be backed over a track in front of him attempted to cross the track with the cut of cars only a few feet away. The railroad maintained that the employee had assumed the risk as incident to his employment. The Supreme Court granted a writ of review, but after the arguments in the case it dismissed the writ as having been improvidently granted.**

Also in the case in which the brakeman stopped a train and then walked toward his own engine between the tracks, the railroad argued that he had assumed the risk of being struck by the train which he had stopped.65

During this term all the cases in which the defense of this type of assumption of risk was set up, resulted in favor of the plaintiffs in the lower courts, and the Supreme Court declined to review their decisions. 

2. In addition to the ordinary incidents of employment, the employee also assumes the hazards created through the negligence of the railroad or its employees, if the employee knowing of the negligent situation, nevertheless continues in the employment. A switchman was instructed to move a water car with engine attached on to a classification track. He was to signal the engineer while riding on the engine, uncouple the car, then get on the car and control its 

64 No. 51-Gulf, Mobile & Northern R. R. vs. Williams. Decided 

December 5, 1929. 

65 See p. 310 supra. [323]

movements to avoid colliding with other cars on the classification track. The switchman took a position on the footboard of the engine, so that he could operate the pin puller, and at the same time signal the engineer. He gave various signals to slow down, but the engineer did not respond. He then gave a washout signal, which required the engineer to stop immediately. The engineer, however, continued reversing the engine, jerking back and forth, which caused the switchman to lose his balance, and in order to avoid falling he took hold of the pin puller. While in this position, the engineer again reversed the engine. The weight of the switchman's body on the pin puller released the pin and the engine and the car became uncoupled. The engineer immediately accelerated his speed in order to get over the switch ahead of the car. The switchman remained on the footboard of the engine until the speed was decreased, so that he could safely alight from it. At the same time the water car was approaching the switch to get on the classification track, and unless its speed were reduced it would collide with an damage the other cars on the track. The switchman attempted to mount the car, he succeeded in taking hold of the grabiron in front, but was unable to get his foot into the stirrup. As he held on, one of his knees struck the rail. 

The injury made him ill, he released his hold, fell and was severely injured.

In the first trial the district court of Salt Lake County in Utah directed a verdict for the railroad on the ground that the employee at the time of injury was not engaged in interstate commerce. That was reversed by the Supreme 

Court of the state and the case was remanded for a new trial. In the second trial a verdict was rendered in favor of the employee in the sum of $10,000. Again the case went to the Supreme Court of Utah, which this time held that in [324]

view of the negligent operations by the engineer, the switchman assumed the risk when he continued to ride on the engine and then attempted to board the moving car. The switchman petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied."

A similar problem arose where a yard switchman was injured when his foot was caught in the exposed gears of the turn-table on which he was working. The railroad maintained that the employee had assumed the risk of his employment by continuing to work about the exposed gears, after he had called the foreman's attention to this negligent condition. It appeared from the evidence that the foreman had promised to have the gears covered, but the time within which he had promised to do that had expired. Here, the courts in Missouri held that under the circumstances the employee had not assumed the risk of his employment, and the Supreme Court denied the application of the railroad to review that decision.67

It would not be too strong to characterize this type of assumption of risk as shocking. Suffice it to imagine what would happen had an employee, upon discovering a negligent situation, laid down on the job until the situation was remedied. The theory that the employee voluntarily continues in the employment and therefore voluntarily assumes such risks, is hardly applicable in an economic system like ours, in which every person, not born to wealth, must work. 

3. The third type of assumption of risk is one not dependent on the work itself, but on the experience of the employee. It is argued, and that argument has been sustained in the decisions of the Supreme Court, that because the employee was an experienced railroad worker, he should 

66 No. 533-Roach vs. Los Angeles & Salt Lake R. R. Certiorari denied January 27, 1930. 

67 See p. 301 supra. [ 325 ]

be held to have assumed risks to which a less experienced employee might not be held. In other words, the scope of assumed risks increases as the employees become more and more experienced, and it is only the human limitations in gaining experience which prevent the most experienced employees from gradually losing all remedies under the law. 

In the case of a railway car inspector who was injured, when a cut of cars was kicked with unusual force onto the track where he was working, the railroad also argued that the car inspector, as an experienced employee had assumed this as a risk of his employment. This case likewise resulted in favor of the plaintiff, and the Supreme Court declined to review the case.69

The only saving feature about the doctrine of assumption of risk, from the standpoint of the employee, is that the jury usually finds in his favor or for his administrator. In other words, there is a tendency in the lower courts to avoid the application of this doctrine. For this reason, in the case of an employee, who was sideswiped from a ladder of a car placed on a spur track too near the lead track, the railroad assigned as error the action of the trial court in submitting to the jury whether the employee had assumed this as a risk of his employment.70 In another case, the trial court ruled that the burden of proving assumption of risk lay with the railroad and it was not necessary for the plaintiff to prove that he did not assume the risk. The Supreme Court declined to rule on this question. 71 

68 Delaware, Lackawanna & Western vs. Koske, 279 U. S. 7. 

69 No. 801-Texas & Pac. Ry. vs. Aaron. Certiorari denied May 19, 1930. 

70 No. 566-Delaware, Lack. & West. R. R. vs. Reardon. Certiorari denied March 3, 1930. 

71 No. 840-Western & Atl. R. R. vs. Lochridge. Certiorari denied 

May 26, 1930. [ 326]

Safety Measures. 

There is an exception in the Federal Employers' Liability Act to the rules concerning contributory negligence and assumption of risk, discussed above. If it is shown that the railroad violated the safety laws passed by Congress for the protection of railroad employees, that is, the Safety

Appliance Act and the Boiler Inspection Act, then the defenses of contributory negligence and assumption of risk cannot be raised. The courts, however, have put limitations on this provision, and some of the limitations, it seems to us, are without merit.

Thus where an employee was injured through a defective appliance in violation of the Safety Appliance Act, the 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit held that the defense of assumption of risk could nevertheless be raised, because the car containing the defective appliance was being withdrawn for repairs; the reason for the decision being that when the defective car was being withdrawn for repairs, the railroad acted not in violation of the Safety Appliance Act, but in accordance with it.72 But in the case of a switchman who was crushed between the couplers of two freight cars, the Supreme Court of Missouri held that the defective couplers in the cars were nevertheless in violation of the Safety Appliance Act, although they were marked "bad order cars" and were destined for the repair shop.73

The most important limitation on the application of this provision in the Act is that the violation of the Safety 

Appliance Act or of the Boiler Inspection Act must be shown to have been the proximate cause of the accident. 

It is not sufficient to show that the violation was a con- 

72 No. 94 Sherry vs. Baltimore & Ohio R. R. Certiorari denied 

October 14, 1929. 

73 No. 359-Chicago, Rock Is. & Pac. R. R. vs. Stottle. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [327]

tributing factor. In the above case of the switchman, the railroad argued that since the cars were being withdrawn for repairs, the violation of the Safety Appliance Act was not the proximate cause of the injury. And in the case of the brakeman who was alleged to have been twisted around the defective handbrake and severely injured, the railroad maintained that there was not sufficient evidence to show that the violation of the Safety Appliance Act, if any, was the proximate cause of the injury.74

What are the rights of an employee, injured through an appliance which complied with the Boiler Inspection Act, but which at the same time created a secondary danger? 

Action was brought under the Federal Employers' Liability Act and the Boiler Inspection Act. It was alleged that the railroad was negligent in constructing a pop-off valve on the top of an engine in such manner that it unnecessarily projected its outlet of steam toward a nearby sand box, so that while the employee was examining the sand, the valve suddenly released, striking him in the face, and causing him to fall backward to the ground, a distance of some twelve feet. This resulted in a fracture of the spinal vertebrae. The employee argued that ordinarily the valve was attached in a safe manner, but that without notice to him the position of the valve on this particular engine had been changed, and that these injuries occurred on his first trip of inspection. In other words, the negligence alleged was the change made in the position of the valve, without notice to the employee.

The railroad defended that the employee, who was the engineer in charge of the engine, had assumed this risk, because the inspection of the engine was incident to his ordinary employment. To this the employee replied that the defense of assumption of risk could not be raised in a suit 

74 See p. 307 supra. [328]

brought under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, where the negligence alleged is a violation of the Safety Appliance 

Act or the Boiler Inspection Act. The Circuit Court of 

Appeals held with the railroad, because in so far as the Boiler Inspection Act was concerned, the construction of the valve fully complied with its requirements; and if the action is based on the Federal Employers' Liability Act, then the railroad may defend on the doctrine of assumption of risk. The Supreme Court denied the employee's petition to review that decision." 75 

Measure of Damages. 

76 After an employee or his administrator succeeds in establishing his right to recover damages, the question arises what should be the measure of the damages recoverable. In the Moser Case, decided by the Supreme Court in the 1927 Term, a rule was laid down concerning the basis for computing damages. The tendency in the lower courts, however, is for the jury to assess "heavy damages," and the trial court sometimes remits a portion of the damages found by the jury.

An interesting question came up in this connection. 

During the trial, counsel for the administratrix, in addressing the jury, repeatedly stated that the employee was murdered in the course of the railroad operations and made other remarks which the company claimed were prejudicial. 

The trial court instructed the jury to disregard these remarks, but the jury brought in a verdict of $18,000. This was reduced by the trial court to $5,000. When the railroad appealed to the appellate court on the ground that counsel for the plaintiff had made prejudicial remarks, the 

75 No. 629-Wilcox vs. New York Central R. R. Certiorari denied 

March 12, 1930. 

76 Gulf, Colorado & Santa Fe Ry. Co. vs. Moser, 275 U. S. 133. [ 329 ]

Court of Appeals held that in view of the evidence in the case, and in view of the fact that the trial court had reduced the verdict, the remarks of the attorney did not result in prejudice against the carrier. The Court of Appeals, moreover, increased the verdict of the trial court from $5,000 to $9,000, and in its petition for a writ of review in the Supreme Court, the railroad maintained that the Court of Appeals of Tennessee had no right to restore a portion of the reduction made by the trial court, since there was no proper assignment of error authorizing such action on the part of the appellate court."

In another case, it appeared that the employee suffered for some four hours before he died. The jury returned a verdict for $48,000, and the railroad argued that this was excessive, because it was more than any amount which could be arrived at on the basis of mortality or probability tables. The Supreme Court of Arkansas, however, held that although on the basis of such tables the maximum verdict to the administratrix for the benefit of the dependents would be less than $48,000, the difference between such proper amount for the death and the verdict was not excessive in view of the suffering sustained by the engineer while pinned under the engine.78

Such is the operation of the Federal Employers' Liability Act. It is said that when it was at first proposed to pass a Workmen's Compensation Act for railroad employees, the main objection came from the employees themselves, and that they were in turn influenced by lawyers who make a practice of these personal injury cases. It is said that one of their methods of convincing railroad employees of the great benefits of this law is to stress the large verdicts 

77 No. 582-Cincinnati, New Orl. & Tex. Pac. R. R. vs. McGill. 

Certiorari denied March 3, 1930. 

78 No. 599-Missouri Pac. R. R. Co. vs. Bushey. Certiorari denied 

March 12, 1930. [330]

obtained by them. Such arguments, it appears, are not confined to places outside of the courtroom, for one attorney remarked to a witness, in the presence of the jury, that he had beaten the railroad in another case brought under the 

Federal Employers' Liability Act.79 

It is admitted, of course, that the verdicts obtained are generally high as compared with compensation paid under the workmen's compensation acts. How much higher they are, and whether the costs involved to the railroads and the employees counter-balance the differences are matters which usually do not come up before the Supreme Court. But one case appeared this year which indirectly indicates that perhaps the injured employees or their dependents get only about one-half of the damages recovered. Action was brought under the Attorneys' Lien Statute of Missouri by an attorney of an employee's administrator against the railroad to recover his contingent fee. It appears that the administrator authorized the attorney to bring suit in the 

Missouri courts for $65,000 under the Boiler Inspection

Act, the Safety Appliance Act and the Federal Employers' 

Liability Act. The administrator also agreed to pay the attorney 50 per cent. of whatever judgment he would collect. Suit was brought, but the case resulted in a non-suit, and shortly afterwards the attorneys filed a second suit in the same cause of action.

While this action was pending in the Missouri courts, the railroad, through its claims agent in Illinois, and without the knowledge of the attorney, settled with the administrator for $4,000. This settlement was made on a form supplied by the Industrial Commission of Illinois and purported to be an award under the Workmen's Compensation Act of that state. The Industrial Commission of Illinois approved this award. 

79 No. 582-Cinc., New Orleans & Tex. R. R. vs. McGill. Certiorari denied March 3, 1930. [331]

When the attorneys learned of the settlement, they brought suit to recover their fee of 50 per cent., or $2,000. 

The railroad introduced in evidence the settlement made with the administrator, the approval of the Illinois Industrial Commission, and in addition argued that there could be no lien for attorneys' services under the compensation act. The trial court in Missouri upheld the railroad's contentions, but was reversed by the St. Louis Court of Appeals which held that the railroad failed to show that a claim was filed under the state law or that a valid claim could have been filed under that law. The railroad petitioned the 

Supreme Court to review this decision, claiming that the Missouri courts had failed to give full faith and credit to the award approved by the Industrial Commission of Illinois, in violation of the Constitution of the United States. 

The petition, however, was denied.80 

Attorneys' fees ranging from thirty-three to fifty per cent. are not the only costs which must come out of the verdicts obtained by employees or their administrators. 

There are the costs of litigation both in the trial courts and in the appellate courts. In addition, there is considerable delay in obtaining compensation at a time when it is most needed, and, what is worse, the uncertainties of the outcome of the litigation. Together with the costs to the employees, one must also consider the additional costs to the carriers, their costs of litigation, their attorneys' fees and general overhead attributable to these cases. In addition to these, one must also consider the costs to the public and the amount of time taken up in the courts by these cases. 

Whether the verdicts obtained by the railroad employees less the expenses, delays and uncertainties, leave them at an advantage as compared with compensation under the com- 

80 No. 868-Chicago & Eastern Ill. Ry. vs. Noell. Certiorari denied 

June 2, 1930. [332]

pensation acts, is a matter worthy of investigation. It would also be interesting to know whether a compensation act covering all railroad employees in all cases would not be more economical for the railroads, in view of their costs. 

And considering the costs to the public, it would be interesting to know whether it would not pay for the state to contribute to a general scheme of insuring compensation rather than sanction the present situation. We recommend a thorough and scientific investigation of the operation of the Federal Employers' Liability Act for the benefit of all classes concerned, the employees, the carriers and the public. Any investigation undertaken, however, must be absolutely impartial and must command the confidence of both carriers and employees.

Jones Seamen's Act. 

81 

Under Section 33 of the Merchant Marine Act of 1920,8 

commonly known as the Jones Seamen's Act, seamen were given the same remedies for death or injury as the Federal 

Employers' Liability Act gave to railroad employees. During this term, the Supreme Court held that the Jones Seamen's Act gives an exclusive remedy to injured seamen or their administrators. In case of death, therefore, seamen can no longer resort to their remedies at common law or to remedies under the statutes of the various states. A third assistant shipkeeper was engaged in repair work on a lifeboat of a merchant vessel owned by the United States, when he was thrown on the drydock and killed. His administrator brought suit in the United States District Court at 

Norfolk, under the Virginia Wrongful Death Statute, claiming that the accident was caused by the unseaworthiness of the boat, and he recovered a judgment of $5,000. 

81 41 Stat. 988. [333]

The reason why action was brought under the state statute, and not under the Jones Act, was that under the latter, in accordance with the provisions of the Federal Employers' Liability Act, the administrator could maintain an action only for the benefit of designated relatives or next of kin, dependent upon the employee. This action was brought for the benefit of a nephew of the seaman, but it did not appear that the nephew was dependent upon him. Under the state statute, however, an action could be maintained by the administrator for the benefit of the next of kin without showing dependency. On appeal, the Circuit Court of Appeals reversed that judgment sustaining the contentions of the United States that action should have been brought under the Jones Seamen's Act, because this law provided an exclusive remedy for death of seamen, and that the state law could not apply. The Supreme Court, in an opinion by 

Justice Sanford, affirmed this decision, holding that the administrator's remedy was exclusively under the Jones 

Seamen's Act.82

Whether the Jones Seamen's Act applies to all seamen within the jurisdiction of the United States is another question which came before the Supreme Court this term, but on which the Court has not yet ruled. The specific question is whether the Act is applicable to injuries or death sustained on vessels of foreign registry while in the navigable waters of the United States. An American seaman, employed by an American stevedoring company, was killed while working on a vessel of German registry, lying in New York Harbor. The widow brought action against the company under the Jones Law, claiming that death was due to negligence of the employer. The New York courts held that the Jones Act could not apply, where the accident occurred on a vessel of foreign registry. The Supreme 

82 No. 25-Lindgren vs. United States. Decided February 24, 1930. 

[334]

Court granted a writ of review83 to the widow, who argued in her petition that it was not the intention of Congress to divide American seamen into two classes, giving a remedy to one class employed on vessels of American registry and denying that right to those working on foreign vessels, though in the waters of the United States. Furthermore, she argued, the decision of the New York courts would disturb the uniformity of the maritime law in such cases. The case will be argued next term.

Since the Jones Seamen's Act merely applies the remedies of the Federal Employers' Liability Act to seamen, here we also find the same limitations as to proof of negligence, the doctrine of contributory negligence and of assumption of risk. Two interesting cases on the question. of negligence were decided by the Supreme Court this year. 

In the first, it appeared that a stevedore was engaged in loading a barge, when his foreman told him to "hurry up." 

To this the stevedore replied, "Leave me alone. * * * I've been here for four or five years and I can do the work and I know what work to do." Thereupon the foreman hit him over the eyes, he fell to the ground and sustained a fractured skull. 

The stevedore brought action in the New York courts against the employer under the Jones Seamen's Act alleging that the assault had been committed within the scope of the foreman's employment and in furtherance of the employer's business. The employer defended on the ground that there was no evidence to show the foreman was of a brutal or vicious nature and therefore there was no negligence in having employed him. The employer also argued that without a showing of negligence he was not liable for the wilful assault of the foreman, under the Jones Law. This action. resulted in favor of the injured employee. 

83 No. 581-Anna Uravic vs. F. Jarka Co. Case pending. 

[335]

It was argued in the Court of Appeals of New York that under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, and therefore under the Jones Seamen's Act, an employer was not liable for a wilful assault made on an employee. This argument was based on a prior decision of the Supreme Court in the Southwell Case, in which a railroad employee was shot and killed by a fellow worker, while in the course of his employment, and as a result of a situation growing out of a strike. The Supreme Court had held in that case that the carrier could not be held liable for the wilful and brutal murder of the employee and that it would be stretching the language of the Federal Employers' Liability Act far beyond its intended meaning to hold a carrier liable for such wilful conduct. 

But the Court of Appeals of New York said: "We cannot with a fair countenance say that a master is now liable to his servants for personal injuries due to a careless foreman, but is not liable for personal injuries inflicted on them by a brutal foreman in directing the performance of the work intended and believed to be in the interest of the master."

In the second case a seaman on board vessel was assaulted by the second engineer, in a moment of anger and as a disciplinary measure, occasioned by the seaman's lateness in coming on watch. It appeared from the testimony that when the engineer asked the seaman why he was late, the latter said: "I'm a bit late, I overslept." The engineer then struck him between the eyes with a monkey-wrench, and as he fell back he hit him again with the wrench. For this assault the seaman recovered a judgment of $12,000 from the steamship company, and this judgment was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals.

Both cases came to the Supreme Court. The employers 

84 Alantic Coast Line R. R. Co. vs. Southwell's Admr., 275 U. S. 64. 

[336]

argued there was no evidence to show they were negligent in their duty to protect the seamen from inhuman treatment, since there was no ratification of the brutal acts and no proof of the previous vicious character of the assaulting employees. Apparently, the employers in these cases argued upon an analogy of the common law doctrine of scienter applied to animals, under which, as the saying goes: "Every dog is entitled to at least one bite." Since the second engineer had never before hit anyone over the eye with a monkeywrench, the steamship company argued that it had no knowledge of the vicious character of the man. The steamship companies also argued that the assaults were not committed in furtherance of the business and therefore the employers should not be held liable as principals for the torts committed by their agents.

In both cases, however, the decisions of the lower courts were affirmed by the Supreme Court in an opinion by Justice Butler.85 As to the question whether the wilful assault of the fellow servant was to be included in the word "negligence" as used in the Federal Employers' Liability Act, Justice Butler said: "The Act is not to be narrowed by refined reasoning or for the sake of giving 'negligence' a technically, restricted meaning. It is to be construed liberally to fulfill the purposes for which it was enacted and to that end the work may be read to include all the meanings given to it by courts and within the word as ordinarily used. * * 'Negligence' is a word of broad significance and may not readily be defined with accuracy. 

Courts usually refrain from attempts comprehensively to state its meaning. While liability arises when one suffers injury as the result of any breach of duty owed 

85 No. 390-Jamison et al. vs. Encarnacion. Decided May 26, 1930; 

and No. 457-Alpha Steamship Co. vs. Cain. Decided May 26, 1930. [ 337 ]

him by another chargeable with knowledge as the probable result of his conduct, actionable negligence is often deemed and we need not pause to consider whether rightly to include other elements. Some courts call wilful misconduct evincing intention or willingness to cause injury to another gross negligence.

As un- questionably the employer would be liable if plaintiff's injuries had been caused by mere inadvertence or carelessness on the part of the offending foreman, it would be unreasonable and in conflict with the purpose of Congress to hold that the assault, a much greater breach of duty, was not negligence within the meaning of the Act."

It seems to us that in these cases the Supreme Court has overruled its prior decision in the Southwell Case. In these cases, as in that, there was a wilful criminal assault. The difference lay in the fact that here the assault resulted in injury, whereas in the Southwell Case it resulted in death. 

Had the engineer, who wielded the monkey-wrench, struck the seaman on the temple the victim would probably have been killed, and it is inconceivable that such graver assault would, by virtue of its gravity, have relieved the steamship company of liability.

In addition to analogous problems under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, suits brought under the Jones Act bear earmarks peculiar to admiralty law. One of these is the limitation of liability under the Harter Act,86 which limits the liability of the owner of the vessel, except as is provided in the Act. This law as to limitation of liability brought two cases before the Supreme Court this term. Suit was brought under the Jones Seamen's Act and the Death on the High Seas by Wrongful Act Statute, by the administrators of eleven seamen, who were drowned when their 

86 Title 46, U. S. C. A., Sec. 183. 

[ 338]

vessel sank at sea. The action was stayed by the owner's petition for limitation of liability, and the administrators filed their claims contending, at the same time, that the owner was not entitled to limitation, because the vessel was unseaworthy, and that the unseaworthiness was with privity and knowledge of the owner. While these proceedings were pending, the owner of the vessel died and the administrators sought to have the owner's estate substituted for the owner in this action. The federal district court in New 

York and the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Cir cuit held that the claims abated with the owner's death, and that the administrators could not therefore substitute the estate as the party defendant. The administrators petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.87

The second case became involved in a question of procedure under the Harter Act. Here action was brought by a seaman in the state courts to recover damages under the Jones Seamen's Act. The plaintiff alleged that the owner of the fishing vessel was negligent because certain metal chain plates on the side of the vessel were not flush with the vessel's side and were left uncovered, so that fishhooks on a line caught in the side of the plates were snapped loose by the pull of the winch, and one of the fishhooks entered the plaintiff's eye and destroyed it. The owner of the vessel then filed an application for limitation of liability in the United States District Court, claiming that his liability should be limited, if he was liable at all. He also entered a defense that the claimant was injured through his own negligence.

The district court first passed on the merits of the claim and finding that there was no negligence on the part of the 

87 No. 779-Frank Guile et al. vs. E. M. Statler, owner of SS. Mira- 

Certiorari denied May 5, 1930. mar. [ 339]

employer, he dismissed the claim without passing on the application for limitation of liability. This was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit, which held that the district court had no jurisdiction to pass on the merits of the claim without first holding that the owner was entitled to limitation of liability, if indeed, he was entitled to it. At the same time, the Circuit Court of 

Appeals held that the owner was not entitled to limitation of liability and therefore ruled that the district court should have dismissed the application and should have remanded the case to the state court for further proceedings. The owner of the vessel petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, the petition was granted, and the case will be argued during the coming term.88 

Longshoremen's and Harbor Workers' 

Compensation Act. 

Until recently the seamen were the only class of persons working on navigable waters who had a remedy for injury sustained in the course of employment. Various attempts were made by Congress to provide compensation for longshoremen, but the acts were declared unconstitutional by the Supreme Court. 89 At last, in 1927, Congress passed the 

Longshoremen's and Harbor Workers' Compensation Act,90 

which now provides compensation for these employees. The 

Act does not affirmatively define classes of persons who are benefited by this Act. If broadly interpreted, it covers everybody working on vessels except those specifically excluded under the Act. These are the master or a member of a crew of any vessel or any person engaged by the master 

88 No. 617-Langnes vs. Green. Case pending. 

89 Act of October 6, 1917 (40 Stat. L. 395); Act of June 10, 1922 (42 Stat. L. 634). 

90 Act of March 4, 1927 (44 Stat. L. 1424). [ 340]

to load or unload or repair any small vessel under eighteen tons net, or an officer or employee of the United States or any agency thereof or of any state or foreign government, or of any political subdivision thereof. 

The question came up this term whether a railroad employee injured while working on a car-float of the railroad could maintain an action for his injuries under the 

Federal Employers' Liability Act or whether his remedy was exclusively under the Longshoremen's and Harbor 

Workers' Compensation Act. For the latter specifically provides that the remedy of the employees covered by that Act shall be exclusive, except where an employer fails to secure payment of compensation as required by the Act. 

The plaintiff in this case brought his action under the Federal Employers' Liability Act, but the federal district court in New York and the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit held that the compensation act was applicable and afforded an exclusive remedy. This was affirmed by the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Chief 

Justice Hughes.91 

91 No. 248-Nogueira vs. N. Y., N. H. & Harford R. R. Decided 

April 14, 1930. [341]

CHAPTER XII. 

Prohibition

The Eighteenth Amendment and the National Prohibi tion Act are not the only laws which figure in the prohibition cases coming before the courts. Problems of prohibition involve the Willis-Campbell Act, the Jones Law, provisions of the Tariff Act and of the Internal Revenue Laws, as well as the liquor laws of the various states. In addition we meet with the conspiracy statutes, laws against bribery, interferences with Government officers, the provision in the 

Constitution against unreasonable searches and seizures, etc.

Nor does this problem give rise only to criminal cases. In addition to the prosecutions brought under these various laws, there is a class of quasi-criminal cases, in which civil suits are instituted for the forfeiture of property used in the commission of liquor offenses. Finally, there are the purely civil cases, such as various claims under the internal revenue laws, and questions concerning licenses issued for the manufacture or use of liquor in various chemical industries. 

Criminal Offenses. 

At this late date, when prohibition has been on the statute books for over a decade, some people have not yet given up the hope that the Supreme Court might find the Eighteenth Amendment itself unconstitutional. This was a point raised in a prosecution under the National Prohibition

Act, wherein the defendant argued that for an amendment to the Constitution to be valid, it must have been passed by two-thirds of each house of Congress, whereas the Eighteenth Amendment passed the House of Representatives only by a [342]

vote of two-thirds of those present.

The Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the Fourth Circuit ruled against this conten tion, and the defendant's petition to the Supreme Court to review that decision was denied.¹

Is the purchase of liquor an offense under the prohibition laws? This question aroused the greatest amount of public interest on the subject of prohibition during this term. A purchaser was prosecuted under the National Prohibition Act. The United States District Court in Massachusetts, where the prosecution was brought, quashed the indictment on the ground that the provision of Section 6, Title II, of the National Prohibition Act, prohibiting the purchase of liquor without a permit, applied only to persons to whom permits to purchase liquor may lawfully issue under the Act. The court held that the mere purchasing of liquor was not an offense under the National Prohibition Law, and from this decision the Government appealed to the 

Supreme Court. 

About the same time another case came from the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit, in which the question arose whether one who orders liquor from a bootlegger may be held guilty of conspiracy to transport. In this case a banker in New York ordered a bootlegger in Philadelphia to send him intoxicating liquors, which were delivered at the banker's residence. The banker and the bootlegger were prosecuted for conspiracy to transport liquor. It was stipulated that the purchaser had ordered the liquor for his own use, and not for the purpose of resale or transportation con trary to the National Prohibition Act. The bootlegger pleaded guilty, while the banker pleaded nolo contendere. 

The trial court sentenced the banker to pay a fine of $200. The court agreed that the mere purchase of liquor was not an offense under the National Prohibition Act, and 

1 No. 735-Jebbia vs. U. S. Certiorari denied April 28, 1930. [ 343]

that the purchaser could not be convicted of aiding or abetting the sale. But in this case, the court held, the defendant was guilty of conspiracy to transport, because he had repeatedly telephoned to Philadelphia ordering liquor and so had become a party to an agreement requiring the transportation of the liquor; that he not only solicited, but caused the bootlegger to commit the offense. 

The purchaser appealed to the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit, which reversed the decision of the trial court on the ground that the evidence failed to show such degree of cooperation by the defendant in the illegal transportation as to render him guilty as a conspirator. 

These two cases were argued before the Supreme Court about the same time. The cases presented a difficult problem in prohibition enforcement. It was felt that should the 

Supreme Court hold the purchase of liquor itself an offense prohibited by the National Prohibition Act, then great numbers of otherwise law-abiding citizens might suddenly find themselves in the class of criminals; and so far as the problem of law enforcement was concerned, it was aptly remarked that one might as well build a roof over the entire United States. On the other hand, should the Supreme 

Court hold that the purchase of liquor was not an offense, then the anomalous situation would arise, where of two parties to the same transaction, one is guilty and the other is innocent.

The Supreme Court was thus confronted with a doublehorned dilemma. It held, however, that the purchase of liquor itself was not an offense.2 In the opinion rendered by Mr. Justice Sutherland, he reviewed the history of prohibition legislation as well as the departmental regulations and found that Congress did not intend to make the purchase of liquor an offense. He pointed out that the administra- 

2 No. 732-U. S. vs. Farrar. Decided May 26, 1930. [344]

tive officers charged with the enforcement of prohibition never attempted to prosecute a purchaser and throughout all these years this fact was well known to the legislature. 

The Court said: "However that may be, it is fair to assume that 

Congress, when it came to pass the Prohibition Act, knew this history and, acting in the light of it, deliberately and designedly omitted to impose upon the purchaser of liquor for beverage purposes any criminal liability. If aid were needed to support this view of the matter, it would be found in the fact, conceded by the government's brief, that during the entire life of the National Prohibition Act, a period of ten years, the executive departments charged with the administration and enforcement of the act have uniformly construed it as not including the purchaser in a case like the present; no prosecution until the present one has ever been undertaken upon a different theory; and 

Congress, of course well aware of this construction and practice, has significantly left the law in its original form."
