The question still remained, however, whether a purchaser of liquor, though not guilty of a substantive offense, might not be held guilty of conspiracy to transport. This question was squarely presented, but was not answered by the Supreme Court. It held that since the banker pleaded nolo contendere there was nothing left for the district court to do but to impose a sentence in the same manner as if the defendant had pleaded guilty, the only difference being that, while a judgment entered upon a plea of guilty may in the future be held to estop the defendant from claiming otherwise, the plea of nolo contendere did not have that effect. The trial court, the Supreme Court held, was no longer concerned with the question of guilt, but only with [345]

the character and extent of punishment. The Supreme 

Court added, however: "As to whether the stipulated facts, if open to consideration, make out a case of criminal conspiracy, we express no opinion."

It seems that here the term "conspiracy" was given too liberal an interpretation by the Circuit Court of Appeals and that considerably less cooperation would have been sufficient to establish a charge of conspiracy in offenses other than under the National Prohibition Act. On the other hand, it seems to us that in the prohibition cases, the Government is resorting too much to the conspiracy statutes rather than to prosecuting the offenders under the substantive offenses prohibited by the law. It may well be that the reason for this mode of prosecution is that the rules concerning allegations and proof in a conspiracy indictment are not as strict as in prosecutions for substantive offenses. This was brought out in a case which came to the Supreme Court toward the close of the term, where a defendant was prosecuted for conspiracy to import liquor into the United States, contrary to the National Prohibition Act and the Tariff Act. The indictment failed to allege that the liquor was of foreign manufacture or that it was brought into the United States from a foreign country. 

The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit held the indictment sufficient for the reason that an indictment for conspiracy required less details than would be necessary to charge a substantive offense. The defendant petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.*

Whatever may be the Government's reasons for resorting to the conspiracy statutes rather than prosecute for a substantive offense, the conspiracy statutes constitute the largest 

3 No. 555-U. S. vs. Norris. Decided May 26, 1930. 

4 No. 940-Satinover vs. U. S. Certiorari denied June 2, 1930. [346]

class of criminal cases occasioned by prohibition. On the other hand, it must also be noted that the defenses set up in these cases were usually frivolous. In one case the indictment charged conspiracy to sell intoxicating liquor "fit for beverage purposes, to wit, whiskey and alcohol," and the defendant petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision because the indictment did not state that the conspiracy was to sell intoxicating liquors "for beverage purposes. 

This question has come up in various circuit courts, and there have been differences of opinion as to the validity of the indictment. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the 

Eighth Circuit held that the indictment was sufficient, and the Supreme Court denied a writ of review in the case.5 

In another case the indictment charged conspiracy to import intoxicating liquors contrary to the provision of 

Section 593 (b) of the Tariff Act of 1922. The defendant maintained that the indictment was defective in that it described the liquors as alcohol, brandy, whiskey, rum, gin, beer, ale, porter or wine and as "distilled spirits containing more than one-half of one per cent. of alcohol by volume,' but did not state that the same were "fit for use for beverage purposes." The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth 

Circuit upheld the validity of that indictment, and the defendant's petition to review this decision was likewise denied by the Supreme Court. 6 In another case charging conspiracy, the defendant complained that the court below failed to instruct the jury properly on the function of character testimony in criminal cases. It appeared that after the Government introduced its evidence, the defendant introduced some testimony as to his good character and reputation. He then asked the trial court to instruct the jury that they shall not find the defen- 

5 No. 551-Carnahan vs. U. S. Certiorari denied March 3, 1930. 

6 No. 938-Capo vs. U. S. Certiorari denied June 2, 1930. 

[347]

dant guilty, unless they found him guilty beyond a reasonable doubt, and that the character testimony in itself was sufficient to raise a reasonable doubt in their minds as to the defendant's guilt. Of course, the trial court refused to give such instructions, on the ground that the credibility of the testimony was entirely within the province of the jury."

In others, the defendants maintained they were convicted of conspiracy to violate the National Prohibition Act upon insufficient testimony or upon testimony of accomplices and co-conspirators. In all these cases the lower courts ruled against the defendants and their petitions to review the decisions were denied by the Supreme Court. In short it may be said, that in none of the cases were the defenses based on the merits of the issues involved. 

The only defenses which had any bearing on human rights were those relating to the admissibility of evidence obtained by government officers through unreasonable searches and seizures. A defendant was prosecuted for possessing and transporting liquors and for conspiracy to violate the Tariff Act of 1922. He was arrested while waiting in his automobile near the wharf, where the liquor was imported. The Government agents searched him and found documents in his possession which disclosed the conspiracy to import the liquor. He maintained that the evidence was unlawfully obtained and should therefore be excluded. The court below, however, ruled that the search was made as an incident to a lawful arrest and was therefore valid. His petition to review that decision was denied.8

The defense of unreasonable searches and seizures was also raised in connection with substantive offenses charged under the Act. A defendant argued that she should not 

7 No. 587-Haffa vs. U. S. Certiorari denied March 3, 1930. 

8 No. 560-Sugarman vs. U. S. Certiorari denied March 3, 1930. 

[348]

have been prosecuted under the Prohibition Act because the Government officers searched her premises under a warrant issued upon an affidavit that she had violated the internal revenue laws. She argued that since the Government thus began prosecution under the internal revenue laws it could not prosecute for the same offense under the 

National Prohibition Act. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit ruled, however, that the issuance of the search warrant under the internal revenue laws did not commence the prosecution under those laws, and that the Government was therefore not estopped from prosecuting under the National Prohibition Act."

Another defendant was prosecuted for operating a still, and he argued that the evidence against him was obtained through an unlawful search and seizure, because in order to come to the still the prohibition agents were compelled to trespass upon private property, though not the property of the defendant. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the 

Seventh Circuit did not give very much weight to this defense.¹ 10 Some states, like Iowa, have not followed the attitude of the Federal Government toward the admissibility of evidence obtained through unlawful searches and seizures.¹¹

Also in a case arising under the Prohibition Act of the State of Washington, it was maintained that evidence should be excluded, because it had been secured through an unreasonable search and seizure by trespassing on the defendant's property. But the Supreme Court of that state held that while the defendant could have an action against the officer for trespass, he could not maintain that the evidence ob- 

9 No. 550-Bevington vs. U. S. Certiorari denied February 24, 1930. 

10 No. 784-Reider vs. U. S. Certiorari denied May 19, 1930. 

11 No. 404-Bamsey vs. Iowa. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. [349]

tained through the trespass should not be admitted.12 We think that if the purpose in refusing to admit such evidence is to discourage government officers from violating constitutional rights, then such government officers will be disuaded by far more effectively through an action for trespass or other civil or criminal action, than through a refusal by the trial court to admit their evidence.13 

Among the substantive offenses under the National Prohibition Act were prosecutions for unlawful sale of liquor, for operating stills, for importation of liquor, concealment of contraband liquor and interference with prohibition officers. In two prosecutions for unlawful sale of liquor the defendants argued that the sale of the liquor was no longer prohibited by the National Prohibition Act, and that the Willis-Campbell Act had to this extent repealed the 

National Prohibition Act. On October 29, 1919, when Congress enacted the National Prohibition Act prohibiting the manufacture, sale, transportation, possession, etc., of intoxicating liquor, it repealed all the Internal Revenue laws which were in effect and which made it a criminal offense to defraud the United States of a tax upon distilled spirits in so far as those laws recognized intoxicating liquor for beverage purposes.

Among the laws then in existence and impliedly repealed by the National Prohibition Act, was Section 3242, Revised Statutes, which made the manufacture or sale of liquor, without payment of the special tax, punishable by a fine of not less than $100 nor more than $5,000, and imprisonment for not less than 30 days nor more than two years. Then came the Willis-Campbell Act, passed on November 23, 1921 (42 Stats. 223), which re-enacted all the penalties for 

12 No. 721-Knudsen vs. Washington. Certiorari denied April 28, 1930. 

13 Hankin: U. S. Supreme Court, 1928-1929, p. 198 et seq. 

[350]

violations of such revenue laws, except those provisions of the laws which were directly in conflict with any provision of the National Prohibition Act. 

Section 3242, Revised Statutes, thus became enacted at a date later than the National Prohibition Act, and the defendants in these cases relied upon the rule that where two statutes undertake to punish for the same offense, the later statute controls. Therefore, the defendants argued that by re-enacting Section 3242, Revised Statutes, Congress in effect repealed the prohibition against sale of liquor in the 

National Prohibition Act. Both the Circuit Court of Ap peals for the Seventh and for the Eighth Circuits ruled contrary to this contention of the defendants.¹ 14 The effect of the Willis-Campbell Act on the National 

Prohibition Act also came up in a case from the Fifth Circuit. This was a suit instituted by the Government to recover taxes and penalties for failure to file a tax return and for failure to pay a special tax imposed for engaging in the business of retail liquor dealer in the State of Louisiana, contrary to the law of that state. Under the National Prohibition Act, a penalty was imposed double the amount of the penalty prescribed by the state. This suit under the 

Internal Revenue Laws was instituted after the defendant had been convicted and sentenced for violation of the 

National Prohibition Act. In the courts below the defendant maintained that he was subjected to double jeopardy, and also that in so far as the federal Government undertook to punish a violation of a state law, such penalty was unconstitutional. The Circuit Court of Appeals did not pass upon this question, but it did sustain the defendant's contention that Section 5 of the Willis-Campbell Act barred an action for penalties under the Internal Revenue Laws after 

14 No. 806-Murphy vs. U. S. Certiorari denied May 19, 1930;

No. 550-Bevington vs. U. S. Certiorari denied February 24, 1930. 

[351]

prosecution had been instituted under the National Prohibition Act. For in re-enacting the previous statutes, the 

Willis-Campbell Act also provided that "if any act is a violation of any such laws and also of the National Prohibition Act or of this Act, a conviction for such act or offense under one shall be a bar to prosecution thereunder under the other." The Supreme Court granted the Government's petition to review that decision.15

In several cases the defendants complained that the courts below exceeded their jurisdiction by imposing heavier penalties than provided by law. A defendant was sentenced to nine months imprisonment and to pay a fine of $500, when he was convicted of illegal possession of intoxicating liquor, after two prior convictions. It so happened that on the first conviction he was sentenced to imprisonment for sixty days on two counts charging him with manufacture and possession of liquor, the latter being only an incident of the former. In the second prior conviction he was charged with possession and sale, for which he was sentenced to ninety days in jail and a fine of $200. He argued that in the present case the sentence should have been no greater than that for a second offense of illegal possession. After the above sentence was imposed, he instituted habeas corpus proceedings on the ground that the court below exceeded its jurisdiction, but the writ was denied by the lower courts and the Supreme Court denied his petition to review the case.16

Other defendants complained of the heavy penalties imposed by the Jones Law," at the same time maintaining that the law was unconstitutional. It appeared from the 

15 No. 800-U. S. vs. La Franca. Certiorari granted May 5, 1930. 

16 No. 661-U. S. ex rel. Derencz vs. Martin, Warden, etc. Certio rari denied March 17, 1930. 

17 Act of March 2, 1929-c. 473; 45 Stat. 1446; U. S. C., Sup. III,

Title 27, Secs. 91, 92. 

[ 352]

evidence in one case, that the defendant had just purchased a restaurant which was conducted like a "speakeasy” with a buzzer, peephole, barricade, etc. On that day, the Government alleged, he sold two ounces of liquor to a prohibition agent. The defendant declared that as a matter of fact he had intended to remove all the paraphernalia when he opened the place for business. He also denied that he had made the sale. This defendant was sentenced to two years at hard labor in the penitentiary. 

The defendant argued that the trial court erred in imposing this sentence when the evidence disclosed only an occasional sale of liquor not a habitual commercialization, or what is known as regular bootlegging. The Circuit 

Court of Appeals sustained the conviction and sentence, and the defendant petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, maintaining that the Jones Law was unconstitutional in that Congress has delegated to the courts the legislative discretion of fixing penalties for violation of the law. 

The Supreme Court denied that petition.18 This constitutional question came to the Supreme Court in another case toward the very end of the term, but the petition in that case has not yet been submitted to the Court.19 

Quasi Criminal Cases. 

Where the National Prohibition Act is violated by means of an automobile or a vessel, the Government may institute forfeiture proceedings against the automobile or vessel itself, or, if liquor is served at a given place, the law provides that the place itself be padlocked, all that irrespective of the owner's participation in the offense. 

The Government instituted proceedings against the 

Butler Hotel Company of Seattle, Washington, charging 

18 No. 886-Ross vs. U. S. Certiorari denied June 2, 1930. 

19 No. 958-McElvogue vs. U. S. Case pending. [353]

the hotel with "keeping" liquor contrary to the National Prohibition Act and seeking to abate the dining room of the hotel as a nuisance. The evidence showed that the patrons in the dining room of the hotel consumed their own liquor in glasses with cracked ice and ginger ale furnished by the hotel.

The United States District Court in Washington and the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit held that under such facts, the hotel was guilty of "keeping liquor on the premises" in violation of the National Prohibition Act, and ordered the dining room to be closed for a year and a day.

The hotel petitioned the Supreme Court to review this decision on the ground that there was conflict among the various Circuit Courts of Appeal as to whether this constituted "keeping" of liquor, but the petition was denied.20 

The Government's proceedings to forfeit automobiles resulted somewhat more favorably to innocent persons who had an interest in the machines. The occupants of an automobile, transporting liquor, were arrested and bound over to the United States Commissioner on a charge of violating the National Prohibition Act. The United States Attorney did not proceed against them under that Act, but had them indicted under Section 3296, Revised Statutes,21, and filed a libel against the automobile under Section 3450, Revised Statutes.22 Had the Government proceeded against the automobile under Section 26 of the National Prohibition 

Act, then an automobile financing corporation, which had a 

20 No. 618-Butler Hotel Co. vs. U. S. Certiorari denied March 17, 1930. 

21 Title 26, U. S. C., Sec. 404. An internal revenue provision making it an offense to remove distilled spirits on which the tax has not been paid.

22 Title 26, U. S. C., Sections 1181 and 1182. This section provides for the forfeiture of means of transportation used in the removal of distilled spirits with intent to defraud the United States of taxes. [354]

lien on the car, would have been entitled to the amount of its lien, while under Section 3450, Revised Statutes, the innocent lien holder loses his lien.

In a previous case, decided two years ago, the Supreme Court held that where proceedings are instituted under the National Prohibition Act against the occupants of the car transporting liquor, the Government may not institute forfeiture proceedings under Section 3450, Revised Statutes, against the automobile, for the reason that the gist of the proceeding was a violation of the National Prohibition Act.23 In the present case, proceedings under the National 

Prohibition Act had not been instituted. The occupants were merely charged with having violated the National Prohibition Act. For this reason the District Court and the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit held that the Government could proceed under Section 3450, Revised Statutes, and the financing company brought the case to the Supreme Court.

This Court held,24 that the Government could not institute proceedings under Section 3450, but was limited to proceedings under the National Prohibition Act, because the essence of the offense was a violation of the National 

Prohibition Act in the transportation of liquor. Justice 

Stone, who delivered the opinion of the Court, said: "In providing for forfeitures under this section, Congress was not unaware that the enactment of the 

National Prohibition Act would enormously increase seizures of vehicles, beyond those made under Section 3450, and that their forfeiture would place an increased and heavy burden on many innocent persons, unless afforded some protection by the new legislation. By 

23 Commercial Credit Co. vs. U. S., 276 U. S. 226. See also Port Gardner Investment Co. vs. U. S., 272 U. S. 564. 

24 No. 452-Richbourg Motor Co. vs. U. S.; No. 569-Davies Motors, Inc. vs. U. S. Decided May 19, 1930. 

[355]

Section 26 it gave such protection in all cases where the prosecution of the person guilty of the transportation is had under the National Prohibition Act. This would have been but an idle gesture and the Congressional purpose would have been defeated if, in practically every case where the transporting vehicle is seized the prosecuting officers could compel forfeiture of the interests of innocent third persons under Section 3450.

Yet, that is the effect of the construction of Section 26 contended for by the government, since, with the enactment of national prohibition, there can be few cases of illegal transportation which do not involve the concealment of non-tax-paid liquor. "The conclusion we reach is not without support in the legislative history of Section 26. The clause protecting the interests of innocent lienors was added by amendment in the House of Representatives to H. R. 

6810, which became the National Prohibition Act. The sponsor for the amendment pointed out that the procedure prescribed by the section as originally drawn protected the interests of the innocent owner and stated that the amendment was designed to save from forfeiture the interests of innocent lienors and innocent owners alike."

A similar question came to the Court toward the close of the term in which liquor was imported into the United States by vessel, and the Government instituted forfeiture proceedings against the vessel and its cargo under Section 4377, Revised Statutes, which provides that if any vessel is employed in any trade other than that for which it was licensed, she and her cargo shall be forfeited. Here the captain of the vessel maintained that the Government could not proceed under that section of the Revised Statutes, but was limited to proceedings against the vessel under Section [356]

of the National Prohibition Act. The Circuit Court of 

Appeals for the First Circuit held that the Government was not limited to proceeding under Section 26 and the Supreme Court denied a writ of review in the case. 25 The National Prohibition Act also provides that property used in the manufacture of liquor shall be forfeitable, and in one case the question arose whether an innocent looking electric plant used in the operation of an illicit still was forfeitable. This plant was sold under a conditional sales contract in which title remained with the seller until all the payments were made. Several payments were still due when it was discovered that the buyer used the plant for the manufacture of liquor, and the Government instituted forfeiture proceedings against the electric plant as "property designed for the manufacture of liquor." The seller intervened and claimed the amount of his lien, and his claim was sustained by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the First Circuit. The 

Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, but its petition was denied.26 

This case did not attract as much interest as another involving the same question. There the Government instituted forfeiture proceedings against a large quantity of empty bottles, barrels, cartons, corks, etc., as forfeitable property used in the manufacture of liquor under Section 

25, Title II, of the National Prohibition Act. It so hap pened that prohibition agents passed by the place where these properties were located and detected an odor of whiskey emanating from the premises. This odor came from some 400 used empty whiskey barrels stored on the premises for sale in the regular course of business. Upon an affidavit filed by the prohibition agents, a search warrant 

25 No. 902-Alksne vs. U. S. Certiorari denied June 2, 1930. 

26 No. 420-U. S. vs. Kohler Company. Certiorari denied November 

4, 1929. [357]

was issued and the premises searched, but no liquor was found, except a very small quantity which the owner had drained from this very large number of containers. The 

Government then instituted forfeiture proceedings against this property and the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit held the property forfeitable under the 

National Prohibition Act. The case came to the Supreme 

Court.

The reason for the great amount of public anxiety in the case was the fear lest an affirmance of the Circuit Court of Appeals might mean the end of the manufacture of home brew. Apparently the Supreme Court was not influenced by that possibility, for in an opinion delivered by Justice Holmes, the Supreme Court held that the Circuit Court of 

Appeals was right.27

It was argued by the owner of the property that the empty barrels, bottles, corks, etc., could not be said to be used in the manufacture of liquor, because they were not used until the manufacture of the liquor had been completed. But the Court held that the word "manufacture" in the Act was used in a looser sense, to express the whole process by which an article is made ready for sale in the open market. Said Justice Holmes: "We are of opinion that the word was used in this looser way, and that if the empty containers and the other objects seized were offered for sale in such a mode as purposely to attract purchasers who wanted them for the unlawful manufacture, as we interpret the word, they were designed for that manufacture and could be seized.'

Much comment arose as a result of this decision until later, when the Commissioner of Prohibition, it was reported, issued a statement that he did not intend to cause the 

27 No. 424-Danowitz vs. U. S. Decided May 5, 1930. 

[ 358]

seizure of such inoffensive objects, unless they were intended for illegal purposes.

The National Prohibition Act also provides for the forfeiture of warehouses, if liquor is unlawfully removed from them. In a case which came to the Supreme Court from the 

Eighth Circuit, it was held that a warehouse from which liquor was removed by persons unknown and without knowledge or consent of the owner of the warehouse, nevertheless rendered the warehouse forfeitable under the provisions of Sections 3257 and 3281 of the Revised Statutes. 

In this case, the owner of a warehouse had leased it to a distillery concern. When the National Prohibition Act was passed, the distillery suspended operations, but the goods which had already been manufactured were kept in the warehouse under a bond securing the payment of taxes in case of removal. Before the expiration of the period covered by the bond, it was discovered that some of the whiskey had been removed. There was no evidence that the removal was done with the knowledge or acquiescence of the distillery company or of the owner of the warehouse. The Government then instituted proceedings to forfeit the premises, on the ground that the distilling business was carried on with intent to defraud the Government of revenue. 

Under these facts, the owner of the warehouse argued that the Government had presented no evidence showing any connection between the removal of the whiskey and the owners of the property, and that, therefore, their property was not subject to forfeiture. The federal district court in 

St. Louis, Missouri, where the proceedings were brought, held that the forfeiture did not depend on whether the owner of the property was himself in the distilling business or had any knowledge of the unlawful removal of the whiskey. It held that the property was subject to forfeiture, and the decision was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals [359]

for the Eighth Circuit. The owner petitioned the Supreme 

Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.28 

A similar case came from the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit. In that case, however, forfeiture proceedings were brought under Sections 3257 and 3281, Revised Statutes, after the distillery corporation had been proceeded against under the National Prohibition Act. The corporation argued that the present suit was barred by Section 5 of the Willis-Campbell Act, in accordance with the decision of the Fifth Circuit discussed above.29 The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit held, however, that the distillery was forfeitable, and the distillery corporation petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision. This case has not yet been submitted for the 

Court's consideration.³

Civil Cases. 

Several prohibition cases came up in connection with income and excess profits taxes. The question was whether an owner of a distillery or brewery was entitled to deduct absolescence and losses of good will, trade-marks, and physical properties, when this business was proscribed by prohibition legislation. As was already stated, the Supreme 

Court held that these deductions were not allowable.31 

The remaining civil prohibition cases dealt with the right of the Commissioner of Prohibition to promulgate a general regulation terminating unlimited licenses for the manufacture or use of denatured alcohol, or for the use of alcohol in medicinal or toilet preparations. 

28 No. 549-Motlow vs. U. S. Certiorari denied February 24, 1930. 

29 See p. 352 supra. 

30 No. 893-Waterloo Distilling Corp. vs. U. S. Case pending. 

31 No. 68-Clarke, Coll'r. vs. Haberle Crystal Springs Brewing Co.;

No. 114-Renziehausen vs. Comm'r. of Internal Revenue. Decided Janu ary 27, 1930. See p. 193 supra. [360]

A number of suits were brought to enjoin the Commissioner of Prohibition and the Prohibition Administrators in

New York and Pennsylvania from enforcing a general order promulgated in 1927, that all permits issued prior to October 1 of any year shall expire on December 31 of that year, and that any permits issued after October 1 shall expire on 

December 31 of the following year.

Three of these cases came to the Supreme Court on a writ of review, and two came on certificate from the Circuit 

Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit. The specific ques tions in the cases were as follows: (1) Whether denatured alcohol or medicinal preparations containing more than one-half of one per cent. of alcohol are "liquor" within the meaning of Section 6 of the National Prohibition Act, for which permits to manufacture may be issued for one year only. The Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Brandeis, answered that question in the negative.32 He said: "We are of opinion that the quoted provision of Section 6 is inapplicable to the permits held by the several plaintiffs. Whether or not the preparations manufactured by the plaintiffs are 'liquor' while being manufactured and before they are prepared for the market is wholly immaterial. We are not here concerned with the nature of the preparations in their varying stages of development. Our concern is with the object of the permits. This object, and the permission granted, is not simply to manufacture varyingly unfinished products; but to manufacture specified articles containing whiskey, alcohol or other spirits. The products are, by Section 4, called 'articles' and are ex- 

32 No. 443-Campbell, Adm'r. vs. Galeno Chemical Co.; No. 444Campbell, Adm'r. vs. D. P. Paul Co., Inc.; No. 445-Campbell, Adm'r. vs. W. H. Long & Co., Inc.; No. 510-Wynne vs. Swanson Chemical 

Corp.; and No. 511-Doran vs. Casper. Decided May 26, 1930. [ 361 ]

pressly excluded from the effect of the term 'liquor.' 

Moreover, Section 4 authorizes the issuance of permits for the use of whiskey, etc., in the manufacture of medicinal and other preparations only 'that are unfit for use for beverage purposes'; while 'liquor' is defined in 

Section 1 as meaning liquids, in addition to those enumerated, 'which are fit for use for beverage purposes.' 

The two definitions are mutually exclusive. If the article to be manufactured is fit for use for beverage purposes, and therefore liquor, a permit under Section 

4 cannot be issued; and vice versa. By express and obvious enactment, therefore, the permits held by the plaintiffs are not permits to manufacture liquor and are not within the expiration limit prescribed by Section 6." (2) Whether the provision in Section 6 of the National Prohibition Act, which directs that every permit shall designate the time limit when the authorized acts may be performed, requires that every permit shall have a definite time limitation; or whether that requirement is satisfied by the provision in the permit that it shall remain in force until revoked, sustained, or renewed, or surrendered by the holder, or cancelled by the Commissioner for violation of the National Prohibition Act. 

This question was likewise resolved in favor of the holders of the permits. The Court distinguished the purposes of Congress in limiting the manufacture of liquor, and in encouraging the manufacture of industrial alcohol. 

Justice Brandeis said: 

"Regulations are imposed only for the purpose of guarding against the diversion of this lawful business into the unlawful business of supplying intoxicating liquor. It is entirely consistent with the avowed pur- [362]

poses of the Act, that the restrictions on the one business should be more severe than those on the other." (3) Whether a permit issued without a time limitation may be terminated by a general regulation that all such permits shall expire on a certain date. The Court held that the power of the Commissioner of Prohibition to revoke unexpired permits is limited by Sections 5 and 9 of the Prohibition Act. Section 9 provides that the Commissioner, upon due notice and hearing, may revoke the permit of any person "who is not in good faith conforming to the provisions of this Act, or has violated the laws of any state relating to intoxicating liquors." Section 5 provides for a judicial review of the action taken by the Commissioner, namely, "by appropriate proceeding in a court of equity." 

Without complying with these provisions, the Commissioner could not revoke the licenses.

A different result, however, obtained in two other cases involving the same problem. A permit had been issued on 

August 26, 1924, and contained an expiration date of "December 31, 1924." That permit was continuously extended until December 31, 1928, when the general regulation terminating the permits became effective. The holder of the permit sought to enjoin the enforcement of the regulation on the theory that the insertion of the expiration date in the original permit was without authority of law, that the permit was therefore unlimited as to time, and that the Bureau of Prohibition could not by a general regulation end the right to manufacture. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit held that the holder of the permit was not entitled to the injunctive relief, and the Supreme Court declined to go into the matter.33 

33 No. 521-Lion Laboratories, Inc. vs. Campbell. Certiorari denied 

January 20, 1930. [363]

In the second case, the question arose whether the Prohibition Administrator could refuse to permit the use of whiskey in medicinal preparations on the ground that ethyl alcohol could be used as effectively, and the whiskey contributed no medicinal value to the drugs. The druggist maintained that there was a great deal of "good will" attached to drugs containing whiskey, because of the general belief that whiskey has curative or medicinal value. 

The District Court, where suit was brought to enjoin the 

Prohibition Administrator from refusing to renew the permit, sustained the Administrator, although the use of ethyl alcohol would hurt the good will of the medicines. 

On appeal, the Circuit Court of Appeals did not pass on the question of the power of the Prohibition Administrator to refuse renewal of the permit for the reason assigned by him, but held that the Administrator was justified in refusing the permit on the ground of misconduct. The alleged misconduct was contained in a report by the Prohibition Administrator that the original permit was granted on the condition that the druggist would do his compounding in the presence of a deputy Prohibition Administrator, and that he had failed to observe this condition. 

The druggist petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case. He claimed that the decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals was erroneous, because there was no evidence as to the alleged misconduct, and, furthermore, that the condition appended to the granting of the permit was unauthorized, and therefore invalid. This petition was likewise denied.³ 34 

34 No. 507-Liscio vs. Campbell. Certiorari denied January 13, 1930. [364]

CHAPTER XIII. 

Criminal Cases 

Although the late Chief Justice Taft had an unusually genial, kindly and cheerful disposition, he took a decidedly firm stand against criminals as well as against attempts to lessen the burdens imposed upon them, when they were made to pay the penalties for the wrongs they committed. 

It was evident from his public addresses that he bore no great love for criminals, and in commenting on the special legislation constantly pressed for their benefit, Chief Justice Taft deplored the condition of the "forgotten man"society. The late Chief Justice also favored increased punishments for habitual offenders and the adoption of statutes similar to those of the Baumes Law of New York as a means of discouraging the commission of further crimes and of safeguarding society from those who cannot otherwise be dissuaded from offending their fellow men.

In the Supreme Court itself, the chances of the criminal were very much diminished with the operation of the Jurisdictional Act of 1925. In cases coming from the state courts, no review of the decisions of the lower courts could be obtained, unless the case involved a substantial federal question. It may even be said that no review was made possible, unless the case involved a very substantial question. No criminal cases could come from the federal district courts, except on appeal by the Government. And in cases from the Circuit Court of Appeals, no review could be obtained, unless the questions presented were of public importance. 

The Supreme Court furthermore adopted a practice of [365]

advancing every criminal case on the docket. This practice may be said to have been adopted for the benefit both of the Government and of the defendant. For a defendant should not have a sentence hanging over him, if he is innocent or if his rights are otherwise being jeopardized; on the other hand, if he is guilty, punishment should not be delayed by long drawn out judicial processes. However, at the Conference of the American Law Institute in 1928, Chief Justice Taft stated that the purpose of the rule was not to permit criminals a "period of rest and contemplation" before they begin to pay their penalties.¹

It is difficult to say how far this treatment of criminal cases was influenced by the late Chief Justice, and how far the results were due to the fact that these cases rarely presented substantial problems. It is natural that these cases lack substantial problems. Under our law, both in the federal courts and in the state courts, many, perhaps too many, safeguards are instituted in favor of the accused. At every step in the criminal proceedings from the indictment to the sentence, technical rules are strictly applied in favor of the accused and, as a finale, the jury is always instructed that it must not find the defendant guilty, unless they believe he is guilty beyond a reasonable doubt. With these safeguards in the trial court and with an affirmance of the judgment by one or more appellate courts, it is to be expected that the criminal cases brought to the Supreme Court by the defendants will either present new and important questions of law, or will be without merit altogether. The great majority of the cases belong to the latter class. 

This is well illustrated by the manner in which the criminal cases were disposed of by the Supreme Court within the last three years. In the year 1927-1928, 101 criminal cases came to the Court; in 1928-1929 the number was 88; 

1 Hankin: U. S. Supreme Court, 1928-1929, p. 184. 

[366]

this term it was 81. In only two out of the 101 criminal cases in the 1927 term did the Court reverse the decisions of the lower courts; one was reversed in favor of the Government, the other in favor of the accused. In the 1928 term only one decision was reversed, and that was reversed in favor of the accused. This term the Court reversed the decisions of the lower courts in four cases; three in favor of the Government and one in favor of the accused. 

Of the 81 criminal cases considered this term, 61 involved federal offenses, while 20 involved offenses against the states. Of the 61 criminal cases involving prosecutions for federal offenses, one came to the Supreme Court on certificate, three came on appeal by the Government from the district courts, while the remainder came from the Circuit Court of Appeals in petitions for writs of certiorari. In the case which came on certificate the Supreme Court answered the question sustaining the decision of the district court against the defendant. In two of the three appeals from the district courts, this Court reversed the decisions in favor of the Government; in the third it affirmed the decision in favor of the accused. The Court granted three out of the 

57 petitions for writs of review, in one of which it reversed the decision of the Circuit Court of Appeals, while the other two cases are still pending.

Of the 20 state cases, eight were appeals by the defendants from the state courts. In one of these the Court affirmed the decision of the highest court of the state; in one case the Court heard part of the argument and then dismissed the case for lack of a substantial federal question; four appeals were dismissed for lack of jurisdiction on the preliminary showing; and two cases were dismissed by the appellants themselves. The remaining 12 cases were petitions for writs of certiorari; one from the United States

Circuit Court of Appeals, the rest from the state courts. In [367]

all these cases the Supreme Court declined to review the decisions of the lower courts. 

Federal Offenses. 

The largest class of criminal cases involved offenses under the National Prohibition Act. There were 25 such cases and the most important of these have already been discussed. The second largest classes were prosecutions for using the mails to defraud and violations of the Federal Reserve Act, with five cases in each class. Prosecutions under the Narcotic Act yielded three cases. 

There were also three cases for contempt of court. The remaining cases involved bribery, offenses under the Bankruptcy Act, interferences with federal employees, traffic violations in the District of Columbia, violation of the Corrupt Practices 

Act, the Naturalization Act and the Neutrality Act. 

In his inaugural address, President Hoover stressed the grave criminal problem throughout the country, and shortly after he took office he appointed a National Commission on 

Law Observance and Enforcement to study the problem. 

The Commission has been working now for more than a year, gathering and analyzing facts. They have issued no final reports; apparently they do not intend to put out a slipshod piece of work. But at the insistence of some members of Congress, the Commission submitted to the President a preliminary report, in which the Commission. treated, among other things, of trial by jury a right guaranteed by the Constitution, one which is regarded by many citizens as the very foundation of liberty, but which others regard as a source of unintelligent administration of justice. 

The Commission favored a limitation of trial by jury. 

About the same time the American Law Institute adopted a 

271st Congress, 2nd Sess., H. R. Doc. 252. 

[368]

model code of criminal procedure in which it likewise favored limitations on the right of trial by jury. This question was also the main question in a number of criminal cases in the Supreme Court this term. In one of these the Court held that the right to a trial by twelve men was not so sacred that it could not be waived by a defendant. Three men were prosecuted for conspiracy to bribe a federal prohibition officer. After a week of trial, one juror became seriously ill, and it was stipulated between the Government and the counsel for the accused that the trial should proceed with eleven jurors. The incomplete jury then returned a verdict of guilty, and the defendants appealed on the ground that they were deprived of their constitutional right of a trial by jury. The question came to the Supreme Court, whether a defendant for a crime, punishable by a penitentiary sentence, may waive trial by a complete jury. In previous decisions the Supreme Court had intimated that in cases involving serious offenses defendants may not waive trial by a complete jury.3

In an opinion by Justice Sutherland, the Supreme Court now held that the defendants could waive trial by a complete jury, that the provision in the Constitution guaranteeing the right of trial by a fair and impartial jury was only a guaranty for the protection of the accused, which he may forego at his election. The Court held, however, that while the right to a complete jury may be waived by the defendant, the right of the accused to a trial by jury should be jealously safeguarded. Justice Sutherland said: "In affirming the power of the defendant in any criminal case to waive a trial by a constitutional jury and submit to trial by a jury of less than twelve persons, or by the court, we do not mean to hold that the 

3 Thompson vs. Utah, 170 U. S. 343. 

4 No. 53-Patton et al. vs. U. S. Decided April 14, 1930. 

[369]

waiver must be put into effect at all events. That perhaps sufficiently appears already. Trial by jury is the normal and, with occasional exceptions, the preferable mode of disposing of issues of fact in criminal cases above the grade of petty offenses. In such cases the value and appropriateness of jury trial have been established by long experience, and are not now to be denied. 

Not only must the right of the accused to a trial by a constitutional jury be jealously preserved, but the maintenance of the jury as a fact finding body in criminal. cases is of such importance and has such a place in our traditions, that, before any waiver can become effective, the consent of government counsel and the sanction of the court must be had, in addition to the express and intelligent consent of the defendant. And the duty of the trial court in that regard is not to be discharged as a mere matter of rote, but with sound and advised discretion, with an eye to avoid unreasonable or undue departures from that mode of trial or from any of the essential elements thereof, and with a caution increasing in degree as the offenses dealt with increase in gravity."

In a case from the District of Columbia, the question arose whether a defendant was entitled to a trial by jury altogether. Here an information was filed in the Police 

Court charging the defendant with reckless driving. He demanded a jury trial, which was refused. On appeal, the 

Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia reversed that decision and held that the defendant was entitled to a jury trial, because the offense of reckless driving, as defined and punished in the District Traffic Act, was not a petty offense within the meaning of the Constitution. On petition of the [370]

District of Columbia, the Supreme Court granted a writ of review in the case, which will be argued next term.5 

The complaints of defendants on the subject of trial by jury ranged from the selection of the jury to the conduct of the jurors in the jury room. Most of the objections were without merit and do not warrant discussion. One defendant, for example, argued in his petition to the Supreme Court that he was deprived of his rights under the Constitution, because one of the jurors, it was discovered after the trial, was not a resident of the judicial district where the prosecution took place, i. e., he was a resident in the Eastern District of New York, whereas the prosecution took place in the Southern District.Ⓡ 

Admissibility of evidence is another pet objection in criminal cases coming to the Supreme Court. As one sits through a criminal trial and hears the testimony constantly interrupted by exclamations of "I object," one often wonders whether the object of a trial is to get at the facts or to play a game of concealing facts. Sometimes, however, the objection to evidence is accompanied by a claim that the evidence was unlawfully obtained, and therefore the defendant was compelled to testify against himself in a criminal case. One defendant prosecuted for using the mails to defraud, argued that the evidence against him should not have been admitted, because his books and records were taken by subpoena, though at the time they were in possession of another." Another, prosecuted for concealing books from a referee in bankruptcy, argued that the testimony of a Government witness should not be admitted, because the evidence was unlawfully obtained, though this witness was not an official 

5 No. 857-District of Columbia vs. Colts. Certiorari granted May 

26, 1930. 

6 No. 290-Rosenstein vs. U. S. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

7 No. 103-Fuller vs. United States. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. [371]

member of the searching party, but had gone to the searched premises after the warrant was served, having been invited there by the brother-in-law of the defendant.8 

The next class of objections is that the evidence was not sufficient to be submitted to the jury. One defendant charged with using mails to defraud argued that he was merely an officer of the corporation involved in the scheme, but that he personally had no direct connection with it; another argued that the letters which contained the illegal material had not been sufficiently identified,10 and in still another, a prosecution under the Narcotic Act, the defendant maintained that his conviction could not be sustained, because no drugs were found on his person, and the burden of proof was put upon him, instead of the Government, to explain his presence when his companion delivered the morphine.¹¹

Defendants frequently object that the juries had not been properly instructed by the court on the law involved in the cases. A defendant, prosecuted for using the mails to defraud, argued that the trial court misinstructed the jury when he stated that "there was no question that the defendants used the mails." " 12 We wonder whether among the numerous assignments of error relating to the instructions to juries on technical questions of the law there are an appreciable number which in any way bear on the merits of the cases; or whether they are merely technicalities raised to becloud the issues. We

wonder how many juries understand the instructions altogether, let alone the hair-splitting distinctions which are sometimes raised in these cases. It takes three years for a student to learn the fundamentals of the law, and we expect a jury to learn the law and apply it upon a single instruction from a judge. Let the reader experiment by reading a set of instructions to an average group picked at random, and then see how much of it is understood.18 We venture a guess that in many cases instructions to the jury on the questions of law are but an idle gesture, and that a great deal of difficulty might be avoided by requiring juries to pass only on specific questions of fact.

The "sufficiency of the indictment" is also frequently the subject of the defendant's complaint in criminal cases, not that the defendant is not apprized of the charges against him, which he understands perfectly well. In a case under the Harrison Narcotic Act, the defendant argued that the indictment was defective, because it failed to state that the morphine sold by him was done with the intent to deprive the United States of revenue, or that it was not made in pursuance of a written order of the person to whom it was sold or on a form issued in blank for that purpose by the 

Commissioner of Internal Revenue.¹4

In a prosecution for interference with federal employees, the defendants maintained that they were prejudiced by a misjoinder of a conspiracy charge with a charge for a substantive offense. This prosecution charged that the defendants had used deadly weapons threatening and intimidating employees of the Bureau of Animal Industry of the Department of Agriculture, and conspired in preventing them from discharging their duties of supervising the dipping of cattle in order to prevent the spread of splenetic fever.

It appeared that the State of Georgia had a law for the eradication of cattle tick, and the state veterinarian had entered into an agreement with the Department of Agriculture whereby the latter would furnish men to cooperate with the state veterinarian. The evidence showed that the defendants in this case resisted the efforts of the federal employees from discharging their duties and killed one of the employees and wounded another. Upon this evidence they were convicted and sentenced by the District Court in Georgia, and the decision was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit.

The defendants petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, maintaining that the prosecution for conspiracy was erroneously consolidated with the substantive offense, and that furthermore it was not shown that the Federal Government had any criminal jurisdiction in the case, because although there was evidence that the cattle in question were in a county in Georgia adjoining Florida, there was no evidence that the cattle roamed into the neighboring state, and therefore the offense, if any, was entirely against the 

State of Georgia.

They further argued that the federal employees in question were not threatened or prevented from discharging their duties as federal employees, because they came into [374]

the State of Georgia for the purpose of enforcing a Georgia act and under an arrangement with the state veterinarian, and that therefore they were for the time being loaned to the State of Georgia and were not in the course of doing federal service.18 15 A question which is often fruitlessly raised is whether in a case charging conspiracy, one of the conspirators may be held guilty when the other is held innocent. Jose Gandara and others were prosecuted for conspiring to set on foot a military expedition against Mexico, in violation of the neutrality of the United States. The indictment was returned against Gandara, Borgaro, Valenzuela and Bishop 

Navarette, and other persons to the grand jurors unknown.

The Government then dropped the prosecution against Borgaro and Valenzuela. Bishop Navarette was acquitted, and the defendant Gandara was found guilty. The United

States District Court in Arizona sentenced Gandara to imprisonment for two years and to a fine of $1,000, and the judgment was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals. 

Gandara petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, maintaining that since the prosecution was dropped against two of the other defendants, and the third was acquitted, he could not be convicted because he could not have committed the crime of conspiracy by himself, and furthermore, that there was no evidence showing, or tending to show, that there was any conspiracy or agreement between himself and any Yaqui Indians, "or other unnamed or unknown persons" to commit any act which was in violation of the neutrality statute referred to in the indictment. But his petition was denied.18 

15 No. 766-Carter vs. United States. Certiorari denied May 19, 1930. 

16 No. 519-Gandara vs. United States. Certiorari denied January 27, 1930. [375]

Among the more interesting criminal questions this term, three involved problems of criminal jurisdiction. In each case, however, the term jurisdiction is used in a different sense. First, the power of Congress to define and punish the offense; second, territorial jurisdiction of the United States; and third, the jurisdiction of the Court to entertain the appeal.

The first came under the Corrupt Practices Act and involved the prosecution of former Congressman Wurzbach of Texas for receiving contributions from federal employees for a political purpose, that is, the promotion of his candidacy for Congress in the Republican primaries. The district court held that since the contributions were received for the "primary" elections, Wurzbach was not guilty of an offense against the United States, because Congress had no jurisdiction to legislate with regard to "primaries."

But the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice 

Holmes, held that the provision in the Federal Corrupt

Practices Act, prohibiting the receiving of contributions from federal employees "for any political purpose whatever" was applicable to contributions for a candidacy for Congress in the primary elections, because Congress may provide that its officers and employees shall not be subjected to pressure for money for political purposes, while they retain their office or employment."7

The second case raised the question whether the Federal Government had jurisdiction over a crime committed on a railroad's right of way within a federal reservation. The defendant was charged with murder in the first degree, committed on a freight train on the tracks of the Chicago & 

Northwestern Railway, within the Fort Robinson Military

Reservation. The defendant interposed a plea to the juris- 

17 No. 66-United States vs. Wurzbach. Decided February 24, 1930. 

See p. 386 infra. [376]

diction, maintaining that the offense was committed within the jurisdiction of the State of Nebraska, and the district court sustained that plea. It held that although the Reservation was exclusively within the jurisdiction of the United States, Congress had divested itself of jurisdiction over the strip of land constituting the railroad's right of way, because by giving the railroad a perpetual grant, the Government had abandoned its use of that right of way for military purposes.

The Supreme Court reversed that decision in favor of the Government and held that when the State of Nebraska ceded the Reservation to the United States, no exception was made as to the jurisdiction over this right of way. 

Furthermore, the grant of land to the railroad was not incompatible with the exclusive jurisdiction of the United States.¹ 18 In the third case, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit held that appeals from the decisions of the Supreme Court of the Territory of Hawaii are limited to federal questions only. But the Supreme Court refused the defendant's petition to review that decision.¹9

State Offenses. 

Of the twenty criminal cases which came from the states, four were prosecutions for murder, three for contempt of court, three came under the sedition law of the State of Pennsylvania,20 and three involved violations of the liquor laws of the respective states. Other state offenses involved prosecutions for rape, robbery, perjury, forgery, gambling and violations of banking and fishing laws. 

18 No. 509-United States vs. Unzeuta. Decided April 14, 1930. 

19 No. 391-Fukunaga vs. Hawaii. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

20 See p. 399 infra. [ 377 ]

The question of trial by jury, which was the most important question in the federal cases, also came up in connection with the state offenses. Specifically, the question related to trial by jury for capital offenses, where the defendant pleads not guilty by reason of insanity.

The frequency of criminal cases in which the defense of insanity is pleaded has caused a number of the states to adopt methods for regulating the procedure involving this defense. In times past, the defense of insanity in a criminal case was of infrequent occurrence. Partly that was due to the fact that the psychological and medical sciences were in an undeveloped state, and a man was not classified as insane unless he was a pronounced idiot or lunatic. Partly it was due to the fact that a defendant was usually tried at the place of his residence. He was known to the members of his community, and the members of the jury would be little influenced by the medical experts who might argue that the defendant was insane, whereas he was known in the community to be sane.

The defense of insanity was narrow in its scope, as also the rule of law for determining whether at the time the act was committed the defendant was sane. The test was, whether at the time of the offense the defendant knew or had an appreciation of the difference between right and wrong, between what he may and what he may not do. In cases of temporary or momentary insanity, as in cases of illusion, the law indulged in a presumption in favor of the accused, assuming his illusion to be a reality. Thus, if the accused killed another because he was under an irresistible illusion that the victim was about to kill him, then assuming the facts as they were imagined, the accused should be acquitted as having killed in self-defense. If, however, the accused had an irresistible impulse to kill a friend, because he thought him an enemy, then he should nevertheless be [ 378]

held guilty, because the law does not sanction the killing of enemies.

These distinctions, it is true, are crude, but they worked, in the sense that the accused was afforded an additional opportunity to escape punishment in order that the state might act consistently with its theory of criminal justice. 

If the object of the sentence is retribution to or correction of the criminal, or to dissuade others from following his example, then punishment would be a useless act if intent in the commission of the offense was lacking. It then becomes clear that if punishment, as a matter of scientific knowledge as presented by some psychologists or psychiatrists, neither corrects the offenders nor dissuades others, and the idea of retribution is barbarous, then the entire method of administration of criminal justice is a farce. Fortunately, these scientific facts still leave a loophole for the administration of criminal justice, namely, that the removal or incarceration of offenders gives society a feeling of security. 

This dissuades people from resorting to individual rather than socialized self-protection and consequently prevents to some degree the further commission of criminal acts.21 

21 I disagree with the senior author on this point. In spite of penalties which have always been imposed on criminals, the criminal situation is not improving, and a serious question arises how far penalties and punishments are practical methods in the prevention of crimes. It is relatively only a very short time ago when most of the serious offenses were punishable by death. Yet society has continued and prospered after capital punishment was abolished in all cases except murder and similar offenses, and there is no evidence that society would suffer should capital punishment be abolished altogether. The same may hold true with other types of cruel punishments. Except for the fact that society seeks to excuse punishments, on the ground that they deter others in the commission of crimes, the administration of punishment would be monstrous. With the development of the psychological sciences, it may be possible to find a reasonable rule for determining the extent and nature of punishment or treatment in order to attain results now sought in the administration of criminal justice. At any rate, it is time that at this late date, at least, a serious effort be made to determine by experimentation what means the psychological sciences offer in the solution of these troublesome problems on a humanitarian basis.-C. A. H. [379]

With the development of psychological studies and of medical sciences, a great many nervous disorders have been discovered. The elements of those disorders are so numerous and the classification so uncertain, however, that now the problem is not so much to find an insane person as it is to find a sane one. Numerous psychological and psychiatrical experts have sprung up who concern themselves not only with the study and correction of nervous or mental diseases, but who have invaded other fields, like industry, finance, sociology and teaching (even the teaching of mathematics), and Professor Muensterberg's book ON

THE WITNESS STAND, has encouraged lesser minds to claim for psychology a paramount place in the law. And now there are those who, it seems, are ready to advocate that criminal law be abolished altogether, and that society, including those who are so unfortunate as to have come in conflict with it, be nursed and cared for by psychological experts. 

The effect of this psychological movement has evidenced itself in the more serious criminal cases in the following manner: If the facts clearly show that the offense was committed, then counsel for the defense immediately resort to the aid of psychological experts, and the prosecution does the same. These scientists, it appears, are "worthy of their hire," and on the basis of their scientific knowledge, those for the defense say that the accused was insane, while those for the prosecution say that he was sane. And where scientists disagree, who should best be able to judge but a jury of twelve who know neither psychology, nor medicine, nor law?

As was indicated, the states have begun to feel the need of putting limitations to such procedure. Some states have passed laws requiring that where the defense of insanity is pleaded, that question should be passed on by a commission of alienists. The State of California passed an act which is [ 380]

intended to avoid confusion between a decision on the question of sanity and a decision on the question of guilt. 

This Act allowed five possible pleas to an indictment: (1) guilty; (2) not guilty; (3) a former judgment of conviction or acquittal of the offense charged; (4) once in jeopardy, and (5) not guilty by reason of insanity. The Act provided that where a defendant pleads "not guilty by reason of insanity" without also pleading "not guilty," he thereby admits the commission of the offense charged, and if he pleads "not guilty by reason of insanity" and also pleads "not guilty," then the trial must be divided into two proceedings.

First, the defendant is tried on his plea "not guilty," and in this trial he is conclusively presumed to be sane. If upon this trial the jury finds him guilty, then a second trial is held upon the question whether the defendant was sane or insane at the time the offense was committed. If, upon the second part of the trial the jury finds the defendant guilty, the defendant is subject to punishment in accordance with the law. If, on the other hand, the verdict is "not guilty by reason of insanity," the defendant must be committed to a state hospital for insane. 

A defendant who was indicted for murder in the first degree, pleaded "not guilty," and also "not guilty by reason of insanity." After the defendant was found guilty on the facts, he objected to being tried by the same jury on the question whether he was insane. He thought it monstrous to permit the same jury which found him guilty on the facts to try him also on the question of his sanity. He maintained that the statute is contrary to the due process clause of the Constitution, because in the trial on the facts, no evidence could be adduced to show that the defendant was insane, and the jury could not consider such [381]

mitigating circumstances as the state of the defendant's mind at the time of the commission of the act. 

These objections were overruled by the trial court on the ground that the statute merely made a change in procedure; that previously a defendant was tried by the same jury, both on the question whether he committed the offense, and on the question of his sanity, and that all the amendment did was to separate two issues into two separate trials. 

The jury found the defendant sane, and on appeal to the Supreme Court of the state, the judgment was affirmed. 

The defendant appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States and presented the arguments already urged before the state courts. The State of California maintained. in opposition, that the defense of insanity is not a defense to the commission of the criminal act itself, but merely a plea which enables the defendant to show by preponderance of evidence that at the time of the commission of the offense he was insane, and therefore he did not have a criminal intent. If the law can presume that a person is sane and permit him to prove otherwise, the law can go further and prescribe the procedure whereby the defendant might overcome this presumption. The state also maintained that the 

Supreme Court of the United States had no jurisdiction in the case, since this question of procedure is a state question, and not a substantial federal question which the Supreme Court can consider. After a few minutes of argument, the 

Supreme Court dismissed the appeal because it did not present a substantial federal question.22 

But the mind of the criminal lawyer is not to be daunted. 

If two trials by the same jury are not without due process according to the Federal Constitution, then two trials by separate juries may nevertheless be. The California law gives the trial court the discretion of having the two issues 

22 No. 247-Troche vs. California. Dismissed December 9, 1929. [382]

determined either by the same or by different juries, and a defendant who was found guilty by both juries appealed to the Supreme Court, but the appeal was dismissed.23 

Another noteworthy criminal problem concerned prosecutions for contempt of court. These cases are growing in importance not so much because of the specific issues involved, but because there is a growing feeling that courts have used their power to punish for contempt, in a manner inconsistent with a most valuable right of a free people, the right to criticize one's own government. Members of the executive and legislative branches of the government, both state and federal, may be criticized ad libidem. In fact the law itself recognizes not only the right, but also the desirability of free criticism. What may be considered slanderous or libellous, if stated concerning a private individual, may be excusable if uttered about a public officer. But not so with the judiciary. Any person criticizing a court may find himself prosecuted for contempt. Furthermore, a citizen may consider an Act of Congress or an Executive Order as being unfair or contrary to the best interests of society, and may advocate disobedience to it. But one may not in the same language criticize a decree of a court or advocate disobedience to it. If one advocates the disobedience of an 

Act of Congress and is then prosecuted under that Act, he may question the validity of the law, but if he advocates disobedience to a decree of court, and is prosecuted, he may not question the validity of the decree. 

This term a case came to the Supreme Court which illustrates some evils resulting from this situation. One 

Owens published a contemptuous statement concerning the manner in which the Supreme Court of Oklahoma decided a case in which he was a party. Later he filed a petition for a rehearing in the case, repeating the statement he had 

23 No. 500-Ex Parte: Murray. Dismissed February 24, 1930. [383]

made in the public press. The Supreme Court of Oklahome then undertook to proceed against Owens for contempt, and Owens asked that seven out of the eight judges disqualify themselves, because they were biased and were to be used as witnesses in the proceedings. This motion was overruled. Owens then refused to introduce any evidence and was sentenced for contempt.

He brought a petition in the Criminal Court of Appeals in Oklahoma for a writ of habeas corpus, and that court issued the writ directing the sheriff to release Owens, but the Chief Justice of the Supreme Court of Oklahoma directed the sheriff to disregard the writ issued by the Criminal Court of Appeals. Owens then brought this petition in the federal courts, but his petition was dismissed on the ground that the federal courts have no jurisdiction in such cases, except where the detention is contrary to the Constitution and laws of the United States. They held that the disqualification of the judges did not affect their jurisdiction in the contempt proceedings, but made their act only voidable or reviewable on appeal; that the order of the 

Chief Justice to dismiss the order of the Criminal Court did not present a federal question and that Owens was properly deprived of assistance of counsel, since the statement in the petition for rehearing was contempt in the presence of the court. The Supreme Court of the United 

States denied Owens' petition to review the case.24

Of course, the answer is that the dignity of the courts must be preserved. But the question then arises whether respect and dignity can be maintained by strong-arm methods, and whether it is desirable to do so. 

24 No. 474-Owens vs. Oklahoma. Certiorari denied December 9, 1929. [384]

CHAPTER XIV. 

Political Problems Federal Political Problems. 

Among the federal political problems considered by the Court during this term was the question of whether the Federal Corrupt Practices Act' was applicable to candidates for federal office in primary elections. As we have seen,2 

Congressman Wurzbach of Texas was prosecuted under this Act for having received contributions from a number of postal employees in his campaign as Republican candidate for Congress in the primaries of 1927.

This prosecution was quashed by the United States District Court in Texas on the ground that the provision in the Act forbidding the receiving or accepting of contributions from federal employees for "any political purpose" meant for any federal political purpose, for to hold otherwise would mean that Congress invaded the jurisdiction of the states, and hence the Act would be unconstitutional. This effort on the part of the District Court to save the Corrupt Practices Act from being declared unconstitutional was based on what the court deemed to be the effect of the deci sion of the Supreme Court in the Newberry Case.³ 

The Government appealed to the Supreme Court, which overruled the decision of the district court. It held that 

Congress had the power, in regulating the conduct of federal officers and employees, to prescribe such limitations 1 43 Stat. 1053, 1073. 2 See p. 376 supra. 

3 Newberry vs. United States, 256 U. S. 232. 

[ 385 ]

when contributions are received for any political purpose, including any state political purpose. To the contention of 

Congressman Wurzbach, that if applied to any state political purpose the Act would be unenforceable, the Supreme Court held that this did not invalidate or change the intent of Congress to prohibit such practices on the part of federal officers or employees.*

This case, though a criminal prosecution, was a political case in a double sense. First, because it concerned the conduct of officers and employees of the body politic, and second, because it was claimed the case was an outgrowth of controversies and antagonisms in Texas politics. For 

Congressman Wurzbach, who was afterwards recognized by Congress as a successful candidate, denied that there was any basis for the prosecution. 

The Teapot Dome Oil Scandals again reappeared in the

Court, and the last act of Mr. Justice Roberts in connection with those prosecutions was the filing of petitions for writs of certiorari in two cases growing out of these scandals. The first concerned the prosecution of Harry M. Blackmer for contempt of court when he refused to appear as a witness in the Fall-Sinclair conspiracy case.

In order to compel his appearance in that case, Congress, on July 3, 1926, had passed an act authorizing United 

States courts in criminal cases to issue subpoenas to be served by United States consuls upon American citizens living abroad, commanding them to return and appear as witnesses in such cases. The penalty for failure to appear was made $100,000. Although it was not stated that the specific purpose of the act was to compel Harry M. Blackmer to appear at these trials, it is a well known fact that this law was enacted for that purpose, for, when the criminal 

4 No. 66-United States vs. Harry M. Wurzbach. Decided February 

24, 1930. [386]

prosecutions in the Teapot Dome cases were begun, Blackmer had moved to Paris. 

Blackmer was living in Paris since 1924. He was wanted as a witness in the Supreme Court of the District of Columbia in the Fall-Sinclair conspiracy case.

In ac- cordance with the provisions in the above-mentioned act, he was served with a subpoena by the American consul in Paris, commanding him to return for the Fall-Sinclair trial. 

In October, 1927, when the Fall-Sinclair conspiracy trial began, Blackmer did not appear. Soon afterwards, a mistrial in the case was declared, on the ground that the jurors had been shadowed by the Burns Detective Agency, hired by Sinclair. On November 16th, the Supreme Court of the 

District of Columbia issued an order directing Blackmer to appear and to show cause why he should not be adjudged guilty of contempt for refusing to appear in said case, and at the same time directed the United States Marshal to seize $100,000 of Blackmer's liberty bonds in the United States, and to hold them for the satisfaction of any judgment which might be rendered against him. 

Blackmer's attorneys appeared for him and filed a motion to vacate that order, on the ground that the Act of Congress, under which it was issued, was a violation of the 

Fourth, Fifth, Sixth and Eighth Amendments to the Con stitution, and that all the proceedings held under that Act were therefore null and void. After this motion was argued, Blackmer was adjudged guilty of contempt of court. He was sentenced to pay a fine of $30,000, and the sequestered property was ordered to be held in the custody of the United States.

In the second conspiracy trial of Fall and Sinclair, Blackmer was again served by the United States Consul in Paris and again he refused to return to the United States 

5 See Hankin: U. S. Supreme Court, 1928-1929, pp. 208, 209, 213, 222. [387]

to testify. The Supreme Court of the District of Columbia again sentenced him for contempt, ordered the liberty bonds in the previous contempt case to be relevied upon, and imposed another fine of $30,000. 

Blackmer appealed both contempt cases to the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia. After the cases were docketed in the Court of Appeals and before arguments were heard, the United States filed a petition for a writ of certiorari in the Supreme Court of the United States to review these cases.

In their brief, Blackmer's attorneys concerned themselves solely with the unconstitutional features of the Act of July 3, 1926. They argued: (1) That the Act violated the Fourth Amendment in regard to unreasonable searches and seizures, because it permitted the sequestration of property for coercive purposes; (2) that the Act violated the 

Fifth Amendment because it permitted a court of the United States to exercise judicial power within the exclusive jurisdiction of a foreign country, and that this was not due process of law, because such judicial power was authorized by an Act of Congress and not by a treaty; (3) that the Act violated the Sixth Amendment providing that the accused in all criminal prosecutions shall be notified of the nature of the accusation against him.
