Since it is unusual, though permissible, to apply for a writ of certiorari in cases pending in the Circuit Court of Appeals or the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia before these are heard by those courts, the Government assigned as a reason for this step, that "It is a matter of grave importance to the administration of justice and the trial of criminal cases that the right of the United States to compel a citizen to return to the United States and give testimony on 

No. 730-United States vs. Blackmer, and No. 731-United States vs. Blackmer. Certiorari denied April 28, 1930. [ 388]

behalf of the Government in a criminal case should be promptly recognized and upheld by this Court." The Supreme Court, however, declined to go into the matter, which means that the cases must take their usual course. 

The same procedure was followed by Government counsel in the case against former Secretary Fall, in which he was convicted and sentenced to pay a fine of $100,000 on the charge of having accepted a bribe in connection with the negotiation and execution of contracts in the famous oil leases between the United States and the Pan American Petroleum and Transport Company, and the construction of storage facilities at Pearl Harbor and the leasing of Naval 

Petroleum Reserve No. 1. 

The former Secretary claimed that the trial court erred in failing to quash the indictment, on the ground that he, Fall, had lacked authority to act with reference to those contracts and leases; that the Naval Petroleum Reserves were within the province of the Secretary of the Navy and therefore it could not be said that he had accepted a bribe in connection with the execution of those leases. He also argued that the Government was estopped from prosecuting him for bribery, because he had already been tried on a charge of conspiracy to defraud the Government, involving the same facts, in which prosecution he had been acquitted. 

Other errors claimed related to questions of evidence and instructions to the jury on the law concerning bribery. 

The jury found that Fall was guilty as charged, and judgment was entered, sentencing him to a payment of a fine of $100,000.00, which was the amount alleged to have been received by him as a bribe. Fall appealed the case to the Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia, but while the case was pending there, the Government petitioned the [389]

Supreme Court to review the decision of the trial court before it had been passed on by the Court of Appeals. The 

Government assigned as a reason for this procedure, that both sides had declared their intention to carry the matter to the Supreme Court; that Secretary Fall was in very poor health without hope of recovery, and therefore he was entitled to a chance for exoneration, if he was not guilty; on the other hand, he should be punished, if the decision of the trial court was correct. This petition was likewise denied."

Relation Between the Federal Government and the States. 

A group of cases came to the Supreme Court this term dealing with the relation between the states and the Federal Government and most of these concerned problems of taxation. In one case, the Circuit Court of Appeals held that the bank of North Dakota, all of whose stock was owned by the state, was obliged to pay a capital stock tax to the Federal Government on the theory that the state engaged in a private enterprise and hence divested itself to that extent of its sovereignty. Toward the end of the term came a case from Alabama in which that state imposed a tax and penalties for failure to pay the tax, on the United States Government. The latter tax was imposed as an excise tax on the production of hydro-electric power, and here the state likewise argued that since the Federal Government engaged in a private enterprise, it divested itself of its sovereignty to that extent."

This problem, unless finally settled by the Supreme Court in the Alabama Case, is likely to become more troublesome in the future, if the Federal and State govern- 

7 No. 785-United States vs. Fall. Certiorari denied May 19, 1930. 

8 North Dakota vs. Olson, Coll'r. See pp. 40, 218, 229 supra. 

9 Alabama vs. United States. 

See p. 229 supra. [ 390]

ments, like the governments of other countries, become more and more interested in such enterprises. Government ownership of public utilities may carry with them not only obligations to the Federal Government for taxation purposes, but also obligations under other laws. A case of this character appeared in the Court this term when the Board of Harbor Commissioners of California was prosecuted under the Federal Safety Appliance Act for violations of that law by a state-owned railroad, managed by them. This case, as has already been mentioned, 10 is still pending before the Supreme Court.

Other problems of taxation, involving the relation between the State and Federal Governments, concerned the taxation of the instrumentalities of the Federal Government; for example, taxation by the states of United States Government securities directly or indirectly, and similarly the taxation by the Federal Government of state-owned securities. 

It has been held this year that the state of Missouri could not impose a tax on insurance companies, based on the net worth of their property, less certain deductions allowed by the law, but which included the value of United States bonds held by the companies.¹¹

The Supreme Court also granted writs of review in two cases in which it was claimed that the Federal Government attempted to impose burdens on state securities. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit held that the Federal Government may not impose an income tax on income received from the sale of municipal bonds.12 In the second case, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth

Circuit held that the Federal Government may not discriminate against the deduction of interest paid to buy and carry 

10 Sherman vs. United States. See p. 86 supra. 

11 Missouri Insurance Co. vs. Gehner. See pp. 11, 135, 226 supra. 

12 Willcutts vs. Bunn. See p. 178 supra. [391]

municipal bonds, while other types of interest on indebtedness are deductible.¹ 13 Another illustration of taxation imposed upon instrumentalities or agencies of Governments, were two cases in which it was argued that the Federal Government, by taxing the fees received by an attorney, representing a municipality or the state, was taxing the income of state officers. As we have seen, the Supreme Court held that these special fees received by counsel, were subject to tax. 14 On the other hand, when the state of Oklahoma sought to impose its gross production tax upon royalties received by an Indian from oil and gas leases, the Supreme Court held that this income of the Indian was protected by a provision in the treaty entered into between the United States and the Indians, that their lands would remain free from taxation. 

The Court went further, and held that the protection of the land from taxation extended also to the protection of any interest in the land, and that the state of Oklahoma could not maintain that it taxed merely personal property, because the royalties were imposed upon the oil, after it had been extracted from the land.15 

A serious problem of the conflict between the state and the federal governments related to the question how far a state or its political subdivisions may call upon the federal judiciary to enforce tax obligations incurred in the state. It is a settled rule of law that the state may bring suit within its own jurisdiction, or suit may be brought within the judicial district in which the state or county is located. But the 

Supreme Court held this year, that a county may not bring suit in the federal courts outside of the territorial jurisdiction of the state in order to recover taxes due.16 

13 Nauts, Coll'r. vs. Slayton. See p. 197 supra. 

14 Comm'r. Int. Rev. vs. Howard; and Comm'r Int. Rev. vs. Reed. 

See p. 177 supra. 

15 Carpenter vs. Shaw. See p. 245 supra. 

16 No. 79-Harry C. Moore, Treasurer vs. Mitchell et al. Decided 

February 24, 1930. [392]

In that case, an action was brought by the treasurer of Grant County, Ind., to recover some $982,000 of personal property taxes. These taxes were assessed against a resident of that county, and after his death this suit was brought against the executor, who was in New York. The federal district court in New York where the action was brought, held that it had no jurisdiction to enforce the revenue laws of the state of Indiana. This was affirmed by the Circuit 

Court of Appeals and later by the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Mr. Justice Butler. He said: "Petitioner (Treasurer of Grant County, Ind.) claims only by virtue of his office. Indiana is powerless to give any force or effect beyond her own limits to the Act of 1927 purporting to authorize this suit or to the other statutes empowering and prescribing the duties of its officers in respect of the levy and collection of taxes. 

And, as Indiana laws are the sole source of petitioner's authority, it follows that he had none in New York.* *

He is the mere arm of the State for the collection of taxes for some of its subdivisions and has no better standing to bring suits in courts outside Indiana than have executors, administrators, or chancery receivers without title, appointed under the laws and by the courts of that State. It is well understood that they are without authority, in their official capacity, to sue as of right in the federal courts in other States. From the earliest time, federal courts in one State have declined to take jurisdiction of suits by executors and administrators appointed in another State. ***”

Controversies Between States. 

From the problems concerning the relation between the Federal Government and the States, we come to the controversies between the states themselves. Here again we [393]

find that the most significant was the Chicago Diversion Case, in which the States of Wisconsin, Minnesota, Ohio, Pennsylvania, Michigan and New York brought suit to enjoin the state of Illinois and the Sanitary District of Chicago from diverting the waters of Lake Michigan into the Mississippi Basin to the great damage of the sovereign and proprietory rights of the complaining states. On January 

14, 1929, the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Chief Justice Taft, held that the diversion of waters was unlawful.' 

The Court remanded the case to Mr. Charles Evans Hughes, as Special Master, to arrive at a method whereby the unlawful diversion might be reduced and gradually eliminated. It was necessary to arrive at a plan which would work a restoration of the just rights of the complaining states and at the same time avoid disaster to the defendants. 18 After taking testimony in the case, the Special Master recommended that the diversion of the waters shall be reduced from 8,500 cubic feet per second to 6,500 cubic feet per second by July 1, 1930; then to 5,000 cubic feet per second by September 31, 1935, and that after December 31, 1938, the state of Illinois and the Sanitary District of Chicago shall be prohibited from diverting any water in excess of the annual average of 1,500 cubic feet per second in addition to domestic pumpage.

Numerous exceptions were taken to this report of the Special Master by the complaining states. They objected not so much to the length of time within which the diversion might be eliminated, but mainly to the recommendation that the Sanitary District should be allowed to divert 1,500 cubic feet per second after 1938. They argued that according to the prior decision of the Supreme Court, the diversion was unlawful altogether. 

17 See Hankin: U. S. Supreme Court, 1928-1929, p. 229. 

18 Wisconsin et al. vs. Illinois and Sanitary District of Chicago, 278 

U. S. 367. [394]

It was interesting to hear the arguments of Mr. Newton 

D. Baker, elaborating on the political aspects of this controversy. For, although on the face the case presents a simple matter of diversion of waters, the controversy has brought into question the sovereign and proprietory rights of states bordering on the Great Lakes, and even, to some extent, the treaty between the United States and Canada with reference to diversion of waters. The case also involved the powers of the Secretary of War under the Rivers and Harbors Act, and how far he could permit the diversion of waters in aid of navigation and also for purposes not primarily related to navigation.

For in this case, although the complaining states maintained that the diversion was primarily for sanitation purposes and for production of power which the Sanitary District sells to municipalities and to others, the defendants maintained that the permit of the Secretary of War in this case was also in aid of navigation, for two reasons. First, that the diversion of the water was necessary in order to keep the Great Lakes navigable and therefore free from pollution. The second argument, in which the states along the Mississippi joined, emphasized that the diversion was a step toward making the Mississippi navigable and establishing a direct waterway from the Great Lakes to the Gulf Ports.

The Supreme Court, after having listened to these arguments, sustained the recommendation of the Special Mas- 19 The case was retained by the Court, however, for any supplemental proceedings which may develop in the future for the enforcement or modification of the decree. ter.

But the Supreme Court is not the only forum where this controversy has been debated. For over a century, Con- 

19 Nos. 7, 11 and 12 Orig.-Wisconsin et al. vs. Illinois and Sanitary 

District of Chicago. Decided April 14, 1930. [395]

gress has considered the development of a Lakes-to-the- Gulf-Waterway. As early as 1817, Congress adopted a resolution requiring the Committee on Roads and Canals to investigate the expediency of a canal between Lake Michigan and the Illinois River. In 1822 Congress passed an act authorizing the State of Illinois to construct such canal, which was begun in 1848.

Since then both the federal Government and the state of Illinois have advanced the general project of a Lakes-tothe-Gulf Waterway, and the last act of Congress in this respect was enacted shortly after the above decision in the 

Lake Diversion Case. The Rivers and Harbors Act, ap proved July 3, 1930,20 adopted the recommendation of the

Chief of Engineers 21 for the development of a commercially useful waterway, and to that end, Congress appropriated a sum not to exceed $7,500,000 in addition to the $20,000,000 appropriated by the state of Illinois. 

It is apparent, however, that Congress intends to go slowly with this enterprise. For in the Act it prescribed that the project shall be constructed so as to require the smallest flow of water with which it can be practically accomplished. The Act also adopted the figure of 1,500 cubic feet per second which was the amount of flow authorized by the Supreme Court, to be used for the navigation of the waterway. It provided, however, that on or before Jan. 1, 1938, the Secretary of War shall report with his recommendations as to the minimum amount of flow that will be required annually to meet the needs of such waterway and which will not substantially injure the existing navigation on the Great Lakes to the end that Congress may take such action as it may deem advisable. 

20 Public Law No. 520-71st Congress. Approved July 3, 1930. 

21 Senate Doc. 126, 71st Congress, 2nd Session. [396]

Of course, one never can tell what action Congress is likely to take in the future. One thing is certain, however, that the decision of the Supreme Court in this case may not be determinable of the rights of the parties in the future. 

The decision of the Supreme Court in favor of the complaining states was based on the proposition that the diversion was essentially for purposes of sanitation, and not for purposes of navigation. Should Congress determine to extend the construction of this waterway, the complaining states will not be in a position to complain of injury to their sovereign and proprietory rights. 

Another proposition in the Diversion Case, which injected additional difficulty, was the fact that the Sanitary District of Chicago utilized the flow of water in the production of hydro-electric power. Since, according to the decision of the Supreme Court, the amount of water abstracted will be materially diminished, this power plant would be practically destroyed. If Congress determines, however, to take complete control over the improvement of navigation and its regulation over the waterway, it may also take over and save the power plant. 

The states of Indiana and Kentucky were involved in a friendly bout over the construction of an interstate bridge between the two states. It appears that both states were anxious to fulfill their obligations under the construction contract, but that a number of citizens in the State of Indiana brought suit to enjoin their officers from carrying out the contract, on the ground that the obligations entered into were in violation of the constitution and laws of the state.

The State of Kentucky wished to avoid a decision by the state courts, which might be adverse to the rights of a sister state, which rights were fixed by contract and approved by Congress. Kentucky therefore brought this suit in the [397]

United States Supreme Court, under its powers of original jurisdiction, against the State of Indiana for specific performance, and also joined as defendants the private citizens of Indiana, who had brought suit to enjoin the performance of the contract. The State of Indiana confessed judgment and stated that it was willing and ready to perform, and the controversy reduced itself to a suit between the state of Kentucky and the private citizens of Indiana. The Supreme 

Court ordered specific performance on the part of Indiana, but held that it was not necessary to enjoin the individual defendants from pressing their suits in the state courts, since any order entered by the state courts in conflict with this decision of the Supreme Court, would be null and void." 

Another contract between two states resulting in litigation, was one entered into by the states of Kansas and Missouri, whereby each agreed that the property of one state shall be free from taxation if located in the sister state. 

Kansas City, Mo., had its waterworks plant located in Kansas, and the city waterworks plant of Kansas City, Kansas, was located in Missouri. Then, Wyandotte County in

Kansas took the position that the agreement did not cover assessments for benefits received by the waterworks belonging to Kansas City, Mo., and imposed an assessment.

In an action brought to recover the amount of the assessment, the U. S. District Court in Kansas rendered judgment in favor of the County, but its decision was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit, which held that the agreement covered assessments of benefits as well as general taxes. Wyandotte County petitioned the Supreme 

Court to review that decision, but its petition was denied.23 

22 No. 16, Orig.-Kentucky vs. Indiana. Decided April 14, 1930. 

23 No. 553-Fairfax Drainage District of Wyandotte County, Kan. vs. 

Kansas City, Mo. Certiorari denied February 24, 1930. [398]

Problems Within the States. 

We now come to the political problems within the states themselves. With the changes taking place the world over in the political and economic ideals of peoples, our states have adopted measures for the suppression of freedom of speech and press, to prevent the importation of those ideas into this country. Without taking sides on the merits of those ideas, we think that some of the statutes adopted by the states are inconsistent with our ideals of freedom of speech and thought, characteristic of a liberty-loving people. 

For illustration we shall take a provision in the Constitution of Pennsylvania adopted at the time of the formation of the Union, and compare it with the recent Pennsyl vania Sedition Act.24 Section 7 of Article I of the Pennsyl vania Constitution provides that "the printing press shall be free to every person who may undertake to examine the proceedings of the legislature or any branch of government and no law shall ever be made to restrain the right thereof. The free communication of thought and opinion is one of the invaluable rights of man, and every citizen may freely speak, write and print on any subject, being responsible for the abuse of that liberty. No conviction shall be had in any prosecution for the publication of papers relating to the official conduct of officers or men in public capacity, or to any other matter proper for public investigation or information, where the fact that such publication was not maliciously or negligently made shall be established to the satisfaction of the jury; and in all indictments for libels the jury shall have the 24 Act of June 26, 1919, P. L. 639, as amended by Act of May 10, 1921, P. L. 435. [399]

right to determine the law and the facts, under the direction of the Court, as in other cases." 

Compare this provision of the Pennsylvania Constitution with the provision in the Pennsylvania Sedition Act, enacted when apparently the state decided to put an embargo on thought coming from foreign sources. The Act defines "sedition" as being "Any writing, publication, printing, cut, cartoon, utterance, or conduct, either individually or in connection or combination with any other person or persons, the intent of which is: (a) To make or cause to be made any outbreak or demonstration of violence against this state or against the United States. (b) To encourage any person or persons to take any measure or engage in any conduct with a view of overthrowing or destroying or attempting to overthrow or destroy, by any force or show of threat of force, the Government of this state or of the United States. (c) To incite or encourage any person or persons to commit any overt act with a view of bringing the Government of this state or of the United States into hatred or contempt. (d) To incite any person or persons to do or attempt to do personal injury or harm to any officer of this state or of the United States, or to damage or destroy any public property or the property of any public official because of his official position. 

It shall also include: (e) The actual damage to, or destruction of, any public property or the property of any public official, perpetrated because the owner or occupant is in official position. [400]

(f) Any writing, publication, printing, cut, cartoon or utterance which advocates or teaches the duty, necessity or propriety of engaging in crime, violence, or any form of terrorism, as a means of accomplishing political reform or change in government. (g) The sale, gift or distribution of any prints, publications, books, papers, documents, or written matter in any form, which advocates, furthers or teaches sedition as herein before defined. (h) Organizing or helping to organize or becoming a member of any assembly, society, or group, where any of the policies or purposes thereof are seditious as herein before defined. 

Section 2. Sedition, as defined in Section One of this Act, shall be a felony, and any person convicted thereof shall be sentenced to a fine of not less than one hundred dollars ($100) and not more than ten thousand dollars ($10,000), and to imprisonment not exceeding twenty years, either or both, in the discretion of the court."

Under this law, three defendants were prosecuted, convicted and sentenced. The indictment alleged and the proof showed that these defendants were members and officers of the Workers Party of America (a communist organization); that this party is under the jurisdiction of the Third 

International, having as its aim a world-wide revolution to overthrow all existing governments, except that of Soviet Russia, and that these defendants frequently taught, by word of mouth, the doctrines contained in the "A. B. C. of Communism" and "The Theory and Price of Leninism." 

The facts on which they were convicted were that on the evening of Armistice Day, November 11, 1926, police officers raided the house of one of defendants where they found a meeting in progress held to celebrate the Ninth Anniver- [401]

sary of the Russian Revolution; that when the officers entered, one of the defendants was standing in front of a table holding a cup and making a speech in a foreign language, and that when the officers entered, he attempted to hide, under the table cloth, a number of buttons with pins attached, which were intended for use in the celebration of this anniversary of the Soviet Republic of Russia; that on these buttons were the insignia of the communist party and the legend "U. S. S. R. 9th Anniversary, Forward to the

Soviet Republic of the U. S. A." The houses of two other defendants also were raided, and quantities of books and pamphlets, alleged to be seditious, were discovered. Upon these facts, these men were convicted and sentenced to imprisonment for five years and fined $500 each. 

Of course, the defendants appealed to the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania, maintaining that the statute was unconstitutional and that by various and diverse instances of misconduct on the part of the prosecuting attorney and of the presiding judge, they were deprived of due process of law.

The Supreme Court of Pennsylvania affirmed the judgment of the trial court holding: "We are satisfied that these three appellants had a fair and impartial trial, in accordance with established principles of procedure, and that their conviction was not the result of prejudice against them by reason of their foreign birth or on account of their political opinions and beliefs. So far as we can discover they were convicted because the evidence indicated that, being hostile to our form of government, they became officers, teachers and leaders in an organization having for its policies and purposes the overthrow of the government of this state and of the United States, not by any of the modes prescribed by our constitution and legislation for [ 402]

the altering, reforming or abolishing thereof, but by means of force, arms and various forms of violence, and that they have repeatedly urged and incited the members of their group to substitute for it, by such means, a form of government in which they believe." 

The defendants then appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States, where the cases were dismissed on the ground that there was no substantial federal question presented.25

Apart from the merits of this case, we think there is danger lurking in the enactment of such drastic laws. The main danger lies in the fact that they are a double-edged sword, and one cannot predict where it might strike. For example, the Pennsylvania delegation to the National Democratic Convention at Houston, Tex., in 1928, might well have been prosecuted under paragraph (c), Section 1 of the Sedition Act, when they elaborated on the shameless corruption in their state, resulting as it did, in the State having only one Senator in the United States Senate, etc., etc. On the other hand, had the Democrats ruled the State, and had the Republicans made the defamatory speeches, they might have been punished under the same provision of the Act. 

And we suppose, that had such offending persons belonged to a party whose local branch consisted of "about twenty members",26 they would undoubtedly have suffered the consequences. But the law often acts without respect of persons, and therefore we must generalize. Suffice it to say, for example, that we cannot look upon "red raids" with equanimity and then expect to protest successfully against the invasion of personal liberty by means of wire-tapping." 27 

25 No. 294 Muselin vs. Pennsylvania; No. 295-Zima vs. Pennsylvania, and No. 296-Resetar vs. Pennsylvania. Appeals dismissed October 

28, 1929. 

26 No. 295 Pa. Supreme Court Reports, 311. 

27 Olmstead vs. U. S., 277 U. S. 438. 

[ 403]

CHAPTER XV. 

International, Race and Related Problems 

We now come to the cases in which the controversies take their origin from the fact that the parties in the litigation belong to such classes, as national, racial or religious groups. 

International Problems.

A divorce proceeding is always of interest to those not concerned in it, and it becomes increasingly interesting when a foreign vice-consul and his American wife are the participants in the conflict. Rarely, however, does such proceeding involve the interpretation of a provision in the Constitution of the United States. But this is what happened when M. Popovici, Roumanian vice-consul at Cleveland, Ohio, was sued for divorce and alimony in the state courts of Ohio. The vice-consul objected to the jurisdiction of the state court, because the Constitution of the United States provides that the judicial power of the United States shall extend to all cases affecting ambassadors, other public ministers and consuls, and the Judicial Code ¹ likewise provides that the jurisdiction of the United States courts shall be exclusive in cases against consuls and vice-consuls. 1 The Court of Common Pleas, where the suit was brought, overruled the vice-consul's objection to the jurisdiction and proceeded to enter a decree for temporary alimony against him. Popovici filed a petition in the Supreme 

Court of Ohio for a writ of prohibition, but that court held that the provisions in the Judicial Code and the Constitu- 

1 Act of March 3, 1911, c. 231, Sec. 256. 

[ 404 ]

tion of the United States must be interpreted as not extending to suits for divorce and alimony, because the federal courts have no jurisdiction in such cases. This was the view finally adopted by the Supreme Court of the United States. in an opinion delivered by Mr. Justice Holmes.2

The Court held that the provision in the Constitution does not of itself exclude the jurisdiction of the state and that it was not intended to cover matters which are without the jurisdiction of the United States courts. The words "suits against consuls and vice consuls," the Court held, "must be taken to refer to ordinary civil proceedings and not to include what formerly would have belonged to the ecclesiastical courts."

Much was said in the case concerning the policy of per. mitting the states to take jurisdiction over matters involving agencies of foreign governments. To this the Court said: "It is true that there may be objections of policy to one of our states intermeddling with the domestic relations of an official and subject of a foreign power that conceivably might regard jurisdiction as determined by nationality and not by domicil. But on the other hand, if, as seems likely, the wife was an American citizen, probably she remained one notwithstanding her marriage.*** Her position certainly is not less to be considered than her husband's, and at all events these considerations are not for us."

In two other cases, the Supreme Court had occasion to consider the privileges and immunities or rights of agencies of foreign countries in proceedings in federal courts. The first of these involved the Royal Administration of the 

Swedish State Railways, the second involved the Russian 

Volunteer Fleet. 

2 No. 35-Ohio ex rel. Popovici vs. Agler et al. Decided January 20, 1930. [405]

The case concerning the Swedish Railways involved the question whether an agency of a foreign power may claim immunity against suit in the United States courts in a counterclaim set up against an action brought by the agency. The 

Royal Administration of the Swedish State Railways brought action against Dexter and Carpenter, Inc., to recover an overpayment on a cargo of coal. Dexter & Carpenter then filed a counterclaim for breach of contract. The defense to the counterclaim was that the Royal Administration of the Swedish State Railways was not a party to that contract, that the counterclaim was really against Beijer & Co. and that there was no connection between the Royal Administration and Beijer & Co. Furthermore, the Administration argued that since it was an agency of the Government of Sweden, it was therefore immune from suit in the 

United States Courts.

At the trial, the District Court admitted evidence showing the connection between the Royal Administration of the Swedish State Railways and the Beijer Company, overruled the defense of immunity, and even ordered the Consul General of Sweden to produce documents in his possession. The 

Consul General in obedience to the subpoena brought the documents under the seal of the Swedish Consulate, and the trial court permitted the attorney for Dexter & Carpenter to break the seal of the Consulate, to take the documents and to introduce them in evidence. The counterclaim resulted in favor of Dexter & Carpenter, and this was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit. 

Throughout these proceedings, the Charge d'Affaires of the Swedish Legation made representations to the Department of State, which, however, advised the Swedish legation to bring the matter to the Department's attention after the case had reached the Supreme Court of the United States, at which time the Department would consider the [ 406 ]

advisability of asking the Department of Justice to inform the Court concerning the status of the Royal Administration of Swedish State Railways. The Swedish Legation brought the case to the Supreme Court mainly on the question of the immunity of a foreign power. The Solicitor General also filed a statement representing to the Court that the Royal Administration of Swedish Railways was an integral part of the Government of Sweden. But the Supreme Court declined to go into the matter.³ 

In the second case, the Russian Volunteer Fleet brought suit against the United States in the Court of Claims to recover just compensation for shipbuilding contracts requisitioned by the Government during the war. The Court of 

Claims dismissed the suit on the ground that the Volunteer Fleet Corporation had no standing in court as a corporation. organized under the laws of Russia, because the government of that country was not recognized by the United States. 

The Russian Vounteer Fleet Corporation offered evidence to show that there is a government in Russia and that therefore, the Fleet Corporation was entitled to maintain the suit as a corporation of a de facto government, at least. But the 

Court of Claims refused to consider such evidence and dismissed the suit for lack of jurisdiction. The Supreme Court granted a writ of review, and the case will be heard in the coming term.*

Although it is more than a decade since the war with Germany was terminated, cases involving confiscated property of German nationals and of others who were mistaken for enemy aliens, keep coming to this Court. In one case, an action was brought to recover liberty bonds and cash seized by the Alien Property Custodian. It was admitted Inc. 

3 No. 273-Kunglig Jarnvagsstyrelsen, etc. vs. Dexter & Carpenter, 

Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

4 No. 623-Russian Volunteer Fleet vs. United States. 

granted April 21, 1930. Certiorari [ 407 ]

that the seizure was unlawful, because the owners were not Germans but citizens of Switzerland. The Alien Property Custodian returned the value of the property less a sum of $55,000 which he withheld as administrative expenses, without showing, however, that any part of that sum had been spent in connection with the property in question. He maintained that he was entitled to deduct the administrative expenses, on the authority of a provision in the Trading with the Enemy Act. This contention was sustained by the 

Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia, but was reversed by the Supreme Court in an opinion rendered by Mr. 

Justice Holmes.5 He said: "It would be extraordinary if the charges, incident to a seizure that the law did not intend the Custodian to make and a possession that the law requires him to surrender, were to be imposed upon the owner whose interests were sacrificed up to the moment of restitution. 

It seems to be going far enough to require him to bear the loss that he has suffered without compelling him to pay the Government for its outlay in doing him harm." 

The Alien Property Custodian had also seized certain railroad stock belonging to citizens of Switzerland. He sold the shares to the Director General of Railroads, and in a suit brought in the courts of the District of Columbia, the administrator of the non-enemy aliens recovered the proceeds received by the Alien Property Custodian. Afterwards, the Director General of Railroads sold the shares to private persons at a profit, and this profit was covered into the Treasury of the United States. The administrator then brought this action in the Court of Claims to recover the profits made by the Director General. The Court of Claims held that the decision of the District of Columbia courts had completely disposed of the matter, and that the admin- 

5 No. 365-Escher vs. Woods, Treas., et al. Decided April 28, 1930. [ 408]

istrator could not bring several actions against the United States to recover piecemeal for the profits realized on the sales of those stocks. The administrator petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.Ⓡ

While we are on the subject of the war and the suits growing out of it, it is apt to mention several cases which involved interpretations of the Treaties of Versailles and Berlin. A corporation, organized under the law of the 

British Crown Colony of the Straits Settlements, had all of its stock owned by German nationals. After the outbreak of the war between Great Britain and Germany, the Liquidators of the Straits Settlements liquidated the affairs of the corporation, but the corporation itself was not dissolved, nor were the shares of stock of the German nationals seized by the Government. When the United States entered the war, the Alien Property Custodian seized the property of the corporation in the Philippine Islands. Later, in an action brought against the Alien Property Custodian, it was held that the seizure was unlawful, because though the stockholders were enemy aliens, the corporation itself was not. 

After the Treaty of Versailles, the Government of the Straits Settlements seized the stock of the corporation vesting the same in the Custodian of Enemy Property. But the 

Liquidators, at the request of the German shareholders, delivered the certificates to them. Suit was now brought against the Alien Property Custodian of the United States both by the German shareholders and by the Custodian of Enemy Property of the Straits Settlements, each claiming to be the lawful stockholders and each claiming in the name of the corporation. The Court of Appeals of the District of Columbia held that the Germans were the real stockholders and that the property belonged to their corporation. The 

6 No. 697-Escher vs. United States. Certiorari denied May 5, 1930. 

[409]

corporation represented by the Custodian of Enemy Property petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision maintaining that the court below erred in failing to give recognition to the executive and judicial acts of a foreign government acting within its own jurisdiction. It further maintained that the German nationals were not entitled to recover in this suit, because the Settlement of War Claims Act of 1928 gave them a different and an exclusive remedy; also that the court below misinterpreted and misapplied the Treaty of Versailles. But the Supreme Court declined to go into the matter."

The Treaty of Berlin was invoked when a German inventor applied for a patent for certain improvements in electric arc welding. The German had obtained a German patent on October 20, 1919, on an application filed on October 11, 1915, and now he wanted an American patent for the same invention. This application was denied on the ground that it was not filed within the time limit, having been filed more than twelve months after the filing of the foreign application for the same subject matter which had resulted in the issuance of a patent. The German inventor and the 

General Electric Company, to whom the patent was assigned, then filed this action for the issuance of the patent. 

They argued that the ruling of the Commissioner was erroneous, because the provision of the Treaty of Berlin between the United States and Germany (ratified Nov. 14, 1921) had the effect of extending for six months, after the effective date of the Treaty, the time limiting the registration of foreign patents.

The District Court in Maryland, where the action was brought, held that the patent should be issued. The decision, however, was reversed by the Circuit Court of Appeals 

7 No. 360-Behn, Meyer Company, Ltd. vs. Sutherland. denied October 21, 1929. Certiorari [410]

on the ground that the Treaty of Berlin did not operate to extend the time limits for registration of foreign patents, and also on the ground that, if it did so, the Treaty was not self executing, and Congress had enacted no legislation to enforce the treaty provision. On these questions, the General Electric Company petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, but its petition was denied." 

Our Treaty with Norway was invoked in a suit by a widow of a citizen of Norway to recover an undivided onehalf interest in a homestead which her husband had sold, without her joining in the conveyance. Her husband came to this country in 1868 and to Nebraska in 1878. He established a homestead and resided there until his death in August, 1923. Shortly before he died, he deeded the property away. Throughout all this time, the wife remained in Norway, and after his death, she brought this suit and claimed the right to take the real estate of her husband by virtue of the Treaty of Amity and Commerce between the United 

States and Norway. The Supreme Court of Nebraska held against her on the basis of the very same treaty, on which she relied. It held that, although under the law of Nebraska a husband may not convey his homestead without the wife joining in the conveyance, and although the constitution of Nebraska provided that in all respects citizens and noncitizens shall be treated alike, the provision in the Treaty between the United States and Norway that "the subjects of the contracting parties in the respective states may freely dispose of their goods and effects" superseded the provisions of the Nebraska law. Because of this provision, the Court below held, the State of Nebraska could not put any limitations on the Norwegian's right to dispose of his land. 

8 No. 220-General Electric Company vs. Robertson. Certiorari denied 

April 21, 1929. [411]

This decision was reversed by the Supreme Court, in an opinion by Chief Justice Hughes, holding that the

Treaty did not operate to override the law of the state as to the disposition of homestead property. For to hold otherwise would mean that the Treaty puts the alien owner of a homestead in Nebraska on a better footing than a citizen of the state, and it was not intended by the Treaty to give the citizens of the respective states greater privileges than to citizens in their home states. The purpose of the Treaty was to avoid injurious discrimination, but not to give additional privileges, and when the citizen of Norway took advantage of the local law as to homesteads, he could not claim the benefits of the law without accepting the burdens attached to it.

Our Treaty with Hungary (ratified in 1926) was involved in an interesting immigration case. A Hungarian citizen came to this country as a temporary visitor after the enactment of the Immigration Act of 1924, but prior to the ratification of the Treaty between the United States and Hungary. This Hungarian became interested in various business enterprises and after the expiration of his temporary stay, he claimed his right under the Treaty to remain here as a merchant. The question arose whether the Treaty provision superseded the Immigration Act of 1924. This question was not passed on by the court below. The Circuit 

Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit held that he was subject to deportation, because his passport was not visaed by the American Consul at Montreal, in addition to the visa by the American Consul at Budapest, since the alien had made a temporary departure from the United States to Canada for a few days. The Hungarian petitioned the 

9 No. 412-Marie Tollefsen Todok vs. Union State Bank. Decided 

May 19, 1930. [412]

Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.10

Race Problems. 

Questions of immigration often involve also questions of race antagonism, especially where Chinese and Japanese are concerned. And it is difficult to tell whether the Chinese 

Exclusion Act and similar laws should be classified under the head of immigration problems or of race problems. 

Sometimes the applications of those laws against Chinese and Japanese work great hardship. 

In one case, a Japanese subject, after twenty-one years of continuous residence in San Francisco, left for a temporary visit abroad, in 1928. Upon his return he was excluded by the Department of Labor under the Immigration Act of 1924, because he could not show a previous lawful admission to "the United States," notwithstanding the fact he did. have a previous lawful admission to the Territory of Ha waii. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit sustained the immigration authorities, holding that the words "the United States" in the Immigration Act meant "continental United States" and did not include the Territory of Hawaii. The Japanese petitioned the Supreme 

Court to review that decision on the meaning of the words "the United States" in the Immigration Act. He also maintained that the decision of the court below should be overruled because it compelled him to do a useless act, that is, permitted him to go to Hawaii, from whence he could return but did not allow him to remain in San Francisco.¹ 

This is but an illustration of the hardship which the immi- 

10 No. 488-U. S. ex rel. Komlos vs. Trudell. Certiorari denied Janu ary 13, 1930. 

11 No. 725-Sugimoto vs. Nagle. Certiorari denied April 28, 1930. 11 [413]

gration laws work on a man, who had established a business in this country and then left for a temporary visit abroad. 

Another case concerned a Chinese boy who came to this country and claimed to be the son of an American citizen of Chinese descent. It appears that as soon as he arrived he was detained by the immigration authorities without permitting him to communicate with either friend, relative or counsel. They examined him concerning many particulars in China, and all these questions were answered intelligently, though he was only twelve years old. Apparently he did not satisfy the immigration authorities on a question concerning a brother of his who had on a previous occasion attempted to enter the United States, but was excluded. 

The immigration authorities concluded that the boy had not established his identity as the son of the American citizen and held him for deportation. In a habeas corpus proceeding instituted in his behalf, the court below sustained the immigration authorities, and the Supreme Court declined to review the case.¹ 12

The states on the western coast have also undertaken to limit the economic power of members of the yellow race. 

Both the California Alien Land Law and the similar statute of the state of Washington have been before the Supreme Court on previous occasions. This term both laws reappeared. This time they illustrated the dangers of drastic legislation enacted against minority groups, in that one never can tell where such legislation might strike. In the case from California, the legislation resulted in financial loss to Americans, while in the case from Washington the party adversely affected was an American corporation whose stock was owned by Canadians. 

On August 1, 1919, the California Delta Farms, Inc., 12 No. 912-Flynn ex rel. Chin King vs. Tillinghast. Certiorari denied June 2, 1930. [414]

entered into a contract with one, Wing, for the sale of a tract of land to him for the price of $893,000, payable in installments. The contract was afterwards assigned by Wing to the Chinese American Farms, Inc., which prior to December 9, 1920, had paid about $300,000 as part payment for the land. On that day California passed an amendment to the Alien Land Law, making it punishable to conspire to effect a transfer of property to aliens. The Chinese corporation then defaulted on its payments and the California Delta Farms, Inc., filed suit to cancel the contract. The 

Chinese corporation filed an answer and a cross complaint, maintaining that the contract became void under the provisions of the Alien Land Law and became impossible of performance, and that it was therefore entitled to recover the amount of the purchase price paid. The trial court held in favor of the California Delta Farms, but was reversed by the Supreme Court of California, which sustained the arguments of the Chinese American Farms. 

The California Delta Farms then appealed to the Supreme Court, maintaining that the provision of the Alien Land Law, as applied, was contrary to the contract clause and the due process clause of the Constitution. The Supreme Court of the United States dismissed the appeal, for the reason that the judgment below was not a final one.¹³ 13 The Washington case will be argued during the coming term.¹ Here a Washington corporation was engaged in the transmission of electric power from a point in Canada over the International Boundary Line into the state of Washington. The electric power is supplied to various cities and also to its own mining properties. Accordingly, it owns various franchises and easements concerning rights of way, 13 No. 392-California Delta Farms, Inc. vs. Chinese American Farms. 

Appeal dismissed October 28, 1929. 

14 No. 763-Northport Power & Light Company vs. Hartley. Juris diction shown June 2, 1930. [415]

on which are located the transmission lines and power stations. The majority of the stock of this corporation, however, is owned by a Canadian corporation, and therefore the Washington corporation is an “alien" within the meaning of the Alien Land Law of the state, forbidding aliens from owning any real estate. 

This suit was brought by the Northport Power Company to enjoin the Governor and Attorney General of the State of Washington from instituting escheat and forfeiture proceedings against the corporation's property. The suit was dismissed by the federal district court in Washington, on the ground that it did not appear that the corporation's rights would be jeopardized in the threatened proceedings.

From this decision, the corporation appealed to the Supreme Court of the United States, arguing that the Alien Land Law, if applied against it, would be contrary to the commerce clause of the Constitution, would destroy its franchises and hence violate the contract clause of the Constitution, and also that this would constitute a violation of the Treaty between the United States and Great Britain. 

We suppose that it would not be considered desirable for Canada to reciprocate by similar legislation, making it impossible for land to be owned by a citizen of any country where Canadians are prohibited to own land. And if an exception is made in favor of Canadians, then we suppose that the Chinese and Japanese may well rely on the most favored nations clauses in their treaties with the United States and claim that the state laws in question are contrary to the treaty provisions.

The greatest racial problem in this country, of course, is the negro problem. Usually a number of cases each term come before the Supreme Court involving rights of negroes under the Thirteenth, Fourteenth and Fifteenth Amendments to the Constitution. This year only one such case 1 [416]

came before the Court. The City of Richmond passed an ordinance forbidding colored persons from acquiring residences on any block on which the majority of the residents are white, and forbidding white persons to acquire residences on blocks where the majority are colored. 

This ordinance was passed in the form of a measure in aid of the Virginia law against miscegenation (an Act to Preserve Racial Integrity) and was worded so as to forbid any person to use as a residence any building on any street, between intersecting streets, where the majority of residences are occupied by those with whom such person is forbidden to intermarry. 

Suit was brought by a colored man to enjoin the enforcement of this ordinance on the ground that it was violative of the Fourteenth Amendment to the Constitution. He also maintained that residence on any street had no reasonable connection with the question of intermarriage. The federal district court in Virginia enjoined the enforcement of the ordinance, and the decree was affirmed by the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit, without an opinion. The 

City of Richmond then appealed to the Supreme Court, which likewise affirmed the decree, without an opinion and without hearing arguments in the case.15 

It is interesting to note that while in cases involving the Chinese, Japanese and negroes, these peoples seek rights usually enjoyed by others, the cases brought by the American Indians, or by the Government in their behalf, usually seek to establish special privileges or immunities derived either from treaties between Indian Tribes and the United States, or from the fact that the Indians are the wards of the Federal Government. One illustration of this is the decision holding that Indians are not subject to taxation in the 

15 No. 729-City of Richmond vs. Deans. Decided May 19, 1930. [417]

same manner as others.16 Another illustration is the safeguard against permitting Indians to involve themselves in debt, and thus lose their allotments. Suit was brought by the 

United States to cancel notes and mortgages given by an adult Osage Indian of 9/64ths degree of Indian blood, on the ground that the Indian had no certificate of competency, and the debt had not been approved by the Secretary of the Interior. The defendant argued that the restriction did not apply in this case, because the Act of Congress of March 3, 1921, removed all restrictions against alienation of allotment sections of adult Indians of less than one-half Indian blood. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Cir cuit held that this act was superseded by the Act of Congress of Feb. 27, 1925, which made such contracts for debt invalid, and the Supreme Court denied a review in the case.¹7 

Where an incompetent Indian makes a gift, the Government may proceed to have that gift set aside. A notorious example of how Indians are often imposed upon is the gift made by one Jackson Barnett. Several years later, the Government had that gift set aside. But this year, two cases growing out of that suit came to the Supreme Court under the following circumstances. 

Barnett, a full-blood Creek Indian, had received royalties from his oil and gas leases, which royalties had accrued and accumulated in the hands of the Secretary of Interior in excess of $1,100,000. He was of an inferior mental capacity, and his intelligence was so stunted and undeveloped that in ordinary business transactions he lacked the capacity to understand and know what he was doing. But on December 15, 1922, he put his thumb mark to an instrument which purported to make a gift of $550,000 in Liberty Bonds to the American Baptist Home Mission Society in 

16 See pp. 245, 392 supra. 

17 No. 262-Osage County Motor Company vs. United States. Cer tiorari denied October 21, 1929. [418]

trust for certain purposes. On February 1, 1923, Secretary

Fall approved this gift, and the American Baptist Home Mission Society delivered the bonds to the Equitable Trust Company of New York under a trust agreement approved by the Secretary.

In 1925, a suit was brought by Elmer S. Bailey, as the next friend of Jackson Barnett, to have the gift declared null and void, and to have the property transferred to him. 

The Government intervened in that suit and asked that the bonds and their proceeds be delivered to the Secretary of the Interior. That suit resulted in declaring the gift null and void. The Court, however, retained jurisdiction in the case for the purpose of entertaining and deciding applications by counsel for allowances out of the property to cover services and disbursements. These applications were postponed, because the American Baptist Home Mission Society appealed the case, but the Circuit Court of Appeals affirmed the decision of the District Court, and the Supreme Court declined to go into that matter.18 

Thereafter, the next friend, and also the defendants in the suit, appealed for allowances. The Court allowed $7,500 to Elmer S. Bailey, and $184,000 to his counsel. The latter amount was reduced by the Circuit Court of Appeals to $100,000, but the appellate court affirmed the decision of the lower court in all other respects. 

The Government petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, maintaining that the courts below had no right to make allowances out of the restricted property of the Creek Indian, and furthermore, that the allowance of $100,000 for attorney's fees, under the circumstances, was beyond fair and reasonable compensation for the services rendered. 

18 American Baptist Home Mission Society vs. Jackson Barnett, 278 U. S. 626. 

[419]

In the second case, suit was instituted by the United States to recover the sum of $15,000, formerly under the control of the Secretary of the Interior as the restricted funds of Barnett. This sum came into the hands of one 

Mr. Mott from the gift Secretary Fall permitted Barnett to make.

In this suit, the Government first charged fraud, but later amended its complaint so as to strike out the allegations of fraud. The question thus was clearly presented, whether with or without allegations of fraud, this gift could be set aside.

The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit held that the Secretary of the Interior had no right or authority to release this portion of the restricted funds, and Mott petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision. 

Writs of review were granted in both cases, which will be heard next term.' 19 The remainder of the cases are usually in the form of suits to quiet title to Indians' allotted lands for the purpose of determining inheritances, of setting aside conveyances because of fraud exercised upon Indians, or because the lands, being restricted, they could not be conveyed without the approval of the Secretary of the Interior.

We said that the cases concerning Indians differ from those of other races, in that they involve claims for special rights. This does not imply that the Indians seek to be, or that they are, at an advantage as compared with others. 

These special rights are indeed counterbalanced by the position in which Indians are kept. Nor can the Indians continue in the enjoyment of these rights without keeping their tribal relations. 

19 No. 530-United States vs. Equitable Trust Co.

January 20, 1930. No. 810-Mott vs. United States. 

May 19, 1930. 

Certiorari granted 

Certiorari granted [420]

A three-eighths Chippewa Indian woman abandoned her tribal relations, married a white man and thereafter resided continually among white people. Four children were born of the marriage, the first three of whom were enrolled on the supplemental rolls of the Chippewa Indians. 

Upon request to enroll the fourth child, an investigation was instituted by the Indian Bureau, enrollment was denied, and the prior enrollment of the first three children was cancelled.

Suit for mandamus was brought in the courts of the District of Columbia to compel the Secretary of the Interior to restore the children to the supplemental rolls of the Indians and to pay to each of them a per capita share of all future distributions from the fund created under Section 7 of the 

Act of January 14, 1889.20

The Supreme Court of the District of Columbia denied the writ, but this was reversed by the Court of Appeals. The 

Supreme Court, in an opinion by Justice Van Devanter, held that the duty of the Secretary of the Interior in this connection was not plainly prescribed, but that it involves the exercise of judgment and discretion, which cannot be controlled by mandamus. The judgment of the Court of 

Appeals was therefore reversed and that of the trial court was affirmed.

Religious Problems. 

Only two cases involved religious questions. One of these pertained to a chaplaincy which was founded in 1820 by a resident of the Philippine Islands, with a fund of some 17,000 pesos, but which has since then increased to over 173,000 pesos. The plaintiff in this case sought to recover the chaplaincy fund from the Roman Catholic Archbishop of Manila. 

20 No. 77-Wilbur, Sec'y. vs. U. S. ex rel. Kadrie. Decided April 

14, 1930. [421]

The foundress of the chaplaincy provided in her will that the chaplain shall celebrate sixty masses annually in behalf of the souls of her parents, brothers, sisters and herself; that her great grandson shall be the first chaplain, and that the chaplaincy shall be held by her nearest relative. 

The great-grandson was appointed chaplain in 1820. From time to time thereafter, four other descendants of the testatrix were successively appointed, and the latest of these renounced the chaplaincy in December, 1910, married soon afterwards, and in 1912 became the father of the present plaintiff who was a legitimate son of the fifth chaplain and the nearest relative in descent from the first chaplain and from the foundress.

In 1922, when this plaintiff was ten years old, he was presented to the Archbishop for appointment. The Archbishop refused to appoint him, on the ground that he did not have the qualifications required for a chaplain of that chaplaincy, and he based his conclusions upon the Code of Canon Law which, by amendment in 1917, adopted in Rome, provided that no one shall be appointed to a collative chaplaincy who was not a cleric. It further provided that in order to be a cleric one must have had "prima tonsura," and in order to have had "prima tonsura," one must have begun the study of theology, and in order to study theology, one must be a "bachiller," that is, must have obtained the first degree in the sciences and liberal arts. Furthermore, no one may validly receive ordination, unless in the opinion of the ordinary he has had the necessary qualifications. 

It was conceded that the plaintiff in this case lacked the necessary qualifications, but the plaintiff claimed that he was entitled to enjoy the revenues of the foundation, subject only to the duty of saying sixty masses a year, if he is qualified to do so, or to cause them to be said by a qualified priest, any paying the customary charge therefor out of the [422]

income. He also claimed that the provisions of the new 

Code of Canon Law were not applicable, and that his rights were to be determined by the Canon Law in force at the time the chaplaincy was founded.

Upon the Archbishop's refusal to admit him to chaplaincy, the plaintiff brought action in the courts of the Philippine Islands to have a trust declared of the funds which had accumulated in the chaplaincy. The trial court held in favor of the plaintiff, but was reversed by the 

Supreme Court of the Philippine Islands. 

21 This was affirmed by the Supreme Court of the United States in an opinion by Mr. Justice Brandeis. He held that this was not a case of retroactive application of the rules of Canon Law, because at the time when the Canon Law was amended, the plaintiff was not qualified to be a chaplain under the Canon Law as it existed prior to the amendment.

An interesting question also arose as to the jurisdiction of the civil courts in this case. The Archbishop insisted that the property in question had become spiritual property of a perpetual character, subject only to the jurisdiction of the ecclesiastical courts, and that every controversy concerning either the right to appointment or the right to the income was removed from the jurisdiction of the secular courts.

On this point the Supreme Court held: "The objection is not sound. The courts have jurisdiction of the parties. For the Archbishop is a juristic person amenable to the Philippine courts for the enforcement of any legal rights; and the petitioner asserts such a right. There is jurisdiction of the subject matter. For the petitioner's claim is, in substance, that he 

21 No. 6-Gonzalez vs. Roman Catholic Archbishop of Manila. De cided October 14, 1929. [423]

is entitled to the relief sought as the beneficiary of the trust. "The fact that the property of the chaplaincy was transferred to the spiritual properties of the archbishopric affects not the jurisdiction of the court, but the terms of the trust. * * * In the absence of fraud, collusion or arbitrariness, the decisions of the proper church tribunals on matters purely ecclesiastical, although affecting civil rights, are accepted in litigation before the secular courts as conclusive, because the parties in interest make them so by contract or otherwise. Under like circumstances, effect is given in the courts to the determinations of judicatory bodies established by clubs and civil associations."

This happened shortly before the establishment of the new Papal State. Query, would the ruling on the question of jurisdiction have been different, had the controversy arisen after the establishment of the new sovereign power? 

The second case of a religious nature also came from the 

Philippine Islands. Here the father of a deceased child arranged with his relatives to place the body of the child in the family vault for a given period. At the expiration of that period, he attempted to remove the mortal remains of his child from the Catholic to another cemetery. Permission was refused by the church authorities, and he brought suit in the courts of the Philippine Islands to compel the church authorities to permit the removal of the mortal remains of his child. The Philippine courts denied the relief sought. The father petitioned the Supreme Court to review that decision, but the petition was denied.22 

22 No. 326-Abnaldo vs. Bishop of Jaro. Certiorari denied October 

28, 1929. [424]

CHAPTER XVI. 

Judicial Veto 

Laws of Congress.

As a result of the criticism directed against courts for exercising their power to nullify legislation, considerable discussion has arisen in defense of such power. The words "Judicial Veto" have been applied as a term of disapproval concerning the exercise of this power, and the advocates thereof argue that the words "judicial veto" are improperly employed because in declaring laws unconstitutional the judiciary does not veto legislation, but merely interprets it in the light of the Constitution. Others have carried the argument further by saying that the courts do not declare laws unconstitutional at all, but merely declare that these enactments will not be enforced.

We think this is a mere play on words. If a court uses such words as "this act is contrary to the Constitution" or "this act is null and void because it is prohibited by the Constitution," the court is declaring such acts unconstitutional. The consequence may be that the law will not be enforced, but that is because the courts have declared the law unconstitutional. Furthermore, if the action on the part of the Executive, in disapproving a given act of the Legislature, is known as the "Executive Veto", and the term has a neutral or descriptive meaning and not an evaluative meaning, then the ruling of a court that a given enactment is not law might be termed a "judicial veto" with a similar descriptive and non-evaluative connotation. 1 Ever since 1924, when the opposition to this power of [425]

the courts reached its climax, resolutions have been introduced in both Houses of Congress to limit the exercise of this power by the Supreme Court of the United States. 

Similar resolutions have also been introduced in the legislatures of the various states and a number of them have been incorporated as amendments to state constitutions. The last of these resolutions was introduced by Senator Brookhart on April 1, 1930.¹ It proposed "that the Supreme

Court of the United States shall not declare an Act of Congress in violation of the Constitution and invalid except by an unanimous decision of such court." The reason for the resolution is stated in the preamble as being that since the Court has stated it will not declare an Act of Congress unconstitutional, unless the question is free from any reasonable doubt, and since the dissent of a single Justice of the Supreme Court is in itself sufficient to raise a reasonable doubt, no acts should be declared unconstitutional except by unanimous consent.

This resolution followed on the heels of an opinion rendered by Chief Justice Hughes in two cases questioning the power of the Ohio Legislature to limit the judicial power of the Supreme Court of that state.2 The State of Ohio amended its constitution, forbidding the Supreme Court of the state from declaring a state act unconstitutional, unless it did so by a vote of at least six out of the seven judges, except in affirming a decision of the intermediate appellate court, in which case a mere majority is sufficient. It was maintained that this provision of the Ohio constitution was contrary to the provision guaranteeing to every state a republican form of government; second, that it was contrary to the due process clause of the Constitution; and 

171st Congress, 2nd Sess., S. J. Res. 162. 

2 No. 237-Ohio ex rel. Bryant vs. Akron Metropolitan Park District; 

and No. 238-Ohio ex rel. Wadsworth vs. Zangerle. Decided March 

12, 1930. [426]

third, that it was contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution. 

To the first objection, Chief Justice Hughes answered that the provision in the federal Constitution guaranteeing a republican form of government in the states, is a political provision, and relief can be had only from Congress and not from the courts. The remaining objections were likewise overruled on the ground that the due process clause of the federal Constitution does not require the states to provide for appellate courts altogether, and that the equal protection clause is not violated by the mere fact that under this provision it is possible for the Supreme Court of Ohio to arrive at different conclusions in two cases involving the same state of facts.

The appellants in that case severely criticized this provision in the state constitution, and also stressed the fact that criticism against it had been made by the State Supreme 

Court itself. Chief Justice Hughes said, however: "It is urged that the situation has been described as deplorable by the Supreme Court of the State, but it is not for this Court to intervene to protect the citizens of the State from the consequences of its policy, if the State has not disregarded the requirements of the 

Federal Constitution." 

It is interesting to note that Chief Justice Hughes, who delivered this opinion, was in his private life one of the strongest supporters of the judicial veto power. In his book on the Supreme Court of the United States, he developed this question and came to the conclusion that for an understanding of the problem, it is necessary to know not only the laws which have been declared unconstitutional, but also the laws which have been upheld. 

In this connection, however, one must consider also the laws which are in the twilight zone, that is, such acts which [427]

are neither upheld nor invalidated, but so interpreted as to limit their operation. Indeed, the judicial power would be little affected were the courts forbidden to declare Acts of Congress unconstitutional, so long as they can interpret them out of existence.

In this connection it is apt to observe that the courts themselves are not anxious to declare laws unconstitutional and have adopted a rule of interpretation that every presumption will be indulged in to favor the constitutionality of a given act. Furthermore, the Supreme Court has held on previous occasions that it will interpret laws limiting their operation in such manner as to dispel any doubts concerning their validity.³

An illustration of this mode of interpretation is to be found in two cases which were decided by the Supreme Court during this term. Relying on the above rule of interpretation, the United States District Court in Texas limited the provision in the federal Corrupt Practices Act which forbids the soliciting or accepting of contributions from federal employees for any political purpose whatever. The court held that these words must be taken to mean for any federal political purpose, because to hold otherwise might lead to the conclusion that the law was not constitutional. 

The question in the case was whether the provision was applicable to receiving contributions in support of a candidacy for Congress in the primaries. And that court held that it was not, because in accordance with the Newberry Decision, primaries constituted a state matter, which Congress could neither regulate nor control. The decision was reversed by the Supreme Court on the ground that Congress had the power to protect federal employees from 

3 Knights Templars & M. Life Ind. Co. vs. Jarman, 187 U. S. 197, 205. 

4 Newberry vs. United States, 256 U. S. 232. 

[428]

: solicitations for contributions for any political purpose whatever.5 

From the standpoint of those who are in favor of the limitation on the judicial veto power, it may seriously be urged that in this case the court below set aside an express provision in the Act of Congress and substituted its own legislation, on the mere supposition that to give effect to the Act of Congress might throw doubt on its constitutionality. 

From the standpoint of the proponents of this power, it can be answered, of course, that the limitation put on this provision by the trial court had no greater effect than if the court below had declared the statute invalid altogether. It was reversed by the Supreme Court, but this is the latter's function, whenever it finds that the lower court is in error. 

On the other hand, it may be further urged by the opponents of the judicial veto power, that the Supreme Court itself might, by this method, set aside an intent of Congress and substitute its own intent on a mere supposition that otherwise the act might be unconstitutional. 

And this brings us to the second case, the Deciduous

Fruit Case, in which the Supreme Court itself resorted to this mode of interpretation. This case involved the constitutionality of the Hoch-Smith Resolution, which authorized and directed the Interstate Commerce Commission to institute the lowest possible lawful freight rates on agricultural products affected by economic depression. The Court did not declare the resolution unconstitutional or otherwise invalid, but, lest its application might raise doubts as to its constitutionality, it interpreted the resolution as authorizing the Commission to institute such rates, only upon a finding that the existing rates were unreasonably high without ref- 

5 No. 66-United States vs. Wurzbach. See pp. 376, 386 supra. 

6 No. 7-Ann Arbor Railroad Co. et al. vs. United States et al. See p. 56 et seq. supra. [ 429]

erence to the standards prescribed in the Hoch-Smith Resolution. But this the Commission was empowered to do without the Hoch-Smith Resolution, so that as interpreted by the Court, the resolution added nothing to the Commission's power in rate making.

The reason for this limitation, declared the opinion, is that if the resolution was intended to do more than the exist ing law authorized, the provision gives rise to serious constitutional doubts. In other words, so far as this resolution itself was concerned, the interpretation was equivalent to declaring the law unconstitutional, although it did not expressly state so.

In deciding this case, it is interesting to note that Justice Van Devanter, who delivered the opinion of the Court, cited the case of Harriman vs. Interstate Commerce Commission, which we think was a most unfortunate example of statutory construction, in view of the later developments with reference to the problems treated in that case. The 

Interstate Commerce Commission undertook an investigation of railroad investments, summoned Mr. Harriman as a witness and propounded several questions concerning his investments. He refused to answer on the ground that the 

Commission had no authority to require the information. 

When the case came to the Supreme Court, it was decided against the Commission. Mr. Justice Holmes, delivering the opinion of the Court, held that Section 12 of the Interstate Commerce Act authorized the Commission to require information in connection with complaints for violation of the Interstate Commerce Act or in connection with an investigation by the Commission of subjects which might have been the object of a complaint, but that it did not authorize the Commission to require testimony for the purpose of keeping itself informed as to the manner and method in 

7 Harriman vs. Interstate Commerce Commission, 211 U. S. 407. 1 [430]

which the business of common carriers is conducted nor in connection with the enforcement of the requirements in respect of reports to be filed by carriers, nor for the purpose of aiding the Commission to recommend additional legislation to Congress.

In the course of the argument in that case it was urged that the Act must be held contrary to the provision in the Constitution forbidding unreasonable searches and seizures, and the Court interpreted the Act limiting the statute in order to avoid constitutional doubts. Mr. Justice Holmes said: "If we felt more hesitation than we do, we still should feel bound to construe the statute not merely so as to sustain its constitutionality, but so as to avoid a succession of constitutional doubts, so far as candor permits."

Compare the wording of the statute with its interpreta tion. Section 12 of the Interstate Commerce Act provided: 

"The Commission hereby created shall have authority to inquire into the management of the business of all common carriers subject to the provisions of this Act, and shall keep itself informed as to the manner and method in which the same is conducted, and shall have the right to obtain from such common carriers full and complete information necessary to enable the Commission to perform the duties and carry out the objects for which it was created; * * * and, for the purposes of this Act, the Commission shall have power to require, by subpoena, the attendance and testimony of witnesses and production of all books, papers, tariffs, contracts, agreements, and documents relating to any matter under investigation."

In view of the above interpretation, to avoid constitutional doubts, one would expect that if Congress enlarged [431]

these powers of the Commission, they would be declared unconstitutional by the Supreme Court. But that was not the case. It so happened that at the time of the Harriman
