Decision, Section 13 of the Interstate Commerce Act con tained the following provision: "Said Commission shall in like manner investigate any complaint forwarded by the railroad commissioner or a railroad commission of any state or territory, at the request of such commissioner or commission, and may institute any inquiry on its own motion in the same manner and to the same effect as though complaint had been made." 

In 1916, after the Harriman Decision, Section 13 was amended to read as follows: "And the Interstate Commerce Commission shall have full authority and power at any time to institute an inquiry on its own motion in any case and as to any matter or thing concerning which a complaint is authorized to be made, to or before said commission by any provision of this Act, or concerning which any question may arise under any of the provisions of this Act, or relating to the enforcement of any of the provisions of this Act." 

Then came the case of Smith vs. Interstate Commerce

Commission, in which the Commission had required testimony with reference to contributions for political purposes and with reference to alleged attempts to suppress competition. It was urged on the authority of the Harriman Case, that the Commission could have no power to require the information. But the Supreme Court in an opinion by 

Justice McKenna held that the Harriman Case was no longer applicable, because the Act had been amended. 

We say therefore that with such powers of the courts to 

8 Smith vs. I. C. C., 245 U. S. 47. [432]

interpret legislation, it is not of great importance if the judicial veto is limited, especially since few are the instances in which laws are expressly invalidated.

In addition to the above federal laws, several Acts of Congress were considered by the Supreme Court during this term. The Supreme Court upheld the Federal Gift Tax in the Revenue Act of 1924, the provision in the Packers and Stockyards Act empowering the Secretary of Agricul ture to regulate rates chargeable on public stockyards,10 and the Railway Labor Act of 1926, creating the Board of 

Mediation for settling disputes between carriers and their employees and prohibiting interferences with one another during such negotiations." The Court also declined to pass on the constitutionality of the Eighteenth Amendment to the

Constitution, which was attacked because it was not passed by two-thirds of the membership of the House of Representatives, but only by two-thirds of those present.' 12 Several cases involving the validity of federal statutes or their specific applications are still pending for consideration by the Court. The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit held that Congress had no power to impose a tax on income received from the sale of municipal bonds.13 The Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Cir cuit held that Congress could not discriminate in the matter of deducting interest from gross income, when such interest is paid on money borrowed to purchase and carry municipal bonds.¹ In another tax case, the Circuit Court of Appeals 

No. 27-Bromley vs. McCaughn, Coll'r. See p. 214 et seq. supra. 

10 No. 45-Tagg Bros. & Moorehead vs. U. S. et al. See p. 263 et seq. supra. 

11 No. 469-Texas & New Orleans R. R. Co. vs. Brotherhood of Rail way and Steamship Clerks. See p. 267 et seq. supra. 

12 No. 735-Jebbia et al. vs. U. S. See p. 343 supra. 

13 No. 535-Willcuts, Collector vs. Bunn. Case pending. 

14 No. 719-Nauts, Coll'r. vs. Slayton. Case pending. [433]

for the Eighth Circuit held that in computing the net taxable estate, under the federal estate tax, real estate situated in Missouri should not be included, and the Government argued that this decision disturbed the uniformity of the federal tax.15 In addition to the tax cases, there are now pending several cases involving the validity of the Federal Radio Act of 1927, and the orders of the Federal Radio Commission made pursuant thereto, in so far as they purport to terminate the right to broadcast.16 All these cases will be considered by the Court during the coming term. 

State Statutes. 

The question whether the Supreme Court of the United States should have power to declare state laws unconstitutional, though historically paramount to the question of the judicial veto over federal legislation, now appears only as a rider to the latter. The question now resolves itself, not into an argument whether the Court shall have power to nullify state legislation on the ground that it is contrary to the Constitution and laws of the United States, but to a complaint that the Court thwarts the efforts of the states in what may be termed "progressive legislation." This complaint is best expressed in the dissenting opinion of Justice 

Holmes in the case of Baldwin vs. Missouri," in which he said: "I have not yet adequately expressed the more than anxiety that I feel at the ever increasing scope given to the Fourteenth Amendment in cutting down what I believe to be the constitutional rights of the States. As the decisions now stand I see hardly any limit but the sky to the invalidating of those rights if they happen to 

15 No. 556-Crooks, Coll'r. vs. Harrelson. Case pending. 

16 No. 573-White vs. Johnson. Case pending. 

17 No. 417 

Baldwin vs. Missouri. See p. 224 supra. [434]

strike a majority of this Court as for any reason undesirable. I cannot believe that the Amendment was intended to give us carte blanche to embody our economic or moral beliefs in its prohibitions. Yet I can think of no narrower reason that seems to me to justify the present and the earlier decisions to which I have referred. Of course the words 'due process of law' if taken in their literal meaning have no application to this case; and while it is too late to deny that they have been given a much more extended and artificial signification, still we ought to remember the great caution shown by the Constitution in limiting the power of the States, and should be slow to construe the clause in the Fourteenth Amendment as committing to the Court, with no guide but the Court's own discretion, the validity of whatever laws the States may pass." 

During the term the Court had occasion to consider over one hundred state statutes, whose validity was challenged on constitutional grounds. We classify these laws into three main groups: (1) laws under the police power of the state; (2) those coming under the state's powers of eminent domain; and (3) those coming under the state's taxing powers. 

Police Power.

The laws under police powers included measures for the protection of health and safety, for conservation of natural resources, for regulation of trade and commerce, and measures passed for the general welfare. 

Health and Safety. 

Among the health measures considered by the Court was the law of the State of Colorado regulating the practice of dentistry. In this case a corporation was engaged in run- [ 435 ]

ning a "dental parlor," and employed qualified and licensed dentists for the purpose. The attorney general of the state instituted proceedings to oust the corporation from the practice of dentistry within the state because it had not taken an examination in dental surgery and had not secured a license, which as a corporation it could not do. 

The corporation denied that it was practicing dentistry, but the courts of Colorado held that it was, and forbade the corporation from further engaging in this business. This corporation petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case, maintaining that it was deprived of property without due process of law, and that the statute as applied was unconstitutional, because there was no reasonable connection between the ownership of a dental parlor and health and safety, in view of the other laws of the state requiring that those who actually do the dental work shall pass an examination and shall obtain the required license. The Supreme 

Court, however, denied a writ of review in the case.18 

The City of Cincinnati passed an ordinance forbidding any person to remove or transport garbage within the city except those who had contracted with the city for this purpose. Suit was brought in the state courts to enjoin the officers of the city from interfering with the plaintiff's right to haul leftover foods from certain hotels, and to compel them to issue to him a permit to haul this garbage. The 

Ohio courts sustained the officers, and the plaintiff petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision. He argued that he had contracted with the hotels to use leftover food for feeding hogs, and that therefore as applied to him, the ordinance deprived him of his contract rights; that since no objections were found to the manner in which he hauled the food, the refusal to issue a permit deprived him of due 

18 No. 186-Painless Parker Dentist vs. Colorado. Certiorari denied 

October 21, 1929. [436]

process of law, and also that the ordinance was a violation of the equal protection clause, since it did not apply to those who had contracted with the city itself for the removal of garbage. This petition was likewise denied.19 

Toward the end of the term a case came to the Court challenging the constitutionality of the Idaho law regulating the butchering and slaughtering industry, and providing among other things for registration fees, inspection fees, the keeping of records, etc. Several meat dealers brought suit in the U. S. District Court in Idaho to restrain the enforcement of the law, arguing that it was contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution because it exempted from its operation certain dealers, subject to the Federal Packers and Stockyards Act, who were competitors of these complainants. The district court held that the law was a valid exercise of the police power of the state in its effort to protect public health. The dealers appealed to the 

Supreme Court, where the case is still pending.20 

Several health and safety measures considered by the Court were in the form of zoning ordinances. The ordinance of the City of Baltimore, prohibiting the erection of apartment houses in an area limited to six families per acre, was attacked as being without due process of law and contrary to the equal protection clause of the Fourteenth Amendment, because the area was not sufficiently defined, and because there were already several apartment houses within the general vicinity. But after arguments were heard the case was dismissed by the Supreme Cour for lack of a properly presented substantial federal question.21 

The next case questioned the validity of the New Orleans

20 No. 780-Jungst vs. Baldridge. Case pending. 

21 No. 40-Greenway Apartment Co. vs. Convention of Protestant 

Episcopal Church. Dismissed December 9, 1929. [437]

Zoning Ordinance. Here, suit was brought by a realty company to enjoin a grocer from continuing his business in a residential zone of the city. The Louisiana courts upheld the realty company, and the grocer petitioned the Supreme Court to review the decision, arguing that the ordinance violated the due process clause, because it had no relation to health, morals or safety; that it was contrary to the equal protection clause, in that within one block of his place of business there was another grocery, and immediately adjacent to the area (six squares in length by two squares in width) there were other business establishments in operation, such as a gas and electric plant and a cemetery. His petition, however, was denied.22

The City of Oxford, Kans., passed an ordinance prohibiting the drilling of an oil and gas well within the territory designated in the ordinance, without first securing a permit. 

The lower federal courts upheld the validity of the ordinance as a valid exercise of the police power, and the plaintiffs petitioned the Supreme Court to review their decision, arguing that they had a vested property right in the oil and gas before they were extracted, which right the city had taken away without constitutional authority. But the Supreme Court declined to go into the matter.23 

The great majority of the safety measures concerned the operation of railroads, street railways and automobiles. The 

Blow Post Law of Georgia required railroads to erect blow posts at grade crossings and required engineers to signal and to keep a lookout when approaching such crossings. This question arose out of a grade crossing accident, and action was brought against the railroad in Tennessee, where the courts of that state upheld the Georgia law. The railroad 

22 No. 102-McDonald vs. Louisiana, ex rel. Realty Co. Certiorari denied October 14, 1929. 

23 No. 166-Ramsey vs. City of Oxford. Certiorari denied October 

21, 1929. [438]

argued that the law was unconstitutional on the ground that it was a burden on interstate commerce. But the Supreme 

Court affirmed the decision of the Tennessee courts by an equally divided court.24

In another grade crossing collision case, the railroad argued that the prima facie statute of the State of Mississippi was unconstitutional, as applied in that case, because the plaintiff's intestate and his agent, the driver of the car, were violators of law and were guilty of negligence per se. 

But the Supreme Court dismissed the appeal of the railroad for lack of a properly presented federal question in the case.2 25 The Shreveport Railway Company attacked the ordinance passed by the City of Shreveport, La., forbidding the use of one-man cars. The company argued that the ordinance was arbitrary, unreasonable and oppressive in view of the safety devices adopted in the construction of one-man cars; also that it resulted in confiscation, because the use of such cars would effect a saving of some $94,000 annually, and still the company would have to operate without a fair return on its investments. The lower federal courts issued an injunction, and the city petitioned to the Supreme Court to review the case, arguing that in the interest of safety it had the right to demand the use of two-men cars, but the petition was denied.26

On the other hand, the Supreme Court upheld an ordinance of the City of New Orleans, requiring a street railway to remove a viaduct on the ground that it was unsafe and not in accord with the ordinance authorizing its construction. The city ordinance also required that the railway con-

struct double tracks at that point instead of single tracks, to meet the growing transportation requirements of the city. 

In the suit brought by the city to compel compliance with the ordinance, the company defended that the city, by taking part of the street for a sidewalk, had made compliance with the original ordinance impossible, that therefore it could not build the viaduct as agreed upon, and that the ordinance requiring its demolition was therefore contrary to the contract clause of the Constitution. The company also maintained that the viaduct was safe; that the construction of double tracks was unnecessary in view of the present transportation needs of the city, and that since a grade crossing would be dangerous, it could not be said that the ordinance was passed as a safety measure. The Supreme Court of Louisiana sustained the company's contention that the construction of double tracks was not yet necessary. It held, however, that on the basis of the evidence presented, the viaduct was unsafe, and that the city could order its demolition under its police powers. This was also the view taken by the Supreme Court of the United States.27 

The City of Mayfield, Kentucky, likewise emerged victorious when the Illinois Contral Railroad questioned the validity of an ordinance prohibiting the use of the railroad's switching and team tracks over their intersection with a certain street and declaring all switching to be a nuisance, punishable by a daily fine. The railroad maintained that, as a city of the fourth class in Kentucky, the City of Mayfield had no authority to promulgate such ordinance and that it was not a proper measure in the aid of safety. The 

Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit, however, 27 No. 460-New Orleans Public Service, Inc. vs. City of New Orleans. 

Decided June 2, 1930. [ 440 ]

held the ordinance valid, and the Supreme Court declined to review that decision.28

Under the laws of New York (Chap. 534, Laws, 1924) every owner of a motor vehicle is responsible for damage done by any person using the vehicle with the express or implied permission of the owner. A resident of Pennsylvania loaned his automobile to another, who drove it into New York state, where a collision occurred inflicting injuries on the plaintiff. In the action brought against the owner of the automobile, under the New York statute, the latter defended that the statute as applied to a non-resident was contrary to the due process clause of the Constitution. 

The New York courts upheld the constitutionality of the law as applied in this case, and the Supreme Court declined to review their decision.2 29 

Conservation. 

In two cases state conservation laws were attacked. The first of these was the law of Alabama, prohibiting non-residents from catching salt-water shrimp within its waters. 

The defendants, who were non-residents, were apprehended, because they were taking shrimp from the Gulf of Mexico within the three-mile limit of the state. They maintained that the state had no criminal jurisdiction over the waters of the Gulf of Mexico and that their detention was therefore contrary to the Fourteenth Amendment. They also argued that if the state did have criminal jurisdiction in the case, the statute in question was contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution, since it did not prohibit residents of the state from taking shrimp from those waters. 

28 No. 504-Illinois Central Railway Co. vs. City of Mayfield. Cer tiorari denied January 13, 1930. 

29 No. 739-Courson vs. Ridall. Certiorari denied April 28, 1930. 

[441]

The Court of Appeals of Alabama sustained the conviction and the judgment of the lower court. The defendants petitioned the Supreme Court to review the case on this question, but the petition was denied." 30 The second was a law of Nebraska, prohibiting the taking of fish from the Missouri River by means of nets, traps or seines. In 1845 Congress had passed an act giving the states of Nebraska and Iowa concurrent jurisdiction over the river. The complainant brought this suit to restrain the enforcement of the Nebraska law, maintaining that, because of this concurrent jurisdiction, the State of Nebraska could not pass such law, without a similar statute enacted by the 

State of Iowa. The Supreme Court of Nebraska upheld the validity of the state law, and the complainant brought the case to the Supreme Court, which affirmed the state court, in an opinion by Justice Brandeis. The Supreme 

Court held that the State of Nebraska was not precluded from passing such law merely because Iowa had not passed a similar statute.31

Regulation of Commerce. 

Several banking laws were considered by the Supreme Court this term. The Court upheld the banking law of 

North Carolina, which was attacked by a stockholder of a state bank on the ground that under the law, assessments to cover stockholders' liability, when recorded, were given the force of judgments. He argued that the law was unconstitutional, because by giving the recorded assessment the force of a judgment the legislature was invading the judicial power of the courts.32 

30 No. 202-Bosarge vs. Alabama. Certiorari denied October 21, 1929. 

31 No. 261-Miller vs. McLaughlin. Decided April 14, 1930. 

32 No. 184-Murphy vs. Corporation Commission, N. Car. See p. 

160 supra. [442]

A similar banking law of Georgia was also upheld.

There the stockholder argued that the law concerning stockholders' liability could not apply to him since he had purchased his stock prior to the enactment of the statute, and that if the act was retroactively applied, the law must be declared unconstitutional, as being without due process of law; also that the law was unconstitutional in that it did not provide specifically for any form of notice to the stockholder concerning the special meeting at which the assessments were to be levied.33 

Another case attacking a Georgia law regulating banking was dismissed by the Supreme Court for want of a substantial federal question. This was the "Small Loan Act," under which the appellant in the case was prosecuted for having engaged in the business of lending money or credit without having obtained a license from the superintendent of banking of the state.34

The constitutionality of several insurance laws and regulations was considered by the Court. The most important of these was the rate-making order of the Superintendent of Insurance of Missouri, who required all the stock fire insurance companies, doing business in the state, to reduce their insurance rates by ten per cent.35 The Oregon law, requiring every insurance company, having more than two agents in any city with a population over fifty thousand, to pay a license fee of $500 for each additional agent, was attacked as an arbitrary and unreasonable limitation on the agent's right to engage in a lawful occupation. The Court, however, upheld the law as a measure imposed, not on the agent, but on the insurance company, which was not before the 

33 No. 485-Toombs vs. Citizens Bank of Waynesboro. See p. 162 supra. 

34 No. 235-Morgan vs. Georgia. See p. 173 supra. 

35 No. 104-National Fire Insurance Co. vs. Thompson. See p. 131 

supra. [ 443 ]

Court to complain concerning the validity of the act.36 Another law affecting insurance agents was the New Jersey law forbidding any insurance company, doing business in the state, from paying to any of its agents a higher commission than it pays to other agents in the state. The validity of this statute is still to be decided upon by the Court.37 

Probably the most important of the state laws regulating commerce was the Oklahoma Cooperative Cotton Gin Law.

It was argued that the law deprived privately owned cotton gins of equal protection, because cotton gins were declared public utilities and their charges were regulated by the state. A private owner maintained that while cotton gins were not permitted to give rebates, cooperative cotton gins were permitted to divide their profits with their patrons, which was tantamount to giving rebates. The Supreme 

Court upheld the validity of the law, holding that the distribution of profits was not tantamount to giving rebates, and that under the law there was nothing to prevent the owners of private cotton gins from distributing their profits in the same way. 38 

General Welfare. 

Among the laws coming under this rather indefinite class of general welfare, are those pertaining to domestic relations, labor laws, laws regulating morals, laws passed for the protection of beneficiaries of insurance policies and those regulating the relations between the races. 

The labor laws have already been discussed. Here, the 

Supreme Court upheld the validity of the provision in the 

New York Compensation Act, requiring that in case of 

36 No. 17-Herbring vs. Lee. See p. 133 supra. 

37 No. 458-O'Gorman & Young vs. Insurance Company. See p. 134 supra.

death of an employee, where compensation cannot be had under the act, the employer shall pay two sums of $500 each into funds maintained by the state for vocational education of injured employees and for additional compensation to employees who are totally disabled after they had been partially disabled. This act also gave the employer a right to recover these amounts from the party through whose negligence the death occurred.39 Here also we find the

Workmen's Compensation Acts of Pennsylvania and Texas, which were held to be unconstitutional, in so far as they were made applicable to compensate employees injured while working on vessels in navigable waters of the United States.40 

The laws regulating the relation between the races have likewise been discussed. Here we find the Alien Land 

Laws of California¹¹ and Washington¹² and the ordinance passed by the City of Richmond forbidding colored persons from acquiring residences on any block on which the majority of the residents are white.48

The Court had occasion to consider one law protecting beneficiaries of life insurance policies. This was a statute of Florida which provided that proceeds of life insurance shall not be subject to garnishment, claims of creditors or any other legal process, and which was attacked by a creditor of an insured, as being contrary to the equal protection clause of the Constitution, because it discriminated as be- 

39 No. 307-Staten Island Rapid Transit Railway vs. Phoenix Indem nity Co. See p. 284 supra. 

40 No. 62-Baizley Iron Works vs. Span. See p. 285 supra. No. 81Employers' Liability Assurance Corp'n. vs. Cooke. See p. 286 supra. 

41 No. 392-California Delta Farms, Inc. vs. Chinese American Farms, Inc. See p. 415 supra. 

42 No. 763-Northport Power & Light Co. vs. Hartley. See p. 415 supra. 

43 No. 729-Richmond vs. Deans. See p. 417 supra. [ 445 ]

tween general creditors and the wife of the insured, who claimed her dower out of the proceeds of life insurance.** 

The law of Louisiana, regulating horse, dog or other animal racing was upheld by the Court. This law provided that no races shall be held on any race track less than one mile in length, or on one located within fifteen miles from any city or town, and prohibited any races between the hours of 6 P. M. and 6 A. M. The law also imposed a license fee based on the amount of admission charged. Evidently this law was aimed more against dog races than horse races, for horse races are not usually conducted over tracks less than one mile in length nor in the late evening hours. The 

Louisiana Greyhound Club, therefore, claimed that this law was contrary to the due process clause and equal protection clause of the Constitution. It appealed from the decision of the Supreme Court of Louisiana, which sustained the validity of the act, to the Supreme Court of the United States, which, however, affirmed the decision on the authority of a recent case sustaining the validity of the Kentucky law which prohibited dog races altogether. 45 The most important of the general welfare laws dealt with problems of domestic relations. The State of New 

York passed an act providing that where a resident absconds, leaving his wife or children without support, the Commissioner of Public Welfare may have a warrant of attachment issued against the absconding husband's or father's property; the Commissioner may take possession of the property and apply it, under the direction and control of the county court, to the maintenance and support of the wife and children. 

This was the course pursued by the Commissioner against a bank deposit of a resident, who had abandoned his wife and infant child. The bank refused to surrender the money, and 

44 No. 450-Tiffany vs. Davis. See p. 139 supra. 

45 No. 43-Louisiana Greyhound Club vs. Clancy. Decided Decem ber 9, 1929. [446]

the Commissioner brought action to recover judgment for the amount of the deposit. The bank maintained that the issuance of a warrant of attachment was contrary to the Constitution, as taking property without due process of law, since the warrant was made upon an ex parte showing, without any notice whatsoever to the depositor; and should it later appear that the warrant had been improvidently issued, the bank would nevertheless be liable to the depositor in the amount of the deposit. 

The courts of New York sustained the procedure prescribed in the Public Welfare Law as a proper exercise of the state's police power in aid of general welfare, and furthermore held that the bank could not complain of the taking of the property, because it was not the bank's property which was being taken. This was the view adopted by the Supreme 

Court when it affirmed the decision. Mr. Justice Mc- 

Reynolds, who delivered the opinion, furthermore added that when the bank accepted the deposit it knew the possibility that it might be called upon to pay a second time, and accepted the consequent responsibility. "At all events, its position is not materially worse than that of a debtor who must pay one who holds letters testamentary issued upon proof of death, though in truth the creditor may be alive with power to repudiate the appointment."46

Measures in Aid of Police Power. 

There were also several laws passed by states in aid of their police power, such as laws regulating procedure in criminal trials. We have already discussed the law of 

California, which put limitations on the plea of insanity in criminal cases.47 Connecticut passed an act intended to 

46 No. 36-Corn Exchange Bank vs. Coler. Decided January 6, 1930. 1930. 

47 No. 247-Troche vs. California. See p. 382 supra. [447]

limit the increasing amount of litigation arising from automobile accidents. The act provided that a driver of an automobile shall not be liable in damages to a guest for injuries, unless he inflicted the injuries wilfully or unless he was guilty of reckless disregard of the rights of others." 

The Pennsylvania Sedition Law, already discussed, is another example of a law in aid of the self-preservation of the state as a sovereign power. 49 An interesting case under this class came from Missouri, involving the validity of a measure passed in aid of the state liquor law. It prohibited any keeper of a soft drink parlor from maintaining any blinds or screens which would obstruct the view from the sidewalk. Owners of such places of business in St. Louis sought to enjoin the enforcement of this law, but the courts of Missouri upheld it as a proper measure in aid of the state's police power, and the Supreme Court affirmed that decision without an opinion.50 

Eminent Domain.

Among the problems of eminent domain, the most important was the constitutionality of the principle of excess condemnation in condemnation proceedings. The City of

Cincinnati determined to widen certain streets and therefore condemned strips of land necessary for the purpose. It was found, however, that a number of small lots would shrink to very small size, that they had old buildings on them, that they were not worth much, but that they would benefit greatly by the proposed improvement. The city proposed to take by condemnation, not only the land necessary for the widening of the streets, but the entire prop- 

48 No. 24-Silver vs. Silver. See p. 151 supra. 

49 No. 294-Muselin vs. Pennsylvania. See p. 403 supra. 

50 No. 34-Blind vs. Brockman. Decided December 9, 1929. 

[ 448]

erties, for which the city was willing to pay their present value.

Suit was brought by the owners of these properties to enjoin the taking of the excess, on the ground that it was not intended for public use and was therefore not within the city's powers of eminent domain. They claimed that the proposed excess condemnations were based on mere speculation upon an anticipated increase in the value of the properties adjacent to the improvement, and that it was the intention of the city officials to take the properties for the purpose of reselling them at a profit to private individuals, for private purposes, and that no public use was contemplated.

The City of Cincinnati took the position that this excess condemnation was for a public use, for by taking the property it will be able to dispose of it in tracts of such size and with such restrictions as will inure to the public advantage; furthermore, that the increase in value of these properties, which may accrue by reason of the improvement, will pay in part for the heavy expenses involved in the project. 

This problem in condemnation proceedings is a serious one. Often when property is taken by condemnation the owners of the properties taken are benefited rather than injured by the condemnation. Yet there is no way of including the benefit in the measure of just compensation to be paid for the property taken. Various states have therefore resorted to this principle of excess condemnation, so that property adjacent to the improvements is taken before the improvements are instituted with the purpose of reselling such property at the increased value and thus equalize the burden of the improvement. The states of Massachusetts, Ohio, Wisconsin, New York, Rhode Island, Michigan,

California and Virginia have adopted constitutional provisions authorizing excess condemnation. The Cincinnati [449]

case raised the question whether this principle of excess condemnation was contrary to the federal Constitution. 

The Supreme Court, in an opinion by Chief Justice Hughes, decided this case in favor of the property owners, but did not pass on the constitutionality of the principle of excess condemnation. It held that the condemnation in this case was invalid, because the city had failed to specify the reasons for the taking of the property as required by the laws of the state.51

A similar problem in condemnation arises from the fact that while a specific piece of property is condemned for public improvement, the adjacent property, which is likewise to be condemned, rises in value, because it then becomes property abutting on a proposed improvement. In jurisdictions where condemnation proceedings are instituted with respect to each piece of property taken and damages are assessed by juries in each case, it has been felt that the government must pay an increased value because of the very proposed improvements. The State of New York adopted a method of condemnation which, in a measure, does away with this payment for excess value. Property can be acquired by the state by means of "entry and appropriation," leaving the property owner to his remedy to recover just compensation in an action brought by him in the Court of Claims of the state. 

This was the method pursued by the Board of Commissioners of the Palisades Interstate Park when it acquired a tract of land within the region of the Palisades. This property consisted of Tallman Mountain, a conspicuous wooded hill on the west bank of the Hudson at the north end of the Palisades, together with adjacent meadow land between the hill and the river. It appears that it was necessary to act quickly in the matter, for the owner of the property had 

51 No. 372-Cincinnati vs. Vester. Decided May 19, 1930. 

[ 450]

leased it to a rock corporation for quarrying purposes, and the latter began making preparations for commencing quarrying operations and was about to remove the trees, shrubbery and surface soil and make alterations in the appearance of the hill.

After the Commissioners entered upon the property, the owner brought suit in the federal district court in New York to enjoin the Commissioners from further remaining in possession, maintaining that condemnation by entry and appropriation was unconstitutional because it provided for no just compensation, but merely left the owner with a moral obligation on the part of the state to pay a judgment of the Court of Claims rendered in favor of the property owner. 

The federal district court in New York upheld the contentions of the owner, and enjoined the Commissioners from remaining in possession. The Commissioners requested the district court to stay its mandate until the matter was decided by the Supreme Court, but the request was refused. 

Immediately upon retaking possession, the rock corporation applied to the War Department for permission to erect a dock and causeway to the Hudson River and was prepared to destroy the scenic qualities of the property. 

The case was advanced on the Supreme Court's calendar and was decided in short order. This Court held, without passing on the constitutionality of this method of condemnation that the lower court, sitting as a court of equity, could not entertain an action for ejectment; also that the owners had an adequate remedy at law in the state courts and could not resort to the equity jurisdiction of the federal courts merely to determine the constitutionality of the state law.52

Similar questions concerning certainty of compensation 

52 No. 336-White vs. Sparkill Realty Corp'n. Decided February 24, 1930. [451]

came from the states of North Carolina and California. 

The North Carolina case was dismissed because the appellants did not comply with the rules of the Supreme Court.53 

In the California case, the City of Los Angeles instituted proceedings before the Railroad Commission of the state to acquire certain properties of the Southern California Edison Company. Among the objections raised to the Commission's award, the company attacked the constitutionality of the Public Utilities Act of the state in so far as it authorized the taking of property by eminent domain before final provision is made for the payment, thus leaving the owner with a mere moral obligation on the part of the state or of the city to pay just compensation, but without a right on the part of the owner to enforce payment of a judgment recovered for just compensation. The Supreme Court, however, dismissed the company's appeal for lack of a final judgment.** 

The second objection in the Cincinnati excess condemnation case was that the excess property was being taken for private use and that therefore the taking was unconstitutional. A similar question was raised in a case which came from Michigan. The law of that state provides for two types of condemnation proceedings. When a railroad institutes condemnation proceedings, the owner is entitled to retain possession of the property until damages have been finally assessed and paid; he has a right to consequential damages for diminution in value of any part of the tract not taken; his right to damages is ascertained without deduction of benefit accruing from the construction of the railroad, he is entitled to attorney's fees and expenses in addition to damages; he is entitled to a trial by jury; and finally, to a review of the proceedings by appeal. Where the State 

53 No. 91-Suncrest Lumber Co. vs. N. Car. Park Comm'n. Decided 

October 7, 1929. 

54 No. 475-Southern Calif. Edison Co. vs. Railway Comm'n. of 

Calif. Dismissed January 20, 1930. [452]

Highway Commissioner acquires property by condemna tion, all these advantages are lacking. 

In this case the State Highway Commissioner was engaged in carrying out a project for the construction and widening of a state highway between Detroit and Pontiac, and as a part of the project it was proposed to include in the highway the adjacent railroad right of way of the Detroit, Grand Haven and Milwaukee Railway Company. The 

Commissioner, therefore, acquired property by condemnation proceedings to be exchanged for the existing railroad right of way. The owner of the property maintained that the acquisition of the property for the purpose of exchange was not for public use, and was therefore unconstitutional. 

The Supreme Court held that the use in question was a public use and that the owner could not complain of the procedure, since it did not deprive him of just compensation. It held that due process and just compensation do not include attorneys' fees and expenses, nor do they guarantee any form or method of state procedure such as a trial by jury or a right of appeal, and that the equal protection clause of the Fourteenth Amendment does not exact uniformity of procedure in all cases.55

Several problems of eminent domain pertained to the question of jurisdiction over the property to be acquired. 

It was maintained that a village in Illinois had no power to take property for improvements outside of the corporate limits of the village. The Supreme Court of Illinois upheld the validity of the ordinance authorizing the improvement, and the Supreme Court dismissed the appeal for lack of a substantial federal question.56 

In the second case, a county in the State of Washington objected to the proceedings by the state to acquire the 

55 No. 338-Dohany vs. Rogers, Comm'r. Decided April 14, 1930. 

56 No. 343-Dianish vs. Village of Broadview. Dismissed October 28, 1929. [ 453 ]

county's share in an interstate bridge over the Columbia River, on the ground that the bridge was entirely within the jurisdiction of the Secretary of War. The county appealed from the decision of the state courts, but the Supreme 

Court dismissed the appeal for lack of jurisdiction.57 She

In a third case, an Indian sought to set aside condemnation proceedings whereby the City of Tulsa acquired her land for park purposes during her minority. Upon reaching majority, she brought suit to recover the property, contending that the acts of Congress did not permit or authorize condemnation of restricted Indian lands by cities. further maintained, that the condemnation was void because she had no notice or hearing on the proceedings or on the award; also that the only manner in which a Creek Indian could be divested of title before reaching majority was by a guardian sale ordered by a court of competent jurisdiction. 

This suit resulted in favor of the City of Tulsa, and the Indian's appeal to the Supreme Court was dismissed for lack of jurisdiction.58 Taxation. 59 Many tax laws, as usual, were considered by the Court." 

For the purpose of the present discussion these will be classified into (1) those attacked because they were contrary to some specific act of Congress; (2) because the act imposed burdens on the federal Government or its instrumentalities; (3) because they imposed a burden on interstate commerce; (4) because they were without due process, and (5) because they were contrary to the equal protection clause of the federal Constitution. Lynn, Mass., and by the State of Kansas.62 It was contended that the taxes discriminated against national bank shares, contrary to the National Bank Act. These cases have already been discussed. 61 63 (2) The outstanding case involving the problem of tax burdens on instrumentalities of the federal Government was the Missouri tax on insurance companies. The Court held that failure to allow a full deduction from the basis of the tax for United States Government bonds, in addition to the exemptions allowed by the law, constituted a tax burden on those tax-exempt securities. A case equally important and still pending for argument is the tax imposed by the State of Alabama on the federal Government for engaging in the business of power production within the state. The Court also held that the Oklahoma Gross Production Tax on oil could not be imposed on royalties received by an Indian, because under the Treaty between the United States and the Indians, their land and hence the proceeds from the land, were to remain tax exempt. 65 These cases have likewise been considered.

In addition, the Court also had occasion to consider the validity of the California Gross Receipts Tax imposed on those engaged in motor vehicle highway transportation, as applied to one who was carrying parcels post, under a contract with the United States Post Office Department. The taxpayer maintained that the tax was imposed on an instrumentality of the federal Government, also that the tax itself was disproportionate as compared with the use made of the road and hence was a burden on such instrumentality. The 

Supreme Court granted a writ of review, and the case will be argued during the coming term.66 (3) In several cases it was maintained that taxes imposed by the states were burdens on interstate commerce. 

The Supreme Court upheld this contention in respect of the New Jersey Gross Receipts Tax imposed on telephone companies. The law provided that on interstate telephone service the company shall pay a tax on that portion of the gross receipts as the length of the lines within the state bore to the length of the entire line over which the service was rendered. The Court held that the tax was disproportionate to the value of the use of the streets and highways for telephone lines and was therefore a burden on interstate commerce. On the other hand, the Supreme Court upheld the validity of the Illinois franchise tax imposed on corporations doing business within the state, including in the basis of the tax the interstate business done by the corporations.68

(4) The great majority of the tax laws were attacked on the ground that they were contrary to the due process clause of the Fourteenth Amendment. The most important of these related to the question whether the particular tax was within the taxing jurisdiction of the state. Three cases raised the question whether the state may impose an inheritance tax on a debt, due to a non-resident decedent, from the state, or from one of its political subdivisions, or from one of its residents. In two of these the Supreme Court held that the taxes imposed were without due process," while the third case has been restored to the docket for re-argument in the coming term."2 The Court also held that the State of Virginia exceeded its taxing jurisdiction when it imposed a personal property tax on securities of an Illinois corporation, transferred by a resident of Massachusetts to a resident of Maryland in trust for the benefit of a resident of the State of Virginia.73 In two instances the Court held that states had no taxing jurisdiction over property located on federal reservations.74

(5) In these special assessment cases, the argument concerning the due process clause is usually accompanied by the contention that the statute or assessment also violated the equal protection clause of the Fourteenth Amendment. 

The taxpayers claimed that they were taxed too much in comparison with others, and that the discrimination was arbitrary and unreasonable and without any relation to the classification adopted for the purpose of the assessment. In several of the tax cases the complaint was that corporations were discriminated against whereas individuals were favored; for example, where it was maintained that corporate stock was assessed at full value, whereas other property was assessed at sixty-five or seventy-five per cent of their actual value.75

U. S. SUPREME COURT SERVICE. 

Several times in the book, we had occasion to mention the U. S. Supreme Court Service, published by the Legal 

Research Service. This is a loose-leaf device giving members of the legal profession current information on all the cases in the Supreme Court, from the time they are filed until they are finally disposed of. 

Since the Supreme Court is the highest authority on all federal questions, whether arising in the federal or state courts, it is important for lawyers and judges to know whether a given federal question before them is pending before or has recently been decided by the Supreme Court. 

This Service is not intended merely for those who practice in the Supreme Court of the United States. It is especially useful for lawyers and judges who have federal questions in the lower courts, or even before the questions come to the courts. 

1. As an illustration of the usefulness of this Service in cases before they get to the courts, we will take case No. 

367, October Term, 1930, Bankers Reserve Life Co. vs.

United States, in which the petitioner complained that it overpaid a tax of $27,553.35, having included in its net taxable income the sum of $449,345.36, which it had received as interest from tax-exempt securities.

Petitioner brought suit in the Court of Claims to recover the amount, but the suit was dismissed, because the petitioner and the Commissioner of Internal Revenue had entered into an agreement under Section 1106 (b) of the Revenue Act of 1926, closing out the tax for that year. This agreement was entered into while the case of National Life 

Insurance Co. vs. United States was pending before the [461 ]

U. S. SUPREME COURT SERVICE 

Court, and in which the Court later held that a full deduction must be allowed for income from tax-exempt securities, irrespective of the other deductions allowed by the law (277 U. S. 508). The petitioner in this case claimed that it should recover the amount of the tax, because at the time of the agreement it did not know that the National Life Insurance Company case was then pending before the 

Supreme Court. 

This is but an illustration of the many similar instances which have come to our attention. Of course, the use of the 

U. S. Supreme Court Service would have made such an oversight impossible, for the Service reports the cases as soon as they are filed. 

2. The following is an illustration of the usefulness of this Service in cases in the lower federal courts: Recently, the Supreme Court decided that in proceedings for contempt for disobedience of a turn-over order, the bankrupt may not introduce facts antedating the order, as evidence of his inability to comply, and that the decision punishing for contempt need not be based on evidence beyond a reasonable doubt. While this case was pending for decision, the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit decided a similar case, and after the Supreme Court rendered its decision, but before it appeared in the regular reports, counsel for the bankrupt filed a petition for writ of certiorari, without mentioning or distinguishing the recent decision of the Supreme 

Court. Of course, the petition was denied.

The use of the U. S. Supreme Court Service would have saved unnecessary work on the part of counsel in writing the petition and brief, and on part of the Supreme Court in passing on the petition, for the Circuit Court of Appeals and counsel would have known of the case while it was pending in the Supreme Court and would have known of the decision by the Supreme Court on the day after it was rendered. [462]

U. S. SUPREME COURT SERVICE 

3. When a case is being prepared for the Supreme 

Court it is invaluable to know of the cases pending before the Court and how they are decided. To give an illustration: The Supreme Court some time ago denied a petition for a writ of mandamus to compel a district judge to convene a three-judge court in a suit against a municipal officer, holding that the provision in the judicial code, with reference to three-judge courts, applied only to suits brought to enjoin state officers. Shortly after this decision, a petition for writ of mandamus was filed, asking the Court to compel a district judge to convene a three-judge court in a suit against the mayor and council of a municipality. Of course, the petition was denied.

And here is an illustration how the use of the U. S. 

Supreme Court Service does result in the saving of time, energy and effort. Suit was brought in the federal district court in New York against a corporation represented by a subscriber to the U. S. Supreme Court Service. The case involved some difficult constitutional questions, which required a considerable amount of research. Shortly before the bill of complaint was filed, a case came to the Supreme Court of the United States from the federal district court in North Carolina, which involved similar questions. The case had not yet been reported in the Federal Reporter, but was reported in the U. S. Supreme Court Service. Upon request from the subscriber, the Legal Research Service furnished him with a copy of the lower court's opinion, taken from the unprinted record in the case. It was an elaborate opinion, and saved the subscriber a great amount of research. This was all the use which the subscriber could make of the North Carolina case, because the appellants did not comply with the rules as to the printing of the record, and the appeal was dismissed by the Supreme Court. [463]

U. S. SUPREME COURT SERVICE 

In the meantime, however, another case involving simi lar questions came from the Supreme Court of California. 

That case was decided by the state court without an opinion, and as it happened, the case was later disposed of by the Supreme Court of the United States, likewise without an opinion. Ordinarily, that case would have been lost entirely to the legal profession. The U. S. Supreme Court 

Service, however, carried a report on the case, and when the briefs were filed they were loaned to the subscriber. These briefs contained a thorough discussion of the law on the subject, which was of tremendous benefit to the subscriber. 

For further information, write to the Legal Research 

Service, Washington, D. C. [ 464]

.
